The Discourses

The full lectures behind the Enchiridion: a former slave teaching Rome's young gentlemen what freedom actually is

by Epictetus

c. 108 AD 2026

This is an AI modernization of The Discourses into contemporary English. The original is available from Standard Ebooks. Also available as an epub.

Introduction

Very little is known about the life of Epictetus. He is said to have been born at Hierapolis in Phrygia, a town between the Maeander and one of its branches, the Lycus. Hierapolis is mentioned in Paul's letter to the Colossians (Colossians 4:13), from which it has been concluded that there was a Christian church there in the apostle's time. The date of Epictetus' birth is unknown. The only recorded fact about his early life is that he was a slave in Rome, and that his master was Epaphroditus, a dissolute freedman of the emperor Nero. There is a story that this master broke his slave's leg by torturing him; but it is better to trust the evidence of Simplicius, the commentator on the Encheiridion, or Manual, who says that Epictetus was physically weak and lame from an early age. How he came to be a slave is not stated, though it has been asserted in modern times that his parents sold the child. I have found no authority for that.

We may suppose that the young slave showed intelligence, since his master sent him — or allowed him to go — to the lectures of Gaius Musonius Rufus, an eminent Stoic philosopher. It may seem strange that such a master should have wanted his slave turned into a philosopher, but Jean-Jacques Garnier, author of a Mémoire sur les ouvrages d'Épictète, explains the matter very well in a letter to Johann Schweighäuser. Garnier writes: "Epictetus, born at Hierapolis in Phrygia of poor parents, apparently owed the advantages of a good education to a fashion common at the end of the Republic and under the first emperors, by which the great men of Rome liked to count grammarians, poets, rhetoricians and philosophers among their many slaves — much as rich financiers in later ages have been moved to assemble large and costly libraries. This is the only supposition that can explain to us how a wretched child, born as poor as Irus, came to receive a good education, and how a rigid Stoic came to be the slave of Epaphroditus, an officer of the Imperial guard. For we cannot suppose that the confidant and manager of Nero's debaucheries would have wanted such a slave out of any liking for Stoic doctrine, or for his own use."

Some writers assume that Epictetus was freed by his master, but I can find no evidence for it. Epaphroditus went with Nero when he fled from Rome before his enemies, and helped the wretched tyrant to kill himself; Domitian afterwards put Epaphroditus to death for that service (Suetonius, Domitian 14). We may conclude that Epictetus somehow obtained his freedom and began to teach at Rome; but after Domitian expelled the philosophers from the city in 89 AD, he withdrew to Nicopolis in Epirus, a city Augustus had built to commemorate his victory at Actium. There Epictetus opened a school, or lecture room, and taught until he was an old man. The date of his death is unknown. He never married, as we learn from Lucian. When Epictetus was finding fault with Demonax and advising him to take a wife and have children — since this too, Epictetus said, was a philosopher's duty, to leave another in his own place in the universe — Demonax refuted the doctrine by answering: then give me one of your daughters, Epictetus. Simplicius says that Epictetus lived alone for a long time. At last he took a woman into his house as a nurse for a child, whom one of his friends had been going to expose because he could not afford to keep it; Epictetus took the child and brought it up.

Epictetus wrote nothing. Everything we have under his name was written down by an affectionate pupil, Arrian — later the historian of Alexander the Great — who tells us himself that he took the philosopher's discourses down in writing (see Arrian's letter to Lucius Gellius). These discourses made eight books, of which only four survive, under the title Diatribai, the Discourses. Simplicius states in his commentary that the Encheiridion, or Manual, was also put together by Arrian, who picked out from the discourses whatever he thought most useful, most necessary, and most likely to move men's minds. Simplicius adds that the contents of the Encheiridion are found almost entirely, and in the same words, scattered through the Discourses. Arrian also wrote a work on the life and death of Epictetus. The events of a scholar's life were probably neither many nor remarkable, but we should have been glad if that book had survived, since — as Simplicius says — it told what kind of man Epictetus was.

Photius lists among Arrian's works Conversations with Epictetus, in twelve books. Upton thinks this is only another name for the Discourses, and that Photius has mistaken the Conversations for a different work; yet Photius counts eight books of the Discourses and twelve of the Conversations. Schweighäuser observes that Photius cannot have seen these works of Arrian at all, which he infers from how briefly Photius notices them. And in fact Photius does not say he has read them, as he generally does say of the books he lists in his Bibliotheca. The conclusion is that we cannot be certain there ever was a work by Arrian called the Conversations of Epictetus.

The Discourses, together with the Encheiridion and the fragments, were translated into English by the learned Elizabeth Carter, who is said to have lived to the age of eighty-nine. The fourth edition, of 1807, contains her last additions and alterations. That translation has an introduction giving a summary view of Stoic philosophy by way of explaining Epictetus, and notes as well. The editor of the fourth edition says that "the Introduction and notes of the Christian translator of Epictetus are, in the estimation of most readers, not the least valuable parts of the work," adding that "this was also the opinion of the late Archbishop Secker, who though he thought very highly of the philosophy of Epictetus, considered the Introduction and notes as admirably calculated to prevent any mistake concerning it, as well as to amend and instruct the world." The introduction is certainly useful, though not free from errors. The notes I do not think valuable. I have used some of them without comment, and others with remarks of my own where I thought Carter mistaken about the original text, or about other matters.

Carter's translation is a good one, and perhaps no Englishman of that time would have made a better. My first intention was to revise it and correct whatever errors I found. I had revised about half when I found I was not satisfied with the work, and a learned friend advised me to translate the whole myself. This was rather a large undertaking for an old man now past seventy-six. I have done it, however, with great care, and as well as I could. I have always compared my translation against the Latin version and against Carter's, which I think the best way of avoiding the errors any translator may fall into. A man who has never tried to translate a Greek or Latin author does not know how hard it is. What seems plain enough while you are reading it often turns very difficult when you try to say it in another language. Epictetus is generally intelligible, it is true; but the manner of the author — or rather of Arrian, who was trying to reproduce what he heard — is sometimes made obscure by the constant run of questions and answers, and for other reasons besides.

Upton remarks, in a note on Book Three, chapter 23, that "there are many passages in these dissertations which are ambiguous or rather confused on account of the small questions, and because the matter is not expanded by oratorical copiousness, not to mention other causes." The discourses of Epictetus were, it is supposed, spoken without preparation, so that one thing after another would come into the speaker's mind as he went (Wolf). Schweighäuser likewise observes that the thread of the argument is sometimes obscure because words needed to show the connection of thought have been left out. The reader will therefore find that he cannot always understand Epictetus unless he reads him very carefully, and some passages more than once. He must think and reflect as well, or he will miss the meaning. I do not say the book is worth all this trouble. Every man must judge that for himself. But I should not have translated it if I had not thought it worth studying, and I think all books of this kind need careful reading, if they are worth reading at all.

The text of Epictetus is corrupt in places, and that corruption causes a few difficulties. They are not numerous enough, however, to cause or even to allow much variety among translations. This will explain why many parts of my translation are the same, or nearly the same, as Carter's. Where that happened, I did not think it necessary to alter my version merely so that it should differ from hers. I made my translation first, and then compared it with hers and with the Latin. I hope I have not made many blunders. I do not suppose I have made none.

The last and best edition of the Discourses, the Encheiridion and the fragments is Johann Schweighäuser's, in six octavo volumes. It contains Simplicius' commentary on the Encheiridion and two volumes of useful notes on the Discourses. These notes are selected from Wolf, Upton and a few other commentators, but a large part of them are Schweighäuser's own, and he was an excellent scholar and a very sensible man. I have read all of them, and I have used them. Many of the notes to this translation are my own.

Arrian's Letter to Lucius Gellius

I did not write these Discourses of Epictetus the way a man writes a book, and I did not make them public myself — indeed, I say I did not write them at all. Whatever I heard him say, I tried to set down in his own words, as nearly as I could, so that I would have a record afterwards of how he thought and how freely he spoke.

They are, naturally enough, the sort of thing one man says to another on the spur of the moment, not the sort of thing anyone writes for others to read. Being what they are, I have no idea how they came into the public's hands, without either my consent or my knowledge.

It matters little to me if I am thought incompetent as a writer, and it matters not at all to Epictetus if anyone despises what he said. At the time he was speaking, it was plain that he had no other aim than to move the minds of the people listening toward the best things. If these Discourses do that, they will have done what I think the words of philosophers ought to do. If they do not, then let whoever reads them know this much: when Epictetus delivered them, the man who heard him could not help being affected exactly as Epictetus meant him to be. And if the Discourses on the page do not manage it, then perhaps the fault is mine — or perhaps it could not have been otherwise.

Farewell.

Book One

Chapter 1: What Is Up to Us and What Is Not

Of all our capacities, you will find not one that can examine itself — and therefore not one that can approve or disapprove of anything. Except one. I will come to it.

Take the art of grammar. How far does its power of examination reach? As far as judging what is written and spoken. And music? As far as judging melody. Does either of them examine itself? Not at all. If you have to write to your friend, grammar will tell you which words to use; whether to write at all, grammar will not tell you. The same with music and musical sound: whether this is the moment to sing and play the lyre, or to do neither, music will not tell you.

What will tell you, then? The one capacity that examines both itself and everything else. And what is that? Reason — the rational faculty, to give it its name. It is the only thing we were given that examines itself: what it is, what it can do, what such a gift is worth. And it examines every other capacity as well. What else tells us that gold is beautiful? Gold does not say so itself. Plainly it is the thing in us that judges impressions. What else judges music and grammar and the rest, works out what they are good for, and points out the moment to use them? Nothing else does.

And so, as was fitting, the one thing the gods put in our power is the thing that is best of all and rules everything else: the right use of impressions. The rest they did not put in our power. Some things are up to us, and some are not. Was that because they did not want to? For my part, I think that if they had been able to, they would have handed us the rest as well — but they could not. We are here on the earth, tied to a body like this and to companions like these. How could we possibly not be blocked by externals?

But listen to what Zeus says. "Epictetus, if it had been possible I would have made this little body of yours and this little property of yours free, and beyond anyone's reach. But do not fool yourself about this: the body is not yours. It is clay, finely mixed. Since I could not give you that, I have given you a portion of myself — this capacity to go after a thing and to refuse it, to want and to decline; in a word, the capacity to use impressions. Look after that, treat it as the only thing you own, and you will never be blocked, never stopped. You will not moan. You will not blame anyone. You will not flatter anyone."

Well? Does that strike you as a small thing? I hope not. Then be content with it, and pray to the gods.

But as it is, when we could look after one thing and attach ourselves to that alone, we would rather look after a dozen and be tied to every one of them — to the body, to property, to a brother, a friend, a child, a slave. Tied to so many things, we are weighed down and dragged under. That is why, when the weather is wrong for sailing, we sit there tormenting ourselves, forever going out to see which way the wind is blowing. North. And what is that to us? When will the west wind blow? When it decides to, my good man — or when Aeolus decides. God did not make you manager of the winds. He made Aeolus.

So what follows? That we make the best use we can of what is up to us, and take everything else as it comes. And how does it come? However God pleases.

"So am I the only man who has to lose his head?"

Why — would you have the whole world beheaded, to console you? Will you not stretch out your neck the way Lateranus did at Rome, when Nero ordered his execution? He stretched out his neck, took a feeble blow, flinched back for a moment, and stretched it out again. And a little before that, when Nero's freedman Epaphroditus came to ask him what his offense had been, he said: "If I decide to say anything, I will say it to your master."

What should a man have ready for a time like that? What else but this: what is mine and what is not mine; what I am allowed and what I am not.

I have to die. Do I have to die groaning as well? I have to be put in chains. Do I have to whine about it too? I have to go into exile. Is anyone stopping me from going with a smile, cheerful and content?

"Tell me the secret you are keeping."

I will not. That much is up to me.

"Then I will put you in chains."

Man, what are you talking about? Chain me? You can chain my leg, but not even Zeus can conquer my will.

"I will throw you in prison."

My poor body, you mean.

"I will cut your head off."

When did I ever tell you that mine was the one head that could not be cut off?

These are the things philosophers ought to be turning over. These are what they should be writing down every day. This is what they should be training on.

Thrasea used to say: I would rather be killed today than banished tomorrow. And what did Rufus say to him? "If you choose death as the heavier of the two, what a foolish choice that is. And if as the lighter — who gave you the choice at all? Why not train yourself to be content with what you have been given?"

And Agrippinus — what did he say? "I am not going to get in my own way." Word came that his trial was going on in the Senate. "I hope it turns out well," he said. "But it is the fifth hour" — the hour when he always took his exercise and then a cold bath — "let us go and take our exercise."

When he had finished, a man came and told him: you have been condemned.

"To exile," he said, "or to death?"

To exile.

"And my property?"

It has not been taken.

"Then let us go to Aricia," he said, "and have dinner."

This is what it means to have studied what a man ought to study: to have made desire and avoidance proof against being blocked, proof against everything you would rather not meet. I have to die. If now, then I am dying now. If in a little while, then I will dine, since it is the dinner hour, and die afterwards. How? Like a man handing back what was never his.

Chapter 2: How to Keep Your Own Character in Every Situation

Only to a rational creature is the irrational unbearable. What is rational, he can bear. Blows are not naturally unbearable. How so? Look at the Spartans, who take a whipping calmly once they have learned that the whipping makes sense. Hanging yourself is not unbearable either: the moment you form the judgment that it is the rational thing, off you go and hang yourself. In short, if we look, we shall find that nothing gives a human being so much pain as what he finds irrational, and nothing draws him so strongly as what he finds rational.

But the rational and the irrational look different to different people, exactly as the good and the bad do, and the profitable and the unprofitable. That, above all, is why we need training: to learn how to fit our preconception of the rational and the irrational to particular cases, in line with nature. And in settling what is rational and what is not, we do not only weigh up externals; we also take into account what suits the particular person.

For one man it is perfectly rational to hold a chamber pot for someone else, looking no further than this: that if he does not hold it he will be beaten and go without food, and if he does hold it nothing hard or unpleasant will happen to him. To another man, holding the pot is unbearable for himself — and unbearable, too, that anybody else should do that service for him. So if you ask me whether you should hold the pot, I shall tell you that getting your food is worth more than not getting it, and being flogged is a worse indignity than not being flogged; so if that is the scale you measure your interests by, go and hold the pot.

"But it would be beneath me."

Then it is you who must bring that consideration into the reckoning, not I. You are the one who knows yourself — how much you are worth to yourself, and the price you sell yourself at. Men sell themselves at very different prices.

That is why, when Florus was deliberating whether to go down to Nero's shows and perform in them himself, Agrippinus told him: "Go down."

"Why don't you go down yourself?" Florus asked.

"Because I do not even deliberate about it. Once a man brings himself to deliberate about such things, and to start pricing up externals, he is very close to the people who have forgotten who they are. Why ask me whether death is preferable, or life? I say life. Pain or pleasure? I say pleasure. — But if I don't act in the tragedy, my head comes off. — Then go and act. I will not. — Why not? — Because you count yourself as one thread among the threads of a tunic. Well, then it is proper for you to see to it that you look like everyone else, just as a thread has no ambition to be anything more than the other threads. But I want to be the purple — that small bright part which makes all the rest look handsome and fine. So why tell me to make myself like the crowd? And if I do, how am I still purple?"

Priscus Helvidius saw this too, and acted on it. When Vespasian sent word forbidding him to go into the senate, he answered: "It is in your power not to let me be a senator. But as long as I am one, I have to go in."

"Go in, then," said the emperor, "but say nothing."

"Do not ask my opinion, and I will be silent."

"But I have to ask your opinion."

"And I have to say what I think is right."

"But if you do, I shall have you killed."

"When did I ever tell you I was immortal? You will do your part and I will do mine. Yours is to kill; mine is to die, and not in fear. Yours is to banish; mine is to go without grieving."

And what good did Priscus do, one man on his own? What good does the purple do for the toga? Why, just this: that it stands out in the toga as purple, and is set there as a fine example for everything else. In the same circumstances another man would have answered Caesar's ban with, "Thank you for sparing me." But Vespasian would never have troubled to forbid such a man the senate at all. He knew he would either sit there like a clay pot, or, if he did speak, would say what Caesar wanted — and throw in a little extra.

An athlete behaved the same way when he was in danger of dying unless his genitals were amputated. His brother, who was a philosopher, came to him and said: "Come, brother, what are you going to do? Are we going to cut this away and go back to the gymnasium?" The athlete would not be moved, and died.

When somebody asked Epictetus whether he had done it as an athlete or as a philosopher: As a man, he replied — a man who had been announced among the competitors at Olympia and had fought there, a man at home in such a place, and not merely rubbed with oil in Baton's training school. Another man would have let even his head be cut off, if he could have lived without it.

That is what regard for your own character amounts to, in people who are used to bringing it into their deliberations of their own accord, along with everything else.

"Come on then, Epictetus, shave off your beard."

If I am a philosopher, I say, I will not shave.

"Then I will have your head off."

If it will do you any good, take it off.

Somebody asked: how is each of us to work out what suits his own character?

How is it, he replied, that when the lion attacks, the bull alone discovers what he can do and puts himself in front of the whole herd? Plainly, having the power and knowing you have it come together; so any of us who has such power will not be in the dark about it. But a bull is not made all at once, and neither is a brave man. We have to train through the winter for the summer's campaign, and not go rushing at what is none of our business.

Only think what price you are selling your own will at. If for no other reason, then at least for this one: do not sell it cheap. The great and superior sort of thing may belong to Socrates and his like. Then why, if this is our nature too, are there not a great many more of us like him? Do all horses turn out fast? Are all dogs good on a scent? Well then — since I am naturally slow, am I to take no trouble on that account? I hope not. Epictetus is no better than Socrates; but if he is not worse, that is enough for me. I shall never be a Milo, and yet I do not neglect my body. I shall never be a Croesus, and yet I do not neglect my property. And in general we do not give up looking after anything just because we despair of being the best at it.

Chapter 3: What Follows from God Being the Father of All

If a man could really assent to this doctrine as he ought — that we are all descended from God in a special way, and that God is the father of men and of gods alike — I do not think he would ever have a low or shabby thought about himself again. If Caesar adopted you, nobody could stand your arrogance. And if you know that you are the son of Zeus, will you not be puffed up?

Yet we are not. Two things are mixed together in the making of a man: a body, which we share with the animals, and reason and intelligence, which we share with the gods. And most of us lean toward the wretched, mortal kinship, and only a few toward the divine and happy one.

Now, since every man necessarily deals with everything according to the judgment he holds about it, the few — the ones who think they were made for loyalty and self-respect and the sound use of impressions — never have a low or shabby thought about themselves. With the crowd it is the exact reverse. "What am I?" they say. "A poor miserable creature, and this wretched bit of flesh of mine."

Wretched it is. But you have something better than a bit of flesh. Why neglect the better thing, and cling to this?

Because of that kinship with the flesh, some of us who lean toward it turn into wolves — treacherous, faithless, out to do harm. Some turn into lions, savage and wild and untameable. But most of us turn into foxes, or something worse. What else is a slanderer, a man with malice in him, but a fox — or some meaner and more miserable animal still?

So look to it. Take care you do not become one of these wretched things.

Chapter 4: On Making Progress

The man who is making progress has learned from the philosophers that desire means desiring good things and avoidance means avoiding bad ones. He has learned, too, that a man gets no happiness and no peace of mind except by never failing to get what he desires and never falling into what he wants to avoid. So he strips desire away from himself altogether and puts it off for the time being, and he directs avoidance only at things that depend on his will. Because if he tries to avoid anything outside his will, he knows he will sooner or later run into the very thing he wanted to dodge, and be miserable.

Now if virtue promises good fortune and peace of mind and happiness, then progress toward virtue is certainly progress toward each of those as well. Whatever the finished thing brings us to, progress is an approach to that point.

How is it, then, that we agree virtue is this, and yet go looking for progress somewhere else, and put that on display? What does virtue produce? Peace of mind. So who is making progress? The man who has read a great many books of Chrysippus? Does virtue consist in having understood Chrysippus? Because if it does, then progress is plainly nothing but knowing a great deal of Chrysippus. Yet here we are, admitting that virtue produces one thing while declaring that getting near it is something else entirely.

"So-and-so can already read Chrysippus on his own," somebody says.

Excellent, sir, you are making great progress. Progress of what kind? Why are you mocking the man? Why are you pulling him away from noticing his own troubles? Will you not show him what virtue actually produces, so that he learns where to look for improvement? Look for it where your work is, you wretch. And where is your work? In desire and avoidance — so that you are not disappointed of what you want and do not fall into what you would rather not meet. In pursuit and refusal — so that you make no mistake. In assent and the withholding of assent — that is, in saying yes to an impression or holding back — so that you are not taken in. These come first and they matter most. If you are still trembling and moaning as you try not to fall into what you avoid, tell me: where exactly is the progress?

So show me your progress in these things. If I were talking to an athlete, I would say: show me your shoulders.

"Here are my weights."

You and your weights can look to that. I want to see what the weights have done.

"Take my treatise on impulse, and see how thoroughly I have worked through it."

Slave, that is not what I am asking. I am asking how you exercise pursuit and refusal, desire and avoidance; how you plan and intend and prepare yourself; whether all this is in line with nature or not. If it is, give me the evidence and I will say you are making progress. If it is not — off with you, and not only expound your books but write some of your own. And what will you have gained? Do you not know the whole book costs five denarii? Is the man who expounds it worth more than five denarii, then? So never look for the thing itself in one place and progress toward it in another.

Where is progress, then? If one of you turns away from externals and toward his own will — to work on it and improve it by hard labour, until it is in line with nature, lifted up, free, unrestricted, unblocked, trustworthy and self-respecting; if he has learned that a man who desires or avoids things not in his power can be neither trustworthy nor free, but must inevitably shift as they shift and be knocked about by them as in a storm, and must inevitably make himself subject to anyone with the power to give him or withhold from him what he wants; and if, finally, when he gets up in the morning he watches for these rules and keeps them, bathes like a man who can be trusted, eats like a man with self-respect — if in whatever comes up he works at his governing principles the way a runner works at running and a voice trainer at the voice — that is the man who is truly making progress, and that is the man who has not travelled here for nothing.

But if he has strained every nerve at reading books, and works at nothing else, and travelled here for that, then I tell him to go home again at once and not neglect his business there — because the thing he came for is nothing. The other thing is something: to study how a man may get the moaning and groaning out of his life, and the "poor me, wretch that I am"; how to be rid of misfortune and disappointment; to learn what death is, and exile, and prison, and hemlock, so that he can say when he is in chains, "Dear Crito, if it is the will of the gods that it be so, let it be so" — and not, "Wretched old man that I am, is this what I kept my grey hairs for?"

Who talks like that? Do you suppose I mean some nobody, some man of no standing? Does Priam not say it? Does Oedipus not say it? Every king in the plays says it. For what is tragedy but the sufferings of men who set store by externals, put into that kind of verse? If a man has to be taught by a made-up story that nothing outside his will has anything to do with him, then for my part I am glad of the story, if it lets me live happily and undisturbed. What you want is for you to decide.

So what does Chrysippus give us? The answer is: knowing that these things are not lies — the things that happiness comes from and peace of mind grows out of. Take my books and you will learn how true, how much in line with nature, are the things that leave me undisturbed.

What great good fortune! What a great benefactor, to show us the road! Men have raised temples and altars everywhere to Triptolemus, because he gave us cultivated food. But to the man who found the truth and brought it to light and passed it on to everybody — not the truth about how to stay alive, but about how to live well — which of you has built an altar for that, or a temple, or dedicated a statue, or worships God on account of it? We sacrifice to the gods because they gave us the vine and the wheat. And because they brought forth in the human mind that fruit by which they meant to show us the truth about happiness — for that we will not thank God?

Chapter 5: Against the Academics

If a man sets himself against plain facts, said Epictetus, it is not easy to find an argument that will change his mind. This is not because the man is strong or because the teacher is weak. When a man has been refuted and then sets hard, like stone, what argument is going to reach him?

There are two ways of setting hard: the understanding can do it, and so can the sense of shame — when a man has made up his mind neither to assent to what is obvious nor to give up contradicting it. Most of us dread the deadening of the body, and would do anything to prevent it; the deadening of the soul we do not care about. And with the soul, if a man is in such a state that he cannot take anything in or follow anything at all, we say he is in a bad way; but if his sense of shame and self-respect has gone dead, we actually call that strength.

Do you grasp that you are awake?

"No," the man says, "because I do not grasp it in my sleep either, when I am dreaming that I am awake."

Is there no difference at all, then, between the one impression and the other?

"None."

Am I still to argue with this man? What fire, what iron, am I to put to him to make him feel that he has gone dead? He does feel it, and pretends he does not. He is worse off than a corpse. One man does not see the contradiction: he is in a bad way. Another sees it and is not moved by it and does not improve: he is in a worse way still. His self-respect and his sense of shame have been cut out of him, and his reason has not been cut away but brutalised. And am I to call this strength of mind? Certainly not — unless we are also going to call it strength in men who have lost all shame about sex, and so say and do in public whatever comes into their heads.

Chapter 6: On Providence

From anything at all that exists or happens in the world it is easy to praise Providence, provided a man has two things: the capacity to see what belongs to each person and each thing and what happens to it, and a grateful temper. Without those two, one man will not see what things are good for, and another will not be thankful for them even when he does see.

If God had made colours but not made the power of seeing them, what use would they have been? None whatever. And the other way round: if He had made the power of sight but had not made objects for that power to fall on, what use would it have been then? None whatever. Well, suppose He had made both, and had not made light? No use again. Who is it, then, who fitted this to that and that to this? Who fitted the knife to its sheath and the sheath to the knife? Nobody?

From the very make of a finished thing we are in the habit of concluding that it is certainly the work of some craftsman, and was not put together for nothing. Does each of those things prove a workman, then, and do visible objects and the power of sight and the light not prove Him? And the existence of male and female, and the desire of each for the other, and the ability to use the parts that were built for it — do these not declare the workman too?

If they do not, then consider how our understanding is made. When we meet things through the senses we do not simply take in impressions of them: we select something out of them, we take something away, we add, we put this and that together by means of them, and in fact we move from some things to others that are in a way like them. Is not even that enough to shift some men, and stop them forgetting the workman? If not, let them explain to us what it is that makes each particular thing — or how things so marvellous, so like the contrivances of art, could exist by chance and by their own motion.

Well then, is all this done only in us? A great deal of it is done only in us, being what a rational creature especially needed. But you will find much that we share with the animals. Do they understand what is being done, then? Not at all. Using a thing and understanding it are two different things. God needed the animals to use impressions, and needed us to understand the use of impressions. That is why for them it is enough to eat and drink and sleep and breed and do the rest of what each of them does; whereas for us, to whom He also gave the power of thought, those things are not enough. Unless we act properly and in an orderly way, in line with the nature and make of each thing, we shall never reach our true end. For where creatures are differently made, their actions and their ends are different too. In a creature made only for use, use by itself is enough; but in a creature that can also understand the use — man — there is no reaching his proper end without the exercise of understanding.

So God makes each animal for something: one to be eaten, another to work the land, another to give cheese, another for some similar use. What need have any of those to understand impressions and tell one from another? But God brought man in to be a spectator of God and of God's works — and not only a spectator, an interpreter. That is why it is a disgrace for a man to begin and end where the animals do. He ought rather to begin where they begin, and to end where nature ends in us; and nature ends in contemplation and understanding and a way of life in line with nature. So see that you do not die without having been a spectator of these things.

You travel to Olympia to look at the work of Phidias, and every one of you counts it a misfortune to die without having seen such things. But where no travelling is needed at all — where a man already stands with the works of God in front of him — will you not want to look at them and understand them? Will you not notice what you are, or what you were born for, or what this power of sight was given to you for?

"But there are disagreeable and difficult things in life."

And at Olympia there are not? Are you not scorched there? Are you not crushed by the crowd? Have you anywhere decent to bathe? Do you not get soaked when it rains? Do you not have your fill of noise and shouting and the rest of it? But I imagine you set all that against the magnificence of the spectacle, and put up with it.

Well then — have you not been given capacities to bear whatever happens? Have you not been given greatness of spirit? Courage? Endurance? If I have greatness of spirit, why should I trouble myself over anything that can happen? What is going to distract me, or disturb me, or look painful to me? Am I not to use the power for the purpose I was given it for, instead of sitting there grieving and moaning over events?

"Yes, but my nose runs."

What have you got hands for, slave? Is it not so that you can wipe your nose?

"So is it reasonable that there should be running noses in the world at all?"

How much better it would be to wipe your nose than to find fault with the universe.

What do you suppose Hercules would have amounted to if there had been no lion, no hydra, no stag, no boar, and no brutal and criminal men for him to drive out and clear away? What would he have been doing if none of it had existed? Plainly he would have rolled himself up and gone to sleep. In the first place, then, he would never have been Hercules, dozing his whole life away in that comfort. And even if he had been, what use would he have been? What use his arms, and the strength of the rest of him, and his endurance and his noble spirit, if circumstances had never come along to rouse him and train him?

Well, is a man to lay on such training for himself, then — to import a lion into his country from somewhere, and a boar, and a hydra? That would be folly and madness. But since they did exist, and were there to be found, they served to show what Hercules was and to give him his exercise.

Come then, you too: notice this, look at the capacities you have, and when you have looked at them, say — send me now, Zeus, any difficulty you please, for I have the means you gave me and the powers to do myself honour through whatever happens.

But no. You sit there trembling in case something happens, and weeping and moaning and groaning over what does happen, and then you blame the gods. What can come of such smallness of spirit but irreverence? And God has not only given us these capacities, by which we shall be able to bear anything that happens without being crushed or broken by it: like a good king and a true father, He has given them to us unblocked, subject to no compulsion, nothing standing in their way, and put them entirely in our own power — without even keeping back for Himself any power of blocking or hindering them.

And you, who were given these powers free and for your own, do not use them. You do not even see what you were given, or who gave it. Some of you are blind to the giver and will not so much as acknowledge your benefactor; others, out of smallness of spirit, take to fault-finding and bringing charges against God.

Yet I will show you that you have powers and means for greatness of spirit and courage. What powers you have for fault-finding and making accusations — that you can show me.

Chapter 7: On the Use of Trick Arguments and Hypotheticals

Handling trick arguments and hypothetical ones, and those that draw their conclusions out of questioning — in a word, handling arguments of every such kind — has to do with the duties of life, though most people do not know it. In every matter we are asking how the wise and good man is to find the right road and the right method of dealing with it. So let people either say that a serious man will not go down into the contest of question and answer, or else that if he does go down into it, he will take no care to avoid conducting himself rashly and carelessly in the questioning and the answering. And if they will allow neither of those, they must admit that some inquiry ought to be made into the very subjects on which questioning and answering are chiefly employed.

For what is the aim of reasoning? To establish true propositions, to get rid of false ones, and to withhold assent where a thing is not plain. Is it enough, then, to have learned only that much?

"It is enough," a man may say.

Is it enough, then, for a man who wants to make no mistake in handling coin, to have heard the rule that he should take the genuine drachmas and refuse the counterfeit? It is not. What has to be added to the rule? What else but the capacity that tests genuine drachmas and counterfeit ones and tells them apart? So in reasoning too, what has been said is not enough: a man must acquire the capacity to examine the true and the false and the not-plain, and to tell them apart. He must.

And besides that, what does reasoning set out to do? That you should accept what follows from what you have properly granted. Well, is it enough here just to know that? It is not. A man has to learn how one thing is a consequence of others, and when one thing follows from one thing, and when it follows from several things taken together. Consider, then, whether this power too must not be acquired by anyone who intends to conduct himself skilfully in reasoning: the power of demonstrating for himself the various things he has put forward, the power of following other men's demonstrations, and of not being taken in by sophists who are only pretending to demonstrate. That is why the practice and exercise of valid arguments and figures has grown up among us, and it has been shown to be necessary.

But it does in fact happen sometimes that we have properly granted the premises, and something results from them which is not true, and results all the same. What am I to do then? Am I to admit the falsehood? How can I? Well then, am I to say that I did not properly grant what we agreed on? I am not allowed to do that either. Am I to say that the conclusion does not follow from what was conceded? That is not allowed either.

So what is to be done? Consider whether it is not this. Having borrowed money is not enough to make a man still a debtor: there has to be added the fact that he goes on owing it and has not paid it back. In the same way, having granted the premises is not enough to force you to admit the inference — you must also stand by what you granted. If the premises stay to the end just as they were when they were granted, then we are absolutely bound to stand by what we granted and to accept the consequences. But if the premises do not stay as they were when granted, then we are just as absolutely bound to withdraw from what we granted, and to refuse a conclusion that does not follow from the words our concession was made in. For the inference is no longer ours, and no longer results with our assent, since we have withdrawn from the premises we granted.

So we ought to examine premises of this kind, and the changes and shifts of meaning in them by which — in the course of questioning or answering, or in drawing the syllogistic conclusion, or in some other such way — the premises come to vary and give foolish men occasion to be thrown into confusion, if they do not see what the consequences are. And why ought we to examine them? So that in this matter we may not be employed improperly or in a muddle.

The same goes for hypotheses and hypothetical arguments. Sometimes it is necessary to ask that a hypothesis be granted, as a kind of bridge to the argument that follows. Must we then allow every hypothesis put to us, or not every one? And if not every one, which ones? And when a man has allowed a hypothesis, must he in every case stand by having allowed it? Or must he sometimes withdraw from it, and accept the consequences but not accept contradictions?

Yes — but suppose a man says: if you admit the hypothesis of something possible, I will drag you to something impossible. Is a man of sense to refuse to take that on, and dodge discussion and conversation with him? But who else besides the man of sense can use argument, and be skilful at questioning and answering, and impossible to cheat and deceive with false reasoning? And is he to go into the contest and then not take care to argue neither rashly nor carelessly? And if he takes no care, how is he the man we take him to be? Yet without some such training and preparation, can he keep up a continuous and consistent argument? Let them show that he can, and all these speculations become unnecessary — absurd, and out of keeping with our idea of a good and serious man.

Why are we still lazy and slack and sluggish? Why do we look for excuses to avoid working and keeping watch over our own reason?

"So if I go wrong in these matters, is it as though I have killed my father?"

Slave, where was there a father here for you to kill? What you have done is the only fault it was possible to commit here.

That is exactly the remark I made to Rufus when he found fault with me for failing to spot the one thing left out of a certain syllogism. I suppose, I said, that I have burnt down the Capitol.

Slave, he replied, was the thing left out here the Capitol? Or are these the only crimes there are — burning the Capitol and killing your father? To use the impressions put before you rashly and foolishly and carelessly; to fail to follow an argument, or a demonstration, or a piece of sophistry; in a word, not to see in questioning and answering what is consistent with what we have granted and what is not — is there no error in that?

Chapter 8: Why Logical Skill Is Not Safe in the Untrained

There are as many ways of varying the forms of arguments and enthymemes in argument as there are of exchanging equivalent expressions for one another. Here is an instance. If you have borrowed and not repaid, you owe me the money. You have not borrowed and you have not repaid; therefore you do not owe me the money. To do this skilfully suits no man better than the philosopher. If an enthymeme is an incomplete syllogism, then plainly a man trained in the complete syllogism must be just as expert in the incomplete one.

Why, then, do we not train ourselves and each other in this way? Because — I answer — as things stand, even though we do not train in these matters, and even though I at least do not draw you away from the study of how to live, we still make no progress in virtue. So what are we to expect if we take this occupation on as well? Especially since it would not merely be an occupation drawing us away from more necessary things: it would also be a cause of conceit and arrogance, and no small one.

The power of arguing and the capacity to persuade are great, particularly when much exercised and given the extra polish of good language. And in general, any capacity acquired by the untrained and the weak brings with it the danger of their being puffed up and swollen by it. What means could you use to persuade a young man who excels at this that he ought not to become an appendage to it, but to make it an appendage to himself? Does he not trample on all such arguments and strut about in front of us, puffed up and swollen, refusing to let anyone correct him or remind him of what he has neglected and where he has turned aside?

"What then — was Plato not a philosopher?"

And was Hippocrates not a physician? But you see how Hippocrates writes. Does Hippocrates write like that in his capacity as a physician? Why do you jumble together things that happen to have been combined in the same men? And if Plato was handsome and strong, ought I to set about becoming handsome or strong myself, as though that were necessary for philosophy, on the ground that a certain philosopher was handsome and a philosopher at once? Will you not see the difference, and distinguish what men become philosophers in respect of from what belongs to them in other respects? And if I were a philosopher, ought you to be made lame as well?

What of it, then? Am I taking these capacities of yours away from you? Not at all — no more than I take away the power of sight. But if you ask me what the good of a man is, I can name you nothing but a certain condition of the will with regard to impressions.

Chapter 9: What Follows from Our Kinship with God

If what the philosophers say about the kinship of God and man is true, what is left for men to do but what Socrates did — never, when asked what country he belonged to, answer "Athenian" or "Corinthian", but "a citizen of the world"?

Why do you say you are an Athenian, instead of saying you belong only to the little corner your poor body happened to be dropped in at birth? Plainly you name yourself Athenian or Corinthian from the wider thing that has the greater authority — the thing that takes in not only that corner and your whole household, but the entire country your ancestors came down to you from.

So a man who has watched the administration of the world intelligently, and has learned that the greatest and highest and most inclusive community is the one made up of men and God; that the seeds have come down from God not only to my father and grandfather but to everything that is begotten and born on the earth, and above all to rational beings, since these alone are formed by nature to share with God, being joined to Him by reason — why should such a man not call himself a citizen of the world? Why not a son of God? And why should he be afraid of anything that happens among men? If kinship with Caesar, or with any other of the great men at Rome, is enough to let us live in safety, above contempt and with nothing to fear at all — then to have God as your maker and father and guardian, will that not release us from griefs and fears?

"But where am I to get bread, when I have nothing?"

And how do slaves manage when they run away — what do they rely on when they leave their masters? On their estates? Their own slaves? Their silver plate? They rely on nothing but themselves, and food does not fail them. And is one of us, a philosopher, to travel abroad and depend on other people and lean on them, and take no care of himself? Is he to be worse than the animals, and more of a coward — every one of which is self-sufficient, and never fails to find its proper food, or a way of living that suits it and is in line with nature?

For my part, I think the old man sitting here ought not to be occupied with keeping you from low thoughts and low, ignoble talk about yourselves. He ought to be making sure there are no young men among us of this other mind: who, having recognised their kinship with God, and that we are shackled by these bonds — the body, I mean, and its possessions, and whatever else we need on account of them for the ordinary running and commerce of life — resolve to throw the whole lot off as an unbearable, painful burden, and go off to their relations.

This is the work your teacher and instructor ought to be at, if he really were what he ought to be. You should come to him and say: "Epictetus, we can no longer bear being tied to this poor body — feeding it, giving it drink and rest, washing it, and for its sake making up to this man and that. Are these things not indifferent, and nothing to us? Is death not no evil? Are we not in a sense relations of God, and did we not come from Him? Let us go back where we came from. Let us be released at last from these bonds that hold us down and weigh on us. Here there are robbers and thieves and law courts, and men called tyrants who think they have some power over us through the body and its belongings. Let us show them they have no power over anybody."

And I, for my part, would answer: "Friends, wait for God. When He gives the signal and releases you from this service, then go to Him. For the present, put up with living in the place where He has stationed you. The time you have to live here is short, and easy enough to bear for men in your frame of mind. What tyrant, what thief, what law court is frightening to men who have come to count the body and the body's possessions as worth nothing? Wait, then. Do not leave without a reason."

Something like that is what a teacher ought to say to well-born young men. But what actually happens? The teacher is a lifeless body and so are you. You eat your fill today, and then sit down and moan about tomorrow and where the next meal is coming from. Wretch, if you have it, you will have it; and if you do not, you will leave life. The door is open. Why grieve? What room is left for tears? Where is the occasion for flattery? Why should one man envy another? Why should anyone admire the rich or the powerful, even if they are very strong and violent-tempered? What are they going to do to us? What they can do, we shall not care about; and what we do care about, they cannot touch.

How did Socrates behave over such matters? How else than as a man who was convinced he was a relation of the gods? "If you say to me now," Socrates told his judges, "we will acquit you on condition that you stop arguing in the way you have argued up to now, and stop troubling our young men and our old ones — then I shall answer that you are making yourselves ridiculous. You think that if one of our commanders posted me somewhere it would be my duty to hold that post and keep it, and to resolve to die a thousand times rather than desert it; but that if God has put us in a certain place and a certain way of life, we ought to desert that." Socrates talks like a man who really is a relation of the gods.

But we think of ourselves as stomachs and guts and private parts. We are afraid, we want things, we flatter the people who can help us with these matters — and are afraid of them too.

A man once asked me to write to Rome on his behalf. Most people thought he had been unlucky: he had been a man of rank and rich, and had been stripped of everything, and was living here. I wrote for him in a humble tone. He read the letter, handed it back to me and said: "I wanted your help, not your pity. Nothing bad has happened to me."

In the same way Musonius Rufus used to test me by saying: this and that will be done to you by your master. And when I answered that these were the sort of things that happen in the ordinary course of human affairs — then why, he said, should I ask him for anything when I can get it from you? For in fact what a man has from himself it is pointless and foolish to take from someone else. Am I, who can get greatness of spirit and a generous temper from myself, to take land and money or public office from you? I hope not. I will not be so ignorant of what I already own.

But when a man is cowardly and small, what else is there to do for him but write letters about him as you would about a corpse? Please make us a present of the body of so-and-so, and a pint of thin blood. For such a person really is a carcass and a pint of blood, and nothing more. If he were anything more, he would know that no man is made miserable by another.

Chapter 10: Against Those Chasing Promotion in Rome

If we applied ourselves to our own work as busily as the old men at Rome apply themselves to the business they are employed on, perhaps we too might get something done.

I know a man older than myself who is now superintendent of the corn supply at Rome. I remember when he came through here on his way back from exile, and what he said as he went over the events of his earlier life — how he declared that from his return onward he would attend to nothing but spending the rest of his days in quiet and peace. How little of life is left to me, he said.

You will not do it, I told him. The moment you get a whiff of Rome you will forget every word of this; and if they let you into the imperial palace, you will shoulder your way in gladly and thank God for it.

"Epictetus," he answered, "if you ever find me setting so much as one foot inside that palace, think what you like of me."

And what did he do? Before he had even entered the city, letters from Caesar met him. He took them, forgot the lot, and has been piling one piece of business on another ever since. I wish I were standing beside him now to remind him what he said as he came through, and to point out what a much better prophet I am than he is.

Well then — am I saying that man is a creature made to do nothing? Certainly not. We are active. Take me: as soon as the day comes I remind myself briefly of what I have to go over with my pupils. Then straight away I say to myself, and what is it to me how a certain person reads? The first thing for me is to get some sleep.

And what likeness is there anyway between what they do and what we do? Look at what they do and you will see. What do they do all day long but make up accounts, hold enquiries, give and take advice about a little grain, a scrap of land and profits of that sort? Is it the same thing to receive a petition and read in it, "I beg you to allow me to export a small quantity of corn," and to read one that runs, "I beg you to learn from Chrysippus how the world is administered, and what place the rational creature holds in it; and to consider who you are, and what your good and your bad consist of"? Are these things alike? Do they call for the same amount of care? Is it as disgraceful to neglect the one as the other?

Well then — are we the only lazy ones, the only ones who love our sleep? No: much more it is you young men. When we old men see young men playing we are eager to join in and play too; and if I saw you keen and hard at work, I should be far more eager still to join you in the serious business.

Chapter 11: On Family Affection

A magistrate came to visit him, and Epictetus asked him about various matters, and among them whether he had a wife and children. The man said he had.

And how do you find it?

"Miserable," said the man.

In what way? Men do not marry and have children in order to be wretched — rather the opposite.

"But I am so wretched over my children," the man said, "that lately, when my little girl was ill and they thought her in danger, I could not bear to stay with her. I left the house until somebody sent word that she had recovered."

Well — do you think you did right?

"I acted naturally," the man replied.

Then satisfy me that you acted naturally, and I will satisfy you that whatever happens according to nature happens rightly.

"That is how all fathers behave," he said, "or at any rate most of them."

I do not deny it. But what we are asking is whether such behaviour is right. On your reasoning we should have to say that tumours come for the good of the body, because they do come; and generally, that doing wrong is natural, because nearly all of us do wrong, or at least most. So show me how your behaviour was natural.

"I cannot," he said. "You show me rather how it was not according to nature, and not rightly done."

Well, said Epictetus, if we were enquiring about white and black, what test would we use to tell them apart?

"Sight."

And about hot and cold, hard and soft?

"Touch."

Then since we are enquiring about what is according to nature, and what is done rightly and not rightly, what sort of test do you think we should use?

"I do not know."

And yet not knowing the test for colours and smells, or for tastes, is perhaps no great harm. But if a man does not know the test for good and bad, and for what is according to nature and against it — does that seem a small harm to you?

"The greatest harm there is."

Come, tell me: does everything that strikes some people as good and proper rightly strike them so? Is it possible that the Jews and the Syrians and the Egyptians and the Romans are all of them right in their views about food?

"How could they be?"

Well, I suppose it is absolutely necessary that if the Egyptians are right the rest must be wrong, and if the Jews are right the rest cannot be.

"Certainly."

And where there is ignorance, there is also a want of learning and training in things that matter.

He agreed.

Then you, said Epictetus, now that you know this, will from now on give your serious attention to nothing else. You will apply your mind to nothing but learning the test of what is according to nature, and to using it to settle each case as it comes. And in the present business I can help you this far. Does affection for your own family strike you as being according to nature, and good?

"Certainly."

And is such affection natural and good, while what is consistent with reason is not good?

"By no means."

Then is what is consistent with reason in conflict with affection?

"I think not."

Quite right. If it were otherwise, then of two conflicting things one being according to nature, the other would have to be against nature. Is that not so?

"It is."

So whatever we find to be both affectionate and consistent with reason, we can confidently declare right and good.

"Agreed."

Well then, to leave your sick child and go away is not reasonable, and I do not suppose you will claim it is. What is left is to enquire whether it was consistent with affection.

"Yes, let us consider that."

You had an affectionate disposition toward your child. Did you do right, then, in running off and leaving her? And has her mother no affection for the child?

"Of course she has."

Ought her mother to have left her too, then, or not?

"She ought not."

And the nurse — does she love the child?

"She does."

Ought she to have left her as well?

"By no means."

And the man who looks after her lessons, does he not love her?

"He does."

Ought he to have deserted her too? So then, should the child have been left alone and helpless, on account of all this great affection of yours and of the people around her — or should she have died in the hands of people who neither loved her nor cared for her?

"Certainly not."

Now this is unfair and unreasonable: to refuse to let people who have as much affection as you do the very thing you think proper for yourself to do, on the ground that you have affection. It is absurd. Come then: if you were ill, would you want your relatives to be affectionate in this way — all of them, children and wife too — to the point of leaving you alone and deserted?

"By no means."

And would you want to be loved by your own people in such a way that, through their excessive affection, you were always left alone when you were ill? Or would you sooner pray, if it were possible, to be loved by your enemies and deserted by them? And if that is so, what follows is that your behaviour was not an act of affection at all.

Well then — was there nothing that moved you and drove you to desert your child? How could there be nothing? But it may have been something of the kind that moved a certain man at Rome to cover his head while the horse he had backed was running; and when the horse unexpectedly won, they had to bring sponges to bring him round from his faint.

What is the thing that moves us, then? Discussing that exactly is perhaps not for now. It is enough to be convinced of this much, if what the philosophers say is true: that we must not look for it anywhere outside, but that in every case it is one and the same thing that causes us to do or not do something, to say or not say something, to be puffed up or downcast, to avoid a thing or go after it — the very thing that is at this moment the cause for me and for you: for you, of coming to me and sitting and listening, and for me, of saying what I am saying.

And what is that? Anything but our will to do so? Nothing else. If we had willed otherwise, what else would we have been doing but the thing we willed? So this was the cause of Achilles' grief — not the death of Patroclus, since another man does not behave like that when his companion dies, but the fact that Achilles chose to. And in your case this was the very cause of your running away then: that you chose to. And on the other side, if you stay with her in future, the cause will be the same. And now you are going to Rome because you choose to; and if you change your mind, you will not go.

In a word: neither death nor exile nor pain nor anything of the kind is the cause of our doing or not doing anything. Our own judgments and our own choices are.

Do I convince you of this, or not?

"You convince me."

Then as the causes are in each case, so are the effects. So from this day on, whenever we do something wrongly, we shall lay it at the door of nothing but the judgment we did it from; and that is what we shall set about removing and cutting out — more than we would cut tumours and abscesses out of the body. And in the same way we shall give the same account of the cause of the things we do rightly. We shall stop putting forward as the cause of any evil to us a slave, or a neighbour, or a wife, or children, being convinced that if we do not think things to be what we do think them, we do not perform the acts that follow from thinking so — and that thinking or not thinking is in our power, and not in externals.

"That is so," he said.

From this day, then, we shall enquire into nothing else and examine nothing else — not what quality or condition anything has, not land, not slaves, not horses, not dogs — nothing but judgments.

"I hope so."

You see, then, that you will have to become a scholar, a creature everyone laughs at, if you really mean to make an examination of your own judgments. And that this is not the work of a single hour or a single day, you know yourself.

Chapter 12: On Being Content

About the gods, there are some who say no divine being exists. Others say one exists, but is idle and indifferent and takes no thought for anything. A third group say such a being exists and does take thought, but only about great things and heavenly things, and about nothing on earth. A fourth group say the divine takes thought about earthly things as well as heavenly ones, but only in a general way, not about particular cases. And there is a fifth group, to which Odysseus and Socrates belong, who say: "I do not move without your knowing it."

So before anything else we have to enquire about each of these opinions, and whether it is stated truly or not. For if there are no gods, how can following them be our proper end? And if they exist but take care of nothing, how in that case can it be right to follow them either? And even if they do exist and do look after things, if nothing is communicated from them to men — and in fact to me — how is it right even then?

The wise and good man, then, having thought all this through, submits his own mind to Him who administers the whole, as good citizens submit to the law of their state. A man coming to be taught ought to come with this intention: how am I to follow the gods in everything? How am I to be content with the divine administration? And how can I become free?

For a man is free when everything happens according to his will and no one can block him.

Is freedom madness, then? Certainly not: madness and freedom have nothing in common.

"But I want everything to turn out just as I like, and in whatever way I like."

You are mad. You are out of your senses. Do you not know that freedom is a fine and valuable thing? For me to wish thoughtlessly that things should happen as I thoughtlessly like — that is not fine at all. It is about as low as anything can be.

How do we go about writing? Do I want to write the name Dion however I choose? No: I am taught to want to write it the way it ought to be written. And with music? The same. And universally, in every art or science? Just the same. If it were otherwise, there would be no value in knowing anything, if knowledge were fitted to each man's whim. And is it in this one thing only — in the greatest and chief thing, freedom — that I am allowed to want thoughtlessly? Not at all. Being taught is precisely this: learning to want each thing to happen just as it does happen.

And how do things happen? As the one who ordered them ordered them. He appointed summer and winter, plenty and scarcity, virtue and vice, and all such opposites, for the harmony of the whole; and to each of us he gave a body, and parts of a body, and property, and companions.

Bearing this arrangement in mind, then, we ought to come to be taught — not so that we may change how things are constituted, since we have no power to do that, and it would not be better if we had, but so that, things around us being what they are and being so by nature, we may keep our minds in tune with what happens.

Can we escape from other men? How could we? And if we live among them, can we change them? Who gives us that power? What is left, then? What method is there for dealing with them? Is there a method by which they do whatever seems right to them, and we are no less in a state that is in line with nature?

But you are unwilling to put up with it, and you are discontented. If you are alone, you call it desolation; if you are with people, you call them crooks and robbers; and you find fault with your own parents and children and brothers and neighbours. When you are alone you ought to call that peace and freedom, and think yourself like the gods; and when you are with a crowd you ought not to call it a mob, or a nuisance, or a burden, but a festival and a gathering — and take it all contentedly.

What is the punishment, then, for those who refuse to accept? To be exactly what they are. Is a man dissatisfied at being alone? Let him be alone. Is a man dissatisfied with his parents? Let him be a bad son, and let him grieve. Is he dissatisfied with his children? Let him be a bad father.

Throw him into prison. What prison? The one he is in already — because he is there against his will, and wherever a man is against his will, that is a prison. So Socrates was not in prison: he was there willingly.

"Must my leg be lamed, then?"

Wretch, are you going to find fault with the universe on account of one poor leg? Will you not gladly surrender it to the whole? Will you not let it go? Will you not hand it over cheerfully to the one who gave it? And will you be annoyed and discontented at the arrangements Zeus made — the ones he settled and put in order, with the Fates standing by and spinning the thread of your birth? Do you not know how small a part of the whole you are? In respect of the body, I mean. As for intelligence, you are not the gods' inferior and not their lesser; the size of an intelligence is not measured by length or height, but by its thoughts.

Will you not choose, then, to put your good in the thing where you are the gods' equal?

"Wretch that I am, to have such a father and mother."

Well — were you allowed to step forward beforehand and make your selection and say, let this man couple with this woman at this moment, so that I may be produced? You were not. Your parents had to exist first, and then you had to be begotten. Of what sort of parents? Of the sort they were. Well then, since they are what they are, is no remedy given to you?

If you did not know what the power of sight was for, you would be unlucky and wretched if you shut your eyes when colours were put in front of them. But you possess greatness of spirit and nobility for every event that can happen, and you do not know you possess them — are you not more unlucky and wretched than that? Things are brought right up to you that match the power you have, and you turn that power away precisely at the moment when you ought to be holding it open and alert.

Why not thank the gods instead, that they have set you above the things they did not put in your power, and made you accountable only for the things that are? For your parents they have left you free of responsibility; the same for your brothers, and your body, and property, and death and life. What have they made you responsible for, then? For the one thing that is in your power: the right use of impressions. Why drag onto yourself the things you are not answerable for? That is simply making trouble for yourself.

Chapter 13: How to Do Everything in a Way That Pleases the Gods

Somebody asked how a man might eat in a way that pleases the gods. He answered:

If he can eat justly and contentedly, evenly, moderately and in good order, will that not please the gods as well? And when you have asked for hot water and the slave has not heard you, or has heard and brought it only lukewarm, or is not even to be found in the house — then not to be irritated, not to burst with temper, is that not pleasing to the gods?

"But how is a man to put up with people like that?"

Slave yourself — will you not put up with your own brother, who has Zeus for his ancestor, who is like a son sprung from the same seed and the same descent from above? And if you have been put in some higher position, are you going to make a tyrant of yourself straight away? Will you not remember who you are, and whom you are ruling? That they are your kinsmen, brothers by nature, the offspring of Zeus?

"But I bought them. They did not buy me."

Do you see which way you are looking? Toward the earth, toward the pit, toward these wretched laws of dead men — and not toward the laws of the gods.

Chapter 14: That God Watches Everything

Somebody asked him how a man could become convinced that everything he does is under God's eye. He answered:

Do you not think that all things are bound together into one?

"I do," the man replied.

Well, do you not think that earthly things are naturally in agreement and in union with heavenly ones?

"I do."

And how else, if not at God's command, do plants flower when He tells them to flower? When He tells them to put out shoots, do they not shoot? When He tells them to bear fruit, how else do they bear it? When He tells the fruit to ripen, does it not ripen? When He tells them to let the fruit fall, how else do they let it fall? When to drop their leaves, do they not drop them? And when He tells them to fold themselves up and stay quiet and rest, how else do they stay quiet and rest? And how else, as the moon waxes and wanes and the sun draws near and goes away again, do we see such great changes and reversals in the things of the earth?

Now if plants and our bodies are so tied and united to the whole, are our souls not far more so? And if our souls are tied to God and in contact with Him as parts and portions of Him, does God not feel every movement of these parts as a movement of His own, born along with Him?

Consider: you are able to think about the divine administration and about all things divine, and at the same time about human affairs; to be moved by ten thousand things at once in your senses and your understanding; to assent to some, to dissent from others, and about others again to suspend judgment. You hold in your soul so many impressions of so many different things, and, moved by them, you arrive at notions like the ones first impressed on you; you keep hold of numerous arts and the memory of ten thousand things. And is God not able to oversee everything, and to be present with everything, and to receive from everything a certain communication?

The sun can light up so large a part of the whole, and leave so little unlit — only the part the earth's shadow covers. And He who made the sun itself, and keeps it going round, and who is a small part of Him compared with the whole — can He not perceive everything?

"But I cannot take all of this in at once," the man may say.

And who told you that you have power equal to Zeus? All the same, He has set beside every man a guardian, each man's own guardian spirit, and put him in that spirit's charge — a guardian who never sleeps and is never deceived. To what better or more careful guardian could He have entrusted each of us?

So when you have shut the doors and made it dark inside, remember never to say that you are alone. You are not: God is within, and your guardian spirit is within, and what need have they of light to see what you are doing?

To this God you ought to swear an oath, just as soldiers swear to Caesar. But they, who are hired for pay, swear to put Caesar's safety above everything. And you, who have received so many and such great favours — will you not swear? Or having sworn, will you not stand by it? And what will you swear? Never to disobey, never to bring charges, never to find fault with anything He has given, and never to do or suffer unwillingly anything that must be. Is this oath like the soldiers'? They swear to put no man above Caesar; here men swear to put themselves above everything.

Chapter 15: What Philosophy Promises

A man was consulting him about how to persuade his brother to stop being angry with him. Epictetus replied:

Philosophy does not undertake to secure any external thing for a man. If it did, it would be taking on something outside its own province. The carpenter's material is wood; the sculptor's is bronze; and the material of the art of living is each man's own life.

"What about my brother's life, then?"

That belongs to his art. As far as yours goes, it is one of the externals — like a piece of land, like health, like reputation. And philosophy promises none of those. In every circumstance, she says, I will keep the governing part — the mind's command centre — in line with nature. Whose governing part? The one belonging to the man I am in, she says.

"So how is my brother to stop being angry with me?"

Bring him to me and I will tell him. To you I have nothing to say about his anger.

The man consulting him said: "What I want to know is this. Even if my brother is not reconciled with me, how am I to keep myself in a state that is in line with nature?"

Nothing great, said Epictetus, is produced all at once — not even a grape or a fig. If you tell me now that you want a fig, I shall answer that it takes time. Let it flower first, then set fruit, then ripen. So the fruit of a fig tree is not brought to perfection suddenly, in a single hour, and you expect to have the fruit of a man's mind in so short a time and so easily? Do not expect it, even if I tell you that you can.

Chapter 16: On Providence in the Smallest Things

Do not be surprised that for the other animals everything the body needs is provided — not only food and drink but bedding too — and that they need no shoes, no bedclothes, no clothing, while we need all these extras. They were not made for themselves but for service, and it would not have been fitting to make them so that they needed other things. Just think what it would be for us to have to look after cattle and donkeys as well as ourselves — how they should be clothed and shod, and how they should eat and drink.

Soldiers stand ready for their commander already shod, clothed and armed; it would be a hard business for the tribune to go round shoeing and clothing his thousand men.
