In the same way nature has made the animals that are meant for service ready and equipped, needing no further care. So one small boy with a stick drives the cattle along.

But as it is, instead of being grateful that we do not have to take the same care over animals as over ourselves, we complain to God about our own case. And yet, in the name of Zeus and the gods, any single one of the things that exist would be enough to make a man see the providence of God — at least a man with modesty and gratitude in him. Never mind the great things: take only this, that milk is produced from grass, and cheese from milk, and wool from hides. Who made these things, or thought them up? Nobody, you say. What amazing shamelessness and stupidity.

Well, let us leave nature's main works aside and look at her side-work. Is there anything less useful than the hair on a man's chin? And has nature not put even this hair to the best possible use? Has she not marked off male from female by it? Does not each man's nature call out from a distance, "I am a man: approach me as such, speak to me as such, look for nothing else — see the signs"? And in women, just as she mixed something softer into the voice, so she left the chin bare.

Not so, you say: the human animal ought to have been left without any marks of distinction, and each of us should have had to announce, "I am a man." But is the sign not beautiful, and fitting, and worthy of respect? How much more beautiful than a cock's comb, how much more becoming than a lion's mane! And so we ought to keep the signs God has given us. We ought not to throw them away, nor to blur the distinctions between the sexes as far as we can manage it.

Are these the only works of providence in us? What words could be enough to praise them and set them out as they deserve? If we had any sense, should we be doing anything else, in company and alone, but singing hymns to the deity and blessing him and telling over his benefits? Should we not sing this hymn to God as we dig and plough and eat? "Great is God, who has given us these tools to work the ground with. Great is God, who has given us hands, and the power of swallowing, and a stomach, and growth we never feel happening, and breathing while we are asleep." This is what we ought to sing on every occasion — and to sing the greatest and most divine hymn of all for his giving us the power to understand these things and to use them in the proper way.

Well then: since most of you have gone blind, ought there not to be somebody to fill this office and sing the hymn to God on everyone's behalf? What else can I do, a lame old man, but sing hymns to God? If I were a nightingale, I would do a nightingale's part; if I were a swan, a swan's. But as it is I am a rational creature, and I ought to praise God. This is my work. I do it, and I will not desert this post as long as I am allowed to keep it — and I urge you to join in the same song.

Chapter 17: Why Logic Is Necessary

Since reason is the capacity that analyses and perfects everything else, and ought not itself to go unanalysed, what is it to be analysed by? Plainly either by itself or by something else. If by something else, then that other thing is either reason too, or something superior to reason — which is impossible. And if it is reason, then who is to analyse that reason? If that reason does the job for itself, then our reason can do it for itself as well. And if we are going to need something further, the business goes on forever and never ends. So reason is analysed by itself.

Yes — but curing our judgments is more urgent, and things of that sort.

Do you want to hear about those, then? Listen. But if you say to me, "I do not know whether you are arguing truly or falsely," and if I express myself somewhere ambiguously and you say, "Draw the distinction," I shall put up with you no longer. I shall say to you: it was more urgent.

That, I suppose, is why the Stoic teachers put the logical art first — as in measuring out corn we start by examining the measure. If we do not settle first what a peck is and what a balance is, how shall we be able to measure or weigh anything at all?

So in this case, if we have not fully learned and accurately examined the test of everything else, by which everything else is learned, shall we be able to examine or learn anything accurately? How could we?

Yes, but the peck measure is only a bit of wood, and bears no fruit.

But it can measure corn.

Logic bears no fruit either.

We shall see about that. But even if a man granted it, it is enough that logic has the power of distinguishing and examining other things, and, so to speak, of measuring and weighing them. Who says so? Only Chrysippus and Zeno and Cleanthes? Does Antisthenes not say it? And who is it who wrote that the examination of names is the beginning of education? Does Socrates not say it? And of whom does Xenophon write that he began with the examination of names, and what each name meant?

Is this the great and marvellous thing, then — to understand Chrysippus, or to expound him? Who says that?

Then what is the marvellous thing?

To understand the will of nature.

Well then, do you grasp it yourself, by your own power? And what more do you need? If it is true that all men go wrong unwillingly, and you have learned the truth, then you must necessarily be acting rightly.

But in fact I do not grasp the will of nature. So who tells us what it is?

They say Chrysippus does.

I go along, and I enquire what this interpreter of nature says. I begin not to understand what he says. I look for an interpreter of Chrysippus. Well, consider how this is put — just as if it were put in Latin.

And what has the interpreter got to be haughty about? There is nothing that could fairly be charged against even Chrysippus as haughtiness, if he only interprets the will of nature and does not follow it himself — and much more so with his interpreter. For we have no need of Chrysippus for his own sake, but so that we may understand nature. We do not need a diviner for his own sake, but because we think that through him we shall know what is coming and understand the signs the gods give. We do not need the entrails of animals for their own sake, but because signs are given through them. And we do not stare in wonder at the crow or the raven, but at God, who gives signs through them.

So I go to the man who interprets these things, the sacrificer, and I say: inspect the entrails for me, and tell me what signs they give.

The man takes the entrails, opens them, and interprets. Man, he says, you have a will that is by nature free from blocking and free from compulsion. It is written here in the entrails. I will show you this first in the matter of assent. Can any man block you from assenting to the truth? No man can. Can any man force you to accept what is false? No man can. You see that in this matter your will is unblocked, uncompelled, unimpeded.

Well then, in the matter of desire and going after an object, is it any different? And what can overcome pursuit but another pursuit? What can overcome desire and avoidance but another desire and avoidance?

"But if you put the fear of death in front of me, you do compel me."

No: it is not what is put in front of you that compels, but your judgment that it is better to do such-and-such than to die. In that case it was your own judgment that compelled you — will compelled will. If God had made that part of himself which he took from himself and gave to us capable of being blocked or compelled, either by himself or by another, he would not be God, and he would not be taking care of us as he ought.

This, says the diviner, is what I find in the victims: these are the signs given to you. If you choose, you are free. If you choose, you will blame no one and bring charges against no one. Everything at once will be according to your own mind and the mind of God.

It is for the sake of that divination that I go to this diviner, and to the philosopher — not admiring the man for his interpreting, but admiring the things he interprets.

Chapter 18: Why We Should Not Be Angry at Other People's Mistakes

If what the philosophers say is true — that in every man there is one governing principle: in assent, the conviction that a thing is so; in dissent, the conviction that it is not; in suspending judgment, the conviction that it is uncertain; and likewise, in any movement toward a thing, the conviction that it is to the man's advantage; and if it is impossible to think one thing advantageous and want another, or to judge one thing proper and move toward another — then why are we angry with the crowd?

"They are thieves and robbers," you may say.

What do you mean, thieves and robbers? They have gone wrong about good and evil. Ought we to be angry with them, then, or to pity them? Show them their error and you will see how they give it up. If they do not see the error, they have nothing better than the opinion they hold now.

"Ought this robber and this adulterer not to be put to death, then?"

Do not put it that way. Put it like this instead: this man who has gone wrong and been deceived about the most important things of all, and been blinded — not in the power of sight that tells white from black, but in the power that tells good from bad — ought we not to destroy him? Put it that way and you will see how inhuman a thing you are saying, and that it is exactly as if you said, "Ought we not to destroy this blind and deaf man?"

If the greatest harm is being deprived of the greatest things, and the greatest thing in any man is a will that is as it ought to be, and a man is deprived of that — why are you angry with him on top of it? Man, you ought not to be affected against nature by another man's bad things. Pity him instead. Drop this readiness to take offence and to hate, and these phrases the crowd throws about: "these damned, disgusting people." How did you get so wise, all of a sudden? And how did you get so bad-tempered?

Why are we angry, then? Is it because we set such store by the things these men rob us of? Stop admiring your clothes and you will not be angry with the thief. Stop admiring your wife's beauty and you will not be angry with the adulterer. Learn that a thief and an adulterer have no foothold in what is yours, only in what belongs to others and is not in your power. Give these things up and count them as nothing, and who is left for you to be angry with? But so long as you set store by them, be angry with yourself rather than with the thief and the adulterer.

Look at it this way. You have fine clothes; your neighbour has not. You have a window; you want to air them. The thief does not know what a man's good consists in — he thinks it consists in having fine clothes, exactly what you think. Is he not bound to come and take them? When you show a cake to greedy men and then eat it all yourself, do you expect them not to snatch it? Do not provoke them. Do not have a window. Do not air your clothes.

I had an iron lamp myself the other day, standing by my household gods. I heard a noise at the door, ran down, and found the lamp gone. I reflected that the man who took it had done nothing surprising. And so? Tomorrow, I said, you will find an earthenware one — because a man only loses what he has.

I have lost my coat. Yes: the reason is that you had a coat. I have a pain in my head. Have you a pain in your horns? Then why are you upset? We lose only the things we have, and have pains only about the things we possess.

But the tyrant will chain — what? A leg. He will take away — what? A neck. What will he never chain and never take away? The will. This is why the ancients taught us the maxim: know yourself.

So we ought to train ourselves on small things, and go on from them to bigger ones. I have a pain in my head. Do not say "alas!" I have a pain in my ear. Do not say "alas!" I am not saying you are forbidden to groan — only do not groan on the inside. And if your slave is slow bringing the bandage, do not shout and put yourself in a state and say, "Everybody hates me." Who would not hate a man like that?

From now on, trusting these judgments, walk about upright and free — not relying on the size of your body, like an athlete. A man ought not to be unbeatable in the way a donkey is.

Who is the unbeatable man, then? The one whom nothing outside his will can disturb.

Then I go over his circumstances one after another, as I would with an athlete. He has come off victorious in the first bout — well then, what about the second? And what if it is very hot? And what if it is at Olympia? Just so here. Throw money in his way: he will despise it. Well, put a young girl in his way — what then? And what if it is in the dark? What if it is a little reputation? Or abuse? Or praise? Or death? He can beat all of them.

And what if it is in blazing heat? What if it is in the rain? What if he is in a black mood? What if he is asleep? He will win even then. This is my unbeatable athlete.

Chapter 19: How to Behave Toward Tyrants

If a man has some advantage, or thinks he has when he has not, and if he is untrained, he is bound to be puffed up by it. The tyrant, for instance, says: "I am master of everything."

And what can you do for me? Can you give me a desire that nothing can block? How could you? Do you have the unfailing power of avoiding what you want to avoid? Do you have the power of moving toward an object without going wrong? And where did you get such a power? Come — when you are on a ship, do you trust yourself or the helmsman? And in a chariot, whom do you trust but the driver? And how is it in all the other arts? Just the same. So where does this power of yours lie?

"Everyone pays me respect."

Well, I pay respect to my dinner plate: I wash it and wipe it. And for the sake of my oil flask I drive a peg into the wall. Are these things my superiors, then? No — but they supply some of my needs, and that is why I look after them. And do I not look after my donkey? Do I not wash his feet and rub him down?

Do you not know that every man looks after himself, and looks after you exactly as he looks after his donkey? Who looks after you as a man? Show me. Who wants to become like you? Who imitates you, the way men imitate Socrates?

"But I can cut off your head."

You are right. I had forgotten that I must pay you the same regard I pay a fever or an attack of bile — and put up an altar to you, as there is an altar to Fever at Rome.

What is it, then, that disturbs and frightens the crowd? The tyrant and his bodyguards? I should hope not. It is not possible that what is free by nature can be disturbed by anything else, or blocked by anything but itself. It is a man's own judgments that disturb him. When the tyrant says, "I will chain your leg," the man who values his leg says, "Don't — have pity on me." But the man who values his own will says, "If it looks more useful to you, chain it."

"Do you not care?"

I do not care.

"I will show you that I am master."

You cannot. Zeus set me free. Do you suppose he meant to let his own son be enslaved? You are master of my carcass. Take it.

"So when you come near me, you pay me no regard?"

No — I pay regard to myself. And if you want me to say I pay regard to you as well, I tell you that I pay you the same regard I pay my cooking pot.

This is not a twisted kind of self-regard. Every creature is made so as to do everything for its own sake. Even the sun does everything for its own sake — even Zeus himself. But when Zeus chooses to be the Giver of Rain and the Giver of Fruits and the Father of Gods and Men, you see he cannot get these offices and these names unless he is useful to mankind. And in general he has made the nature of the rational creature such that it cannot get any of its own proper interests unless it contributes something to the common interest. In that sense it is not antisocial for a man to do everything for himself. What did you expect — that a man should neglect himself and his own interest? And in that case how could there be one and the same principle in all creatures, the principle of attachment to themselves?

What follows, then? When absurd notions about things outside our will lie underneath our judgments — as though those things were good or bad — we cannot help paying regard to tyrants. Though I could wish men paid regard only to tyrants, and not to their bedchamber servants as well.

How is it that a man becomes wise all at once, the moment Caesar puts him in charge of the imperial chamber pot? How is it that we say straight away, "Felicion spoke very sensibly to me"? I wish he were thrown out of the bedchamber, so that he could look like a fool to you again.

Epaphroditus had a shoemaker whom he sold because he was good for nothing. By some stroke of luck the man was bought by one of Caesar's people and became Caesar's shoemaker. You should have seen the respect Epaphroditus paid him then. "And how is my good Felicion, may I ask?" And if one of us asked what the master was doing, the answer was, "He is consulting Felicion about something." Had he not sold the man as good for nothing? Who made him wise all of a sudden?

That is what it is to value something other than the things that depend on the will.

A man has been raised to a tribuneship. Everyone who meets him offers congratulations: one kisses his eyes, another his neck, the slaves kiss his hands. He goes home and finds the torches lit. He climbs the Capitol and offers sacrifice for the occasion. Now who ever sacrificed for having had good desires? For having acted in line with nature? We thank the gods, in fact, for the things we locate our good in.

Somebody was talking to me today about the priesthood of Augustus. Man, I said, let it alone; you will spend a great deal for nothing.

"But the men who draw up contracts will write my name in them."

Are you going to stand next to everyone who reads them and say, "That is my name written there"? And even if you could be present at all of them now, what will you do when you are dead?

"My name will survive."

Carve it on a stone and it will survive. But come — what memory of you will there be outside Nicopolis?

"But I shall wear a golden crown."

If you want a crown so badly, get a crown of roses and put that on. You will look far more elegant.

Chapter 20: On Reason, and How It Examines Itself

Every art and every capacity has certain things it particularly examines. When the capacity is of the same kind as the objects it examines, it necessarily examines itself as well; when it is of a different kind, it cannot. The shoemaker's art, for instance, works on leather, but is itself something entirely distinct from leather, and so it does not examine itself. The grammarian's art works on speech; is the art itself speech? Not at all — and that is why it cannot examine itself.

Now reason: what has nature given it for? For the right use of impressions. And what is reason itself? A system built out of certain impressions. So by its own nature it also has the capacity of examining itself.

And good sense — what does it belong to us to examine with it? Good and evil, and things that are neither. And what is good sense itself? Good. And the want of sense? Evil. Do you see, then, that good sense necessarily examines both itself and its opposite?

This is why the first and chief work of a philosopher is to test impressions, and tell them apart, and let none in untested. Look at coin, where our interests seem to be somewhat involved: see what an art we have invented, and how many ways the assayer uses to test the value of a coin — sight, touch, smell, and finally hearing. He throws the coin down and listens to the ring, and is not satisfied with hearing it ring once; by sheer attention he turns himself into a musician. So where we think it makes a great difference whether we are mistaken or not, there we apply great attention to spotting whatever can deceive us. But over our poor governing part we yawn and doze, and let in every impression without question — because the harm goes unnoticed.

So when you want to know how careless you are about good and evil, and how energetic about things that are neither, look at how you feel about losing your eyesight, and how you feel about being deceived. You will find you are a long way from feeling as you ought about good and evil.

But this is a matter that needs much preparation and much labour and study. Well — did you expect to acquire the greatest of all the arts with a small effort? And yet the chief doctrine of the philosophers is very short. Read Zeno's writings if you want to see. How few words it takes to say that man's end is to follow the gods, and that the nature of the good is the right use of impressions.

But if you say, "What is God? What is an impression? What is particular nature, and what is universal nature?" — then a great many words are needed. And if Epicurus turns up and says the good must be in the body, then again many words are needed, and we have to be taught what the leading thing in us is, the fundamental and substantial thing. As it is not likely that a snail's good lies in its shell, is it likely that a man's good lies in his body?

But you yourself, Epicurus, possess something better than that. What is it in you that deliberates? What is it that examines everything? What is it that forms the judgment about the body itself, that the body is the principal part? And why do you light your lamp and work for us and write so many books? Is it so that we shall not be ignorant of the truth — who we are and what we are in relation to you? So the discussion takes a great many words.

Chapter 21: Against Those Who Want to Be Admired

When a man holds the position in life that properly belongs to him, he does not stand about gaping after things outside it. Man, what is it you want to happen to you? I am satisfied if I desire and avoid in line with nature, if I move toward a thing and away from it as I am naturally formed to do, and likewise in my intentions and my plans and my assent.

Then why do you strut in front of us as though you had swallowed a spit?

"What I have always wanted is that the people who meet me should admire me, and the people following me should exclaim: what a great philosopher."

And who are these people you want to be admired by? Are they not the very people you are in the habit of calling mad? Well then — do you want to be admired by madmen?

Chapter 22: On Preconceptions

Preconceptions are common to all men, and one preconception does not contradict another. Which of us does not take it that the good is useful and worth choosing, and that in all circumstances we ought to follow it and go after it? Which of us does not take it that justice is beautiful and fitting?

So where does the contradiction come in? It comes in fitting the preconceptions to particular cases. When one man says, "He did well, he is a brave man," and another says, "Not at all — he acted like a fool," that is where the quarrels between men begin.

This is the quarrel among the Jews and the Syrians and the Egyptians and the Romans: not whether holiness should be preferred to everything and pursued in every case, but whether eating pork is holy or unholy.

You will find the same quarrel between Agamemnon and Achilles. Call them up. What do you say, Agamemnon — ought the proper and right thing not to be done?

"Certainly."

And you, Achilles: do you not agree that what is good ought to be done?

"I do, most certainly."

Then fit your preconceptions to the case in front of you. And there the quarrel starts. Agamemnon says, "I ought not to give Chryseis back to her father." Achilles says, "You ought." Plainly one of the two is fitting the preconception of "ought" to the case wrongly. Then Agamemnon says, "Well, if I have to give Chryseis back, it is only right that I take one of your prizes." Achilles answers, "You would take the girl I love?" "Yes — the one you love." "And am I to be the only man who goes without? The only one with no prize at all?" And so the quarrel begins.

What is education, then? Education is learning how to fit our natural preconceptions to particular things in line with nature; and then to distinguish that some things are in our power and others are not. In our power are the will and every act that depends on the will. Not in our power are the body, the parts of the body, property, parents, brothers, children, country — and in general everybody we live among.

So where should we locate the good? What sort of thing shall we fit it to? To the things in our power?

Is health not a good thing, then, and having sound limbs, and life itself? And are children and parents and country not good? Who will put up with you if you deny it?

Well, let us transfer the notion of good over to those things, then. Is it possible for a man to be happy when he suffers loss and does not get the good things? It is not. And can he keep up proper behaviour toward the people around him? He cannot. For I am naturally formed to look after my own interest. If it is in my interest to have an estate, it is also in my interest to take my neighbour's. If it is in my interest to have a coat, it is also in my interest to steal one at the baths. This is where wars come from, and civil disorder, and tyrannies, and conspiracies.

And how shall I still be able to keep up my duty toward Zeus? If I suffer loss and come off badly, then he is taking no care of me. What is he to me if he cannot help me? And what is he to me, further, if he lets me stay in the state I am in? At that point I begin to hate him. Then why do we build temples and set up statues to Zeus, as men do to evil spirits like Fever? How is he the Saviour, and the giver of rain, and the giver of fruits? And in truth, if we locate the nature of the good in things of that kind, all this follows.

So what are we to do? This is the question the true philosopher is in labour with. As things stand I do not see what the good is or what the bad is. Am I not mad? Yes.

But suppose I put the good somewhere among the things that depend on the will. Everyone will laugh at me. Some grey-haired man will come along with a lot of gold rings on his fingers, and shake his head and say: listen, my boy. It is quite right that you should study philosophy; but you ought to have some brains as well. All this you are doing is silly. You learn the syllogism from philosophers — but you know better than any philosopher how to behave.

Man, if I know it, then why are you blaming me? What am I to say to this slave? If I say nothing he will burst. I have to put it like this: forgive me, as you would forgive a man in love. I am not my own master. I am mad.

Chapter 23: Against Epicurus

Even Epicurus sees that we are social by nature; but having once located our good in the husk, he cannot go on to say anything else. On the other side he strongly maintains that we ought not to admire or accept anything cut off from the nature of the good — and he is right to maintain it.

How is it, then, that we feel any misgiving, if we have no natural affection for our children? Why do you advise the wise man not to bring up children? Why are you afraid he may fall into trouble that way? Does he fall into trouble on account of the mouse that lives in his house? What does he care if a little mouse in the house squeaks at him?

But Epicurus knows that once a child is born it is no longer in our power not to love it and care about it. That is why he says that a man with any sense does not go into politics either — he knows what a man in politics has to do. If you mean to behave among men as you would among a swarm of flies, what is stopping you?

And knowing this, Epicurus still dares to say that we should not bring up children. But a sheep does not desert its young, nor does a wolf. And is a man to desert his child? What do you mean — that we should be as stupid as sheep? They do not desert their young. As savage as wolves? Not even wolves desert their young.

Come now: who would take your advice if he saw his child fall on the ground and start crying? For my part I think that even if your own mother and father had been told by an oracle that you would say what you have just said, they would not have thrown you away.

Chapter 24: How to Wrestle with Circumstances

It is circumstances that show what men are. So when a difficulty falls on you, remember that God has matched you, like a wrestling coach, against a rough young opponent.

"What for?" you may say.

So that you may become an Olympic champion. And it is not done without sweat. In my opinion no man has ever had a more useful difficulty than the one you have had, if you choose to use it the way an athlete uses a young opponent.

We are sending a scout to Rome at the moment. But nobody sends a cowardly scout — one who hears a noise and sees a shadow somewhere and comes running back in terror, reporting that the enemy is at the gates. So if you come now and tell us, "Things at Rome are frightful. Death is terrible, exile is terrible, slander is terrible, poverty is terrible — run, my friends, the enemy is close," we shall answer: get out. Prophesy for yourself. Our one mistake was sending a scout like you.

Diogenes was sent out as a scout before you, and brought back a different report. He says that death is no evil, since there is nothing shameful in it. He says that reputation is the noise of madmen. And what did this spy have to say about pain, and pleasure, and poverty? He says that being naked is better than any purple robe, and that sleeping on bare ground is the softest bed there is. And as proof of every claim he makes he offers his own courage, his peace of mind, his freedom, and the healthy, solid look of his body. "There is no enemy near," he says. "All is quiet."

"How so, Diogenes?"

"Look," he answers. "Have I been struck? Have I been wounded? Have I run from anybody?"

That is what a scout ought to be. But you come to us with one thing after another. Go back, and take another look with the fear laid aside.

"So what am I to do?"

What do you do when you get off a ship? Do you carry away the tiller, or the oars? What do you take? What is yours — your bottle and your bag. And now, if you keep in mind what is yours, you will never lay claim to what belongs to somebody else.

The emperor says: "Take off your senator's stripe." Very well: I put on the narrower one. "Take that off too." Very well: I have only my toga. "Take off the toga." See — I am naked now. "But you still make me envious." Then take my poor body too. If I can throw away my body at a man's command, am I still afraid of him?

But a certain person will not leave me his estate. Well — had I forgotten that none of it was ever mine? Why do we call such things ours, then? The way we call a bed at an inn ours. If the innkeeper dies and leaves you the beds, well and good. If he leaves them to someone else, that man will have them, and you will look for another bed. And if you cannot find one, you will sleep on the ground. Only sleep willingly, and snore; and remember that tragedies belong among the rich and among kings and tyrants, and that no poor man has a part in a tragedy except in the chorus.

Kings begin in prosperity — "hang the palace with garlands!" — and then about the third or fourth act they cry out, "O Cithaeron, why did you receive me?" Slave, where are your crowns? Where is your diadem? Your bodyguards are no use to you at all.

So when you go up to one of these men, remember that you are going up to a tragic figure — not to the actor, but to Oedipus himself.

"But such a man is happy: he walks about with a crowd around him." And I place myself among the crowd and walk about with them too.

In sum, remember this: the door is open. Do not be more timid than small children. When a thing does not please them they say, "I won't play any more." So you: when things look to you like that, say, I won't play any more, and be off. But if you stay, do not complain.

Chapter 25: On the Same Subject

If these things are true, and if we are not being silly or acting a part when we say that a man's good is in the will and his evil too, and that everything else has nothing to do with us — why are we still disturbed? Why are we still afraid? The things we have been busy about are in nobody's power, and the things that are in other people's power we do not care about. What sort of trouble have we left?

"Give me instructions."

Why should I give you instructions? Has Zeus not given you instructions? Has he not given you what is your own, free from blocking and hindrance, and what is not your own, subject to blocking and hindrance? What instructions did you bring with you, then, when you came from him? Hold on to what is yours by every means; do not desire what belongs to others. Trustworthiness is your own. Self-respect is your own. Who can take these things from you? Who but yourself will block you from using them?

And what do you do? When you go after what is not your own, you lose what is. With such prompting and such orders from Zeus, what more do you want from me? Am I more powerful than he is? More to be trusted? If you keep to his, do you need any others?

"But he did not give those orders."

Produce your preconceptions. Produce the philosophers' proofs. Produce what you have often heard, and what you have said yourself; produce what you have read and what you have thought over. Then you will see that all of it comes from God.

How long is it right to keep to these instructions from God, and not break up the game? As long as the game is played properly. At the Saturnalia a king is chosen by lot, since it has been the custom to play at this. The king gives orders: you drink, you mix the wine, you sing, you go, you come. I obey, so that the game shall not be broken up because of me. But if he says, "Consider yourself in a bad way," I answer, I do not consider myself so — and who is going to force me to?

Again, we agreed to play Agamemnon and Achilles. The man appointed to play Agamemnon says to me, "Go to Achilles and take Briseis from him." I go. He says, "Come," and I come.

We ought to behave in life as we behave with hypothetical arguments. "Suppose it is night." I suppose it is night. "Well then, is it day?" No, because I granted the hypothesis that it was night. "Suppose you think it is night." Suppose I do. "But also think that it is night." That does not follow from the hypothesis.

So it is in this case too. "Suppose you are unlucky." Well, suppose I am. "Are you unhappy, then?" Yes. "Well, are you troubled by an unfavourable fortune?" Yes. "But think also that you are in misery." That does not follow from the hypothesis — and Another forbids me to think it.

How long, then, must we obey such orders? As long as it pays; and that means, as long as I keep what is fitting and consistent. Some men are sour and bad-tempered, and say, "I cannot dine with this man and have to listen to him telling me every day how he fought in Mysia. I told you, brother, how I climbed the hill — and then the siege started again." Another man says, "I would rather get my dinner and let him talk as much as he likes." And you must weigh these judgments up. Only do nothing in a downcast mood, nor as a man afflicted, nor as one who thinks himself miserable — because nobody forces you into that.

Has somebody made smoke in the room? If it is moderate, I shall stay. If it is too much, I go out. For you must always remember this and hold on to it: the door is open.

"Do not live in Nicopolis," you say to me. I will not live in Nicopolis. "Nor in Athens." I will not live in Athens. "Nor in Rome." I will not live in Rome. "Live on Gyarus." I will live on Gyarus — but living on Gyarus seems like a great deal of smoke to me, and I depart to the place where nobody will block me from living, since that dwelling is open to everyone. And as to the last garment, which is this poor body, nobody has any power over me beyond it.

This was why Demetrius said to Nero: "You threaten me with death, and nature threatens you."

If I fix my admiration on the poor body, I have handed myself over as a slave. If on my little property, the same: I show at once what I can be caught by. When a snake pulls in his head I tell you to strike the part he is guarding — and you may be sure that whatever part you choose to guard is the part your master will attack. Remember that, and whom will you go on flattering, or fearing?

"But I should like to sit where the senators sit."

Do you see that you are hemming yourself in, squeezing yourself?

"How else am I going to get a good view in the amphitheatre?"

Man, do not be a spectator at all, and you will not be squeezed. Why give yourself trouble? Or wait a little, and when the show is over sit down in the seats reserved for senators and sun yourself.

Remember this general truth: it is we who squeeze ourselves and hem ourselves in — that is, our judgments squeeze us and hem us in. What is it to be insulted? Stand by a stone and insult it; what will you get out of it? So if a man listens like a stone, what has the insulter gained? But if the insulter has the weakness of the insulted man as a foothold, then he does accomplish something.

"Strip him." What do you mean, him? Take hold of his coat and strip that off. "I have insulted you." Much good may it do you.

This was Socrates' practice, and it is why he always had the same face. But we would rather practise and study anything at all than the means by which we might be unblocked and free.

"Philosophers talk in paradoxes," you say. And are there no paradoxes in the other arts? What is more paradoxical than puncturing a man's eye so that he may see? If somebody said that to a man who knew nothing of surgery, would he not laugh at him? So where is the wonder if in philosophy too many things that are true look paradoxical to the inexperienced?

Chapter 26: What the Rule of Life Is

While somebody was reading out hypothetical arguments, Epictetus said:

This too is a hypothetical law — that we must accept what follows from the hypothesis. But long before that law comes the law of life: that we must act in line with nature. If we want to observe what is natural in every matter and every circumstance, then plainly in everything we ought to aim at not letting the consequence escape us, and at not admitting the contradictory.

So philosophers train us first in theory, which is easier, and then lead us on to the harder things; because in theory there is nothing pulling us away from following what we are taught, whereas in the business of living there are a great many distractions. A man is ridiculous, then, who says he wants to start with the business of real life, since it is not easy to start with the harder things.

And this is the argument to use on parents who are annoyed at their children studying philosophy. Am I doing wrong, then, father? Do I not know what is suitable and proper for me? If this can neither be learned nor taught, why do you blame me? And if it can be taught, then teach me; and if you cannot, let me learn from the men who say they know how to teach it. What do you suppose — that I fall into evil and miss the good on purpose? I hope not. What is the cause of my going wrong, then? Ignorance. Do you not want me to be rid of my ignorance? Who was ever taught the pilot's art, or music, by anger? Do you suppose I shall learn the art of living by means of your anger?

Only a man who really has that intention is entitled to talk this way. But if a man reads these arguments and attends the philosophers merely to make a display at a dinner party and show that he knows about hypothetical arguments, what other object has he than that some senator sitting beside him should admire him? For that is where the really great opportunities are, and the wealth here at Nicopolis looks like small change there. This is why it is hard for a man to master his impressions where the things that upset his judgment are so large.

I know a man who complained, clutching Epaphroditus by the knees, that he had only a million and a half denarii left. And what did Epaphroditus do? Laugh at him, as we slaves of Epaphroditus did? No — he cried out in astonishment: "Poor man! How did you keep quiet about it? How did you bear it?"

When Epictetus had pulled up the man who was reading the hypothetical arguments, and the teacher who had set the reading was laughing at the reader, Epictetus said to the teacher: "You are laughing at yourself. You did not prepare the young man, and you did not find out whether he was able to follow this material; perhaps you are only using him as a reader."

Well then, said Epictetus: if a man has not the ability to follow a complex argument, do we trust him when he gives praise? Do we trust him when he gives blame? Do we allow that he is able to form a judgment about good or bad? And if such a man blames somebody, does the man care about the blame? And if he praises somebody, is that man puffed up — when in so small a matter as a hypothetical syllogism the man praising him cannot see what follows from the hypothesis?

This, then, is where philosophy begins: a man's perception of the state of his own governing part. When a man knows it is weak, he will not use it on the hardest things. But as it is, men who cannot swallow a mouthful buy whole volumes and try to bolt them down; and so they vomit them up again, or get indigestion, and then come the cramps and the running colds and the fevers. They ought to have considered what they were capable of.

In theory it is easy to convince an ignorant man; in the business of real life nobody offers himself up to be convinced, and we hate the man who has convinced us. But Socrates advised us not to live a life that goes unexamined.

Chapter 27: The Ways Impressions Come at Us, and What Defenses We Have

Impressions come to us in four ways. Either things are as they appear; or they are not, and do not appear to be; or they are, and do not appear to be; or they are not, and yet appear to be. And in all four cases hitting the mark is the business of an educated man.

Whatever it is that troubles us, that is what we must apply the remedy to. If the sophisms of Pyrrho and the Academics are what trouble us, we must apply the remedy there. If it is the persuasiveness of impressions, by which some things look good when they are not, then let us look for a remedy for that. If it is habit that troubles us, we must try to find help against habit. And what help is there against habit? The opposite habit.

You hear the ignorant say: "That poor man is dead. His father and mother are overwhelmed with grief. He was cut off before his time, and in a foreign country." Listen to the opposite way of talking. Tear yourself away from these phrases. Set one habit against the other. Against sophistry set reason, and the exercise and drilling of reason. Against persuasive impressions we ought to have plain preconceptions ready to hand, cleaned of every impurity.

When death appears an evil, we ought to have this rule ready: that it is proper to avoid evil things, and that death is a necessary thing. What am I to do, and where shall I escape it? Suppose I am not Sarpedon, son of Zeus, and cannot speak in that noble way: I will go, and I am resolved either to act bravely myself or to give another man the chance of doing so; and if I cannot manage anything myself, I will not grudge another man the doing of something fine.

Suppose acting that way is beyond our power. Is reasoning that way beyond it? Tell me where I can escape death. Find me the country, show me the men I must go to, whom death never visits. Find me a charm against death. If I have none, what do you want me to do? I cannot escape death. Am I not to escape the fear of it either? Am I to die moaning and trembling?

For this is where being thrown comes from: wanting something, and that thing not happening. So if I can change externals to suit my wish, I change them; and if I cannot, I am ready to tear out the eyes of the man in my way. It is not in a man's nature to bear being deprived of the good, or to bear falling into the bad. Then at last, when I can neither change the circumstances nor tear out the eyes of the man in my way, I sit down and groan and abuse whoever I can — Zeus and the rest of the gods. If they take no care of me, what are they to me?

"Yes, but you will be an irreverent man."

And how will I be worse off than I am now?

To sum up: remember this, that unless reverence and your own interest lie in the same place, reverence cannot be kept up in any man. Do these things not seem necessarily true?

Let the followers of Pyrrho and the Academics come and raise their objections. For my own part I have no leisure for these disputes, and I am not able to undertake the defence of common consent. If I had a lawsuit about so much as a bit of land, I would call in somebody else to defend my interests.

What evidence satisfies me, then? The evidence that belongs to the matter in hand. How perception works — whether through the whole body or through a part — I perhaps cannot explain; both opinions perplex me. But that you and I are not the same, I know with perfect certainty.

"How do you know it?"

When I mean to swallow something I never carry it to your mouth, always to my own. When I mean to take bread I never take hold of a broom; I go straight to the bread as to a target. And you yourselves, who do away with the evidence of the senses — do you behave any differently? Which of you, meaning to go into a bath, ever walked into a mill?

What follows, then? Ought we not to hold on with all our strength to this too — maintaining the common view, and fortifying ourselves against the arguments aimed at it? Who denies that we ought? Well, the man who is able should do it, the man who has the leisure. But the man who trembles and is thrown and is broken inside had better spend his time on something else.

Chapter 28: Why We Should Not Be Angry at People; and What Counts as Small and Great

What is the cause of assenting to anything? The fact that it appears true. So it is not possible to assent to what appears untrue. Why? Because this is the nature of the understanding: to lean toward the true, to be dissatisfied with the false, and to withhold assent where things are uncertain.

What is the proof? Persuade yourself, if you can, that it is night now. You cannot. Take away your conviction that it is day. You cannot. Persuade yourself, or unpersuade yourself, that the stars are even in number. Impossible.

So when a man assents to something false, be sure he did not mean to assent to it as false. Every soul is deprived of the truth unwillingly, as Plato says: the falsehood looked true to him.

Well — in actions, what have we that corresponds to true and false here? We have the fitting and the unfitting, the profitable and the unprofitable, what suits a person and what does not, and the like. Can a man think a thing useful to him and not choose it? He cannot.

Remember how Medea puts it.

	I know the evil that I am about to do,
	but passion is stronger than my better judgment.

She thought that indulging her passion and taking revenge on her husband was more profitable than sparing her children. It was — to her. But she was deceived. Show her plainly that she is deceived and she will not do it; but so long as you do not show her, what can she follow but the way things look to her? Nothing else.

Why are you angry with the unhappy woman, then, for having gone astray about the most important things and become a viper instead of a human being? Why not rather pity her — as we pity the blind and the lame — since she has been blinded and crippled in the highest of her capacities?

Whoever clearly holds on to this — that a man's measure for every act is how the thing appears to him, whether it appears good or bad; that if it is good he is free from blame, and if bad he suffers the penalty himself, since it is impossible that the man deceived should be one person and the man who suffers another — whoever holds on to that will never be angry with anybody, or vexed with anybody, or insult or blame anybody, or hate or quarrel with anybody.

So do all these great and terrible deeds have their origin in how things appear? Yes: that origin and no other.

The Iliad is nothing but impressions and the use of impressions. It appeared to Paris that he should carry off the wife of Menelaus; it appeared to Helen that she should go with him. Now if it had appeared to Menelaus that being rid of such a wife was a gain, what would have happened? Not only would the Iliad have been lost, but the Odyssey too.

So did such great things hang on so small a matter? But what do you mean by "such great things"? Wars and civil disorder, and the destruction of many men and many cities. And what is so great about that? Is it nothing?

Well — what is so great about the death of many oxen and many sheep, or the burning and wrecking of many nests of swallows and storks? Are those things like these? Very like. Men's bodies are destroyed, and so are the bodies of oxen and sheep. Men's dwellings are burned, and so are storks' nests. What is great or terrible in it? Show me the difference between a man's house and a stork's nest, as far as each is a dwelling — except that a man builds his little house out of beams and tiles and bricks, and the stork builds hers out of sticks and mud.

Are a stork and a man alike, then? What do you say? In body they are very much alike.

Does a man differ from a stork in no respect at all? Do not suppose I say that. There is no difference in the respects I have mentioned. Where is the difference, then? Search and you will find it lies elsewhere. See whether it is not in a man's understanding of what he does; in his fellowship with others; in trustworthiness; in self-respect; in steadiness; in intelligence.

Where is the great good and the great evil in men, then? Where the difference is. If the difference is preserved and stays walled in — if self-respect is not destroyed, nor trustworthiness, nor intelligence — then the man is preserved too. But if any of these is destroyed and stormed like a city, then the man perishes; and this is what the great things consist of.

Paris suffered great damage, you say, when the Greeks invaded and sacked Troy and his brothers died. Not at all: no man is damaged by an act that is not his own. What happened then was only the destruction of storks' nests. The ruin of Paris was when he lost self-respect, trustworthiness, regard for hospitality and decency. And when was Achilles ruined? When Patroclus died? No. It happened when he began to be angry, when he wept over a girl, when he forgot that he was at Troy not to collect mistresses but to fight. These things are the ruin of men; this is being besieged; this is the destruction of cities — when right judgments are destroyed, when they are corrupted.

"But when women are carried off, and children taken captive, and the men killed — are these not evils?"

And how is it that you add these opinions to the facts? Explain that to me too.

"No — you explain how you can say they are not evils."

Let us come to the rules. Produce your preconceptions. It is because this is neglected that we cannot wonder enough at what men do. When we mean to judge weights, we do not judge by guesswork. Where we mean to judge straight from crooked, we do not judge by guesswork. In every case where it is in our interest to know the truth of a matter, not one of us ever does anything by guesswork. But in the things that depend on the first and only cause of doing right or wrong, of happiness or unhappiness, of good luck or bad — there alone we are careless and reckless. There we have nothing like a balance and nothing like a rule: an impression turns up, and straight away I act on it.

Am I to suppose I am better than Achilles or Agamemnon, then — that they, by following their impressions, do and suffer such great evils, while for me the impression is quite good enough?

And what tragedy has any other beginning? The Atreus of Euripides — what is it? An impression. The Oedipus of Sophocles? An impression. The Phoenix? An impression. The Hippolytus? An impression. What sort of a man do you suppose he is, then, who pays no attention to this matter? And what do we call the people who follow every impression? We call them madmen. And are we behaving any differently?

Chapter 29: On Steadiness

The nature of the good is a certain kind of will; the nature of the bad is a certain kind of will. What are externals, then? Materials for the will, which the will works on and by which it gets its own good or evil. How does it get the good? By not overvaluing the materials. Judgments about the materials, if they are right, make the will good; twisted and distorted judgments make it bad.

God has laid down this law, and says: if you want anything good, get it from yourself. You say: no, I will have it from somebody else. Do not. Get it from yourself.

So when the tyrant threatens me and summons me, I say: whom are you threatening? If he says, "I will put you in chains," I say, you are threatening my hands and my feet. If he says, "I will cut off your head," I say, you are threatening my head. If he says, "I will throw you into prison," I say, you are threatening the whole of this poor body. And if he threatens exile, I say the same.

Is he not threatening you at all, then? If I feel that none of these things concerns me, he is not threatening me at all. If I fear any of them, then it is me he is threatening. Whom do I fear, then? The master of what? The master of things in my own power? There is no such master. Do I fear the master of things not in my power? And what are they to me?

"So do you philosophers teach us to despise kings?"

I hope not. Which of us teaches you to claim against them the power over things they actually possess? Take my poor body, take my property, take my reputation, take the people around me. If I advise anybody to claim these things, let him accuse me and welcome.

"Yes, but I intend to command your judgments as well."

And who gave you that power? How are you going to conquer another man's judgment? "By putting terror on it," he says. "That will conquer it." Do you not know that a judgment is conquered by itself and not by anything else? Nothing can conquer the will except the will itself.

This is why the law of God is the most powerful and the most just: let the stronger always prevail over the weaker. Ten are stronger than one. For what? For chaining, for killing, for dragging a man where they please, for taking away what he has. So the ten conquer the one in the thing they are stronger in. And where are the ten weaker? Where the one has right judgments and they have not. Can the ten conquer in that? How could they? If we were put in the scales, must not the heavier pull its own scale down?

"How strange, then, that Socrates should have been treated so by the Athenians."

Slave, why do you say Socrates? Say the thing as it is: how strange that the poor body of Socrates should have been seized and dragged off to prison by stronger men; that somebody should have given hemlock to the poor body of Socrates, and that it should breathe its life out. Do these things seem strange? Do they seem unjust? Do you blame God for them? Had Socrates nothing to set against them? Where did the nature of good lie, for him? Whom shall we listen to, you or him? And what does Socrates say? Anytus and Meletus can kill me, but they cannot harm me. And further: if it so pleases God, so let it be.

Show me the man with the inferior principles overpowering the man whose principles are superior. You will never show it, or come anywhere near showing it. This is the law of nature and of God: the superior always overpowers the inferior. In what? In the thing it is superior in. One body is stronger than another. Many are stronger than one. A thief is stronger than a man who is not a thief. That is why I lost my lamp too: the thief was better than me at staying awake. But he bought the lamp at a price — for a lamp he became a thief, a man who cannot be trusted, a wild animal. It looked like a good bargain to him. Very well.

But suppose a man has taken hold of my cloak and is dragging me to the marketplace, and the others start shouting: "Philosopher, what good have your judgments done you? Look, you are being dragged to prison, you are going to have your head cut off."

And what introduction to philosophy could I have put together so that if a stronger man took hold of my cloak I should not be dragged off, and if ten men laid hold of me and threw me into prison I should not be thrown in? Have I learned nothing else, then? I have learned to see that everything which happens, if it is outside my will, is nothing to me. Have you not gained by that? Then why do you look for advantage anywhere but where you have learned advantage lies?

And then, sitting in prison, I say: the man who shouts like that neither hears what words mean nor understands what is said, and does not care in the least to know what philosophers say or what they do. Leave him alone.

But now he says to the prisoner: come out of your prison. If you have no further need of me in prison, I come out. If you should need me there again, I will go back in.

"How long will you go on like this?"

As long as reason requires me to stay with the body. When reason does not require it, take the body away, and goodbye to you. Only we must not do it thoughtlessly, or weakly, or on some slight excuse — because on the other side, God does not want it done, and He has need of a world like this and inhabitants like these in it. But if He sounds the retreat, as He did to Socrates, we must obey the one who gives the signal, as we would a general.

Well then — ought we to say such things to the crowd? Why should we? Is it not enough for a man to be convinced himself? When children come clapping their hands and shouting, "Today is the good Saturnalia," do we say to them, "The Saturnalia are not good"? Not at all: we clap along with them. So you too, when you cannot make a man change his mind, be sure that he is a child, and clap along with him; and if you would rather not do that, keep quiet.

A man must keep this in mind, and when he is called to any such difficulty he should know that the moment has come to show whether he has been taught anything. A man who has come into a difficulty is like a young man fresh from school who has practised resolving syllogisms: if somebody sets him an easy one he says, set me a properly complicated syllogism instead, so that I can exercise myself. Even athletes are dissatisfied with slight young opponents — "he cannot lift me." There is a young man of noble disposition.

But you do not behave so. When the moment of trial comes, one of you has to weep and say, "I wish I had learned more." More of what? If you did not learn these things in order to show them in practice, why did you learn them?

I think there is somebody among you sitting here who is in labour like a woman, saying to himself: I only wish a difficulty would come to me like the one that has come to this man. Am I to waste my life in a corner when I might be crowned at Olympia? When will anyone announce such a contest for me? That is how every one of you ought to be disposed.

Even among Caesar's gladiators there are men who complain bitterly that they are not brought forward and matched, and who pray to God and go to their trainers begging to be allowed to fight. And will none of you show himself such a man? I would gladly take a voyage to Rome to see what my athlete is doing, and how he is studying his subject.

"I do not want a subject of this kind," he says.

Why — is it in your power to take whatever subject you choose? You have been given the body you have, the parents you have, the brothers, the country, the position in your country. And then you come to me and say, change my subject. Have you not the abilities to manage the subject you were given? What you ought to say is: it is your business to set the subject, and mine to work at it well. But you do not say that. You say: do not set me that mode of argument, set me this one; do not press that objection on me, press this one.

There will come a time, perhaps, when tragic actors think that they are nothing but masks and high boots and a long robe. Man, these things are your material and your subject. Say something, so that we can tell whether you are a tragic actor or a clown — because everything else you both have in common. If somebody takes away the tragic actor's boots and his mask and brings him on stage as a mere shadow of himself, is the tragic actor lost, or is he still there? If he has a voice, he is still there.

Here is another example. "Take the governorship of a province." I take it, and having taken it, I show how a man who has been taught behaves. "Now put off the senator's stripe, put on rags, and come forward in that character." And what of it? Have I lost the power of showing a good voice?

How do you appear now, then? As a witness summoned by God. "Come forward, you, and give evidence for me — you are worthy to be brought forward by me as a witness. Is anything outside the will good or bad? Do I harm any man? Have I made each man's interest depend on anybody but himself? What evidence do you give for God?"

I am in a wretched state, Lord, and I am unlucky. Nobody cares about me, nobody gives me anything, everybody blames me, everybody speaks ill of me.

Is this the evidence you mean to give? Will you disgrace the summons of the one who did you such honour, and thought you worthy to be called to give such testimony?

But suppose the man with the power has pronounced: "I judge you to be irreverent and unholy." What has happened to you? I have been judged irreverent and unholy. Nothing else? Nothing else.

Now if the same man had passed judgment on a hypothetical syllogism and declared, "the conclusion that if it is day it is light — I pronounce that false," what has happened to the syllogism? Who is being judged here? Who has been condemned — the syllogism, or the man who was deceived about it?

Does the man who has power to make a declaration about you know what is holy and unholy? Has he studied it? Has he learned it? Where? From whom? A musician pays no attention to a man who declares that the lowest string of the lyre is the highest, and neither does a geometer if the man declares that the lines from the centre of a circle to the circumference are unequal. And is a man who has really been taught to pay attention to an untaught man passing judgment on what is holy and unholy, just and unjust? What a signal wrong the educated do. Was this what they learned here?

Will you not leave these little arguments about such matters to other people — to lazy fellows, so that they can sit in a corner and collect their miserable fees, or grumble that nobody gives them anything — and come forward and use what you have learned? It is not little arguments that are wanted now: the writings of the Stoics are full of them. What is wanted, then? A man who will apply them; a man who by his acts will bear witness to his words. Take on that character, I beg you, so that we need no longer use the ancients as our examples in the schools, but may have an example of our own.

And whose business is it to think about these matters? The man who has leisure — for man is a creature that loves contemplation. But it is shameful to contemplate them the way runaway slaves do. We should sit as we sit in a theatre, undistracted, listening now to the tragic actor and now to the lute player, and not behave as slaves do. The slave takes his place and praises the actor, and at the same time looks over his shoulder; and then if somebody calls out his master's name he is frightened and thrown at once. It is shameful for philosophers to contemplate the works of nature like that.

For what is a master? A man is not another man's master. Death is, and life, and pleasure and pain. Bring Caesar to me without these things and you will see how steady I am. But when he comes with them, thundering and lightening, and I am afraid of them — what am I doing then but recognising my master, like the runaway slave? So long as I have any respite from these terrors I stand as the runaway slave stands in the theatre: I bathe, I drink, I sing — and I do all of it in terror and unease. But if I once free myself from my masters — that is, from the things that make masters formidable — what trouble have I left? What master have I now?

Well then: ought we to announce all this to everybody? No. We ought to accommodate ourselves to the untaught and say: this man is recommending to me what he thinks good for himself. I excuse him.

Socrates excused the jailer too, the man who had charge of him in prison and wept when Socrates was about to drink the poison. How generously he grieves for us, he said. Does he say to the jailer that this is why we sent the women away? No: he says that to his friends, who were able to take it in. The jailer he treats as a child.

Chapter 30: What to Have Ready When Things Get Hard

When you are going in to see some great man, remember that somebody else is watching what happens from above, and that it is him you ought to please rather than the other. So the one who watches from above asks you:

"In the schools, what did you use to say about exile and chains and death and disgrace?"

I used to say they are indifferent — neither good nor bad.

"And what do you say about them now? Have they changed at all?"

No.

"Have you changed, then?"

No.

"Tell me, then: what things are indifferent?"

The things outside the will.

"And tell me what follows from that."

That the things outside the will are nothing to me.

"Tell me about the good, too. What was your view?"

A will such as it ought to be, and the right use of impressions.

"And the end — what is it?"

To follow you.

"Do you still say this now?"

I say the same now.

Then go in to the great man boldly, remembering these things, and you will see what a young man who has studied them looks like among men who have not. For my part I imagine you will be thinking something like this: why do we make such great and elaborate preparations for nothing? Is this the thing men call power? Is this the antechamber? These the chamberlains? These the armed guards? Was it for this that I listened to so many lectures? All this is nothing — and I had been preparing myself as though for something great.

Book Two

Chapter 1: Why Confidence and Caution Are Not Opposites

The philosophers' view may strike some people as a paradox, but let us examine as best we can whether it is true that everything can be done both cautiously and confidently. Caution looks in a way like the opposite of confidence, and opposites are never consistent with each other.

What seems to many a paradox in this matter is, I think, of this kind. If we asserted that we ought to use caution and confidence about the same things, men could fairly accuse us of joining what cannot be joined. But as it is, where is the difficulty in what has been said? If it is true — as has often been said and often proved — that the nature of good lies in the use of impressions, and the nature of evil likewise, and that things outside our will admit of neither, what paradox are the philosophers asserting when they say that where things do not depend on the will you should use confidence, and where they do depend on the will you should use caution?

If the bad consists in a bad exercise of the will, then caution ought to be used only where things depend on the will. And if things outside the will and not in our power are nothing to us, then toward those we must use confidence. So we shall be both cautious and confident — indeed confident because of our caution. By being cautious about things that are really bad, we shall end up confident about the things that are not.

We behave like deer bolting in fright from the hunters' feathers. Where do they turn, and where do they look for safety? They run into the nets — and so they die, having confused what they ought to fear with what they ought not.

We do exactly the same. What do we fear? Things outside the will. What do we treat with confidence, as though there were no danger in it? Things that depend on the will. To be deceived, to act rashly or shamelessly, to chase after something out of low desire — none of that concerns us at all, so long as we hit the mark in things outside our will. But where there is death, or exile, or pain, or disgrace, there we try to run, there we are struck with terror.

So, as one would expect of men who go wrong in the greatest matters, we turn our natural confidence into recklessness, desperation, rashness, shamelessness; and we turn our natural caution and self-respect into cowardice and meanness, full of fear and confusion. If a man moved his caution over to the will and the acts of the will, then simply by willing to be careful he would also have the power of avoiding what he chose. But if he moves it to things not in his power and not in his will, and tries to avoid what is in other people's power, then he must fear, he must be unsteady, he must be thrown. For death and pain are not the terrible things; the fear of death and pain is.

This is why we approve the poet who said:

	Death is not the evil, but a shameful death.

Confidence, then, ought to be used against death, and caution against the fear of death. As it is, we do the reverse: against death we use flight, and toward our judgment about it we use carelessness, rashness and indifference.

Socrates rightly used to call these things tragic masks. Masks look frightening and terrible to children because they have no experience of them, and we are affected by events for exactly the same reason. What is a child? Ignorance. What is a child? Want of knowledge. Once a child knows these things, he is no worse off than we are.

What is death? A tragic mask. Turn it round and look at it. See — it does not bite. The poor body has to be separated from the spirit, now or later, as it was separated before. So why are you upset if it happens now? If it does not happen now, it will happen afterwards. Why? So that the cycle of the universe may be completed, since it needs the present, and the future, and the past.

What is pain? A mask. Turn it round and look. The poor flesh is moved roughly, and then smoothly again. If that does not satisfy you, the door is open; if it does, put up with it. The door ought to be open on every occasion, and then we have no trouble.

What is the fruit of these judgments, then? Exactly what ought to be the finest and most fitting fruit for men who are really educated: release from being thrown about, release from fear, freedom.

On these matters we must not believe the crowd, who say only free men ought to be educated; we should rather believe the philosophers, who say only the educated are free.

"How is that?"

Like this. Is freedom anything but the power of living as we choose? "Nothing else." Then tell me, men: do you want to live in error? "We do not." So nobody who lives in error is free. Do you want to live in fear? In grief? Thrown about? "By no means." Then nobody who is in fear or grief or turmoil is free; and whoever is delivered from griefs and fears and turmoil is at the same moment delivered from slavery.

So how can we go on believing you, most excellent lawgivers, when you say, "We allow only free men to be educated"? For the philosophers say: we allow nobody to be free except the educated — that is, God does not allow it.

"So when a man has turned his own slave round in front of the praetor, has he done nothing?"

He has done something.

"What?"

He has turned his own slave round in front of the praetor.

"Nothing more?"

Yes — he is also liable to pay the five per cent tax on him.

"Well then, has the man who went through that ceremony not become free?"

No more than he has become free of being thrown about. You, who are able to turn other men round and free them — have you no master? Is money not your master, or a girl, or a boy, or some tyrant, or some friend of a tyrant? Why do you tremble, then, when you go off to face a trial of that kind?

This is why I say so often: study and keep ready the principles by which you may settle what things you ought to be confident about and what things you ought to be cautious about — brave about what does not depend on your will, careful about what does.

"But have I not read to you? Do you not know what I have been doing?"

In what? In your little essays. Show me how you stand with regard to desire and avoidance. Show me that you do not fail to get what you want and do not fall into what you would avoid. As for those long, laboured sentences, you will take them and rub them out.

"What then — did Socrates not write? And who wrote so much?"

But how? Since he could not always have somebody at hand to argue against his principles, or to be argued against in turn, he used to argue with himself and examine himself, and he was always working through some subject in a practical way. These are the things a philosopher writes. Little essays and the method I am talking about he leaves to others — to the stupid, or to those happy men who are free from disturbance and have time on their hands, or to men too foolish to reckon consequences.

And are you now, when the opportunity invites, going to go off and display what you have, and recite it, and make an idle show, and say, "See what dialogues I can make"? Do not do it, man. Say instead: see how I am not disappointed of what I want. See how I do not fall into what I would avoid. Set death in front of me and you will see. Set pain in front of me, and prison, and disgrace, and a sentence.

That is the proper display for a young man who has come out of the schools. Leave the rest to others; let nobody ever hear a word from you about it; and if anyone praises you for it, do not accept it — think yourself nobody and think you know nothing. Show only that you know this one thing: how never to be disappointed of your desire and never to fall into what you would avoid.

Let other men labour at court cases and problems and syllogisms. You labour at thinking about death, chains, the rack, exile — and do it all with confidence, relying on the one who called you to these sufferings, who judged you worthy of the post where, being stationed, you will show what the rational governing power can do when it takes its stand against forces outside our will.

And then this paradox will no longer look impossible or paradoxical: that a man ought to be cautious and courageous at once — courageous toward things that do not depend on the will, and cautious in the things that are within its power.

Chapter 2: On Peace of Mind

Consider, you who are going into court, what it is you want to keep hold of and what you want to succeed in. If you want to keep a will that is in line with nature, you have complete security, complete ease, no troubles at all. If you want to keep what is in your own power and is naturally free, and you are content with that, what else is there to worry about? Who is master of such things? Who can take them away? If you choose to be self-respecting and trustworthy, who will not allow it? If you choose not to be restrained or compelled, who is going to force you to desire what you think you should not desire, or to avoid what you do not think fit to avoid?

But what do you say? The judge will decide something against you that looks frightening. But how can he make you suffer in trying to avoid it as well? So when the pursuit of things and the avoiding of them is in your power, what else is there to worry about? Let this be your opening, your statement of the case, your proof, your victory, your closing speech, and your applause.

This is why Socrates said to the man who was reminding him to prepare for his trial: "Do you not think I have been preparing for it all my life?"

With what kind of preparation?

"I have kept hold of what was in my own power."

And how was that?

"I have never done anything unjust, in private life or in public."

But if you want to hold on to externals as well — your poor body, your little property, your little reputation — then I advise you to start making every possible preparation this minute, and then to study both the character of your judge and the character of your opponent. If you have to clasp his knees, clasp his knees; if you have to weep, weep; if you have to groan, groan. When you have made what is your own subject to externals, then be a slave and stop resisting. Do not choose to be a slave one moment and not the next; be one thing or the other with your whole mind — free or a slave, educated or uneducated, a well-bred fighting cock or a cheap one. Either take the beating until you die, or give in at once; do not let it happen that you take a great many blows and then give in anyway.

But if that is shameful, then settle the question now. Where is the nature of good and evil? Where truth is. Where truth is and where nature is, there is caution; where truth is, there is courage.

What do you think? If Socrates had wanted to preserve externals, would he have come forward and said, "Anytus and Meletus can certainly kill me, but they cannot harm me"? Was he such a fool that he could not see this road leads somewhere other than to the preservation of life and property? Why does he take no account of his opponents, and even provoke them?

Just as my friend Heraclitus did. He had a small suit at Rhodes about a piece of land, and when he had proved to the judges that his case was just, he said in his closing speech: "I shall not beg you, and I do not care what verdict you give — and it is you who are on trial rather than I." And that was the end of that.

What need was there for it? Simply do not beg. But do not say "I do not beg" either, unless there is a proper occasion for deliberately provoking the judges, as there was with Socrates. And you — if you are preparing a closing speech like that, why are you waiting? Why do you obey the summons to stand trial at all? If you want to be crucified, wait and the cross will come. But if you choose to submit and plead your case as well as you can, then you must do what is consistent with that aim — provided you keep hold of what is your own.

For the same reason it is ridiculous to say, "Suggest something to me."

What should I suggest? "Well, shape my mind so that it can adapt to any event." That is exactly as if a man who could not read said, "Tell me what to write when a name is put to me." If I tell him to write Dion, and then somebody comes along and puts to him not Dion but Theon, what will happen? What will he write? But if you have practised writing, you are ready to write whatever is called for. If you have not, what can I suggest now? If the circumstances call for something else, what will you say, or what will you do?

Remember this general rule, then, and you will need no suggestions. But if you stand gaping after externals, you must of necessity wander up and down at your master's will. And who is the master? Whoever has power over the things you are trying to get or trying to avoid.

Chapter 3: To Those Who Send People to Philosophers with a Recommendation

Diogenes answered a man who asked him for a letter of recommendation very well. "That you are a man," he said, "he will know as soon as he sees you.
