Whether you are good or bad he will know if he has the experience to tell the good from the bad; and if he has not, he will never know it, though I write to him ten thousand times."

It is exactly as if a silver coin asked to be recommended to somebody for testing. If the man is skilled at testing silver, he will know what you are — you will recommend yourself.

So in life too we ought to have a skill of the same sort as with coin, so that a man can say, as the assayer of silver says: bring me any coin you like and I will test it. With syllogisms I could say: bring me any man you please and I will pick out for you the man who knows how to resolve syllogisms from the man who does not. Why? Because I know how to resolve syllogisms myself. I have the very power a man must have if he is to identify the men who have it.

But in life, how do I behave? At one moment I call a thing good, and at the next I call it bad. What is the reason? The opposite of what holds with syllogisms: ignorance and inexperience.

Chapter 4: Against a Man Caught in Adultery

Epictetus was saying that a man is made for trustworthiness, and that whoever undermines trustworthiness undermines the very thing that makes a man a man, when in came one of those who pass for men of letters — a man who had once been caught in adultery in the city. Epictetus went on:

If we lay aside the trustworthiness we were made for, and start scheming after our neighbour's wife, what are we doing? What but pulling down and destroying? Destroying whom? The man who can be trusted, the man with self-respect, the man who keeps what is holy. Is that all? Are we not also pulling down neighbourliness, and friendship, and the community? And where does that put us?

How am I to regard you, man? As a neighbour? As a friend? What sort of friend? As a fellow citizen? What am I to trust you in?

If you were a pot so worthless that nobody could use you, you would be thrown out on the dung heap and no one would pick you up. And if, being a man, you cannot fill any place that suits a man, what are we to do with you? Suppose you cannot hold the place of a friend: can you hold the place of a slave? And who will trust you? So should you not be content to be thrown out on some dung heap yourself, as a useless pot, a piece of dung?

And then will you say, "Nobody cares about me, a man of letters"? No, they do not — because you are bad and useless. It is exactly as if the wasps complained that nobody cares about them, that everyone runs from them, and that a man will strike one down if he can. You have a sting of that kind: whoever you wound with it, you throw into trouble and pain. What would you have us do with you? There is nowhere to put you.

"What of it? Are women not common property by nature?"

I say so too. A suckling pig at a dinner is common property to all the guests. But once the portions have been handed out, go and snatch up the portion of the man next to you, if you think that is right — or steal it slyly, or slide your hand over and get hold of it, and if you cannot tear off a piece of the meat, then grease your fingers and lick them. A fine companion over the wine you are, a real Socratic guest.

"Well, is the theatre not common property to the citizens?"

Then when they have taken their seats, go and throw one of them out, if you think that is proper. Women are common by nature in just this way. When the lawgiver, like the host at a feast, has distributed them, will you not look for your own portion instead of pilfering and pawing what belongs to somebody else?

"But I am a man of letters, and I understand Archedemus."

Then understand Archedemus, and be an adulterer, and a man nobody can trust; and instead of a man, be a wolf or an ape. What difference is there?

Chapter 5: How Greatness of Spirit Goes with Taking Care

The materials themselves are indifferent; the use of them is not. So how is a man to keep his firmness and his peace of mind, and at the same time be careful, and neither reckless nor slack? By copying men who play at dice. The counters are indifferent, the dice are indifferent. How do I know what the throw will be? But to use the throw carefully and skilfully — that is my business.

So in life too the chief business is this: distinguish things and separate them out, and say: externals are not in my power; the will is in my power. Where am I to look for good and bad? Inside, in what is my own. And in what does not belong to you, never use the words good or bad, or profit or loss, or anything of the kind.

What follows? Should we handle such things carelessly? Not at all: that is bad for the will, and so against nature. We should act carefully, because the use is not indifferent; and we should also act firmly and undisturbed, because the material is indifferent. Where the material is not indifferent, nobody can block me or compel me. Where I can be blocked and compelled, getting the thing is not in my power, and is neither good nor bad; but the use is good or bad, and the use is in my power.

It is difficult to mix these two things together — the carefulness of a man who is engaged with the material, and the steadiness of a man who does not care about it. Difficult, but not impossible; and if it is impossible, then happiness is impossible.

We should do as we do on a voyage. What can I do? I can choose the master of the ship, the crew, the day, the moment. Then a storm comes. What more is there for me to care about? My part is done. The business belongs to somebody else — the master.

But the ship is going down. What am I to do then? The only thing I can: not drown full of fear, not screaming, not blaming God, but knowing that what has come into being must also perish. I am not immortal. I am a man, a part of the whole, as an hour is part of a day. I must arrive like the hour and pass like the hour. So what does it matter to me how I pass — whether by suffocation or by fever? I have to go by some such means.

You will see the same thing in men who are good at ball. Nobody cares about the ball as being good or bad; they care about throwing and catching. That is where the skill lies, and the art, the speed, the judgment — so that I may not be able to catch it even if I spread out my coat, while another man catches it when I throw. But if we take the ball and throw it in fear and agitation, what sort of game is that? How is a man to keep steady, and how is he to see the pattern of the play? One will say "throw" and another "do not throw", and a third "you have already thrown once". That is quarrelling, not play.

Socrates knew how to play ball. How? By joking in the court where he was tried. "Tell me, Anytus," he says, "how do you say I do not believe in God? Who do you think the spirits are? Are they not sons of gods, or a mixture of gods and men?" And when Anytus admitted it, Socrates said, "Then who do you think can believe that there are mules and no donkeys?" He said it as though he were playing ball.

And what was the ball in that game? Life, chains, exile, a draught of poison, being taken from his wife, leaving his children orphans. Those were the things he was playing with — and he still played, and threw the ball skilfully.

So we should behave. We must bring all the care of the players and show the same indifference about the ball. We ought by all means to apply our art to some external material — not valuing the material, but showing our art in whatever it happens to be. The weaver does not make the wool; he practises his art on the wool he is given. Somebody else gives you food and property, and is able to take them away again, and your poor body with them. So when you have been given the material, work on it.

Then if you come out of the trial having suffered nothing, everyone who meets you will congratulate you on your escape. But the man who knows how to look at such things, if he sees you have behaved properly, will praise you and be glad; and if he finds you owe your escape to behaving badly, he will do the opposite. Where there is reason to rejoice, there is reason to congratulate.

How is it said, then, that some external things are according to nature and others contrary to it? It is said as it would be said if we were cut off from society. To a foot I shall say that it is according to nature for it to be clean; but if you take it as a foot, and not as something detached, it will be fitting for it to step into mud and tread on thorns, and sometimes to be cut off for the good of the whole body — or else it is not a foot at all.

We ought to think about ourselves in some such way. What are you? A man. If you consider yourself as detached from other men, it is according to nature to live to old age, to be rich, to be healthy. But if you consider yourself as a man and a part of a certain whole, then for the sake of that whole it is fitting that at one time you should be ill, at another take a voyage and run into danger, at another be in want, and in some cases die before your time.

Why are you troubled, then? Do you not know that as a foot is no longer a foot once it is detached from the body, so you are no longer a man once you are separated from other men? For what is a man? A part of a state — first of the state that is made up of gods and men, and then of the one we call next to it, which is a small copy of the universal one.

"What — must I be brought to trial? Must this man have a fever, and that man go to sea, and another die, and another be condemned?"

Yes. In such a body, in such a universe of things, among so many living together, it is impossible that such things should not happen, some to one and some to another. Your duty, since you have come here, is to say what you ought and to arrange these matters as is fitting.

Then somebody says: "I shall charge you with doing me wrong." Much good may it do you. I have done my part; whether you have done yours, you must see to. And there is some danger there too — that it may escape your notice.

Chapter 6: On Things That Are Neither Good nor Bad

A hypothetical proposition is indifferent; the judgment about it is not — that is either knowledge or opinion or error. In the same way life is indifferent, and the use of it is not.

So when somebody tells you that these things too are indifferent, do not become slack; and when somebody urges you to be careful about them, do not become abject and struck with admiration for material things.

It is good for you to know your own preparation and your own strength, so that in matters where you have not been prepared you can keep quiet and not be annoyed if others have the advantage of you. In syllogisms you will claim the advantage over them yourself; and if they are annoyed at that you will console them by saying, I have learned this and you have not. In the same way, wherever some practice is needed, do not go looking for what that practice brings; give way there to the men who have had it, and be content yourself with steadiness of mind.

"Go and pay your respects to so-and-so."

And how am I to do it?

Not meanly.

"But I was shut out. I never learned to get in through the window; and when I found the door shut I had either to come away or go in by the window."

Still, speak to him.

"In what way?"

Not meanly.

But suppose you did not get what you wanted. Was that your business, or his? Why do you claim what belongs to somebody else? Always remember what is your own and what belongs to another, and you will not be disturbed.

So Chrysippus put it well: "So long as what is coming is uncertain, I always hold to the things that are better suited to getting what is according to nature; for God himself gave me the power of making that choice. But if I knew it was fated for me to be ill, I would move toward the illness." The foot too, if it had understanding, would move to step into the mud.

Why are the ears of corn produced? Is it not so that they may ripen and dry? And do they not dry so that they may be reaped? They are not cut off from their fellowship with everything else. If they had feeling, then, ought they to wish never to be reaped? But never being reaped is a curse on ears of corn. And so we must understand that with men too it is a curse not to die — just as it is a curse not to ripen and not to be reaped.

But since we have to be reaped, and know that we are being reaped, we are annoyed about it; because we neither know what we are, nor have we studied what belongs to a man, as men who study horses know what belongs to horses.

Chrysantas, when he was on the point of striking his enemy, checked himself on hearing the trumpet sound the retreat: it seemed better to him to obey the general's order than to follow his own impulse. But not one of us is willing to obey readily, even when necessity summons; we suffer what we suffer weeping and groaning, and we call it "circumstances".

What kind of circumstances, man? If you give the name of circumstances to the things around you, then everything is circumstances. But if you mean hardships by the word, what hardship is there in the dying of something that came into being? And the thing that does the destroying is a sword, or a wheel, or the sea, or a roof tile, or a tyrant. Why do you mind which road you take down to Hades? They are all the same length. And if you will listen to the truth, the road the tyrant sends you by is shorter. No tyrant ever took six months to kill a man; a fever often takes a year over it. All this is only sound, and the noise of empty names.

"I am in danger of my life from Caesar."

And am I not in danger, living in Nicopolis where there are so many earthquakes? And when you cross the Adriatic, what do you risk? Is it not your life?

"But I am in danger about opinion too."

Your own? How? Who can force you to hold an opinion you do not choose to hold? Somebody else's, then? And what danger is it to you if other men hold false opinions?

"But I am in danger of being exiled."

What is it to be exiled? To be somewhere other than Rome.

"Yes — and what if they send me to Gyara?"

If it suits you, you will go there. And if it does not, you can go somewhere else instead of Gyara — where the man who sends you to Gyara will also go, whether he likes it or not.

Why do you go up to Rome, then, as though it were something great? It is not worth all this preparation, that a well-born young man should say afterwards, "It was not worth listening to so much, and writing so much, and sitting so long beside an old man who is not worth much."

Only remember the division by which what is yours is separated from what is not. Never claim anything that belongs to others. A tribunal and a prison are each a place, one high and one low; but the will can be kept the same in both, if you choose to keep it the same. And we shall be imitators of Socrates when we are able to write hymns in prison.

But in the state we are in now, consider whether we could bear to have somebody in prison say to us: would you like me to read you some hymns? "Why are you bothering me? Do you not know the troubles I am in? Can I listen to hymns in circumstances like these?" What circumstances? "I am going to die." And are other men going to be immortal?

Chapter 7: How to Use Divination

Out of an unreasonable regard for divination, many of us leave many duties undone. What more can the diviner see than death, or danger, or disease, or things of that general kind? So if I have to expose myself to danger for a friend, and if it is my duty even to die for him, what need have I of divination?

Have I not a diviner inside me, who has told me the nature of good and evil and explained the signs of both? What need have I to consult the entrails of victims or the flight of birds? And why do I submit when he says, "It is in your interest"? Does he know what is in my interest? Does he know what good is? Has he learned the signs of good and evil as he learned the signs in the entrails? If he knows those signs, then he knows the signs of the beautiful and the ugly too, and of the just and the unjust.

Tell me, man, what is being signified for me — life or death, poverty or wealth? But whether these things are in my interest or not, I do not intend to ask you. Why do you not give your opinion on points of grammar? And why do you give it here, on the very things we are all in error about and quarrelling with one another over?

So the woman who meant to send a month's provisions by ship to Gratilla in her exile gave a good answer to the man who told her Domitian would only confiscate them. "I would rather," she said, "that Domitian confiscated it all than that I did not send it."

What is it that makes us resort to divination so often, then? Cowardice — the dread of what is coming. That is why we flatter the diviners. Please, master, shall I inherit my father's property? "Let us see; let us sacrifice about it." Yes, master, as fortune decides. And when he has said, "You will inherit," we thank him as though we had the inheritance from him. And the result is that they play with us.

What should we do, then? We ought to come to divination without desire and without avoidance — as a traveller asks a man he meets which of two roads leads where he is going, with no wish for it to be the right-hand one rather than the left, since he has no wish to take any road but the one that leads to his destination.

That is how we ought to come to God as our guide: as we use our eyes, not asking them to show us the things we would prefer, but taking the appearances of things as the eyes present them.

But as it is, we take the augur's hand trembling, and while we call on God we plead with the augur: "Master, have mercy on me. Let me come safely out of this trouble." Wretch, would you have anything other than what is best? And is there anything better than what pleases God? Why do you do your utmost to corrupt your judge and lead your adviser astray?

Chapter 8: What the Nature of the Good Is

God is beneficial. And the good is beneficial too. So it follows that where the nature of God is, there the nature of the good is also.

What is the nature of God, then? Flesh? "Certainly not." An estate in land? "By no means." Fame? "No." Is it intelligence, knowledge, right reason? "Yes."

Then look for the nature of the good simply here. I take it you do not look for it in a plant. "No." Do you look for it in an animal without reason? "No." Then if you look for it in a rational creature, why do you go on looking anywhere except in what makes rational creatures superior to irrational ones?

Plants have not even the power of using impressions, and that is why you do not call them good. So the good requires the use of impressions. Does it require only that? If you say it requires only that, then say that good, and happiness and unhappiness, exist in animals too. But you do not say so, and you are right. Even if they have the use of impressions in the highest degree, they do not have the power of understanding that use — and with good reason, since they exist to serve others and exercise no superiority.

The donkey, I take it, does not exist for any superiority over others. No: we needed a back that could carry something, and in truth we needed him to be able to walk as well, and for that he was given the power of using impressions, since without it he could not have walked. And there the matter stopped. If he had also been given the power of understanding that use, then plainly, and reasonably, he would not have been subject to us and would not have done us these services; he would have been our equal and our like.

Will you not look for the nature of good in the rational creature, then? If it is not there, you will not want to say it exists in anything else.

"What of it? Are plants and animals not God's works too?"

They are; but they are not superior things, and they are not parts of the gods. You are a superior thing. You are a portion broken off from the divine. You have in yourself a certain portion of him.

Why, then, are you ignorant of your own high descent? Why do you not know where you came from? Will you not remember, when you are eating, who you are who eat, and whom you are feeding? When you lie with a woman, will you not remember who you are who do it? When you are in company, when you are exercising, when you are arguing, do you not know that you are feeding a god, that you are exercising a god? Wretch, you are carrying a god about with you and you do not know it.

Do you suppose I mean some god of silver or gold, outside you? You carry him inside yourself, and you do not notice that you are defiling him with unclean thoughts and dirty acts. If an image of God were standing here you would not dare to do any of the things you do; but when God himself is present inside you, seeing everything and hearing everything, you are not ashamed to think such things and do such things — ignorant of your own nature, and lying under the anger of God.

Then why are we afraid, when we send a young man out of the school into active life, that he may do something improper, eat improperly, sleep with women improperly? Afraid that the rags he is wrapped in may debase him, or that fine clothes may make him proud? A young man who behaves so does not know his own God; he does not know who set out with him.

And can we bear it when he says, "I wish I had God with me"? Have you not got God with you? And do you look for another when you have him? Or would God tell you anything different from this?

If you were a statue by Phidias — an Athena or a Zeus — you would think both of yourself and of the artist; and if you had any power of perception you would try to do nothing unworthy of the man who made you, or of yourself, and try not to appear in an unfitting posture to the people looking at you. And now, because Zeus made you, is that a reason for not caring how you will appear?

And is the one artist anything like the other? Is the one work anything like the other? What work of any artist has in itself the powers the artist shows in making it? Is it not marble, or bronze, or gold, or ivory? Phidias' Athena, once she has stretched out her hand and taken the figure of Victory into it, stands in that attitude forever. But the works of God have the power of movement, they breathe, they can use impressions and examine them.

Being the work of such an artist, will you dishonour him? And what shall I say — not only that he made you, but that he entrusted you to yourself and made you a deposit in your own keeping? Will you not think of that either? Will you dishonour your guardianship too?

If God had put an orphan into your care, would you neglect him like this? He has handed you over to your own care, and says: I had nobody fitter to entrust him to than yourself. Keep him for me as he is by nature — self-respecting, trustworthy, upright, unterrified, free from passion and free from being thrown about.

And then you do not keep him so.

But some will say: where has this fellow got his arrogance and these superior looks? I have not yet the gravity that suits a philosopher; I have not yet any confidence in what I have learned and assented to; I am still afraid of my own weakness. Let me get the confidence, and then you shall see the face I ought to have and the bearing I ought to have. Then I will show you the statue, when it is finished and polished.

What do you expect — a superior look? Does the Zeus at Olympia raise his eyebrow? No: his look is fixed, as befits one who is ready to say

	Irrevocable is my word, and it shall not fail.
	— Iliad, i 526.

That is how I will show myself to you: trustworthy, self-respecting, noble, undisturbed.

What, and immortal too, and exempt from old age and sickness? No — dying as a god dies, falling sick as a god falls sick. This power I have; this I can do. The rest I do not have, and cannot do.

I will show you the sinews of a philosopher. What sinews are those? A desire that is never disappointed. An avoidance that never lands on the thing it would avoid. An impulse that is proper, a purpose that is careful, an assent that is not rash. These you shall see.

Chapter 9: How We Claim the Title of Philosopher Because We Cannot Live Up to Being Human

It is no small thing just to do this one thing: to fulfil the promise a man's nature makes. For what is a man? The answer is: a rational and mortal creature. And by the rational part, what are we separated from? From wild beasts. And from what else? From sheep and animals like them.

So take care to do nothing like a wild beast. If you do, you have lost the character of a man; you have not kept your promise. See that you do nothing like a sheep — and if you do, in that case too the man is lost.

When do we behave like sheep? When we act out of greed, out of lust, rashly, filthily, thoughtlessly — what have we sunk to? To sheep. What have we lost? Our reason. And when we act quarrelsomely and harmfully and in a rage and violently, what have we sunk to? To wild beasts. So some of us are great wild beasts and others little ones, mean and small — which is why a man may say, let me at least be eaten by a lion.

In all these ways the promise a man makes by acting as a man is destroyed. When is a compound proposition maintained? When it does what its nature promises — so a compound proposition is preserved when it is a conjunction of truths. When is a disjunctive proposition maintained? When it does what it promises. When are flutes preserved, or a lyre, a horse, a dog? When each keeps its promise.

Is it any wonder, then, that a man is preserved and lost in the same way? Every man is improved and preserved by the acts that correspond to him: the carpenter by acts of carpentry, the grammarian by acts of grammar. But if a man gets into the habit of writing ungrammatically, his art must be corrupted and destroyed.

So acts of self-respect preserve the self-respecting man, and shameless acts destroy him; acts of good faith preserve the trustworthy man, and the contrary acts destroy him. And on the other side, contrary acts strengthen contrary characters: shamelessness strengthens the shameless man, faithlessness the faithless man, abuse the abusive man, anger the bad-tempered man; and taking more than he gives makes the greedy man greedier.

This is why philosophers warn us not to be satisfied with learning only, but to add study to it, and then practice. We have been used to doing the opposite for a long time, and we put into practice judgments that are the opposite of the true ones. So if we do not also put the right judgments into practice, we shall be nothing but expounders of other men's opinions.

Which of us cannot already discourse by the rules of the art about good and bad things? That some things are good and some bad and some indifferent; that the good are the virtues and what shares in the virtues, the bad their opposites, and the indifferent things are wealth, health, reputation.

And then, if in the middle of our talk there is some louder noise than usual, or somebody present laughs at us, we are thrown. Philosopher, where are the things you were talking about? Where did you get them and bring them out from? From your lips, and nowhere else. So why do you spoil the help other men provided? Why do you treat the weightiest matters as though you were playing at dice?

It is one thing to store bread and wine in a storehouse and another to eat them. What has been eaten is digested and distributed, and has become sinew, flesh, bone, blood, good colour, good breathing. What is stored up you can take out and show whenever you like — but you have no other advantage from it than seeming to possess it.

For what is the difference between expounding these doctrines and expounding those of men who hold the opposite? Sit down now and expound the opinions of Epicurus by the rules of the art, and perhaps you will expound them more usefully than Epicurus himself. Why call yourself a Stoic, then? Why deceive the crowd? Why act the part of a Jew when you are a Greek?

Do you not see on what grounds each man is called a Jew, or a Syrian, or an Egyptian? When we see a man leaning both ways we are used to saying, this man is no Jew, he is only acting the part. But when he takes on the feelings of a man who has been steeped in the doctrine and has adopted that sect, then he is one in fact, and he is called one.

So we too, dipped falsely, are Jews in name and something else in fact. Our feelings do not match our words. We are a long way from practising what we say, and from what we are proud of as though we knew it. And so, being unable to fulfil even what the character of a man promises, we take on the profession of a philosopher as well — a burden as heavy as if a man who cannot carry ten pounds tried to lift the stone that Ajax lifted.

Chapter 10: How to Work Out Your Duties from the Names You Bear

Consider who you are. In the first place, a man — that is, one who has nothing superior to his own will, and everything else subject to it, while the will itself he holds unenslaved and free.

So consider what reason has separated you from. It has separated you from wild beasts; it has separated you from the flocks. Beyond that, you are a citizen of the world and a part of it — not one of the parts that serve, but one of the parts that rule, since you are capable of following the divine administration and of thinking about how things are connected.

And what does the character of a citizen promise? To hold nothing as profitable to himself alone; to deliberate about nothing as though he were cut off from the community, but to act as the hand or the foot would act if they had reason and understood how nature is constituted — never setting themselves in motion or wanting anything except with reference to the whole.

So the philosophers say rightly that if the good man knew in advance what was going to happen, he would work along with his own sickness and death and maiming, knowing that these things are allotted him by the arrangement of the whole, and that the whole is superior to the part and the state to the citizen. But as it is, since we do not know the future, it is our duty to hold to the things that are by their nature more suitable to choose — because that too is one of the things we were made for.

After this, remember that you are a son. And what does that character promise? To count everything the son has as belonging to the father; to obey him in everything; never to blame him to anybody else; never to say or do anything that harms him; to give way to him in everything and yield, working with him as far as you can.

After this, know that you are a brother too, and that this character calls for making concessions, for being easily persuaded, for speaking well of your brother, for never claiming against him any of the things outside the will but readily giving them up, so as to have the larger share in what is inside it. See what a thing it is to gain a good disposition in exchange for a lettuce, if it comes to that, or a seat. What an advantage.

Next, if you are a councillor of some city, remember that you are a councillor; if a young man, that you are young; if an old man, that you are old. Each of these names, when it is examined, marks out its proper duties.

But if you go and blame your brother, I say to you: you have forgotten who you are and what your name is. If you were a smith and used the hammer wrongly, you would have forgotten the smith. And if you have forgotten the brother, and instead of a brother have become an enemy, will you seem to have exchanged nothing for nothing? And if instead of a man — a tame and social creature — you have become a mischievous wild animal, treacherous and given to biting, have you lost nothing?

Must you lose a little money, then, in order to have suffered damage? Does the loss of nothing else damage a man? If you lost your grasp of grammar or of music, would you count the loss a damage? And if you lose self-respect, restraint and gentleness, do you think that is no loss at all?

And yet the first things are lost through some external cause outside the will, while the second are lost by our own fault. Neither having the first nor losing them is shameful; but not having the second, and losing them, is shameful, and a reproach, and a misfortune.

What does a man lose who lets himself be used for another man's pleasure? He loses being a man. And what does the man lose who uses him so? A great many things — and he loses being a man no less than the other does. What does the adulterer lose? He loses the self-respecting man, the moderate man, the decent man, the citizen, the neighbour. What does the angry man lose? Something else. What does the coward lose? Something else.

No man is bad without suffering some loss and damage. So if you look for the damage only in lost money, then none of these men suffers any harm or damage at all — it may even be that they profit and gain, if they pick up a little money by any of these acts.

But consider: if you refer everything to a small coin, then not even the man who loses his nose is damaged, in your view. "Yes he is," you say, "he is mutilated in his body." Well — and does the man who has only lost his sense of smell lose nothing? Is there no energy of the soul, then, that is an advantage to the man who has it and a damage to the man who has lost it?

"Tell me what sort of energy you mean."

Have we not a natural sense of shame? "We have." And does the man who loses that suffer no damage? Is he deprived of nothing? Does he part with nothing that belonged to him? Have we not natural good faith, natural affection, a natural readiness to help others, a natural readiness to bear with them? Can the man who lets himself be damaged in these matters be unharmed and uninjured?

"What of it? Am I not to hurt the man who has hurt me?"

First consider what hurt is, and remember what you have heard from the philosophers. If the good lies in the will and the bad in the will too, then see whether what you are saying does not come to this: since that man has hurt himself by doing me an injustice, am I not to hurt myself by doing him one?

Why do we not put it to ourselves in some such way? Where there is any injury to the body or to our property, there we find harm; but where the same thing happens to the will, there — you suppose — there is no harm. The man who has been deceived, or who has done an unjust act, suffers nothing in his head, or his eye, or his hip, and he does not lose his estate; and we want nothing except that these things should be safe.

But whether we shall have a will that is self-respecting and trustworthy, or shameless and faithless, we do not care in the least — except in the school, and there only as far as a few words go. And that is why our progress is limited to those few words, and beyond them does not exist in the slightest degree.

Chapter 11: Where Philosophy Begins

Philosophy begins — for the man who comes to it the right way, through the door — with an awareness of his own weakness and inability in the things that matter.

We come into the world with no innate notion of a right-angled triangle, or of a quarter tone, or of a semitone; we learn each of these by a sort of instruction according to an art, and that is why men who do not know them do not think they know them. But as for good and evil, beautiful and ugly, fitting and unfitting, happiness and misfortune, proper and improper, what we ought to do and what we ought not — who ever came into the world without an inborn idea of these?

So we all use the words, and try to fit our preconceptions to particular cases: he did well, he did not do well; he did as he ought, not as he ought; he has been unlucky, he has been lucky; he is unjust, he is just. Which of us does not use these words? Which of us puts off using them until he has learned them, as he puts off using the words for lines or for musical notes?

The reason is that we come into the world already taught something on this subject by nature, so to speak — and starting from that, we have added conceit on top.

"Why should I not know the beautiful and the ugly?" a man says. "Have I not the notion of it?"

You have.

"Do I not fit it to particulars?"

You do.

"Then do I not fit it properly?"

There lies the whole question, and there is where the conceit comes in. Starting from what is agreed, men go on by an unsuitable fitting to what is in dispute. If they had this power of fitting as well as the notions, what would stop them being perfect?

But since you think you fit your preconceptions to particulars properly, tell me where you get that from.

"Because I think so."

But somebody else does not think so, and he thinks he is fitting them properly too. Or does he not think so?

"He does think so."

Is it possible, then, that you can both be fitting your preconceptions properly to things you hold contrary opinions about?

"It is not possible."

Then can you show us anything better as regards fitting the preconceptions than your thinking that you do? Does the madman do anything other than what seems right to him? Is that test enough for him too?

"It is not enough."

Then come to something better than seeming.

"And what is that?"

Notice: here is the beginning of philosophy. A perception that men disagree with one another; an enquiry into the cause of the disagreement; a condemnation of mere seeming and a distrust of it; a certain investigation of what seems, to see whether it seems rightly; and the discovery of some standard — as we discovered the balance for settling weights, and the carpenter's rule for straight and crooked. This is where philosophy begins.

Must we say that everything is right which seems right to everybody? And how can contradictions be right? So not everything, then — only what seems right to us. And why more than what seems right to the Syrians? Why more than what seems right to the Egyptians? Why more than what seems right to me, or to any other man? Not more at all.

So what seems so to each man is not sufficient for settling what is so. With weights and measures we are not satisfied with the bare appearance either; in each case we have found a standard. And in this matter, then, is there no standard better than seeming? How could it be that the most necessary things among men should have no mark, and be impossible to find out?

So there is a standard. Then why do we not look for it and find it, and afterwards use it without ever departing from it — never so much as stretching out a finger without it? For this, I think, is the thing which, once found, cures the madness of men who use mere seeming as their measure and misuse it; so that from then on, starting from certain known and settled principles, we may use in particular cases the preconceptions we have clearly fixed.

What is the matter put before us for enquiry? Pleasure, let us say. Put it to the standard; throw it into the balance. Ought the good to be the sort of thing we can properly have confidence in? Yes. And the sort of thing we ought to trust? It ought to be. Is it right to trust anything insecure? No. And is pleasure secure? No. Then take it and throw it out of the scale, and drive it far away from the place where the good things stand.

And if you are not sharp-sighted and one balance is not enough for you, bring another. Is it right to be lifted up over what is good? Yes. Is it proper, then, to be lifted up over a present pleasure? Take care not to say that it is; and if you do say it, I shall not think you worth even the balance.

This is how things are tested and weighed, when the standards are ready. And philosophising is this: examining the standards and establishing them. Using them once they are known is the work of a wise and good man.

Chapter 12: On Argument and Discussion

What a man must learn in order to practise the art of argument has been set out accurately by our philosophers. But as for the proper use of it, we have no practice at all.

Give any one of us you like an uneducated man to argue with, and he cannot find out how to handle him. He shifts the man a little, and then if the man answers off the point he does not know what to do with him, and either abuses him or laughs at him and says, "He is uneducated; you can do nothing with him."

But a guide, when he has found a man off the road, leads him into the right one; he does not laugh at him and abuse him and walk away. So do you show the uneducated man the truth, and you will see that he follows. And as long as you do not show it to him, do not laugh at him — feel your own incapacity instead.

How did Socrates behave? He used to make his opponent in an argument bear witness for him, and he wanted no other witness. So he could say: I do not care about other witnesses; I am always satisfied with the evidence of the man I am arguing with. I do not ask other people's opinion, only the opinion of the man disputing with me.

He used to make the conclusions that follow from our natural notions so plain that every man saw the contradiction, if there was one, and backed out of it. Like this. Does the envious man feel joy? "By no means — he feels pain." Well, do you take envy to be pain over evils? "And what envy is there of evils?" So he brought his opponent to say that envy is pain over good things. Well then, would any man envy people who are nothing to him? "By no means."

And having completed the notion and fixed it clearly, he would go away — without saying to his opponent, "Define envy for me"; and if the man had defined it, without saying, "You have defined it badly, the terms of the definition do not answer to the thing defined."

Those are technical phrases, and for that reason unpleasant and barely intelligible to uneducated men — and we philosophers cannot do without them. But that the uneducated man himself, following the impressions put before him, should be able to concede a point or reject it, we shall never move him to by using such phrases. And so, conscious of our own inability, we do not attempt it — those of us with any caution, at least. But most men, the rash ones, go into such arguments, confuse themselves and confuse others, and finally walk away abusing their opponents and abused in turn.

Now this was the first and chief thing about Socrates: never to be irritated in argument, never to say anything abusive or insulting, but to bear with abusive people and put an end to the quarrel. If you want to know how great his power was in this, read Xenophon's Symposium and see how many quarrels he ended. That is why the poets too praise this power so highly:

	Quickly and skilfully he settles great disputes.
	— Hesiod, Theogony v 87.

Well then — the business is not very safe nowadays, and particularly at Rome. Anyone who attempts it must not do it in a corner, you may be sure; he must go to a man of consular rank, if it so happens, or to a rich man, and ask him:

Sir, can you tell me who you have put in charge of your horses? "I can tell you." You have not entrusted them to just anybody, to a man with no experience of horses? "By no means." Well, can you tell me who you entrust your gold and silver to, or your clothes? "I do not entrust even those to just anybody." Well — and your own body: have you thought about who to trust the care of it to? "Certainly." To a man of experience, I suppose — a trainer, or a doctor? "Without doubt."

And are these the best things you have, or do you possess something else better than all of them? "What sort of thing do you mean?" I mean the thing that uses all these, and tests each of them, and deliberates. "Do you mean the soul?" You are right, that is what I mean. "In truth I do think the soul is a far better thing than anything else I possess."

Then can you show us how you have taken care of the soul? It is hardly likely that a man as wise as you, with a reputation in the city, would thoughtlessly and carelessly let the most valuable thing he owns go neglected and perish. "Certainly not." But have you taken care of it yourself? And did you learn how from somebody else, or work out the means on your own?

And here comes the danger. First, that he may say, "What business is this of yours, my good man? Who are you?" And then, if you go on bothering him, that he may raise his hands and hit you. I was an admirer of this method of teaching myself once, until I fell into these dangers.

Chapter 13: On Anxiety

When I see a man anxious I say: what does this man want? If he did not want something outside his power, how could he be anxious?

This is why a lute player singing on his own has no anxiety, but is anxious when he comes into the theatre, even if he has a good voice and plays well. He wants not only to sing well but to be applauded — and that is not in his power. Where he has skill, he has confidence. Bring him any single person who knows nothing about music and the musician does not care. But where he knows nothing and has had no practice, there he is anxious.

What is he ignorant of? He does not know what a crowd is, or what the praise of a crowd is. He has learned to strike the lowest string and the highest; but what the praise of the many is, and what power it has in life, he neither knows nor has thought about. So he must tremble and turn pale.

Now I cannot say a man is not a lute player when I see him afraid; but I can say something else, and not one thing but several. First of all I call him a stranger, and say: this man does not know what part of the world he is in. He has been here all this time and is ignorant of the laws of the state and its customs, of what is allowed and what is not; and he has never employed a lawyer to tell him and explain the laws to him.

A man does not draw up a will if he does not know how a will ought to be drawn; or else he employs somebody who does. He does not seal a bond carelessly, or write a guarantee. But he uses his desire without legal advice — and his avoidance, and his pursuit, and his intention, and his purpose.

"What do you mean, without legal advice?"

He does not know that he is willing what is not allowed, and not willing what must be; and he does not know what is his own and what is another man's. If he did know, he would never be blocked, never hindered, never anxious.

"How so?"

Is any man afraid of things that are not evils? No. Is he afraid of things that are evils, but so far in his own power that they need not happen? Certainly not. So if the things outside the will are neither good nor bad, and everything that does depend on the will is in our power, and nobody can either take them from us or give them to us unless we choose — where is there room left for anxiety?

But we are anxious about our poor body, our little property, about what Caesar wills; and not anxious about anything inside us. Are we anxious about not forming a false judgment? No — because that is in my power. About not making a movement contrary to nature? No, not about that either.

So when you see a man pale, then — as the doctor says, judging by the complexion, this man's spleen is disordered and that man's liver — you too should say: this man's desire and avoidance are disordered; he is not on the right road; he is in a fever. Nothing else changes a man's colour, or makes him tremble, or sets his teeth chattering, or makes him

	sink at the knees and shift from foot to foot.
	— Iliad, xiii 281.

This is why Zeno was not anxious when he was going to meet Antigonus: Antigonus had no power over anything Zeno valued, and Zeno did not care about the things Antigonus did have power over. But Antigonus was anxious when he was going to meet Zeno, because he wanted to please him — and that was an external. Zeno did not want to please Antigonus, since no man skilled in an art wants to please a man who has no skill in it.

Am I to try to please you? Why? I suppose you know the measure by which one man is judged by another. Have you taken any trouble to learn what a good man is and what a bad man is, and how a man becomes either? Then why are you not good yourself?

"How am I not good?" he replies.

Because no good man laments or groans or weeps; no good man turns pale and trembles, or says, "How will he receive me? How will he listen to me?" Slave, just as it pleases him. Why do you care about what belongs to somebody else? Is it not his fault now if he takes badly what comes from you? "Certainly." And can the fault be one man's and the evil in another? "No." Then why are you anxious about what belongs to another?

"That is a fair question. But I am anxious about how I shall speak to him."

Can you not speak to him as you choose?

"But I am afraid of being put out."

If you were going to write the name Dion, would you be afraid of being put out? "By no means." Why not? Is it not because you have practised writing the name? "Certainly." And if you were going to read the name, would you not feel the same? Why? Because every art has a certain strength and confidence in the things belonging to it.

Have you not practised speaking, then? What else did you learn at school — syllogisms and trick propositions? What for? Was it not for the purpose of arguing skilfully? And is arguing skilfully not the same as arguing at the right time, and carefully, and intelligently, and without making mistakes and without getting blocked, and on top of all that, with confidence? "Yes."

Well then, when you are mounted on a horse and ride out onto the plain, are you anxious at being matched against a man on foot — anxious in a matter you have practised and he has not?

"Yes, but the man I am going to speak to has power to kill me."

Then tell the truth, unhappy man, and stop bragging, and stop claiming to be a philosopher, and stop refusing to admit who your masters are. So long as you offer this handle in your body, follow every man who is stronger than you.

Socrates used to practise speaking — he who talked as he did to the tyrants, to the jurymen, and in his prison. Diogenes had practised speaking — he who spoke as he did to Alexander, to the pirates, to the man who bought him. These men were confident in what they had practised.

But you go off to your own affairs and never leave them. Go and sit in a corner and weave syllogisms and set them to somebody else. There is no man in you who can govern a state.

Chapter 14: To Naso

A certain Roman came in with his son and listened to one reading. Epictetus said: This is the method of instruction — and stopped. When the Roman asked him to go on, he said:

Every art, while it is being taught, is laborious to a man who does not know it and has no skill in it. The things that come out of the arts show their use at once, in the purpose they were made for, and most of them have something attractive and pleasing about them. It is no pleasure to stand and watch a shoemaker learning his trade; but the shoe is useful, and not unpleasant to look at either. Watching a smith at his training is very disagreeable to a bystander who knows nothing of the art; but the work shows what the art is for. And you will see this much more clearly with music: if you are present while somebody is learning, the drill will seem thoroughly disagreeable, and yet the results of music are pleasant and delightful even to people who know nothing about it.

Here too we conceive the work of a philosopher to be something of this kind: he must fit his wishes to what happens, so that nothing that does happen happens against our wish, and nothing that fails to happen fails when we wish it would. And the result, for men who have arranged the work of philosophy in this way, is not to fail in their desires, and not to fall into what they would avoid; to pass through life themselves without unease, without fear, without being thrown about — and along with their companions to keep up every relation, natural and acquired: son, father, brother, citizen, man, wife, neighbour, fellow traveller, ruler, subject.

This is roughly what we conceive the work of a philosopher to be. It remains to enquire how it is to be accomplished.

Now we see that a carpenter becomes a carpenter by learning certain things, and a pilot becomes a pilot by learning certain things. Is it not likely, then, that in philosophy too it is not enough simply to wish to be wise and good, and that there are certain things one must learn? So we enquire what those things are.

The philosophers say we ought to learn first that God exists and provides for everything, and that it is not possible to hide our acts from him — nor even our intentions and thoughts. The next thing is to learn what the nature of the gods is; because whatever they are found to be, a man who wants to please them and obey them must try with all his power to be like them. If the divine is trustworthy, a man must be trustworthy. If it is free, a man must be free; if generous, generous; if great in spirit, great in spirit. So as an imitator of God he must do and say everything consistently with that.

Where must we begin, then? If you will go into the discussion, I will tell you: you must first understand words.

"So you are saying I do not understand words now."

You do not understand them.

"Then how do I use them?"

The way the illiterate use writing, the way cattle use impressions. Using and understanding are two different things. But if you think you understand them, produce any word you please and let us test whether we understand it.

"It is a disagreeable thing for a man to be refuted when he is old, and has perhaps served his three campaigns."

I know it. You have come to me as a man in want of nothing — and what could you even imagine yourself to be short of? You are rich; you have children, and perhaps a wife, and many slaves; Caesar knows you; you have many friends at Rome; you pay everybody their dues; you know how to repay a man who does you a favour and to pay back in kind a man who does you a wrong. What are you lacking?

Now suppose I show you that you lack the most necessary things of all, the chief things for happiness; that so far you have attended to everything except what you ought; and, to crown it, that you know neither what God is nor what man is, nor what good is nor what bad. If I say you are ignorant of the other matters, that might perhaps be borne. But if I say that you know nothing about yourself — how could you possibly bear with me and stand the proof and stay here? It is not possible; you go off at once in a bad temper.

And yet what harm have I done you? Unless the mirror harms an ugly man too, by showing him himself as he is. Unless the doctor insults the sick man when he tells him: man, do you think there is nothing wrong with you? You have a fever. Go without food today and drink water. Nobody says, "What an insult!" But say to a man, "Your desires are inflamed, your avoidances are low, your intentions are inconsistent, your impulses are not in line with nature, your judgments are rash and false" — and off he goes at once saying, "He insulted me."

Our way of doing things is like a crowded fair. Cattle and oxen are brought to be sold, and most of the men come to buy and sell; and there are a few who come to look at the market and enquire how it is run, and why, and who fixed the day of the meeting and for what purpose.

So it is in this assembly of ours too. Some, like cattle, trouble themselves about nothing but their fodder. To all of you who are busy about property and land and slaves and public office, these things are nothing but fodder.

But there are a few who attend the fair as men who love to look on and consider: what is the world? Who governs it? Has it no governor? How is it possible that a city or a household cannot last even the shortest time without an administrator and a guardian, and that a system so great and so beautiful should be run in such good order and yet without purpose, by chance? So there is an administrator. What kind, and how does he govern? And who are we, whom he produced, and what for? Have we some connection with him and some relation to him, or none?

That is how these few are affected; and then they apply themselves to this one thing only, to examine the meeting, and then to go away.

And what of it? They are laughed at by the crowd, as the sightseers at a fair are laughed at by the traders. And if the cattle had any understanding, they would laugh at anybody who admired anything but fodder.

Chapter 15: Against Those Who Stubbornly Stick to What They Have Decided

When some people have heard that a man ought to be firm, and that the will is naturally free and not subject to compulsion while everything else is subject to blocking and to slavery and lies in other men's power, they suppose they ought to hold without deviation to everything they have decided.

But in the first place what has been decided ought to be sound. I want tone in a body — but the tone a healthy body has, an athlete's body. If it is plain to me that you have the tone of a man in a frenzy, and you boast of it, I shall tell you: man, go and find a doctor. That is not tone; that is the loss of it.

Something of the same kind, in a different way, happens to men who listen to these lectures the wrong way. One of my own companions decided for no reason to starve himself to death. I heard of it on the third day of his fast and went to ask what had happened.

"I have decided," he said.

Still — tell me what made you decide. If you decided rightly, we shall sit with you and help you on your way; but if the decision was unreasonable, change your mind.

"We ought to keep to our decisions."

Man, what are you doing? Not to all our decisions — to the right ones. If you are now convinced that it is night, do not change your mind, if you think fit; hold on and say, we ought to stand by our decisions. Will you not begin at the beginning and lay the foundation — an enquiry into whether the decision is sound or unsound — and then build firmness and security on top of it? If you lay a rotten and crumbling foundation, will your miserable little building not come down all the faster, the more and the heavier the materials you pile on it?

Would you take a man out of life for no reason at all — a friend and a companion, a fellow citizen of the same city, both the great city and the small one? And then, while you are committing murder and destroying a man who has done no wrong, do you say you must stand by your decisions? And if it ever somehow came into your head to kill me, would you have to stand by that decision too?

Now this man was persuaded to change his mind, though with difficulty. But there are some you cannot convince at all these days; so that I feel I now know what I did not know before — the meaning of the saying that you can neither persuade a fool nor break him. May it never be my lot to have a wise fool for a friend: there is nothing harder to deal with.

"I have made up my mind," the man says. So have madmen; and the more firmly they form a judgment about things that do not exist, the more hellebore they need to bring them round.

Will you not behave like a sick man and call in the doctor? "I am ill, master; help me. Consider what I must do; it is my part to obey you." So it is here too: I do not know what I ought to do, and I have come to learn. "Not at all — talk to me about something else. On this I have decided."

About what else? What is greater or more useful than convincing you that it is not enough to have made your decision and to refuse to change it? This is the tone of madness, not of health.

"I will die if you force me to this."

Why, man? What has happened?

"I have decided."

I have had a lucky escape, then, that you did not decide to kill me.

"I will take no money."

And why is that?

"I have decided."

You may be sure that with the very same energy you are now using to refuse it, there is nothing to stop you at some point leaning without any reason toward taking money, and then saying: I have decided. As in a diseased body full of fluxes the humour flows now to these parts and now to those, so a sick soul does not know which way to lean; and if to that leaning and that movement obstinacy is added as well, then the trouble is past help and past cure.

Chapter 16: That We Do Not Practice Applying Our Judgments About Good and Evil

Where is the good? In the will. Where is the evil? In the will. Where is neither of them? In the things outside the will.

Well then — does anybody among us think about these lessons outside the schoolroom? Does anybody practise on his own, giving answers to things as he would to questions? Is it day? Yes. Is it night? No. Well, is the number of the stars even? I cannot say.

When money is held out to you, have you practised making the proper answer, that money is not a good thing? Have you drilled yourself in those answers, or only against sophisms? Why be surprised, then, if in the cases you have studied you have improved, and in the cases you have not studied you are exactly as you were?

When the public speaker knows that he has written well, that he has learned by heart what he wrote, that he brings a pleasant voice with him — why is he still anxious? Because he is not satisfied with having studied. What does he want? To be praised by the audience. So he has been trained for the purpose of being able to declaim; but as to praise and blame, he has not been trained at all. When did he ever hear from anybody what praise is, and what blame is, and what the nature of each is, and what kind of praise should be sought and what kind of blame avoided? And when did he go through the drill that follows from all that?

Why be surprised, then, if in the matters a man has learned he surpasses everybody, and in the matters he has never been trained in he is just like the crowd? So the lute player knows how to play, sings well, has a fine costume — and still trembles when he comes on stage. He understands his own business, but he does not know what a crowd is, or the shouting of a crowd, or what ridicule is. He does not know what anxiety is either, whether it is his own work or somebody else's, whether it can be stopped or not. And so if he is praised he leaves the theatre puffed up, and if he is laughed at, the swollen bladder is punctured and goes down.

It is the same with us. What do we admire? Externals. What are we busy about? Externals. And then do we still wonder why we are afraid and anxious?

What happens when we take the things coming toward us to be evils? It is not in our power not to be afraid; it is not in our power not to be anxious. And then we say: "Lord God, how am I not to be anxious?" Fool, have you not got hands? Did God not make them for you? Sit down now and pray that your nose may not run. Wipe it instead, and stop blaming him.

Well then — has he given you nothing for the present case? Has he not given you endurance? Has he not given you greatness of spirit? Has he not given you courage? When you have hands like these, are you still looking for somebody to wipe your nose for you?

But we neither study these things nor care about them. Give me a man who cares how he does a thing — not about getting the thing, but about his own activity in doing it. Who cares about his own activity while he is walking about? Who, when he is deliberating, cares about his own deliberating rather than about getting the thing he is deliberating over? If he gets it he is puffed up and says, "How well we deliberated! Did I not tell you, brother, that when we have thought a thing out it cannot turn out otherwise?" And if it turns out otherwise, the poor wretch is humbled and does not even know what to say about what has happened.

Which of us ever consulted a seer about this matter? Which of us ever went sleepless over his own acts? Which? Name me one, so that I may see the man I have been looking for so long, who is truly noble and well-born, young or old. Name him.

Why are we still surprised, then, that we are well practised at thinking about a subject, and in our actions are low, indecent, worthless, cowardly, unwilling to work, altogether bad? We do not care about these things and we do not study them.

If we had feared not death or exile but fear itself, we should have studied not to fall into the things that look like evils to us. As it is, in the schoolroom we are quick-tempered and full of words, and if some small question comes up about any of these matters we are quite able to go through it. But drag us out into practice and you will find us miserably shipwrecked. Let one disturbing impression come at us and you will know what we have been studying and what we have been training at.

And so, for want of discipline, we are always adding something to the impression and making things out to be bigger than they are. When I am at sea and look down into the deep, or look around and see no land, I go out of my mind and imagine I shall have to drink up the whole of it if I am wrecked — and it does not occur to me that three pints are enough. So what is disturbing me? The sea? No: my own judgment. And when there is an earthquake, I imagine the city is going to fall on me; but is one small stone not enough to knock my brains out?

What are the things that lie heavy on us and disturb us, then? What but judgments? What but judgments lies heavy on the man who goes away and leaves his companions and friends and places and habits?

Little children, for instance, cry when the nurse leaves them for a moment, and forget their grief the moment they are given a small cake. Would you like us to compare you with little children? No, by Zeus — I do not want to be quieted with a small cake, but with right judgments.

And what are those? What a man ought to study all day long: not to be affected by anything that is not his own, not by a companion, not by a place, not by the gymnasium, not even by his own body; but to remember the law and keep it in front of his eyes.

And what is the divine law? To keep hold of your own; not to lay claim to what belongs to others; to use what is given, and not to want it when it is not given; and when something is taken away, to give it up readily and at once, and to be grateful for the time you had the use of it — unless you would rather cry for your nurse and your mother.

For what difference does it make what a man is mastered by, and what he depends on? How are you better than the man who cries over a girl, if you grieve over a little gymnasium and some little colonnades and young men and places of amusement? Another man comes and laments that he will no longer drink the water of Dirce. Is the Marcian water worse than Dirce's? "But I was used to the water of Dirce." And you will get used to the other in turn. And then if you become attached to that too, cry over it as well, and try to make a line of verse in Euripides' manner:

	The hot baths of Nero and the Marcian water.

See how tragedy is made, when ordinary things happen to silly men.

"When shall I see Athens again, and the Acropolis?" Wretch, are you not content with what you see every day? Have you anything better or greater to look at than the sun, the moon, the stars, the whole earth, the sea? And if you really understand the one who administers the whole, and carry him about inside you, do you still hanker after little stones and a handsome rock?

What will you do, then, when you are going to leave the sun and the moon themselves? Sit and weep like a child?

Well — what have you been doing in the school? What did you hear? What did you learn? Why did you write yourself down a philosopher when you might have written the truth: "I made some introductory studies and I read Chrysippus, but I never even came to the door of a philosopher"?

How could I have anything of the kind Socrates had, who died as he died and lived as he lived? Or anything of the kind Diogenes had? Do you suppose either of those men ever wept or grieved because he was not going to see a certain man, or a certain woman, or because he would be not in Athens or Corinth but, as it might happen, in Susa or Ecbatana? If a man can leave the banquet whenever he chooses and stop amusing himself, does he stay and complain? Does he not stay, as at any entertainment, only so long as he is enjoying it? A man like that, I take it, would put up with exile for life, or with a death sentence.

Will you not be weaned now, like children, and take more solid food, and stop crying after mothers and nurses — which are old women's laments?

"But if I go away, I shall cause them grief."

You cause them grief? By no means. What causes you grief is what will cause them grief: judgment. So what have you to do? Take away your own judgment; and if these women are wise they will take away theirs, and if they do not, they will lament through their own fault.

Man, as the proverb says, make one desperate effort for peace of mind, for freedom, for greatness of spirit. Lift up your head at last, like a man released from slavery. Dare to look up to God and say: deal with me from now on as you will. I am of one mind with you; I am yours. I refuse nothing that pleases you. Lead me where you will. Dress me in whatever clothes you choose. Is it your will that I should hold office, or live as a private man; stay here, or go into exile; be poor, or be rich? I will defend you before men on account of every one of these conditions. I will show what the nature of each thing is.

You will do no such thing. You will sit in the belly of an ox and wait for your mother to come and feed you.

What would Hercules have been if he had sat at home? He would have been Eurystheus, not Hercules. And in his travels through the world, how many close friends did he have? But nothing was dearer to him than God. That is why he was believed to be the son of God — and he was. In obedience to God he went about clearing away injustice and lawlessness.

But you are not Hercules and cannot clear away other men's wickedness; and you are not Theseus either, able to clear the evils out of Attica. Clear away your own. Out of yourself, out of your own thoughts, cast — instead of Procrustes and Sciron — sadness, fear, desire, envy, spite, greed, softness, lack of self-control.

And it is not possible to throw these things out except by looking to God alone, fixing your affections on him alone, and being consecrated to his commands. If you choose anything else, you will follow with sighs and groans whatever is stronger than yourself, always looking for peace of mind and never able to find it — because you look for it where it is not, and neglect to look where it is.

Chapter 17: How to Fit Preconceptions to Particular Cases

What is the first business of the man who takes up philosophy? To throw away conceit. It is impossible for a man to begin learning what he thinks he already knows.

As to what ought to be done and ought not to be done, and good and bad, and beautiful and ugly, we all talk about them at random and then go to the philosophers. On these matters we praise, we censure, we accuse, we blame, we pass judgment about what is honourable and dishonourable. And why do we go to the philosophers? Because we want to learn what we do not think we know. And what is that? Theorems. We want to learn what philosophers say, as something elegant and sharp; and some want to learn so as to profit by it.

It is ridiculous to suppose that a man wants to learn one thing and will learn another, or that he will make progress in what he does not learn.

But most men are taken in by what took in Theopompus the rhetorician, when he blames even Plato for wanting everything defined. What does he say? "Did none of us use the words good or just before you? Or did we utter the sounds meaninglessly and emptily, without understanding what each of them signified?"

Now who tells you, Theopompus, that we had no natural notions of these things, no preconceptions? But it is not possible to fit preconceptions to the objects that answer to them unless we have analysed them and enquired what object has to be put under each preconception.

You could bring the same charge against doctors. Which of us did not use the words healthy and unhealthy before Hippocrates lived? Did we utter them as empty sounds? We have a certain preconception of health too — but we are not able to fit it. That is why one man says "go without food", another says "give food", another says "bleed him", and another "use cupping". What is the reason? What but that a man cannot properly fit the preconception of health to particular cases?

So it is here too, in the matters that concern living. Which of us does not talk about good and bad, useful and useless? Which of us has not a preconception of each? And is it a clear and complete preconception? Show me. How shall I show it? By fitting the preconception properly to particular things.

Plato, for instance, puts definitions under the preconception of the useful, and you put them under the preconception of the useless. Is it possible that you are both right? How could it be? Does one man not fit the preconception of good to wealth, and another not to wealth but to pleasure, or to health? In general, if all of us who use these words know each of them well enough and need take no trouble over making the notions distinct, why do we differ? Why do we quarrel? Why do we blame one another?

And why am I bringing up this quarrelling with each other at all? If you fit your own preconceptions properly, why are you unhappy? Why are you blocked?

Let us leave aside for the moment the second topic, about impulses and the study of the duties belonging to them. Let us leave aside the third as well, about assent. I make you a present of those two. Let us stay with the first, which offers an almost obvious proof that we do not fit our preconceptions properly.

Do you now desire what is possible, and possible for you? Then why are you blocked? Why are you unhappy? Do you not now try to avoid the unavoidable? Then why do you fall into anything you would avoid? Why are you unlucky? Why, when you desire a thing, does it not happen; and when you do not desire it, does it happen? This is the greatest proof of unhappiness and misery: I wish for something and it does not happen. What is more wretched than I am?

It was because she could not bear this that Medea came to murder her children — an act of a great spirit, at least in this respect: she had a correct view of what it is for a thing one wishes for not to come off. And then she says: so I shall be avenged on the man who wronged and insulted me. And what do I gain if he is punished so? How is it to be done? I will kill my children — but I shall be punishing myself as well. And what do I care?

This is the wandering of a soul with great energy in it. She did not know where the power of doing what we wish actually lies — that you cannot get it from outside, nor by rearranging things and refitting them. Do not desire the man, and nothing you desire will fail to happen. Do not obstinately desire that he live with you. Do not desire to stay in Corinth. In a word, desire nothing but what God wills. And who will block you then? Who will compel you? No man will compel you, any more than he will compel Zeus.

When you have such a guide, and your wishes and desires are the same as his, why do you still fear disappointment? Hand your desire over to wealth and your avoidance to poverty, and you will be disappointed of the one and fall into the other. Hand them over to health, and you will come off badly. Hand them over to public office, honours, country, friends, children — in a word to anything not in a man's power — and you will come off badly.

But hand them over to Zeus and the rest of the gods. Surrender them to the gods; let the gods govern; let your desire and your avoidance be ranged on the gods' side — and where will you be unhappy any longer?

But if, you lazy wretch, you envy and complain and are jealous and afraid, and never let a single day pass without complaining both of yourself and of the gods, why do you still talk of being educated? What kind of education, man? Do you mean that you have busied yourself with shifting syllogisms? Will you not, if it is possible, unlearn all that and begin at the beginning — and see at the same time that so far you have not even touched the matter? And then, starting from that foundation, will you not build up everything that comes after, so that nothing happens that you do not choose, and nothing fails to happen that you do?

Give me one young man who has come to the school with this intention, who has become a champion for this cause and says: I give up everything else, and it is enough for me if it ever comes into my power to pass my life unblocked and untroubled, to hold out my neck to everything like a free man, and to look up to heaven as a friend of God and fear nothing that can happen.

Let any of you point such a man out to me, so that I may say: come, young man, into possession of what is your own. It is your destiny to be an ornament to philosophy. Yours are these possessions, yours these books, yours these discourses.

And then, when he has laboured enough and trained himself in that part of the matter, let him come to me again and say: I want to be free of passion and free of disturbance; and I want, as a devout man and a philosopher and a careful one, to know what my duty is to the gods, to my parents, to my brothers, to my country, to strangers. Come to the second matter as well, I shall say; this too is yours.

"But I have studied the second part enough now, and I would gladly be secure and unshaken — not only awake, but asleep too, and when I am full of wine, and when I am low."

Man, you are a god. Your designs are great.

No: "what I want is to understand what Chrysippus says in his treatise on the Liar." Will you not go and hang yourself, wretch, with an intention like that? And what good will it do you? You will read the whole thing in sorrow, and speak about it to others trembling.

And this is just what you do. "Would you like me to read to you, brother, and you to me?" You write excellently, my man. And you, excellently, in the style of Xenophon; and you in Plato's style; and you in the style of Antisthenes. And then, having told each other your dreams, you go back to exactly the same things: the same desires, the same avoidances, the same pursuits, the same plans and purposes; you want the same things and work for the same things.

And after that you do not even look for anybody to give you advice — you are annoyed if you hear the sort of thing I am saying. Then you say: "A bad-tempered old fellow. When I was leaving he did not weep, and he did not say, 'What danger you are going into! If you come through safe, my child, I will light lamps for it.' That is what a kindly man would do."

It will be a great thing indeed if you do come back safe, and well worth lighting lamps over — for such a person as you. You ought to be immortal, of course, and exempt from disease.

So, as I say, throwing away this conceit of thinking we know something useful, we must come to philosophy as we come to geometry and to music. Otherwise we shall not so much as approach making progress, though we read every collection and commentary of Chrysippus, and Antipater's and Archedemus' as well.

Chapter 18: How to Fight Against Impressions

Every habit and every capacity is kept up and increased by the acts that correspond to it: the habit of walking by walking, the habit of running by running. If you want to be a good reader, read; if a writer, write. But when you have not read for thirty days running and have done something else instead, you will see the result.
