In the same way, lie down for ten days and then get up and try to take a long walk, and you will see how weak your legs have got.

So, in general, if you want to make anything a habit, do it; and if you do not want to make it a habit, do not do it — get into the way of doing something else instead.

It is the same with the states of the soul. When you have been angry, you must understand that not only has this evil happened to you, but that you have also strengthened the habit, and thrown fuel on the fire, so to speak. When you have been beaten by the desire to sleep with somebody, do not count it as a single defeat: count that you have also fed and increased your own lack of self-control. It is impossible for habits and capacities not to be produced by the corresponding acts where they did not exist before, or not to be increased and strengthened by them where they did.

This is certainly how diseases of the mind grow up, as the philosophers say. Once you have desired money, if reason is applied so as to bring you to see the evil, the desire is stopped and the governing part of the mind is restored to its original authority. But if you apply no cure, it does not go back to the same state; roused again by the corresponding impression, it takes fire into desire quicker than before. And when this happens over and over, the mind grows callous, and the disease of the mind fixes the love of money in place.

A man who has had a fever and been relieved of it is not in the state he was in before, unless he has been completely cured. Something of the kind happens with diseases of the soul as well. Certain traces and blisters are left behind, and unless a man rubs them out completely, the next time the lash falls on the same spot it will raise not blisters but open sores.

So if you do not want to be a bad-tempered man, do not feed the habit. Throw nothing on it that will make it grow. At first keep quiet, and count the days on which you have not been angry. I used to lose my temper every day; then every other day; then every third, then every fourth. And if you have gone thirty days without, sacrifice to God. For the habit is weakened at first, and then destroyed altogether.

"I was not vexed today, nor the day after, nor on any day since, for two or three months — but I was careful when provoking things happened." Be sure you are on a good road.

Today, when I saw a handsome woman, I did not say to myself, I wish I could have her, and happy is her husband — because a man who says that is saying, happy is her lover too. Nor do I picture the rest of it: the woman there, undressing, lying down beside me. I stroke my own head and say: well done, Epictetus, you have solved a fine little puzzle, much finer than the one they call the Master Argument. And if the woman is willing as well, and makes signs and sends messages, and if she even touches me and comes close, and I hold off and win — that would be a puzzle solved beyond the Liar and the Quiescent. A man may fairly be proud of a victory like that, and not of setting the Master Argument.

How is it to be done, then? Be willing at last to have your own approval. Be willing to look beautiful to God. Set your heart on being pure, in company with your own pure self and with God.

Then, when any such impression visits you, Plato says: turn to rites of purification, go as a suppliant to the temples of the gods who avert evil. And it is even enough to go into the company of noble and just men and compare yourself with them, whether you can find one living or dead.

Go to Socrates, and see him lying beside Alcibiades and making fun of the young man's beauty. Think what a victory he found in the end that he had won over himself — what an Olympic victory; what his place was in the list running back to Hercules — so that a man might justly greet him, by the gods: hail, extraordinary man; you have beaten not these poor boxers and wrestlers, nor men like them, the gladiators.

Put these things on the other side of the scale and you will beat the impression; you will not be dragged off by it. But first of all, do not be swept away by the speed of it. Say: impression, wait for me a little. Let me see who you are and what you are about. Let me put you to the test. And then do not let the impression lead you on and paint vivid pictures of what will follow, or it will carry you off wherever it likes. Bring in some other impression instead, a beautiful and noble one, and throw this low one out.

If you get used to training in this way, you will see what shoulders you have, what sinews, what strength. As it is, you have nothing but trifling words.

This is the true athlete — the man who trains himself against impressions like these. Stay, wretch; do not be carried off. The fight is great, the work divine: it is for kingship, for freedom, for happiness, for peace of mind. Remember God. Call on him as your helper and protector, as men at sea call on the Dioscuri in a storm.

And what greater storm is there than the one that comes from violent impressions that drive reason out? What is the storm itself but an impression? Take away the fear of death and bring on as much thunder and lightning as you please, and you will find what calm and clear weather there is in the governing part.

But if you are once beaten and say you will win next time, and then say the same thing again, be sure that you will end in such a wretched and feeble condition that you will not even notice afterwards that you are doing wrong; you will start making excuses for it. And then you will prove the truth of Hesiod's line:

	The man who puts things off wrestles with troubles all his life.

Chapter 19: Against Those Who Take Up Philosophy Only in Words

The argument called the Master Argument seems to have been put forward from principles of this sort. There is a general contradiction between these three propositions, any two of them being in contradiction with the third. The propositions are: that everything past must necessarily be true; that an impossibility does not follow from a possibility; and that something is possible which neither is true nor ever will be.

Diodorus, noticing this contradiction, used the force of the first two to prove that nothing is possible which is not true and never will be. Another man will hold the other two — that something is possible which is neither true nor ever will be, and that an impossibility does not follow from a possibility — but will not allow that everything past is necessarily true; which is what the followers of Cleanthes seem to think, and Antipater defended them at length. Others again maintain the other pair — that something is possible which is neither true nor will be, and that everything past is necessarily true — and then have to maintain that an impossibility can follow from a possibility. But holding all three is impossible, because of the contradiction between them.

So if anybody asks me which of these propositions I maintain, I shall answer that I do not know; all I have is the story that Diodorus held one opinion, and the followers of Panthoides, I think, and Cleanthes another, and Chrysippus' people a third.

"And what is your own opinion?"

I was not made for this purpose — to examine my own impressions, compare what other men say, and form an opinion of my own on the question. So I am no different from a grammarian. Who was Hector's father? Priam. Who were his brothers? Paris and Deiphobus. Who was their mother? Hecuba. That is the story I have heard. Heard from whom? From Homer. And Hellanicus writes about the same things too, I think, and perhaps others like him.

And what more have I to say about the Master Argument? Nothing. But if I am a vain man, especially at a dinner party, I can astonish the guests by listing everybody who has written on the subject. Chrysippus has written marvellously in his first book On Possibilities; and Cleanthes has written a special treatise on it, and so has Archedemus. Antipater has written on it not only in his work On Possibilities but separately in his work on the Master Argument.

"Have you not read the book?"

I have not.

"Read it."

And what good will it do a man? He will be more trifling and more of a nuisance than he already is. What else have you gained by reading it? What opinion have you formed on the question? None. But you will tell us about Helen and Priam and the island of Calypso, which never was and never will be.

In that case it does not much matter if you keep the story and have formed no opinion of your own. But in questions of how to live, this happens to us far more than in the matters we are talking about.

"Talk to me about good and evil." Listen:

	The wind from Ilium carried me
	to the shores of the Cicones.
	— Odyssey, ix 39.

Of things, some are good, some bad, and others indifferent. The good are the virtues and what shares in the virtues; the bad are the vices and what shares in them; the indifferent are the things lying between the virtues and the vices — wealth, health, life, death, pleasure, pain.

"And how do you know this?"

Hellanicus says so in his Egyptian History. And what difference does it make whether I say that, or say that Diogenes has it in his Ethics, or Chrysippus, or Cleanthes? Have you examined any of it and formed your own opinion?

Show me how you behave in a storm at sea. Do you remember this distinction when the sail is rattling and you are screaming, and some man who knows nothing about timing stands beside you and says: tell me, in the gods' name, what you were saying just now — is being shipwrecked a vice? Does it have any share in vice? Will you not pick up a stick and lay it across his head? "What have we to do with you, man? We are dying and you come to make fun of us."

And if Caesar sends for you to answer a charge, do you remember the distinction then? If, as you are going in pale and trembling, somebody comes up and says: why are you trembling, man? What is this business you are engaged in? Does Caesar, sitting in there, hand out virtue and vice to the men who go in to him? — you answer, "Why do you mock me too, and add to my troubles?"

"Still, tell me, philosopher, why are you trembling? Is it not death that you are risking, or prison, or bodily pain, or exile, or disgrace? What else is there? Is any of it a vice, or does any of it share in vice? And what did you use to say about these things?"

"What have you to do with me, man? My own evils are enough for me."

And you are right. Your own evils are enough for you: your meanness, your cowardice, the boasting you did sitting in the school. Why did you decorate yourself with what belonged to other men? Why did you call yourself a Stoic?

Watch yourselves like this in your actions and you will find out which school you really belong to. You will find that most of you are Epicureans, and a few Peripatetics — and feeble ones at that. Where will you show that you really regard virtue as equal to everything else, or superior to it?

Show me a Stoic, if you can. Where? How? You can show me any number of men who recite the little arguments of the Stoics. But do the same men recite the Epicurean opinions any worse? And the Peripatetic ones — do they not handle those just as exactly?

So who is a Stoic? We call a statue Phidian when it is made according to the art of Phidias; so show me a man made according to the doctrines he recites. Show me a man who is sick and happy, in danger and happy, dying and happy, exiled and happy, in disgrace and happy. Show me. By the gods, I want to see a Stoic.

You cannot show me one finished; then show me at least one being formed, one who has turned in that direction. Do me this favour. Do not grudge an old man the sight of something he has never yet seen. Do you suppose you have to show me the Zeus of Phidias, or the Athena, a work of gold and ivory? Let one of you show me a human soul that is ready to think as God thinks; that does not blame God or man; that is not disappointed of anything; that does not count itself damaged by anything; that is not angry, not envious, not jealous — and why should I not say it straight out? — that wants to change from a man into a god, and that in this poor dead body is thinking of its fellowship with Zeus.

Show me the man. But you cannot. Then why do you deceive yourselves and cheat other people? Why do you put on a costume that is not yours, and walk about as thieves and pilferers of names and things that do not belong to you?

And now I am your teacher, and you are being taught in my school. And I have this purpose: to make you free of restraint, compulsion and blocking; to make you free, prosperous, happy, looking to God in everything great and small. And you are here to learn these things and practise them.

Why do you not finish the work, then, if you also have the purpose you ought to have, and if I, besides the purpose, have the qualifications I ought to have? What is missing?

When I see a craftsman with his material lying beside him, I expect the work. Well, here is the craftsman and here is the material — what are we short of? Is the thing not teachable? It is. Is it not in our power, then? It is the only thing of all things that is in our power. Wealth is not in our power, nor health, nor reputation, nor in a word anything else except the right use of impressions. That alone is by nature free of restraint; that alone is free of obstruction.

So why do you not finish the work? Tell me the reason. It is either my fault that you do not finish it, or your own, or it lies in the nature of the thing. The thing itself is possible, and the only thing in our power. So it remains that the fault is in me, or in you — or, nearer the truth, in both.

Well then: are you willing that we should at last start bringing such a purpose into this school, and take no notice of the past? Only let us make a beginning. Trust me, and you will see.

Chapter 20: Against the Epicureans and the Academics

Propositions that are true and evident have to be used even by the men who contradict them; and perhaps the greatest proof that a thing is evident is that even the man who denies it turns out to need it at the same moment.

For instance, if a man denies that anything is universally true, plainly he has to make the contradictory statement: that nothing is universally true. Wretch, will you not admit even this? For what is it but affirming that whatever is universally affirmed is false?

Again, suppose a man comes forward and says: know that nothing can be known, and that nothing admits of sure evidence. Or another says: believe me — you will be the better for it — that a man ought not to believe anything. Or another: learn from me, man, that it is not possible to learn anything; I am telling you so, and I will teach you if you like.

How do these differ from — whom shall I name? — the men who call themselves Academics? "Gentlemen, agree with us that nobody agrees with anybody. Believe us that nobody believes anybody."

In the same way Epicurus, when he sets out to destroy the natural fellowship of mankind, uses the very thing he is destroying. What does he say? "Do not be deceived, men, do not be led astray, do not be mistaken. There is no natural fellowship among rational creatures. Believe me. The men who say otherwise are deceiving you and leading you on with false reasoning."

What is it to you? Let us be deceived. Will you be any worse off if all the rest of us are convinced that there is a natural fellowship between us and that it ought by all means to be preserved? It will be much better and safer for you.

Man, why do you trouble yourself about us? Why do you lie awake for us? Why do you light your lamp? Why do you get up early? Why do you write so many books — so that not one of us shall be deceived about the gods and believe they take care of men, or suppose the nature of the good to be anything other than pleasure?

If that is how things are, then lie down and sleep and live the life of a worm, which is what you judged yourself fit for. Eat and drink and enjoy women, and relieve yourself, and snore. What is it to you how the rest of us think about these matters, rightly or wrongly? What have we to do with you?

You take care of sheep because they give us wool and milk, and their flesh in the end. Would it not be a fine thing if men could be lulled and enchanted by the Stoics and sleep and present themselves to you and your kind to be sheared and milked? This is what you ought to be saying to your fellow Epicureans — and ought you not to keep it from everybody else, and above all persuade them that we are naturally fitted for fellowship and that self-control is a good thing, so that everything may be kept safe for you?

Or ought we to keep up this fellowship with some men and not with others? With whom, then? With those who keep it up on their side, or with those who violate it? And who violates it more than you, who lay down doctrines like these?

What was it, then, that woke Epicurus from his sleep and forced him to write what he wrote? What but the strongest thing in men — nature, which drags a man to her own will though he is unwilling and complaining? Since you think, she says, that there is no community among mankind, write this opinion down and leave it for others, and break your sleep for it, and by your own practice condemn your own opinions.

Shall we say that Orestes was driven by the Furies and roused out of his deep sleep — and that fiercer Furies and Pains did not rouse Epicurus and refuse to let him rest, but forced him to publish his own troubles, as madness and wine forced the priests of Cybele to publish theirs?

So strong and unconquerable is a man's nature. How can a vine be moved to behave like an olive tree, or an olive tree like a vine? It is impossible; it cannot even be imagined. So it is not possible for a man to lose the movements of a man completely — and even men who have been castrated cannot rid themselves of a man's desires. In the same way Epicurus cut away all the offices of a man, of a head of a household, of a citizen and of a friend; but he did not cut away human desires, because he could not — any more than the lazy Academics can throw away their own senses or blind them, though they have tried with all their might.

What a shameful thing it is, when a man has been given by nature measures and standards for knowing the truth, that he should not try to add to them and improve them, but do the exact opposite and try to take away and destroy whatever lets us discern the truth.

What do you say, philosopher? Piety and holiness — what do you take them to be? "If you like, I will prove that they are good things." Very well, prove it, so that our citizens may turn round and honour the divine and stop neglecting the things of highest value. "And do you have the proofs?" I have, and I am thankful for them.

"Well then, since you are so pleased with them, hear the opposite: that there are no gods, and that if there are, they take no care of men, and there is no fellowship between us and them; and that this piety and holiness people talk of is the lying of braggarts and sophists — or, certainly, of lawgivers, for the purpose of frightening wrongdoers and holding them back."

Well done, philosopher. You have done something for our citizens. You have brought all the young men round to contempt for things divine.

"What — does that not satisfy you? Learn now that justice is nothing, that self-respect is folly, that a father is nothing, a son nothing."

Well done, philosopher. Keep at it. Persuade the young men, so that we may have more people holding your opinions and saying what you say. It was from principles like these that our well-ordered states grew. Sparta was founded on these; Lycurgus fixed these opinions in the Spartans by his laws and his education — that slavery is no more shameful than honourable, and that the condition of free men is no more honourable than shameful; and it was from these opinions that the men who died at Thermopylae died. And what other opinions were they that made the Athenians leave their city?

And then the men who talk like this marry and have children, and busy themselves in public affairs, and get themselves made priests and interpreters. Of what? Of gods who do not exist. And they consult the priestess at Delphi so as to hear lies, and report the oracles to other people. Monstrous impudence and imposture.

Man, what are you doing? Refuting yourself every day? And will you not give up these chilly efforts? When you eat, where do you carry your hand — to your mouth or to your eye? When you wash, what do you get into? Do you ever call a pot a dish, or a ladle a spit?

If I were the slave of one of these men, even if I had to be flogged for it daily, I would torment him. If he said, "Boy, throw some olive oil in the bath," I would take fish pickle and pour it over his head. "What is this?" he would say. An impression came to me, I swear by your guardian spirit, that could not be told from oil and was exactly like it. "Bring me the barley drink," he says. I would fill a dish with sharp sauce and carry it to him. "Did I not ask for the barley drink?" Yes, master — this is the barley drink. "Take it and smell it; take it and taste it." And how do you know our senses do not deceive us?

If I had three or four fellow slaves of the same mind, I would drive him to hang himself in a rage, or else to change his opinion. As it is, these men make fun of us: they use everything nature gives, and destroy it in words.

Grateful men indeed, and modest, who if they do nothing else eat bread every day and still have the effrontery to say we do not know whether there is any Demeter, or her daughter Persephone, or any Pluto — to say nothing of their enjoying the night and the day, the seasons of the year, the stars, the sea, the land, and the cooperation of mankind, and not being moved in the least by any of it to give it any attention. They only want to belch out their little problem, and having exercised their stomachs, to go off to the baths.

But what they are going to say, and about what, and to whom, and what their hearers will learn from the talk — that they do not care about in the smallest degree. Nor whether some well-born young man, after hearing such talk, may take harm from it; nor whether, having taken harm, he may lose every seed of his own good nature. Nor whether we are helping an adulterer to be shameless in what he does; nor whether some public embezzler may find a clever excuse in these doctrines; nor whether another man who neglects his parents may be confirmed in his boldness by this teaching.

So what, in your view, is good or bad? This, or that?

Why should a man say anything more in answer to people like these, or give them any reason, or listen to any reason from them, or try to convince them? By Zeus, one might much sooner expect to change the minds of men who have lost all shame about sex than of men who have gone this deaf and this blind to their own troubles.

Chapter 21: On Inconsistency

There are some things men admit readily and others they do not. Nobody will admit that he is a fool or that he lacks understanding; quite the contrary — you will hear every man saying, "I wish my luck were equal to my understanding."

But men readily admit that they are timid. "I am rather timid, I confess; but in other respects you will not find me a fool." A man will not readily admit that he lacks self-control, and that he is unjust he will not admit at all. He will certainly not admit that he is envious or a busybody. Most men will admit that they are soft-hearted.

What is the reason? The chief thing is inconsistency and confusion about good and evil. But different men have different reasons, and in general they will not admit at all what they take to be shameful. They suppose timidity is a mark of a good disposition, and so is pity; but stupidity they take to be the absolute mark of a slave. And they will not admit at all the faults that are offences against society.

With most errors, the reason they are brought to admit them is that they imagine there is something involuntary in them, as with timidity and pity. And if a man does admit to a lack of self-control, he pleads love as his excuse, as though it were involuntary. But men do not imagine injustice to be involuntary at all. In jealousy too, they suppose, there is something involuntary, and so they admit to jealousy as well.

Living among men as confused as this, then — so ignorant of what they are saying, and of what evils they have and have not, and where they came from and how they are to be rid of them — I think it worth a man's trouble to keep watch on himself constantly and ask: am I one of them too? What do I imagine about myself? How am I conducting myself? Am I conducting myself as a sensible man? As a self-controlled man? Do I ever say this — that I have been taught to be ready for whatever may happen?

Have I the consciousness a man who knows nothing ought to have, that I know nothing? Do I go to my teacher as men go to an oracle, prepared to obey? Or do I go to school like a snivelling boy, to learn some history and understand books I did not understand before, and, if it turns out that way, to explain them to other people too?

Man, you have had a fight at home with a wretched slave, you have turned the household upside down, you have frightened the neighbours — and then you come to me as though you were a wise man, and take your seat and pass judgment on how I explained some word, and on how I babbled whatever came into my head.

You come full of envy and humbled, because you bring nothing from home; and you sit through the discussion thinking about nothing but how your father and your brother feel about you. "What are they saying about me back there? They think I am doing well now; they are saying he will come back knowing everything. I wish I could learn it all before I go back — but that takes a lot of work, and nobody sends me anything, and the baths at Nicopolis are filthy. Everything is bad at home and bad here."

And then they say nobody gets any profit from the school. Why — who comes to the school? Who comes for the purpose of being improved? Who comes to hand over his judgments to be cleaned? Who comes to learn what he lacks?

Why be surprised, then, if you carry away from the school exactly what you brought into it? You do not come to lay your principles aside, or correct them, or take on others in their place. Nothing of the kind. What you look to instead is whether you already have what you came for.

You want to prattle about theorems? Well then — do you not become a greater trifler? Do your little theorems not give you some opportunity for showing off? You solve trick syllogisms. Do you not examine the premises of the syllogism called the Liar? Do you not examine hypothetical syllogisms? Then why are you still annoyed, if you get the things you came to the school for?

"Yes — but if my child dies, or my brother, or if I have to die or be racked, what good will these things do me?"

Well, is that what you came for? Is that what you sit beside me for? Did you ever light your lamp or stay awake for that? Or, when you went out to the walking-place, did you ever put up an impression you had been struck by instead of a syllogism, and go through it with your friends? Where? When?

And then you say theorems are useless. Useless to whom? To men who make bad use of them. Eye ointment is not useless to men who use it as they should and when they should. Hot compresses are not useless. Dumbbells are not useless — but they are useless to some men and useful to others.

If you ask me now whether syllogisms are useful, I shall say they are, and prove it if you like.

"And how are they going to be useful to me?"

Man, did you ask whether they are useful to you, or whether they are useful in general? Let a man with dysentery ask me whether vinegar is useful; I shall say it is. "Will it be useful to me, then?" I shall say: no. First get the discharge stopped and the ulcers closed.

And you, gentlemen — first cure your ulcers and stop your discharge. Get your mind quiet, bring it into the school free of distraction, and then you will know what power reason has.

Chapter 22: On Friendship

Whatever a man applies himself to seriously, he naturally loves. Do men apply themselves seriously to things that are bad? Not at all. To things that do not concern them in any way? Not to those either. So it remains that they apply themselves seriously only to things that are good; and if they apply themselves seriously to them, they love them too.

So whoever understands what good is can also know how to love. But the man who cannot tell good from bad, and cannot tell what is neither good nor bad from either — how can he have the power of loving? To love, then, is in the power of the wise alone.

"How can that be?" a man may say. "I am a fool, and I love my child all the same."

I am surprised that you have begun by admitting you are a fool. What are you lacking? Can you not use your senses? Do you not tell one impression from another? Do you not use food that suits your body, and clothing, and a house? Why do you admit you are a fool, then?

In truth it is because you are often thrown by impressions and perplexed by them, and their power of persuasion often beats you; and sometimes you think these same things good, and then bad, and finally neither; and in short you grieve, fear, envy, are disturbed, are changed about. That is why you confess you are a fool.

And are you not changeable in loving too? Wealth and pleasure — in a word, things themselves — do you not think them good sometimes and bad at others? And do you not think the same men good at one moment and bad at another? Do you not feel friendly toward them at one time and hostile at another? Do you not praise them at one time and blame them at another?

"Yes, I have these feelings too."

Well then: do you think a man who has been deceived about somebody is that person's friend? "Certainly not." And a man who chooses somebody for a friend and is of a changeable disposition — has he good will toward him? "He has not." And a man who abuses somebody now and admires him later? "He has no good will either."

Well then, did you never see little dogs fondling each other and playing, so that you would say there is nothing more affectionate? But to learn what their friendship is worth, throw a bit of meat between them, and you will find out.

Throw a small estate between you and your son, and you will discover how soon he will want to bury you, and how soon you want your son dead. Then you will change your tune and say: what a son I have brought up. He has been wanting to bury me for a long time. Throw a pretty girl between you, and let the old man love her and the young one love her too. Let a little reputation come between, or dangers, and it will be just the same. You will say what Admetus' father said:

	Life is sweet to you: and why not to your father?

Do you suppose Admetus did not love his own child when the boy was small? That he was not in agony when the child had a fever? That he did not often say, I wish I had the fever instead of the child? And then when the test came and was close at hand, see what words they use.

Were Eteocles and Polynices not sons of the same mother and the same father? Had they not been brought up together, lived together, drunk together, slept together, kissed each other often? So that if any man had seen them, I think, he would have laughed at the philosophers for the paradoxes they utter about friendship. But when a quarrel arose between them over the throne, like dogs over a bit of meat, see what they say:

	Polynices: Where will you take your station before the towers?
	Eteocles: Why do you ask me that?
	Polynices: I will place myself opposite you and try to kill you.
	Eteocles: I want to do the same.

Those are the wishes they utter.

For do not be deceived: in general every creature is attached to nothing so much as to its own interest. Whatever seems to it to stand in the way of that interest — brother, father, child, the one it loves or the one who loves it — it hates, and shoves aside, and curses. Its nature is to love nothing so much as its own interest; that is father and brother and kinsman and country and God to it. And so when the gods look to us like an obstacle to it, we abuse them and throw down their statues and burn their temples, as Alexander ordered the temples of Asclepius burned when his dear friend died.

So if a man puts his interest and his holiness and his goodness and his country and his parents and his friends all in the same place, then all of them are safe. But if he puts his interest in one place and his friends and his country and his family and justice itself in another, all these give way, borne down by the weight of interest.

For wherever the I and the Mine are put, that is the way the creature must lean. If they are in the flesh, the governing power is there; if in the will, it is there; if in externals, it is there. So if I am where my will is, then and only then shall I be the friend I ought to be, and the son, and the father — because it will then be in my interest to keep the character of a man who is trustworthy, self-respecting, patient, restrained, ready to help, observing my relations toward everyone. But if I put myself in one place and honesty in another, then the doctrine of Epicurus grows strong, which says either that there is no such thing as honesty, or that it is whatever opinion holds it to be.

It was through this ignorance that the Athenians and the Spartans quarrelled, and the Thebans with both; and the great king with Greece, and the Macedonians with both; and the Romans with the Getae. And earlier still the Trojan war happened for the same reasons. Paris was the guest of Menelaus, and if anybody had seen how friendly they were, he would not have believed a man who told him they were not friends. But a bit of meat was thrown between them — a handsome woman — and war came out of it.

So now, when you see brothers who seem to be friends and of one mind, do not conclude anything about their friendship from that, not even if they swear to it and say it is impossible for them ever to be parted. The governing part of a bad man cannot be trusted; it is insecure, it has no fixed rule to steer by, and it is overpowered now by one impression and now by another.

Do not examine what other men examine — whether they were born of the same parents and brought up together and under the same tutor. Examine this only: where they put their interest, in externals or in the will. If in externals, do not call them friends, any more than you would call them trustworthy or steady or brave or free. Do not even call them men, if you have any judgment.

For it is no principle of human nature that makes men bite one another and abuse one another and occupy waste places or public squares as if they were mountains, and in the law courts put on the performance of robbers; nor that makes them intemperate, adulterers, corrupters; nor that makes them do anything else men do against each other — nothing but this one opinion, that they place themselves and their interests among the things outside the will.

But if you hear that these men truly think the good is only where the will is and where impressions are rightly used, then do not trouble yourself about whether they are father and son, or brothers, or have been companions for a long time. Once you have established that one thing, declare confidently that they are friends, as you declare that they are trustworthy and just. For where else is friendship but where there is good faith and self-respect, and a sharing of honest things and of nothing else?

"But so-and-so treated me with regard all these years — did he not love me?" How do you know, slave, whether he regarded you as he regards the sponge he wipes his shoes with, or the way he takes care of his beast? How do you know whether, when you have stopped being useful as a pot, he will not throw you out like a broken dish?

"But this woman is my wife, and we have lived together so long." And how long did Eriphyle live with Amphiaraus, and bear him children, and many of them? But a necklace came between them. And what is a necklace? It is the opinion about such things. That was the brutish principle; that was what broke the friendship of husband and wife apart; that was what would not let the wife be a wife or the mother be a mother.

So let every man among you who has seriously resolved either to be a friend himself or to have another man as a friend cut these opinions out, hate them, and drive them out of his soul. And then, first of all, he will not reproach himself, or be at odds with himself, or change his mind, or torture himself. And in the second place, to another man like himself he will be altogether and completely a friend. And with a man unlike himself he will be patient, kind, gentle, ready to pardon him for his ignorance and for being mistaken in the greatest matters; but he will be harsh to nobody, being thoroughly convinced of Plato's doctrine that every mind is deprived of truth unwillingly.

If you cannot do that, you can still do everything else that friends do — drink together, lodge together, sail together. You may even be born of the same parents; so are snakes. But neither they nor you will be friends, so long as you keep these brutish and accursed opinions.

Chapter 23: On the Power of Speaking

Every man reads a book with more pleasure, or at least more easily, if it is written in a finer hand. So every man will also listen more readily to what is spoken, if it is put in fitting and becoming words. We must not say, then, that there is no such thing as a capacity for expression. To say so is the mark of an irreverent man, and of a cowardly one as well.

Of an irreverent man, because he undervalues the gifts that come from God — as if he were doing away with the usefulness of sight or hearing. Has God given you eyes for nothing? Has he put into them for nothing a spirit so strong and so cunningly contrived that it reaches a long way off and shapes the forms of what is seen? What messenger is so swift and so watchful? And has he made the air in between so efficient and so elastic for nothing, that vision passes through it when it is set moving? And has he made light for nothing, without which nothing else would be of any use?

Man, be neither ungrateful for these gifts nor forgetful of the things that are better than they are. Give thanks to God for sight and hearing, and indeed for life itself and the things that support it — for dried fruits, for wine and oil. But remember that he has given you something better than all of them: the power of using them, testing them, and estimating what each is worth.

For what is it that gives you information about each of these powers, and what each is worth? Is it the capacity itself? Did you ever hear the capacity of sight say anything about itself? Or hearing? Or wheat, or barley, or a horse, or a dog? No: they are appointed as servants and slaves to serve the capacity that has the power of using impressions.

And if you enquire what each thing is worth, whom do you enquire of? Who answers you? How can any other capacity be more powerful than this one, which uses the others as its servants and tests each of them itself and pronounces on them? Which of them knows what it is itself, and what it is worth? Which of them knows when it ought to employ itself and when not?

What is the capacity that opens and shuts the eyes, and turns them away from things they ought not to be applied to, and applies them to others? Is it the capacity of sight? No — it is the will. What capacity closes and opens the ears? What makes them curious and enquiring, or on the other hand unmoved by what is said? Is it hearing? It is nothing but the will.

Here is a capacity standing among all the others, which are blind and dumb and unable to see anything except the very acts they are appointed to in order to serve it — and this one alone sees sharply and sees what each of the rest is worth. Will this capacity tell us that something else is best, and not itself?

And what else does the eye do, when it is opened, but see? But whether we ought to look at a certain man's wife, and in what manner — who tells us that? The will. And whether we ought to believe what is said or not; and if we do believe it, whether we ought to be moved by it — who tells us? Is it not the will?

And this capacity of speaking and of ornamenting words, if there really is any such special capacity — what else does it do, when there happens to be talk about something, than ornament the words and arrange them, as hairdressers arrange hair? But whether it is better to speak or keep silent, and better to speak this way or that, and whether this is becoming or not, and the right moment for each and the use of it — what tells us that but the will? Would you have it come forward and condemn itself?

What then? the will says: if that is how things stand, can the servant be superior to the one it serves? Can the horse be superior to the rider, or the dog to the huntsman, or the instrument to the musician, or the servants to the king?

What is it that makes use of the rest? The will. What takes care of everything? The will. What destroys the whole man — sometimes by starvation, sometimes by hanging, sometimes by a cliff? The will. Is anything in men stronger than this, then? And how can things that are subject to restraint be stronger than what is not?

What things are naturally formed to block sight? Both the will and things outside the will. It is the same with hearing, and the same with speaking. But what has a natural power of blocking the will? Nothing outside the will — only the will itself, when it is twisted. And that is why the will alone is vice, or alone is virtue.

Then, being so great a capacity and set over all the rest, let it come forward and tell us that the most excellent of all things is the flesh. Not even if the flesh itself declared it was the most excellent would anybody put up with hearing it say so.

But what is it, Epicurus, that pronounces this? What wrote about the End of our being, and about the Nature of Things, and about the Standard of truth? What made you grow a beard? What wrote, as it was dying, that it was spending its last day, and a happy one? Was that the flesh, or the will? Then do you admit that you possess something superior to it — and are you not mad? Are you really so blind and so deaf?

What of it, then? Does anybody despise the other capacities? I should hope not. Does anybody say there is no use or excellence in the capacity for speaking? I should hope not. That would be foolish, irreverent, ungrateful toward God.

A man gives each thing its due value. There is some use in a donkey, but not as much as in an ox. There is use in a dog, but not as much as in a slave. There is use in a slave, but not as much as in citizens; and use in citizens, but not as much as in magistrates. And because some things are superior we must not undervalue the use the others have.

There is a certain value in the power of speaking, but not as great as in the power of the will. So when I talk like this, let nobody think I am asking you to neglect the power of speaking — any more than I ask you to neglect your eyes, or your ears, or your hands or feet, or your clothes and shoes.

But if you ask me what is the most excellent thing of all, what must I say? I cannot say the power of speaking. I must say the power of the will, when it is straight. For it is this that uses the other, and all the rest, great and small. When the will is set straight, a man who was not good becomes good; and when it fails, a man becomes bad. It is through this that we are unlucky and that we are lucky, that we blame one another and are pleased with one another. In a word, it is this which, neglected, makes unhappiness, and which, carefully looked after, makes happiness.

But to do away with the capacity of speaking and say that there is no such thing in reality is the act not only of a man ungrateful to those who gave it, but of a cowardly man. Such a person seems to me to be afraid that if there is any such capacity we shall not be able to despise it.

It is the same with men who say there is no difference between beauty and ugliness. Then a man would be affected in the same way by the sight of Thersites and the sight of Achilles; in the same way by Helen and by any other woman. These are stupid, boorish notions, the notions of men who do not know the nature of each thing but are afraid that if a man sees the difference he will be seized and carried off, beaten, on the spot.

But this is the great thing: to leave each thing the capacity it has, and, leaving it that, to see what the capacity is worth; and to learn what is the most excellent thing of all, and to pursue that always, and be diligent about it, counting everything else of secondary value in comparison — while still, as far as we can, not neglecting those other things. We must take care of the eyes too, not as being the most excellent thing, but for the sake of the most excellent thing, since that will not be in its true natural condition unless it uses the other capacities rightly and prefers some things to others.

And what usually happens? Men behave like a traveller on his way home who comes to a good inn and, finding it pleasant, stays there. Man, you have forgotten your purpose. You were not travelling to this inn; you were passing through it. "But it is a pleasant inn." And how many other inns are pleasant? How many meadows are pleasant? Yet only to pass through. Your purpose is to get back to your own country, to relieve your family's anxiety, to do a citizen's duties, to marry, to have children, to hold the usual offices. You did not come to pick out the pleasanter places, but to live in the place where you were born and were made a citizen.

Something of the kind happens in the matter we are considering. Since it is by speech and the sort of instruction you get here that you must advance toward perfection, and clean your will, and correct the capacity that uses impressions; and since the teaching of theorems must be done in a certain style, with a certain variety and sharpness — some men are captivated by these very things and stay among them. One is captivated by the expression, another by syllogisms, another by sophisms, another by some other inn of the kind; and there they stay and waste away, as though among the Sirens.

Man, your business was to make yourself capable of using in line with nature the impressions put before you; not to be frustrated in your desires; not to fall into what you would avoid in your avoidances; never to be out of luck, as one might say, and never to have bad luck; to be free, unblocked, uncompelled, fitting yourself to the administration of Zeus and obeying it, well satisfied with it, blaming nobody, charging nobody with fault, able to say these lines with your whole soul:

	Lead me, Zeus, and you too, Destiny,

And with that purpose in front of you, if some little turn of phrase pleases you, or some theorems please you, do you settle down among them and choose to live there, forgetting what is at home? And do you say, "These things are fine"? Who says they are not fine — but only as a road home, as inns are.

What stops you being an unlucky man even if you speak like Demosthenes? What stops you, if you can resolve syllogisms like Chrysippus, from being wretched, from grieving, from envying — in a word, from being disturbed and unhappy? Nothing.

So you see that these were inns, worth nothing, and that the purpose set before you was something else. When I talk like this to some people they think I am rejecting care about speaking, or care about theorems. I am not rejecting the care. I am rejecting the settling down among these things forever and putting our hopes in them. If a man does harm to his hearers by this teaching, count me too among the men who do that harm; for when I see one thing that is most excellent and supreme, I am not able to say that something else is, in order to please you.

Chapter 24: To a Man Epictetus Did Not Think Much Of

A certain person said to him: "I have often wanted to hear you, and I came to you, and you never gave me any answer. Now, if it is possible, I beg you to say something to me."

Epictetus said: Do you think that as there is an art in everything else, so there is an art in speaking, and that a man who has the art will speak skilfully and a man who has not will speak unskilfully?

"I do think so."

Then a man who benefits himself by speaking and is able to benefit others will be speaking skilfully; and a man who is damaged by speaking and damages others will be unskilled in this art? And you can find that some men are damaged by speaking and others benefited.

And are all who listen benefited by what they hear? Or will you find that among them too some are benefited and some damaged?

"There are both among these as well," he said.

Then in this case too, those who listen skilfully are benefited and those who listen unskilfully are damaged. He agreed.

Is there an art in listening, then, as there is in speaking?

"It seems so."

If you like, look at it this way as well. The practice of music — whom does it belong to? A musician. And the proper making of a statue — whom does that belong to? A sculptor. And looking at a statue skilfully: does that seem to you to need no art at all?

"That needs art too."

Then if speaking properly is the business of a man with the skill, do you see that listening with benefit is the business of a man with the skill as well?

Now as to speaking and listening perfectly and usefully, let us say nothing more for the present, if you please; we are both a long way from anything of that kind. But I think every man will grant this much: that a man who is going to listen to philosophers needs some amount of practice in listening. Is it not so?

Tell me, then, what I should talk to you about. What subject are you able to listen to?

"Good and evil."

Good and evil in what? In a horse? "No." In an ox? "No." In what, then? In a man? "Yes."

Do we know, then, what a man is? What notion we have of him? Have our ears had any practice at all in this matter? Do you understand what nature is? Can you follow me at all when I say I shall use demonstration on you? How could you? Do you understand this very thing — what a demonstration is, or how anything is demonstrated, or by what means? Or what things resemble demonstration and are not demonstration? Do you know what is true and what is false? What follows from a thing, and what is inconsistent with it or out of keeping with it?

But am I to stir you up to philosophy — and how? Shall I show you the inconsistency in most men's opinions, which is why they differ about good and evil and about what is profitable and unprofitable, when you do not know this very thing, what inconsistency is?

Show me what I shall accomplish by talking to you. Rouse my inclination to do it. When suitable grass is put in front of a sheep, it stirs the sheep's inclination to eat; but put a stone or a piece of bread in front of it and it will not be stirred to eat. In the same way there are natural inclinations in us to speak, when the listener turns out to be somebody, when he stirs us himself. But when he sits beside us like a stone or like grass, how can he rouse a man's desire to speak?

Does the vine say to the farmer, "Take care of me"? No: by showing in itself that it will be profitable to the farmer if he does take care of it, it invites him to take care. When children are attractive and lively, whom do they not invite to play with them and crawl about with them and lisp with them? But who is eager to play with a donkey, or to bray with it? Because it is small, it is still only a little donkey.

"Why do you say nothing to me, then?"

I can only say this to you: that a man who does not know who he is, or what he exists for, or what this world is, or whom he shares it with; who does not know what things are good and bad, beautiful and ugly; who understands neither argument nor demonstration, and does not know what is true and what is false and cannot tell them apart — such a man will neither desire nor turn away nor move toward anything according to nature, nor intend, nor assent, nor dissent, nor suspend judgment. To put it in a few words, he will go about deaf and blind, thinking he is somebody when he is nobody.

Is this something new? Has it not been the case ever since the human race existed that all errors and misfortunes have come out of this ignorance?

Why did Agamemnon and Achilles quarrel with each other? Was it not through not knowing what is profitable and what is not? Does the one not say it is profitable to give Chryseis back to her father, and the other say it is not? Does the one not say he ought to take somebody else's prize, and the other that he ought not? Did they not, for these reasons, forget both who they were and what they had come there for?

Man, what did you come for? To get mistresses, or to fight? "To fight." With whom — the Trojans or the Greeks? "The Trojans." Then do you leave Hector alone and draw your sword on your own king?

And you, most excellent sir: do you neglect a king's duties, you who are the people's guardian and have such cares on you? Are you quarrelling over a girl with the most warlike of your allies, whom you ought to be taking care of and protecting by every means? Do you make yourself worse than a well-behaved priest who treats these fine gladiators with all respect?

Do you see what ignorance of what is profitable does?

"But I am rich too." Are you richer than Agamemnon? "But I am handsome too." Are you handsomer than Achilles? "But I have beautiful hair as well." Did Achilles not have more beautiful hair, and golden? And he did not comb it elegantly or dress it. "But I am strong too." Can you lift as great a stone as Hector or Ajax? "But I am well-born as well." Is your mother a goddess? Is your father sprung from Zeus? And what good did any of that do him, when he sat weeping over a girl? "But I am an orator." And was he not? Do you not see how he handled the most skilful of the Greeks in speaking, Odysseus and Phoenix, and stopped their mouths?

That is all I have to say to you — and I say even this unwillingly. Why? Because you have not roused me. What am I to look to in order to be roused, as men who know horses are roused by a fine one? To your body? You treat it disgracefully. To your clothes? They are luxurious. To your behaviour, to your look? They are as good as nothing.

When you want to listen to a philosopher, do not say to him, "You tell me nothing." Only show yourself worth being spoken to, fit to be spoken to — and you will see how you move the speaker.

Chapter 25: That Logic Cannot Be Skipped

When one of the men present said, "Convince me that logic is necessary," he answered:

Do you want me to prove it to you?

"Yes."

Then I shall have to use a demonstrative form of argument.

This was granted.

And how will you know whether I am cheating you with my argument?

The man had nothing to say.

Do you see, said Epictetus, that you are admitting logic is necessary yourself — since without it you cannot even know this much, whether logic is necessary or not?

Chapter 26: What Is Really Going On in a Mistake

Every error contains a contradiction. Since the man who goes wrong does not want to go wrong but to be right, plainly he is not doing what he wants.

What does the thief want to do? What is in his own interest. So if the theft is not in his interest, he is not doing what he wants.

Now every rational soul is by nature offended by contradiction; and so long as it does not see the contradiction, nothing stops it doing contradictory things. But once it does see the contradiction, it must necessarily back away from it, exactly as a man must dissent from a falsehood once he sees the thing is false — while as long as the falsehood does not appear to him, he assents to it as true.

So the man who is strong in argument, and has the power of urging others on and refuting them, is the man who can show each person the contradiction he is going wrong through, and prove clearly that he is not doing what he wants and is doing what he does not want. Show a man that, and he will withdraw from what he is doing of his own accord; and as long as you do not show it, do not be surprised if he keeps on. He does what he does because it looks to him like the right thing.

That is why Socrates, trusting to this power, used to say: I am not in the habit of calling any other witness to what I say. I am always satisfied with the man I am arguing with; I ask him for his opinion and call him as my witness, and though he is only one man, he does instead of all of them.

For Socrates knew what moves a rational soul: it is like a pair of scales, and then it must tip, whether it chooses to or not. Show the rational governing part a contradiction and it will back away from it. And if you do not show it, blame yourself rather than the man who is not convinced.

Book Three

Chapter 1: On Personal Beauty and Finery

A young man who studied rhetoric came to see Epictetus with his hair done more carefully than usual and the rest of him dressed up. Epictetus said:

Tell me whether you do not think that some dogs are beautiful and some horses, and so with all the other animals.

"I do think so," the young man replied.

And are some men beautiful too, and others ugly?

"Certainly."

Now do we call each of them beautiful in the same kind for the same reason, or is each beautiful for something of its own? You can judge the matter this way. Since we see that a dog is naturally made for one thing and a horse for another and a nightingale, say, for another still, we may declare quite properly, in general, that each of them is beautiful when it is at its best according to its own nature. But since the nature of each is different, each of them seems to me to be beautiful in a different way. Is it not so?

He agreed that it was.

Then what makes a dog beautiful makes a horse ugly, and what makes a horse beautiful makes a dog ugly — if it is true that their natures are different.

"It seems so."

I think what makes a champion in the all-in fight beautiful makes a bad wrestler and a completely ridiculous runner; and a man who is beautiful for the pentathlon is very ugly for wrestling.

"That is so," he said.

Then what makes a man beautiful? Is it the same sort of thing that makes a dog or a horse beautiful in its kind?

"It is," he said.

And what makes a dog beautiful? Having the excellence of a dog. And a horse? Having the excellence of a horse. And what makes a man beautiful? Is it not having the excellence of a man?

So if you want to be beautiful, young man, work at this — at acquiring human excellence.

And what is that? Notice whom you praise, when you praise people without partiality: do you praise the just or the unjust? The just. The moderate or the immoderate? The moderate. The self-controlled or the uncontrolled? The self-controlled.

So if you make yourself such a man, you will know you are making yourself beautiful; and so long as you neglect these things you must be ugly, however much you contrive to look beautiful.

Beyond that I do not know what to say to you. If I say what I think, I shall offend you, and you will perhaps leave the school and not come back. And if I do not say what I think, look what I shall be doing: you come to me to be improved, and I improve you not at all; you come to me as to a philosopher, and I say nothing to you as a philosopher. And how cruel it is to you, to leave you uncorrected.

If you ever afterwards come to your senses, you will blame me with good reason and say: "What did Epictetus see in me? He saw me in such a state, coming to him in such a scandalous condition, and neglected me and never said a word. Did he despair of me so completely? Was I not young? Was I not able to listen to reason? How many other young men at that age make plenty of mistakes like it? I hear that a certain Polemo went from being a thoroughly dissolute youth to a great change. Well, suppose he did not think I would be a Polemo — he might at least have put my hair right, he might have stripped off my ornaments, he might have stopped me plucking the hair off my body. But when he saw me dressed like — what shall I call it? — he kept silent."

I do not say like what; but you will say it when you come to your senses, and know what it is and what sort of men dress that way.

If you bring that charge against me hereafter, what defence shall I make? Shall I say the man will not be persuaded by me? Was Laius persuaded by Apollo? Did he not go off and get drunk and pay no attention to the oracle? And did Apollo refuse to tell him the truth for that reason? I do not know whether you will be persuaded by me or not; but Apollo knew perfectly well that Laius would not be persuaded, and spoke all the same.

And why did he speak? I answer: why is he Apollo? Why does he deliver oracles? Why has he set himself up in that place as a prophet and a source of truth, for the whole world to come to? And why are the words "Know yourself" written in front of the temple, though nobody takes any notice of them?

Did Socrates persuade all his hearers to take care of themselves? Not a thousandth part of them. But all the same, having been put in that position by the deity, as he says himself, he never left it. And what does he say even to his judges? "If you acquit me on condition that I stop doing what I do now, I will not agree, and I will not stop. I shall go up to young men and old, and, to put it plainly, to every man I meet, and ask the questions I ask now; and I shall do it most of all to you, my fellow citizens, because you are more closely related to me."

Are you so inquisitive, Socrates, and such a busybody? What business is it of yours how we behave? And what is this you are saying? "You are a member of the same community and the same family, and you neglect yourself, and show yourself a bad citizen to the state, a bad relation to your relations, a bad neighbour to your neighbours."

And who are you, then?

Here it is a great thing to say: I am the man whose duty it is to take care of men. It is not every little heifer that dares to face a lion; but if the bull comes up and faces him, say to the bull, if you like, "And who are you? What business have you here?"

Man, in every kind something is produced that excels — among oxen, among dogs, among bees, among horses. Do not say to the one that excels, "And who are you?" If you do, it will find a voice somehow and say: I am the sort of thing the purple is in a garment. Do not expect me to be like the rest, and do not blame my nature for having made me different from other men.

What of it? Am I such a man? Certainly not. And are you the sort of man who can listen to the truth? I wish you were. But since I have in a way been sentenced to wear a white beard and a cloak, and you come to me as to a philosopher, I will not treat you cruelly, nor as though I despaired of you. I will say:

Young man, whom do you want to make beautiful? First know who you are, and then adorn yourself accordingly. You are a human being; that is, a mortal creature with the power of using impressions rationally. And what does "rationally" mean? In line with nature, and completely.

So what do you have that is your own? Is it the animal part? No. The fact that you are mortal? No. The power of using impressions? No. What you have as your own is reason. Adorn that and make that beautiful — and leave your hair to the one who made it as he chose.

Come: what other names have you? Are you a man or a woman? A man. Then adorn yourself as a man, not as a woman. A woman is naturally smooth and delicate, and if she has a great deal of hair on her body she is a freak and is put on show at Rome among the freaks. And in a man it is monstrous not to have hair; if he has none by nature, he is a freak. But if he cuts his hair off and plucks it out, what are we to do with him? Where shall we exhibit him? Under what label shall we put him on show? "I will exhibit to you a man who would rather be a woman than a man." What a terrible sight. There is nobody who will not wonder at such a notice.

Indeed I think the men who pluck their hair out do it without knowing what they are doing. Man, what fault do you find with your nature? That it made you a man? Then ought nature to have made every human creature a woman? And what advantage would you have got out of adorning yourself in that case? Whom would you have adorned yourself for, if every human creature were a woman?

But you are not pleased with the business. Then set to work on the whole of it. Take away — what is it called? — the thing that causes the hair. Make yourself a woman in every respect, so that we are not left guessing. Do not make one half a man and the other half a woman.

Whom do you want to please? Women? Then please them as a man. "But they like smooth men." Will you not go and hang yourself? And if women took their pleasure in men who let themselves be used that way, would you become one? Is this your business? Is this what you were born for — that dissolute women should take pleasure in you?

Shall we make a man like you a citizen of Corinth, and perhaps a city magistrate, or chief of the young men, or a general, or superintendent of the games? And when you have taken a wife, do you mean to go on having your hair plucked out? To please whom, and for what? And when you have had children, will you bring them into the state as hair-pluckers too? A fine citizen, and senator, and public speaker. We ought certainly to pray for young men like that to be born among us and brought up.

Do not do it, I beg you by the gods, young man. But having once heard these words, go away and say to yourself: "Epictetus did not say this to me — how could he? Some kindly god said it through him. It would never have come into his head to say it, since he is not in the habit of talking that way to anybody. Come then, let us obey God, so that we may not be under his anger."

You say no. But if a crow signifies something to you by its croaking, it is not the crow that signifies but God through the crow. And if he signifies something through a human voice, will he not make the man say this to you, so that you may know the power of the divine — that he signifies to some men one way and to others another, and that about the greatest and chief matters he signifies through the noblest messenger?

What else does the poet mean:

	For we ourselves had warned him, and had sent
	Hermes the sharp-eyed watcher, Argus' slayer:
	not to kill the husband, nor to wed the wife.

Was Hermes going to come down from heaven to say this to Aegisthus? And now the gods say this to you, and send the messenger, the slayer of Argus, to warn you not to twist what is already well arranged, and not to meddle with it, but to let a man be a man and a woman a woman, a handsome man be a handsome man and an ugly man an ugly man. For you are not flesh and hair: you are will. And if your will is beautiful, then you will be beautiful.

Up to now I have not dared to tell you that you are ugly, because I think you are readier to hear anything else than that. But see what Socrates says to Alcibiades, the most beautiful and blooming of men. "Try, then, to be beautiful." And what does he tell him? Dress your hair and pluck the hairs off your legs? Nothing of the kind. Adorn your will; get rid of your bad opinions.

And what about the body? Leave it as nature made it. Somebody else has looked after that; entrust it to him.

What then — is a man to go uncleaned? Certainly not. But clean what you are and what nature made you. A man should be clean as a man, a woman as a woman, a child as a child. You say no. Then let us pluck out the lion's mane too, so that he shall not go uncleaned, and the cock's comb, since he ought to be cleaned as well. Cleaned, yes — but as a cock, and the lion as a lion, and the hunting dog as a hunting dog.

Chapter 2: What a Student Making Progress Should Train In, and What We Neglect

There are three areas in which a man who intends to be wise and good has to train himself.

The first concerns desires and avoidances: that he may not fail to get what he desires, and may not fall into what he does not want. The second concerns impulses toward a thing and away from it, and in general doing what a man ought to do — that he may act in order, with reason, and not carelessly. The third concerns freedom from deception and rashness in judgment, and in general it concerns assent.

Of these, the chief and most urgent is the one dealing with the passions; for a passion is produced in no other way than by failing to get what one desires or falling into what one wants to avoid. This is what brings in disturbances, disorders, ill luck, misfortunes, griefs, lamentations and envy; this is what makes men envious and jealous; and these are the causes that make us unable even to listen to what reason tells us.

The second area concerns a man's duties. I ought not to be free of feeling like a statue: I ought to keep up my relations, natural and acquired, as a devout man, as a son, as a father, as a citizen.

The third is the one that concerns men who are already making progress. It concerns the security of the other two — so that not even in sleep shall any impression take us by surprise unexamined, nor in drink, nor in a black mood. This, it may be said, is beyond our power.

But the philosophers of today neglect the first and the second and busy themselves with the third: shifting arguments, conclusions drawn by questioning, hypotheses, liar-puzzles. A man engaged on such matters must take care not to be deceived, as the saying goes. Who must? The wise and good man.

So this is all you are short of? Have you successfully worked out the rest? Are you proof against deception in the matter of money? If you see a beautiful girl, do you stand up to the impression? If your neighbour is left an estate in a will, are you not annoyed? Is there nothing else you are short of now except unshakeable firmness of mind?

Wretch, you are hearing these very words in fear and anxiety that somebody may look down on you, asking what people are saying about you. And if a man comes and tells you that in a certain conversation, when the question was who is the best philosopher, somebody present said that a certain person was the leading one, then your little soul, which was a finger's length, stretches out to a yard. But if somebody else present says, "You are mistaken; that man is not worth listening to; what does he know? he has the first principles and nothing more" — then you are dumbfounded, you turn pale, you cry out at once, "I will show him who I am, that I am a great philosopher."

It is shown by these very things. Why do you want to show it by others?

Do you not know how Diogenes pointed out one of the sophists? By sticking out his middle finger. And then when the man went wild with rage, "That," said Diogenes, "is the man in question. I have pointed him out to you." For a man is not shown by pointing a finger at him, as a stone or a piece of wood is; when somebody shows a man's principles, then he shows him as a man.

Let us look at your principles too. Is it not plain that you set no value at all on your own will, and look outside yourself to things not in your power? What will so-and-so say? What will people think of you? Will you be reckoned a man of learning? Have you read Chrysippus, or Antipater? And if you have read Archedemus as well, then you have everything.

Why are you still uneasy in case you fail to show us who you are? Would you let me tell you what sort of man you have shown us you are? You have exhibited yourself as a mean fellow, a complainer, quick-tempered, cowardly, finding fault with everything, blaming everybody, never quiet, vain. That is what you have exhibited.

Go away now and read Archedemus. And then if a mouse drops down and makes a noise, you are a dead man. Such a death is waiting for you as came to — what was the man's name? — Crinis; and he too was proud, because he understood Archedemus.

Wretch, will you not let go of things that are no concern of yours at all? These things suit men who can learn them without being thrown — men who can say: "I am not subject to anger, or grief, or envy. I am not blocked, I am not restrained. What is left for me? I have leisure, I am at peace. Let us see how we must deal with shifting arguments; let us see how a man who has granted a hypothesis is not to be dragged into something absurd."

Such things belong to them. It is right for men who are doing well to light a fire and have dinner, and, if they like, to sing and dance too. But you come to me and hoist the sails when the ship is going down.

Chapter 3: What a Good Person Should Work On, and What We Should Practice Most

The material of the wise and good man is his own governing part, as the body is the material of the doctor and of the trainer who rubs men with oil, and the land is the material of the farmer. And the business of the wise and good man is to use impressions in line with nature.

Now just as it is the nature of every soul to assent to the true, to dissent from the false, and to hold back over what is uncertain, so it is its nature to be moved toward desiring the good and avoiding the bad, and to feel neither way about what is neither good nor bad.

A banker is not allowed to refuse Caesar's coin, nor is a greengrocer; show the coin and, willing or not, he must hand over what it buys. It is the same with the soul. When the good appears it draws the soul to itself at once; the bad repels it. The soul will never refuse a plain impression of the good, any more than men refuse Caesar's coin. On this principle every movement depends, of man and of God alike.

This is why the good is preferred to every family tie. There is no family tie between me and my father, but there is between me and the good.

"Are you so hard-hearted?"

Yes, because that is my nature, and this is the coin God has given me. If the good is something other than the beautiful and the just, then father goes, and brother, and country, and everything.

But am I to overlook my own good so that you may have it? Am I to give it up to you? Why? "I am your father." But you are not my good. "I am your brother." But you are not my good.

And if we put the good in the right direction of the will, then the very keeping up of the relations of life is a good, and a man who gives up any external thing gets what is good.

Your father takes your property. But he does not injure you. Your brother will have the larger part of the estate. Let him have as much as he likes. Will he have a larger share of self-respect, of good faith, of brotherly affection? Who is going to evict you from that property? Not even Zeus — because he did not choose to; he put it in my power and gave it to me exactly as he holds it himself, free from blocking, compulsion and obstruction.

Now when another man's currency is a different coin, then if you present that coin you get what it buys. Suppose a thieving proconsul comes into the province: what currency does he use? Silver. Show it to him and carry off what you like. Suppose an adulterer comes: what currency does he use? Young girls. Take the coin, a man says, and sell me the little thing. Hand it over, says the seller, and buy. Another is keen on boys. Give him the coin and take what you want. Another is fond of hunting: give him a fine horse or a dog. He may groan and complain, but he will sell you what you want for it. Somebody else compels him, from inside — the man who fixed that currency.

This above all is what a man should train himself against. As soon as you go out in the morning, examine every man you see and every man you hear, and answer as if to a question. What have you seen? A handsome man or woman. Apply the rule. Is this outside the will or inside it? Outside. Then take it away.

What have you seen? A man grieving over the death of a child. Apply the rule. Death is outside the will. Take it away.

Did the proconsul meet you? Apply the rule. What kind of thing is a proconsulship — outside the will or inside? Outside. Take that away too. It does not stand examination. Throw it away; it is nothing to you.

If we practised this and drilled ourselves in it every day from morning to night, something would actually get done. As it is, we are caught half asleep by every impression, and only in the school, if ever, are we roused a little. Then we go out, and if we see a man grieving we say, "He is finished." If we see a consul we say, "He is a happy man." If we see an exile we say, "Poor wretch." If we see a poor man we say, "Miserable creature — he has nothing to eat."

These are the bad opinions we ought to root out, and this is where all our effort should go. For what is weeping and lamenting? Opinion. What is bad luck? Opinion. What is civil strife, disagreement, blame, accusation, irreverence, empty talk? All of these are opinions and nothing else — opinions about things outside the will, as though those things were good and bad.

Let a man shift these opinions over to the things inside the will, and I will guarantee that he will be steady and constant, whatever the state of things around him.

The soul is like a dish of water, and impressions are like the ray of light falling on the water. When the water is disturbed the ray seems to be disturbed too, though it is not. So when a man is seized with dizziness, it is not the arts and the virtues that are thrown into confusion but the spirit they are impressed on; and if the spirit is restored to its settled state, they are restored too.

Chapter 4: Against a Man Who Cheered Too Loudly in the Theater

The governor of Epirus had shown his support for an actor in an unseemly way and had been publicly criticised for it. Afterwards he told Epictetus that he had been criticised and that he was annoyed with the men who criticised him. Epictetus said:

What harm were they doing? They were taking sides too, just as you were.

"But does anybody show his support like that?"

When they see you — their governor, a friend of Caesar and his deputy — showing your support like that, was it not to be expected that they would show theirs the same way? If it is not right to show it that way, then do not do it yourself; and if it is right, why are you angry when they follow your example?

Whom have the many to copy but you, who are their superiors? Whose example should they look to when they go to the theatre, if not yours? "See how Caesar's deputy behaves. He has shouted, so I shall shout too. He jumps up from his seat, so I shall jump up. His slaves are sitting all over the theatre calling out; I have no slaves, but I will shout for myself as loud as I can, as loud as all of them together."

So you ought to know, when you go into the theatre, that you go in as a rule and an example to the rest of how they should watch a performance.
