Why did they criticise you, then? Because every man hates whatever gets in his way. They wanted one man crowned, you wanted another. They got in your way and you got in theirs. You turned out to be the stronger, and they did what they could — they criticised the thing that was blocking them.

What would you have, then? That you should do as you please and they should not even say what they please? What is surprising about it? Do farmers not abuse Zeus when he gets in their way? Do sailors not abuse him? Do people ever stop abusing Caesar? And what of it? Does Zeus not know? Is what is said not reported to Caesar? And what does Caesar do? He knows that if he punished everybody who abused him he would have nobody left to rule.

So when you go into the theatre you ought to say — not "Let Sophron be crowned", but this: "Come, let me keep my will in this matter in line with nature. No man is dearer to me than myself, so it would be ridiculous for me to be hurt in order that some actor may be crowned. Whom do I want to win the prize, then? The actor who does win it. And so the man I want to win will always win."

"But I want Sophron crowned." Hold as many games as you like in your own house — Nemean, Pythian, Isthmian, Olympian — and proclaim him the victor. But in public do not claim more than your share, and do not try to take for yourself what belongs to everybody. And if you will not agree to that, then put up with being abused: when you behave like the crowd, you put yourself on their level.

Chapter 5: Against Those Who Go Home Because They Are Ill

"I am ill here," said one of the pupils, "and I want to go home."

At home, I suppose, you were never ill? Do you not consider whether you are doing anything here that might be useful to the training of your will, so that it may be put right? Because if you are doing nothing toward that end, you came for nothing. Go away. Look after your business at home.

If your governing part cannot be kept in a condition that is in line with nature, your land can. You will be able to increase your money, look after your father in his old age, spend time in the marketplace, hold public office — and being a bad man, you will do badly at whatever else you have to do.

But if you understand yourself, and know that you are throwing away certain bad opinions and taking on others in their place; if you have shifted your way of life from the things outside your will to the things inside it; and if, when you ever say "Alas", you say it not on account of your father or your brother but on account of yourself — do you still plead your illness?

Do you not know that both illness and death must catch us doing something? The farmer while he is tilling, the sailor while he is at sea. What would you like to be doing when death catches you — since it must catch you doing something? If you can be doing anything better than this when it comes, do that.

For my part, I want to be caught by illness or death while I am looking after nothing except my own will: undisturbed, unblocked, uncompelled, and free. I want to be found practising these things, so that I can say to God:

Have I broken your commands in any way? Have I misused the powers you gave me? Have I misused my senses, or my preconceptions? Have I ever blamed you? Have I ever found fault with the way you run things? I have been ill, because it was your will, and so have others — but I was content to be ill. I have been poor, because it was your will — but I was content with that too. I have held no public office, because it was not your pleasure that I should; and I never wanted it. Did you ever see me look sour on that account? Have I not always come before you with a cheerful face, ready to do your commands and obey your signals? Is it your will now that I should leave this gathering of men? I go. I thank you with all my heart that you allowed me to join in this gathering of yours, and to see your works, and to follow the way you run things.

May death catch me thinking these thoughts, writing this, reading this.

"But my mother will not be there to hold my head when I am ill."

Then go to your mother — you are exactly the sort of person who needs his head held when he is ill.

"But at home I used to lie in a lovely bed."

Then go away to your bed. Indeed you are fit to lie in such a bed even when you are well. So do not miss out on what you can do there.

And what does Socrates say? As one man is pleased at improving his land and another at improving his horse, so I am pleased every day at noticing that I am getting better.

"Better at what — at using nice little phrases?" Man, do not say that. "At little points of theory?" What are you talking about? "And in fact I do not see what else philosophers spend their time on."

Does it seem nothing to you never to have found fault with anybody, God or man? To have blamed nobody? To carry the same face always, going out and coming in? That is what Socrates knew — and yet he never said he knew anything or taught anything. If any man asked him for nice little phrases or little theories, he would take him off to Protagoras or Hippias; and if a man came asking for greens, he would take him to the gardener.

So which of you has that purpose? If you really had it, you would be content in illness, and in hunger, and in death. And if any of you has ever been in love with a charming girl, he knows I am telling the truth.

Chapter 6: Miscellaneous Remarks

Somebody asked how it was that, although reason is more cultivated now than it used to be, greater progress was made in earlier times.

In what respect, he answered, is it more cultivated now, and in what respect was the progress greater then? Because in whatever respect it is more cultivated now, in that respect the progress will be found now too.

At present it is cultivated for the purpose of resolving syllogisms, and progress is being made. In earlier times it was cultivated for the purpose of keeping the governing part in a condition in line with nature, and progress was made in that.

So do not mix up different things, and do not expect to make progress in one thing while you are labouring at another. But see whether any man among us who is intent on this — on keeping himself in a state that is in line with nature, and living that way always — fails to make progress. You will not find one.

The good man is unbeatable, because he does not enter a contest where he is not the stronger. If you want his land and everything on it, take the land; take his slaves, take his public office, take his poor body. But you will not make his desire fail of what it goes after, nor his avoidance fall into what he would escape. The only contest he enters is over things within the power of his will. How can he not be unbeatable?

Somebody asked what common sense is, and Epictetus replied: There is a kind of common hearing that only tells one vocal sound from another, while the hearing that tells musical sounds apart is not common but trained. In the same way there are certain things that men who are not altogether twisted see by the common notions everybody has. A mind constituted that way is called common sense.

It is not easy to encourage weak young men — no easier than holding soft cheese with a hook. But men with a good natural disposition cling to reason all the harder when you try to turn them away from it. This is why Rufus generally tried to discourage his pupils: he used it as a test of who had a good natural disposition and who had not. He used to say, "As a stone thrown upward will be brought down to earth by its own nature, so the man whose mind is naturally good turns all the more toward what he is naturally inclined to, the harder you push him away."

Chapter 7: To the Governor of the Free Cities, Who Was an Epicurean

The administrator came to visit him, and the man was an Epicurean. Epictetus said:

It is proper for us who are not philosophers to make enquiries of you who are — as men coming to a strange city make enquiries of the citizens, of people who know the place — about what the best thing in the world is, so that we too, having enquired, may go after what is best and look at it, as visitors do with the sights of a city.

That there are three things belonging to a man — soul, body, and externals — hardly anybody denies. It remains for you philosophers to answer which is best. What are we to tell people? Is the flesh the best? And was it for that that Maximus sailed as far as Cassiope in winter with his son, and went with him, so that his flesh might be gratified?

When the man said it was not, and added, "Far be it from him," Epictetus said: Is it not fitting, then, to be actively occupied with what is best?

"It is the most fitting thing of all."

Then what do we possess that is better than the flesh?

"The soul," he replied.

And the good things of the best — are they better, or the good things of the worse?

"The good things of the best."

And are the good things of the best within the power of the will or not?

"They are within it."

Then is the pleasure of the soul something within the power of the will?

"It is," he replied.

And what will this pleasure depend on? On itself? That cannot even be conceived. There must first exist a certain substance of the good, by getting which we shall feel pleasure in the soul.

He agreed to this as well.

Then what shall we depend on for this pleasure of the soul? If it depends on things of the soul, the substance of the good has been found: for good cannot be one thing and the thing we are rationally delighted by another; nor, if what comes first is not good, can what follows be good, since for the thing that follows to be good, the thing that comes first must be good.

But you would not say that, if you are in your right mind, since you would then be saying something inconsistent with Epicurus and with the rest of your doctrines. So it remains that the pleasure of the soul is the pleasure that comes from things of the body — and again that those bodily things must be what comes first, and the substance of the good.

So Maximus acted foolishly if he made that voyage for any reason but the flesh, that is, for the best. And a man acts foolishly if he keeps his hands off what belongs to others when he is a judge and able to take it.

But if you like, let us consider only this: how the thing may be done secretly and safely, so that nobody knows. For Epicurus himself does not declare stealing to be bad; he only admits that being caught is. And because there is no way to be secure against being caught, he says: do not steal.

But I tell you that if the stealing is done cleverly and carefully we shall not be caught. And besides, we have powerful friends in Rome, both men and women, and the Greeks are weak, and nobody will dare go up to Rome to complain. Why do you hold back from your own good? It is senseless, foolish. And even if you tell me you do hold back, I shall not believe you.

For as it is impossible to assent to what appears false, and to turn away from what appears true, so it is impossible to hold back from what appears good. And wealth is a good thing, and certainly the most efficient producer of pleasure. Why not acquire wealth? And why should we not seduce our neighbour's wife, if we can do it without being caught? And if the husband talks foolishly about it, why not throw him out of the house?

If you want to be the sort of philosopher you ought to be — a complete one, consistent with your own doctrines — this is how you must act. And if you will not, then you will be no different from us so-called Stoics: we too say one thing and do another. We talk about what is fine and do what is base. But you will be perverse in the opposite direction: teaching what is bad and practising what is good.

In God's name, do you imagine a city of Epicureans? "I do not marry." "Nor I — a man ought not to marry, nor have children, nor take part in public business."

What will happen, then? Where will the citizens come from? Who will bring them up? Who will be in charge of the young men, who will preside over the gymnasium? And what will the teacher teach them? What the Spartans were taught, or what the Athenians were taught?

Come, take a young man and bring him up according to your doctrines. The doctrines are bad, they undermine the state, they are ruinous to families, and they do not become women. Drop them, man.

You live in a chief city. It is your duty to hold office, to judge justly, to keep your hands off what belongs to others. No woman should look beautiful to you but your own wife, and no youth, and no silver vessel, and no gold one. Look for doctrines that are consistent with what I say, and by taking them for your guide you will hold back with pleasure from things that have such power to persuade us and overpower us.

But if, on top of the persuasive power of these things, we also invent a philosophy like this one, which helps push us toward them and strengthens us in that direction, what will the result be?

In a piece of chased metalwork, which is the better part — the silver or the workmanship? The substance of the hand is flesh; but the work of the hand is the leading thing.

So there are three kinds of duty as well: those directed toward a thing's existing at all; those directed toward its existing in a particular way; and third, the leading things themselves. And so in a man too we ought not to value the material, the poor flesh, but the leading things.

And what are those? Taking part in public business, marrying, having children, revering God, taking care of one's parents; and in general, having desires and avoidances and pursuits and refusals in the way we ought to have them, and according to our nature.

And how has nature constituted us? Free, noble, capable of shame. What other creature blushes? What other can receive the impression of shame? And we are so constituted that pleasure is made subordinate to these things, as a servant, an attendant, so that it may call out our activity and keep us steady in acts that are in line with nature.

"But I am rich and I need nothing."

Then why pretend to be a philosopher? Your gold and silver vessels are enough for you. What need have you of principles?

"But I am also a judge of the Greeks."

Do you know how to judge? Who taught you to know?

"Caesar wrote me a commission."

Let him write you a commission to judge music; what use will it be to you? And how did you become a judge anyway? Whose hand did you kiss — Symphorus' or Numenius'? Outside whose bedroom did you sleep? Whom did you send presents to? Do you not see, then, that being a judge is worth exactly as much as Numenius is?

"But I can throw any man I please into prison."

So you can a stone.

"But I can beat with sticks whoever I please."

So you can a donkey.

That is not governing men. Govern us as rational creatures. Show us what is good for us and we will follow it; show us what is bad for us and we will turn away from it. Make us imitators of yourself, as Socrates made men imitators of himself. He was a governor of men in the true sense: he made them submit their desires to him, and their avoidances, and their movements toward a thing and away from it.

"Do this. Do not do that. If you disobey, I will throw you into prison." That is not governing men as rational creatures. But I say: act as Zeus has ordained; and if you do not, you will feel the penalty, you will be punished. And what will the punishment be? Nothing but not having done your duty. You will lose the character of a man who is trustworthy, self-respecting, decent. Do not look for heavier penalties than those.

Chapter 8: How to Train Yourself Against Impressions

As we train ourselves against trick questions, so we ought to train ourselves daily against impressions; because impressions put questions to us too.

So-and-so's son has died. Answer: that is not within the power of the will; it is not an evil. A father has disinherited his son. What do you think of it? It is outside the power of the will; not an evil. Caesar has condemned somebody. Outside the power of the will; not an evil. The man is crushed by it. Being crushed depends on the will: that is an evil. He has borne the sentence bravely. That is within the power of the will: that is a good.

If we train ourselves this way we shall make progress, because we shall never assent to anything of which we have no impression we can properly take in.

Your son is dead. What has happened? Your son is dead. Nothing more? Nothing. Your ship is lost. What has happened? Your ship is lost. A man has been taken off to prison. What has happened? He has been taken off to prison. That he has come off badly in it — every man adds that out of his own opinion.

"But Zeus does not do right in these matters." Why? Because he has made you able to endure? Because he has made you great in spirit? Because he has taken from what happens to you the power of being an evil? Because it is in your power to be happy while you suffer what you suffer? Because he has opened the door for you when things do not please you? Man, go out, and stop complaining.

If you would like to know how the Romans feel about philosophers, listen to this. Italicus, who had the highest reputation among them, was once annoyed with his own friends while I was there, and said, as though he were suffering something unbearable: "I cannot stand it — you are killing me. You will turn me into that man there." And he pointed at me.

Chapter 9: To a Lawyer Going Up to Rome for a Lawsuit

A man came to him who was going up to Rome about a lawsuit concerning his rank. Epictetus asked why he was going to Rome, and the man then asked what Epictetus thought about the business. Epictetus replied:

If you are asking me what you will do in Rome — whether you will win or lose — I have no rule about that. But if you ask me how you will fare, I can tell you: if your judgments are right, you will fare well; if they are false, you will fare badly. For in every man the cause of his acting is a judgment.

What was the reason you wanted to be elected governor of the Cnossians? Your judgment. What is the reason you are now going up to Rome? Your judgment. And going in winter, at danger and expense. "I have to go." What tells you that? Your judgment.

Then if judgments are the causes of every action, and a man has bad judgments, the effect will be of the same quality as the cause.

Well then, do we all have sound judgments — you and your opponent both? Then how do you differ? Or do you have sounder ones than he has? Why? You think so. And he thinks his are better. So do madmen. This is a bad test.

Show me instead that you have made some enquiry into your own judgments and taken some trouble over them. You are sailing to Rome now to become governor of the Cnossians, and you are not content to stay at home with the honours you already had; you want something greater and more conspicuous. So when did you ever make a voyage for the purpose of examining your own judgments and throwing out any bad ones? Whom did you go to for that? What time did you set aside for it? What age of your life?

Go over the times of your life on your own, if you are ashamed to do it in front of me. When you were a boy, did you examine your judgments? Did you not do then exactly as you do everything now? And when you had become a youth and were attending the teachers of rhetoric and practising rhetoric yourself, what did you imagine you were short of? And when you were a young man engaged in public business, pleading cases yourself and gaining a reputation, who seemed your equal then? And at what point would you have put up with any man examining you and showing that your judgments were bad?

So what do you want me to say to you?

"Help me in this business."

I have no rule for that. And if that is what you came to me for, you have not come to me as to a philosopher, but as to a greengrocer or a shoemaker.

Then what do philosophers have rules for? For this: that whatever happens, our governing part may be, and go on being, in line with nature. Does that seem a small thing to you?

"No — the greatest thing there is."

Well then: does it take only a short time? Can you snatch it up as you pass by? If you can, snatch it.

Then you will say: "I met Epictetus as I might meet a stone or a statue." You saw me, and that was all. But a man meets a man as a man when he learns his judgments and shows his own in return. Learn my judgments, show me yours, and then say that you have visited me. Let us examine each other. If I have a bad judgment, take it away; if you have one, show it to me. That is what meeting a philosopher means.

"Not at all: this is only a passing call, and while we are hiring the ship we can look in on Epictetus too. Let us see what he has to say." Then you go away and say: "Epictetus was nothing. He made grammatical mistakes and talked like a foreigner." What else did you come as, if not as judges?

Well — but somebody may say to me: if I attend to such matters as you do, I shall have no land, as you have none; no silver cups, as you have none; no fine cattle, as you have none.

Perhaps it is enough to answer: I have no need of such things. But you, who possess a great many things, have need of others; and whether you choose to admit it or not, you are poorer than I am.

"What do I need, then?"

What you have not got: steadiness, a mind in line with nature, freedom from being thrown about.

Whether I have a patron or not, what is that to me? But it is something to you. I am richer than you. I am not anxious about what Caesar will think of me. And so I flatter nobody. This is what I have instead of vessels of silver and gold. Your utensils are gold, but your talk, your judgments, your assents, your pursuits, your desires are earthenware.

And when I have these things in line with nature, why should I not spend my studies on reason as well? I have leisure; my mind is not pulled about. What shall I do, having nothing to distract me? What have I got that suits a man better than this?

When you have nothing to do you are disturbed; you go to the theatre, or wander about with no purpose. Why should a philosopher not work at improving his reason? You busy yourself with crystal vessels, I with the syllogism called the Liar; you with fluorspar vessels, I with the one called the Denying. To you everything you have looks small; to me everything I have looks great. Your desire cannot be satisfied; mine is satisfied already.

It is like children putting a hand into a narrow-necked jar to get figs and nuts. If they fill the hand they cannot get it out, and then they cry. Drop a few and you will get your hand out. And you — part with some of your desires. Do not desire many things, and you will have what you want.

Chapter 10: How to Bear Being Ill

When the need for each judgment arises, we ought to have it ready: at breakfast, the judgments that have to do with breakfast; in the bath, those that concern the bath; in bed, those that concern bed.

	Let sleep not fall upon your weary eyes
	before you have gone over the day's acts:
	what was done wrong, what done, what left undone.
	Examine all of it from first to last;
	then blame what was amiss, and take joy in what was right.

And we ought to keep these lines in a way that lets us use them, not in a way that lets us recite them aloud, as men cry out "Hail, Apollo".

Again, in a fever we should have ready the judgments that concern fever; and we ought not, the moment the fever starts, to let go of everything and forget. A man with a fever may say: "If I do any more philosophy, let me be hanged. Wherever I go I have to look after this poor body, so that a fever may not come."

But what is doing philosophy? Is it not preparing yourself against what may happen? Do you not see that you are saying something of this kind: "If I go on preparing myself to bear what happens with patience, let me be hanged"? It is exactly as if a man gave up the all-in fight after taking a few blows. In that fight it is in our power to stop and take no more blows. But in this business, if we give up philosophy, what do we gain?

So what should a man say when something painful happens? This is what I trained for. This is what I disciplined myself for.

God says to you: give me proof that you have trained properly as an athlete, that you have eaten what you should have, that you have exercised, that you have obeyed your trainer. And then do you go weak when the moment for action comes?

Now is the time for the fever. Bear it well. Now is the time for thirst. Bear it well. Now is the time for hunger. Bear it well. Is it not in your power? Who is going to stop you? The doctor will stop you drinking, but he cannot stop you bearing thirst well; and he will stop you eating, but he cannot stop you bearing hunger well.

"But I cannot get on with my philosophical studies."

And what do you pursue them for? Slave, is it not so that you may be happy, so that you may be steady, so that you may be in a state that is in line with nature and live that way? What stops you, when you have a fever, from having your governing part in line with nature? Here is the proof of the thing; here is the test of the philosopher. This too is a part of life, like walking, like sailing, like travelling overland — and so is fever.

Do you read while you are walking? No. Nor do you while you have a fever. But if you walk well, you have everything that belongs to a man walking. And if you bear a fever well, you have everything that belongs to a man in a fever.

What is it to bear a fever well? Not to blame God or man; not to be crushed by what is happening; to expect death well and nobly; to do what has to be done. Not to be frightened by what the doctor says when he comes in — and not to be overjoyed if he says you are doing well. What good has he told you? And when you were healthy, what good was that to you? And even if he says you are in a bad way, do not lose heart.

For what is being ill? Is it that you are near the parting of soul and body? What harm is there in that? If you are not near it now, will you not be near it later? Is the world going to be turned upside down when you die?

Why do you flatter the doctor, then? Why do you say, "If you please, master, I shall get well"? Why do you give him the chance to raise his eyebrows at you? Do you not value a doctor as you value a shoemaker measuring your foot, or a carpenter building your house? Treat the doctor as concerned with a body that is not yours, and is by nature dead already.

A man with a fever has the chance to do this; and if he does it, he has what belongs to him. It is not a philosopher's business to look after externals — not his wine, nor his oil, nor his poor body — but his own governing power. And how must he treat externals? So far as not to be careless about them.

Where is there reason for fear, then? Where is there any longer reason for anger, or for fear about what belongs to others, about things of no value?

We ought to have these two principles ready. First: that nothing is good or bad except the will. Second: that we ought not to lead events but to follow them. "My brother ought not to have treated me like that." No — but he will answer for that; and however he behaves, I will conduct myself toward him as I ought. That is my own business; the other is somebody else's. Nobody can stop me in mine; the other thing can be blocked.

Chapter 11: More Miscellaneous Remarks

There are certain penalties fixed, as if by law, for men who disobey the divine administration. Whoever thinks anything good except what depends on the will — let him envy, let him crave, let him flatter, let him be thrown about. Whoever thinks anything else evil — let him grieve, let him lament, let him weep, let him be unhappy. And yet, punished as severely as that, we cannot stop.

Remember what the poet says about the stranger:

	Stranger, I must not — not even if a worse man comes.

This can be applied to a father as well: I must not, even if a worse man than you should come, treat a father unworthily; for all fathers come from Zeus the father. And the same about a brother, for all come from Zeus who presides over kinsmen. And so in the other relations of life we shall find Zeus watching.

Chapter 12: On Training

We ought not to make our training consist in means that are against nature and calculated to astonish people; if we do, we who call ourselves philosophers will be no different from street performers. It is difficult to walk a rope — difficult and dangerous too. Ought we to practise rope-walking for that reason, or setting up a palm tree, or throwing our arms round statues? Not at all.

Not everything difficult and dangerous is fit for practice. What is fit is whatever helps toward working out the thing set before us. And what is set before us to work out? To live with desire and avoidance unrestrained. And what is that? Neither to be disappointed of what you desire nor to fall into what you would avoid. That is what training ought to aim at.

Since it is not possible to have your desire undisappointed and your avoidance free of falling into what it flees without great and constant practice, you must understand that if you let your desire and avoidance turn toward things outside the power of the will, your desire will not be able to reach its object and your avoidance will not be able to escape what it flees.

And since strong habit is what prevails, and we are used to employing desire and avoidance only on things outside the will, we ought to set a contrary habit against this one; and where impressions are most slippery, there we should set the habit of training.

I am rather inclined toward pleasure: I will lean to the opposite side beyond measure, for training. I shrink from pain: I will rub and exercise against the impressions that come to me, for the purpose of drawing my avoidance away from every such thing.

For who is a man in training? The man who practises not using desire, and applies his avoidance only to things within the power of his will, and practises hardest at what is hardest to beat. So one man must train himself more against one thing and another against another.

What is the use, then, of setting up a palm tree, or carrying about a leather tent, or a mortar and pestle? Practise, man, if you are quick-tempered, to put up with being abused, and not to be upset at being treated with contempt. Then you will make so much progress that even if a man strikes you, you will say to yourself: imagine you have thrown your arms round a statue.

Then train yourself to use wine properly, so as not to drink much — for there are men who foolishly train themselves at this too. But first of all you should keep off it altogether, and keep off young girls and rich cakes. Then at last, if the occasion offers, you will go down into the arena at a proper time to test yourself, and find out whether impressions overpower you as they used to. But at first run a long way from what is stronger than you. The contest between a charming young girl and a beginner in philosophy is not an equal one. The earthenware jar and the rock, as the saying goes, do not agree.

After desire and avoidance comes the second topic, of impulses toward action and away from it: that you may be obedient to reason, and do nothing out of season or out of place, or contrary to any propriety of that kind.

The third topic concerns assent, and has to do with what is persuasive and attractive. As Socrates said we ought not to live a life without examination, so we ought not to accept an impression without examination, but should say: wait, let me see what you are and where you come from — like the sentry at night who says, show me your password. Have you the signal from nature that an impression must have to be accepted?

And finally, whatever means men use on the body when they train it, if these tend in any way toward desire and avoidance, they too may be fit means of training. But if they are for show, they mark a man who has turned toward something external, who is hunting for something else and looking for spectators to say, "What a great man."

This is why Apollonius put it well: when you mean to train yourself for your own benefit, and you are thirsty in hot weather, take a mouthful of cold water, spit it out, and tell nobody.

Chapter 13: What Loneliness Is, and What Kind of Person Is Truly Alone

Loneliness is a certain condition of a helpless man. A man is not lonely just because he is by himself, any more than a man among crowds is not lonely.

So when we have lost a brother, or a son, or a friend we used to lean on, we say we have been left lonely — though we are often in Rome, though such a crowd goes by us, though so many people live in the same place, and though we sometimes have a great number of slaves. The man who counts as lonely is understood to be a helpless one, exposed to anybody who wants to harm him.

That is why, when we travel, we say we are lonely above all when we fall among robbers. It is not the sight of a human creature that takes us out of loneliness, but the sight of one who is trustworthy and decent and helpful to us.

If being by oneself were enough to make loneliness, you might say that Zeus himself is lonely at the great conflagration, and bewails himself, saying: unhappy that I am — I have no Hera, no Athena, no Apollo, no brother, no son, no descendant, no kinsman. Some people say this is what he does when he is alone at the conflagration. But they do not understand how a man passes his time when he is alone, because they start from a certain natural principle — from the natural desire for community and mutual affection, and from the pleasure of human conversation.

All the same, a man ought to be prepared for this too: to be able to be sufficient to himself and to be his own companion. As Zeus lives with himself and is at peace by himself and thinks about his own administration and its nature, and busies himself with thoughts that suit him, so we too ought to be able to talk with ourselves and not need other people; not to be at a loss for ways of passing the time; to observe the divine administration and our own relation to everything else; to consider how we used to be affected by what happens and how we are now; what things still give us pain, and how those too can be cured and taken away; and if anything needs improving, to improve it by reason.

You see that Caesar appears to give us a great peace: there are no more enemies, or battles, or great bands of robbers and pirates, and we can travel at any hour and sail from east to west. But can Caesar give us security against fever as well? Against shipwreck? Against fire, or earthquake, or lightning? Can he — I will go further — give us security against love? He cannot. Against grief? He cannot. Against envy? He cannot. In a word he cannot protect us against any of these things.

But the doctrine of the philosophers promises to give us security even against these. And what does it say? Men, if you will attend to me, then wherever you are and whatever you are doing you will feel no grief, no anger, no compulsion, no blocking; you will pass your time undisturbed and free of everything.

When a man has this peace — proclaimed not by Caesar (how could he proclaim it?) but by God through reason — is he not content when he is alone? When he looks and reflects: no evil can happen to me now; for me there is no robber, no earthquake; everything is full of peace, full of quiet. Every road, every city, every gathering, every neighbour, every companion is harmless. One whose business it is supplies me with food; another with clothing; another with senses and preconceptions. And if he does not supply what is needed, he gives the signal for retreat, opens the door, and says to you: go.

Go where? To nothing terrible — to the place you came from, to your friends and kinsmen, to the elements. What was fire in you goes to fire, what was earth to earth, what was breath to air, what was water to water. No Hades, no Acheron, no Cocytus, no Pyriphlegethon: everything is full of gods and spirits.

When a man has such things to think about, and sees the sun and the moon and the stars, and enjoys the earth and the sea, he is not lonely, and not even helpless.

"Well then — suppose somebody comes on me when I am alone and murders me?"

Fool, he does not murder you. He murders your poor body.

What sort of loneliness is left, then? What lack? Why do we make ourselves worse than children? What do children do when they are left alone? They pick up shells and ashes and build something, then knock it down and build something else, and so they are never at a loss for a way to pass the time. Am I to sit down and weep, then, if you sail away, because I have been left alone and lonely? Am I to have no shells and no ashes? Children do what they do out of a want of understanding; and we are unhappy out of understanding.

Every great capacity is dangerous to beginners. So you must bear such things as you are able, and in line with nature; but not — [the text is defective here.]

Practise sometimes living like a man in poor health, so that at some point you may live like a man in health. Go without food, drink water, hold off from desire altogether sometimes, so that at some point you may desire in accordance with reason; and if in accordance with reason, then when you have something good in you, you will desire well.

Not at all — we want to live like wise men straight away and be useful to mankind. Useful how? What are you doing? Have you been useful to yourself? But I suppose you want to urge them on. You, urge them on! You want to be useful to them. Then show them by your own example what sort of men philosophy makes, and stop trifling.

When you are eating, do good to the men eating with you; when you are drinking, to the men drinking with you. By giving way to everybody, yielding, putting up with them — do them good that way, and do not spit your bad temper over them.

Chapter 14: Further Miscellaneous Remarks

As bad tragic actors cannot sing alone but only in company with many, so some men cannot walk about alone. Man, if you are anything, walk alone and talk to yourself, and do not hide in the chorus. Examine yourself a little at last; look around; stir yourself up, so that you may know who you are.

When a man drinks water, or does anything else for the sake of training, he tells everybody about it whenever he gets the chance: "I drink water." Is that what you drink water for — for the purpose of drinking water? Man, if it is good for you to drink it, drink. And if not, you are being ridiculous. But if it is good for you and you do drink it, say nothing about it to men who dislike water-drinkers. What — do you want to please those very men?

Of the things men do, some are done for their own sake, some as the occasion arises, some with an eye to circumstances, some to fall in with other people, and some according to a settled plan of life.

There are two things you must root out of men: arrogance and distrust. Arrogance is the opinion that you lack nothing; distrust is the opinion that you cannot be happy with so many circumstances pressing round you. Arrogance is removed by being refuted, and Socrates was the first man to practise that. And to learn that the thing is not impossible, enquire and search. The search will do you no harm; and in a way this is what philosophising is — to look for how it is possible to use desire and avoidance without being blocked.

"I am better than you: my father was a consul." Another says, "I have been a tribune and you have not." If we were horses, would you say: my father was faster? I have plenty of barley and fodder, and elegant harness? And if, while you were saying it, I said: very well, let us race, then. Well — is there nothing in a man corresponding to running in a horse, by which we can tell which is the better and which the worse? Is there not self-respect, good faith, justice? Show yourself better in those, so that you may be better as a man. If you tell me that you can land a heavy kick, I shall tell you that you are proud of what a donkey does.

Chapter 15: Why We Should Think Before We Start Anything

In every act, consider what comes before it and what follows from it, and then go on to the act. If you do not consider, you will start off full of spirit, having given no thought to the consequences; and afterwards, when some of them have shown themselves, you will drop the thing shamefully.

"I want to win at the Olympic games." And so do I, by the gods — it is a fine thing. But consider what comes before and what follows, and then, if it is good for you, take the thing on.

You will have to keep to rules, follow a strict diet, keep off delicacies, exercise under compulsion at fixed hours in heat and in cold, drink no cold water and no wine when you have the chance of it. In a word you must hand yourself over to the trainer as you would to a doctor. Then in the contest itself you must be covered in sand, sometimes dislocate a hand or sprain an ankle, swallow a quantity of dust, be flogged with the whip — and after going through all of it, sometimes lose.

When you have counted all that up, then, if you still have the inclination, go to the training ground. If you do not count it up, look what you will be doing: behaving like children, who play at wrestlers one moment, then at gladiators, then blow a trumpet, then act a tragedy, whenever they have seen and admired such things.

So you too: one moment an athlete, then a gladiator, then a philosopher, then a public speaker — and with your whole soul, nothing. Like an ape you copy everything you see, and one thing after another pleases you while whatever has become familiar bores you. You have never taken anything on after consideration, or after exploring the whole matter and putting it to a strict examination; you have taken it on at random and with a lukewarm desire.

So some men, having seen a philosopher and heard one speak like Euphrates — and who can speak like him? — want to be philosophers themselves.

Man, consider first what the business is, and then your own nature, and what it can bear. If you are going to wrestle, look at your shoulders, your thighs, your loins: different men are naturally made for different things.

Do you suppose you can be a philosopher and go on doing what you do every day? Do you suppose you can eat as you eat now, drink as you drink now, and be angry and out of temper in the same way? You must go without sleep, work hard, master certain desires, leave your family, be looked down on by your slave, laughed at by the men you meet, and come off worse in everything — in office, in honours, in the law courts.

When you have thought all this over thoroughly, then, if you think proper, come to philosophy — if you want to get, in exchange for these things, freedom from being thrown about, and liberty, and peace of mind. And if you have not thought it over, do not come near philosophy. Do not behave like children: a philosopher one moment, then a tax collector, then a public speaker, then an agent of Caesar. These things do not go together. You must be one man, either good or bad. You must work either at your own governing part or at externals — either at what is inside or at what is outside. That is, you must take the place either of a philosopher or of one of the crowd.

Somebody said to Rufus, when Galba had been murdered: "Is the world governed by Providence now?" And Rufus answered: "Did I ever, even in passing, build an argument on Galba that the world is governed by Providence?"

Chapter 16: Why We Should Be Careful About the Company We Keep

If a man mixes often with other people — for conversation, or drinking together, or social life generally — he must either become like them or bring them round to his own way. Put a piece of dead charcoal next to a burning one, and either the dead one puts the other out or the burning one lights it.

Since the danger is so great, we must be careful about entering into these intimacies with ordinary people, and remember that it is impossible to keep company with a man covered in soot and not get some of the soot on yourself.

What will you do if a man talks about gladiators, about horses, about athletes — or, worse, about men? So-and-so is bad, so-and-so is good; this was done well, that was done badly. And what if he sneers, or ridicules, or shows a spiteful temper?

Is any man among us prepared as a lute player is when he picks up a lute, so that the moment he has touched the strings he knows which are out of tune and tunes the instrument? Has any of us the power Socrates had, who in all his social dealings could bring his companions round to his own purpose? How should you have that power? So it necessarily follows that you are carried along by ordinary people.

Why are they stronger than you, then? Because they say their useless things out of real conviction, while you say your elegant things only with your lips. That is why yours are feeble and dead, and why it turns the stomach to listen to your exhortations and your miserable virtue that gets talked about up and down. This is how the crowd has the advantage of you: every conviction is strong and unbeatable.

So until fine sentiments are fixed in you, and you have acquired some power to keep yourself safe, I advise you to be careful about your company. If you are not, then every day, like wax in the sun, whatever you write on your minds in the school will be melted away. Get well away from the sun, then, as long as your sentiments are made of wax.

This is also why philosophers advise men to leave their own country: old habits distract them and do not let a different habit get started, and we cannot put up with the people who meet us and say, "Look, so-and-so is a philosopher now, who used to be such and such." In the same way doctors send patients with lingering diseases to a different country and a different air; and they are right.

You too, then: bring in other habits than the ones you have. Fix your judgments and train yourself in them. But you do not. You go from here to a show, to a gladiator display, to the exercise ground, to the circus; then you come back here, and then off you go again to those places, and always the same people.

And there is no good habit in it, no attention, no care about yourself, no watching of this kind: how shall I use the impressions put before me? In line with nature or against it? How am I answering them? As I ought, or as I ought not? Am I saying to the things outside the will that they are nothing to me?

If you are not yet in this condition, run from your old habits, run from the crowd, if you ever intend to begin to be something.

Chapter 17: On Providence and Why the Wicked Prosper

When you bring a charge against Providence, consider it, and you will find the thing has happened according to reason.

"Yes, but the unjust man has the advantage."

In what? "In money." Yes — because he is better than you at this: he flatters, he has no shame, he is wide awake. What is surprising about it? But see whether he has the advantage of you in good faith and in self-respect. You will not find that he has; you will find that you have the advantage wherever you are the better man.

I once said to a man who was upset because Philostorgus was doing well: would you be willing to go to bed with Sura?

"May that day never come," he answered.

Then why are you upset if the man gets something in return for what he sells? And how can you count him happy, when he acquires those things by means you find disgusting? And what wrong does Providence do, if it gives the better things to the better men? Is it not better to be self-respecting than to be rich?

He agreed that it was.

Then why are you upset, man, when you have the better thing?

So remember always, and keep it ready, that this is a law of nature: the superior has the advantage of the inferior in the thing he is superior in. And you will never be upset.

"But my wife treats me badly." Well: if anybody asks you what this is, say, my wife treats me badly. Is there nothing more? Nothing. "My father gives me nothing." What is this? My father gives me nothing. Is there nothing else? Nothing.

But to say that this is an evil is something that has to be added on from outside, and added falsely. This is why we must get rid, not of poverty, but of our opinion about poverty — and then we shall be happy.

Chapter 18: Why No News Should Disturb You

When something is reported to you that is of a kind to disturb you, have this principle ready: the news is about nothing within the power of your will.

Can any man report to you that you have formed a bad judgment, or had a bad desire? Not at all. But perhaps he will report that somebody has died. What is that to you? He may report that somebody is speaking ill of you. What is that to you? Or that your father is plotting something. Against whom? Against your will? How could he? Against your poor body, then, and your little property? You are perfectly safe: it is not against you.

"But the judge declares that you have committed an act of irreverence." And did the judges not make the same declaration about Socrates? Does it concern you that the judge has made the declaration? No. Then why trouble yourself about it any longer?

Your father has a certain duty, and if he does not carry it out he loses the character of a father, of a man with natural affection, of a gentle man. Do not wish him to lose anything else on that account. For a man never does wrong in one thing and suffers in another.

And on your side, it is your duty to make your defence firmly, with self-respect, and without anger; and if you do not, you too lose the character of a son, of a man of decent behaviour, of a generous man.

Well then, is the judge out of danger? No — he is in equal danger. So why are you still afraid of his verdict? What have you to do with somebody else's evil? Making a bad defence is your own evil; guard against that alone. Being condemned or not condemned is another man's act, and so it is another man's evil.

Somebody threatens you. Me? No. He blames you. Let him see to how he manages his own affairs. He is going to condemn you unjustly. He is a wretched man.

Chapter 19: The Difference Between an Ordinary Person and a Philosopher

The first difference between an ordinary man and a philosopher is this. The ordinary man says, "Alas for me, on account of my little child, my brother, my father." The philosopher, if he is ever forced to say "Alas for me", stops and adds: "on my own account."

For nothing outside the will can block or damage the will; only the will can block or damage itself. So if we lean this way ourselves — so that when we come off badly we blame ourselves, and remember that nothing else is the cause of being thrown or of losing our peace of mind except our own opinion — then I swear to you by all the gods that we have made progress.

But as things stand we have gone another way from the very beginning. While we were still children, if we ever stumbled through carelessness, the nurse did not scold us: she beat the stone. And what had the stone done? Ought the stone to have moved out of the way on account of your child's carelessness?

Again, if we came out of the bath and found nothing to eat, the tutor never checked our appetite: he flogged the cook. Man, did we make you tutor to the cook and not to the child? Correct the child. Improve him.

And so, even when we are grown, we are like children. A man with no music is a child in music; a man with no letters is a child in learning; a man with no teaching is a child in life.

Chapter 20: How Everything External Can Be Turned to Advantage

With impressions that come through the eyes, nearly everybody has allowed that good and evil are in us and not in externals. Nobody calls it good that it is day, or bad that it is night, or calls it the greatest evil that somebody thinks three are four. What do men say instead? They say that knowledge is good and error is bad — so that even where falsehood itself is concerned there is a good result: knowing that it is false.

It ought to be the same in life. Is health a good thing and sickness a bad one? No, man. What is it, then? To be healthy, and healthy in the right way, is good; to be healthy in a bad way is bad. So it is possible to get an advantage even out of illness, I tell you.

Is it not possible to get an advantage even out of death? Even out of being maimed? Do you think Menoeceus gained little by dying? Could a man who says so gain as much as Menoeceus gained? Come, man: did he not keep the character of a lover of his country, a man of great spirit, faithful and generous? And if he had gone on living, would he not have lost all of it? Would he not have gained the opposite — the name of coward, ignoble, a hater of his country, a man afraid of death? Well then, do you think he gained little by dying? I suppose not. And did the father of Admetus gain much by dragging his life out so ignobly and miserably? Did he not die afterwards anyway?

Stop, I beg you by the gods, admiring material things. Stop making yourselves slaves — first of things, and then, on account of things, slaves of the men who can give them or take them away.

Can advantage be got out of these things, then? Out of all of them — and out of the man who abuses you. What good does a sparring partner do an athlete before the contest? A very great deal. This man becomes my sparring partner: he trains me in endurance, in keeping my temper, in mildness.

You say no. But the man who grabs my neck and works my loins and shoulders does me good, and the trainer is right when he says, "Lift him with both hands, and the heavier he is, the better for me." And if a man trains me in keeping my temper, does he not do me good?

This is not knowing how to get an advantage out of people. Is my neighbour bad? Bad for himself, good for me: he exercises my good temper and my moderation. Is my father bad? Bad for himself, good for me.

This is the wand of Hermes: touch what you like with it, as the saying goes, and it turns to gold. I say something else: bring what you like and I will turn it into a good. Bring disease, bring death, bring poverty, bring abuse, bring a trial on a capital charge — all of them shall be made profitable by the wand of Hermes.

"What will you do with death?" What else but make it do you honour, or show by means of it what a man is who follows the will of nature? "What will you do with disease?" I will show what it is; I will be at my best in it; I will be steady, I will be happy, I will not flatter the doctor, I will not pray to die.

"What more are you looking for?" Whatever you give me, I will make it happy, fortunate, honoured — a thing a man will go looking for.

You say: no, take care you do not fall ill, it is a bad thing. That is exactly as if you said: take care never to get the impression that three are four, that is bad. Man, how is it bad? If I think about it as I ought, how will it damage me? Will it not even do me good?

So if I think as I ought about poverty, about disease, about holding no office, is that not enough for me? Will it not be an advantage? Then why should I go on looking for good and evil in externals?

And what happens? These doctrines are maintained here, and nobody carries them home. Straight away every man is at war with his slave, with his neighbours, with the men who sneered at him, with the men who laughed at him. Good luck to Lesbius, who proves every day that I know nothing.

Chapter 21: Against Those Who Rush to Set Up as Teachers

Men who have taken in bare theorems want to bring them straight up again, as people with a bad stomach do with food. Digest the thing first, and do not vomit it up like that. If you do not digest it, the thing really does become an emetic — raw food, unfit to eat.

But after you have digested it, show us some change in your governing part, as athletes show in their shoulders what they have trained at and what they have eaten, and as men who have taken up a trade show what they have learned. The carpenter does not come along and say, "Hear me talk about carpentry." He takes on the building of a house, builds it, and proves that he knows the art.

You ought to do something of the same kind. Eat like a man, drink like a man, dress, marry, have children, do a citizen's duty, put up with being abused, bear with an unreasonable brother, bear with your father, your son, your neighbour, your companion. Show us these things, so that we can see you have really learned something from the philosophers.

"No — come and hear me read my commentaries."

Go away and find somebody else to be sick over.

"But I will expound the writings of Chrysippus to you as no other man can. I will make his text perfectly clear. I will add the force of Antipater and Archedemus too, if I can."

Is it for this that young men are to leave their country and their parents — to come here and hear you explain words? Ought they not to go home able to endure, active in dealing with other people, free of passions, free of being thrown about, with such provisions for the journey of life that they will be able to bear well whatever happens and take honour from it? And how are you to give them any of that, when you do not have it yourself?

Have you done anything at all, from the beginning, except busy yourself with resolving syllogisms and shifting arguments and the ones that work by questioning?

"But so-and-so has a school. Why should I not have one too?"

These things are not done carelessly, man, or just as it happens. There has to be a suitable age, and a life, and God as a guide. You say no. But no man sails out of harbour without having sacrificed to the gods and asked their help; nobody sows without having called on Demeter. And is a man who has taken on so great a work going to undertake it safely without the gods? Are men who take this work on going to come to it successfully?

What are you doing, man, but giving away the mysteries? You say: "There is a temple at Eleusis, and there is one here too. There is a hierophant at Eleusis, and I will make a hierophant. There is a herald, and I will appoint a herald. There is a torchbearer at Eleusis, and I will appoint a torchbearer. There are torches at Eleusis, and I will have torches here. The words are the same. How do the things done here differ from the things done there?"

Most irreverent of men, is there no difference? Those things are done in the right place and at the right time, accompanied by sacrifice and prayers, when a man has first been purified and has brought his mind to the thought that he is about to approach sacred rites, and ancient ones. That is how the mysteries are useful; that is how we come to the notion that all these things were established by the ancients for the instruction and correction of life.

But you publish them and give them away at the wrong time, in the wrong place, without sacrifices, without purity. You do not have the robes the hierophant ought to have, nor the hair, nor the headdress, nor the voice, nor the age; and you have not purified yourself as he has. You have only learned the words by heart, and you say: the words are sacred in themselves.

You ought to come at these matters another way. The thing is great, it is a mystery, it is not a common thing and it is not given to every man. Not even wisdom is perhaps enough to let a man take charge of young men: he needs a certain readiness and fitness for it as well, and a certain bodily quality — and above all he needs God to advise him to take the office, as God advised Socrates to take the place of a man who refutes error, and Diogenes the office of kingship and reproof, and another man the office of teaching precepts.

But you open a doctor's shop with nothing in it but drugs, and you do not know where or how they are to be applied, and you have taken no trouble to find out. "Look," the man says, "I have ointments for the eyes too." And have you the power of using them? Do you know when and how they will do good, and whom they will do good to?

Why do you take chances in matters of the greatest importance, then? Why are you careless? Why do you take on a thing that in no way suits you? Leave it to men who can do it, and do it well. Do not bring disgrace on philosophy by your own acts, and do not be one of the men who give it a bad name.

If theorems please you, sit still and turn them over on your own — but never say you are a philosopher, and do not let anybody else say it. Say instead: he is mistaken; my desires are no different from what they were before, my activity is not directed at other objects, I do not assent to other things, and in the use of impressions I have not changed at all from what I was.

This is what you must think and say about yourself, if you want to think as you ought. And if not, take your chances and go on doing what you are doing — it suits you.

Chapter 22: On the Cynic Life

One of his pupils, who seemed inclined toward the Cynic life, asked Epictetus what sort of man a Cynic ought to be, and what the idea of the thing was.

We shall look into that at leisure, said Epictetus. But I have this much to say to you: a man who takes on so great a matter without God is hateful to God, and has no other purpose than to disgrace himself in public.

In any well-run household no man comes forward and says to himself, I ought to be the steward here. If he does, the master turns round, sees him giving orders insolently, drags him out and flogs him. So it is in this great city, the world; here too there is a master of the house who arranges everything.

"You are the sun. By going round you can make the year and the seasons, make the fruits grow and feed them, stir the winds and let them drop, and warm men's bodies properly. Go, travel round, and administer things from the greatest to the least. You are a calf: when a lion appears, do your proper business — run. If you do not, you will suffer for it. You are a bull: come forward and fight, for that is your business and it suits you and you can do it. You can lead the army against Troy: be Agamemnon. You can fight Hector in single combat: be Achilles."

But if Thersites had come forward and claimed the command, either he would not have got it, or if he had, he would have disgraced himself in front of a great many witnesses.

So think the matter over carefully yourself. It is not what it looks like to you. You say: "I wear a cloak now and I shall wear one then. I sleep hard now and I shall sleep hard then. I shall add a little bag and a staff, and go about begging and abusing the men I meet; and if I see a man plucking the hair off his body I shall rebuke him, or if he has had his hair dressed, or if he goes about in purple."

If you imagine the thing is like that, keep well away from it. Do not go near it; it is not for you at all. But if you imagine it as it really is, and do not think yourself unfit for it, consider what a great thing you are taking on.

In the first place, in the things that concern yourself you must be nothing like what you are now. You must blame neither God nor man. You must do away with desire altogether. You must move your avoidance to the things within the power of the will alone. You must feel no anger, no resentment, no envy, no pity. No girl must look handsome to you; you must not love a little reputation; you must not be pleased by a boy or a cake.

For you should understand that other men put walls around themselves, and houses, and darkness, when they do any such thing, and they have plenty of means of concealment. A man shuts the door and posts somebody outside the room: "If anyone comes, say he is out, he is busy." But the Cynic must use self-respect for all these — and if he does not, he will be indecent in his nakedness and under the open sky. This is his house, his door, the slave outside his bedroom, his darkness.

He must not want to hide anything he does; and if he does, he is finished. He has lost the character of a Cynic, of a man who lives under the open sky, of a free man. He has begun to fear something external; he has begun to need concealment, and he cannot have concealment when he wants it. Where would he hide, and how? And if this public teacher, this tutor of mankind, does happen to be caught, what will he be forced to suffer?

So when a man is afraid of these things, is it possible for him to be bold with his whole soul and take charge of other men? It is not. It is impossible.

First, then, you must make your governing part clean, and this way of life clean too. "Now the material I work on is my understanding, as wood is the carpenter's and hides the shoemaker's, and my business is the right use of impressions. The body is nothing to me; its parts are nothing to me. Death? Let it come when it likes, death of the whole or of a part. Go into exile, you say. And where to? Can any man throw me out of the world? He cannot. Wherever I go, there is the sun, there is the moon, there are the stars, dreams, omens, and conversation with the gods."

And then, if he is prepared like that, the true Cynic cannot be satisfied with it. He must know that he has been sent as a messenger from Zeus to men, about good things and bad, to show them that they have gone astray and are looking for the substance of good and evil where it is not, and never think of it where it is. He is a spy, as Diogenes was taken to Philip after the battle of Chaeronea as a spy. In fact a Cynic is a spy on what is good for men and what is bad, and his duty is to examine carefully and to come back and report truly — not to be struck with terror and point out as enemies men who are not enemies, nor to be thrown or confused by impressions in any other way.

So it is his duty to be able, if the occasion arises, to come forward on the tragic stage and say in a loud voice, as Socrates did:

Men, where are you rushing to? What are you doing, wretches? You are wandering up and down like blind people. You are on another road and have left the true one. You look for prosperity and happiness where they are not, and if somebody else shows you where they are, you do not believe him.

Why do you look for it outside? In the body? It is not there. If you doubt me, look at Myro, look at Ophellius. In possessions? It is not there. If you do not believe me, look at Croesus; look at the rich men of today, and what lamentation their lives are full of. In power? It is not there. If it were, men who have been consul twice and three times would have to be happy — and they are not.

Whom shall we believe about this? You, who see their affairs from outside and are dazzled by an appearance, or the men themselves? What do they say? Listen to them when they groan, when they grieve, when they think themselves more wretched and in greater danger precisely because of these consulships and this glory and splendour.

Is it in royal power? It is not; if it were, Nero would have been happy, and Sardanapalus. But not even Agamemnon was happy, and he was a better man than Sardanapalus and Nero. While the others are snoring, what is he doing?

	Much of his rooted hair he tore from his head.
	— Iliad, x 15

And what does he say himself?

	"I am at a loss," he says, "and
	I am shaken," and "my heart is leaping
	out of my breast."
	— Iliad x 91

Wretch, which of your affairs is going badly? Your possessions? No. Your body? No. You are rich in gold and bronze. What is the matter with you, then? That part of you, whatever it is, has been neglected by you and has gone rotten — the part we desire with, and avoid with, and move toward things and away from things with.

Neglected how? He does not know the nature of the good he was made by nature for, nor the nature of evil; nor what is his own and what belongs to another. And when something belonging to others goes badly he says, "Alas for me, the Greeks are in danger." Wretched is his governing part, and it alone is neglected and uncared for.

"The Greeks are going to be destroyed by the Trojans and die." And if the Trojans do not kill them, will they not die? "Yes — but not all at once." And what difference does that make? If death is an evil, it is equally an evil whether men die all together or one by one. Is anything else going to happen but the parting of soul and body? Nothing. And if the Greeks perish, is the door shut? Is it not in your power to die? It is.

Then why do you lament, and say, "You, a king, holding the sceptre of Zeus"? An unhappy king does not exist, any more than an unhappy god. What are you, then? A shepherd, in truth — because you weep as shepherds do when a wolf has carried off one of their sheep. And these men you govern are sheep.

And why did you come here? Was your desire in any danger? Your avoidance? Your pursuits? Your refusals? "No," he says, "but my brother's wife was carried off." Then was it not a great gain to be rid of an adulterous wife? "Are we to be despised by the Trojans, then?" What sort of people are the Trojans, wise or foolish? If they are wise, why are you fighting them? If they are fools, why do you care what they think?

Where is the good, then, since it is not in these things? Tell us, you who are lord and messenger and spy.

It is where you do not think it is, and do not choose to look for it. If you chose to look, you would have found it inside yourselves; and you would not be wandering off the road, or seeking what belongs to others as though it were your own.

Turn your thoughts inward. Notice the preconceptions you have. What sort of thing do you imagine the good to be? "Something that runs smoothly, something happy, something unblocked." Come — do you not naturally imagine it to be great? Do you not imagine it to be valuable? Do you not imagine it to be beyond harm?

In what material, then, ought you to look for what runs smoothly and is unblocked — in what serves, or in what is free? "In what is free." Then do you possess the body free, or in a condition of slavery? "We do not know." Do you not know that it is the slave of fever, of gout, of eye disease, of dysentery, of a tyrant, of fire, of iron, of everything stronger than itself? "Yes, it is a slave."

Then how is it possible for anything belonging to the body to be unblocked? And how is a thing great or valuable that is by nature dead — earth, mud?

Well then, do you possess nothing that is free? "Perhaps nothing." And who is able to force you to assent to what appears false? "No man." And who can force you not to assent to what appears true? "No man." Then by this you see that there is something in you naturally free.

And to desire, or to be averse, or to move toward an object or away from it, or to prepare yourself, or to plan to do something — which of you can do this without having received an impression of what is profitable or of what is a duty? "No man." So in these things too you have something unblocked and free.

Wretched men, work at this. Take care of this. Look for the good here.

"And how is it possible for a man who has nothing, who is naked, homeless, without a hearth, dirty, with no slave and no city, to lead a life that runs smoothly?"

Look — God has sent you a man to show you that it is possible. Look at me: no city, no house, no possessions, no slave. I sleep on the ground. I have no wife, no children, no official residence — only earth and sky and one poor cloak. And what do I lack? Am I not free from sorrow? Am I not free from fear? Am I not free?

When did any of you ever see me fail to get what I desired, or fall into what I would avoid? Did I ever blame God or man? Did I ever accuse anybody? Did any of you ever see me with a sorrowful face? And how do I meet the men you are afraid of and admire? Do I not treat them like slaves? Who, seeing me, does not think he is looking at his king and his master?

This is the language of the Cynics, this is their character, this is their purpose. You say: "No — what marks them is the little bag and the staff and the big jaws; bolting everything you give them, or hoarding it; abusing everybody they meet at the wrong moment; and showing off a fine shoulder."

Do you see how you are going about taking on so great a business? First take a mirror. Look at your shoulders. Look at your loins and your thighs. You are going, my man, to be enrolled as a competitor in the Olympic games — no feeble, wretched contest. At the Olympic games a man is not allowed to be beaten and simply take himself off: first he must be disgraced in the sight of the whole world, not only of Athenians or Spartans or Nicopolitans; and next he must be whipped as well, if he entered the contests rashly; and before being whipped he must endure thirst and heat and swallow a great deal of dust.

Think it over more carefully. Know yourself. Consult the divinity. Attempt nothing without God. If he does advise you to do it, be sure that he intends you either to become great or to take a great many blows.

For this very entertaining quality goes along with being a Cynic: he must be flogged like a donkey, and while he is being flogged he must love the men who flog him, as though he were the father of them all and the brother of them all.

You say: "No — if a man flogs you, stand in the public square and call out, Caesar, what am I suffering under your protection in this time of peace? Let us take the offender before the proconsul."

But what is Caesar to a Cynic, or a proconsul, or anybody except the one who sent him down here and whom he serves — Zeus? Does he call on anybody but Zeus? Is he not convinced that whatever he suffers, it is Zeus who is training him?

Hercules, when Eurystheus was training him, did not think himself wretched; he set about carrying out everything he was given without hesitation. And is the man who is being trained and exercised by Zeus going to cry out and be annoyed — a man fit to carry the staff of Diogenes?

Listen to what Diogenes says to the passers-by when he is in a fever. "Miserable wretches, will you not stop? You go all the way to Olympia to see athletes destroyed or fighting, and you will not choose to watch a fight between a fever and a man?"

Would such a man accuse the God who sent him down, as though God were treating him unworthily — a man who gloried in his circumstances and claimed to be an example to the people going past? What would he accuse him of? Of keeping his behaviour decent? Of showing his virtue more plainly?

And what does he say about poverty, about death, about pain? How did he compare his own happiness with that of the great king of Persia? Or rather, he thought there was no comparison to be made. Where there are disturbances and griefs and fears and unsatisfied desires and avoidances of what cannot be avoided, and envies and jealousies — how is there any road to happiness there? And where the principles are rotten, all these things must necessarily be.

The young man asked: if a Cynic falls ill and a friend asks him to come to his house and be looked after, should the Cynic accept the invitation?

And where, I ask you, are you going to find a friend of a Cynic? The man who invites him would have to be another such man himself, if he is to be worth counting as a Cynic's friend. He would have to share the Cynic's staff and his kingship, and be a worthy servant of it, if he means to be thought worthy of a Cynic's friendship — as Diogenes was the friend of Antisthenes, and Crates of Diogenes.

Do you think that a man who comes up to a Cynic and greets him is the Cynic's friend, and that the Cynic will judge him fit to have a Cynic in his house? So think about this too, if you please: look round instead for some convenient dunghill where you can have your fever, sheltered from the north wind so that you do not get chilled. But it looks to me as though you want to get into somebody's house and be well fed there for a while. Why are you thinking of attempting so great a thing at all?

"But," said the young man, "should a Cynic take on marriage and the raising of children as a chief duty?"

Give me a community of wise men, Epictetus replied, and perhaps no man will readily take up the Cynic practice at all. On whose account would he take up that way of life? But suppose he does; then nothing will stop him marrying and having children, for his wife will be another like himself, and his father-in-law another like himself, and his children will be brought up like him.

But in the present state of things, which is like an army drawn up in battle order, is it not right that the Cynic should be free of distraction and engaged only in the service of God — able to go about among men, not tied down to the ordinary duties of mankind, not entangled in the ordinary relations of life? Because if he neglects those relations he will not keep the character of an honourable and good man, and if he observes them he will lose the character of the messenger and spy and herald of God.

Consider: he has a duty to his father-in-law, and something to the rest of his wife's family, and something to his wife too, if he has one. And being a Cynic shuts him out from nursing his own family in sickness and from providing for their keep. To say nothing of the rest, he must have a vessel for heating water for the baby so that he can wash it in the bath, and wool for his wife when she has given birth, and oil, a bed, a cup — and so the furniture of the house grows. I say nothing of his other occupations and his distractions.

Where is that king now, the man who gives himself to the public interest,

	the guardian of the people, weighed down with cares,
	— Homer, Iliad ii 25

whose duty it is to look after other men — the married ones and the ones with children; to see who treats his wife well and who badly; who quarrels; which household is well run and which is not — going about as a doctor does and feeling pulses? He says to one, you have a fever; to another, you have a headache, or gout. He tells one to go without food, another to eat, another to keep out of the baths; and another that he needs the knife or the cautery.

How is a man tied to the duties of ordinary life to have time for this? Is it not his duty to supply his children with clothing, and send them to the schoolmaster with writing tablets and pens? And must he not supply them with beds as well? They cannot be full-grown Cynics the moment they are born. If he is not going to do these things, it would be better to expose the children as soon as they are born than to kill them this way.

See what we are bringing the Cynic down to, and how we are taking his kingship away from him. "Yes, but Crates took a wife." You are talking about a case that arose out of love, and about a woman who was another Crates. We are enquiring about ordinary marriages, free of complications; and making that enquiry, we do not find marriage in this state of the world a thing especially suited to the Cynic.

How is society to keep going, then? In God's name, which men do society more good — the ones who bring two or three squalling children into the world to take their own places, or the ones who watch over the whole of mankind as far as they can, and see what men do, how they live, what they attend to and what they neglect against their duty?

Did the Thebans who left small children behind do them more good than Epaminondas, who died childless? Did Priam, who fathered fifty worthless sons, or Danaus, or Aeolus, contribute more to the community than Homer?
