Well then: shall a general's duty, or a writer's work, excuse a man from marriage and from having children, and shall such a man not be judged to have taken on childlessness for nothing — and shall the kingship of a Cynic not be counted as worth the want of children? Do we not see his grandeur? Do we not rightly contemplate the character of Diogenes? And do we instead turn our eyes to the Cynics of today, dogs waiting at tables, who imitate the old Cynics in nothing at all except perhaps in breaking wind?

Otherwise such matters would not have troubled us in the least, and we should not have wondered at a Cynic not marrying and not having children. Man, the Cynic is father of all men. The men are his sons and the women his daughters; that is how carefully he visits them all, that is how well he cares for them all. Do you suppose it is out of idle impertinence that he rebukes the people he meets? He does it as a father, as a brother, and as the servant of the father of all — the servant of Zeus.

Ask me too, if you like, whether a Cynic shall take part in the government of the state. Fool, do you look for a greater form of government than the one he is already engaged in? Do you ask whether he shall appear before the Athenians and say something about revenue and supply — a man who has to talk to all men alike, to Athenians, to Corinthians, to Romans, and not about supply or revenue or peace or war, but about happiness and unhappiness, good fortune and bad, slavery and freedom? When a man has taken on the government of such a state as that, do you ask me whether he shall go into government?

Ask me too whether he shall hold office. Again I shall say to you: fool, what greater office shall he hold than the one he holds now?

Such a man also needs a certain bodily condition. If he looks consumptive, thin and pale, his testimony does not carry the same weight. He must not only prove to ordinary people, by showing the qualities of his soul, that it is possible to be a good man independently of the things they admire; he must also show by his body that a simple, frugal life in the open air does not damage even the body. "Look," he says, "I am the proof of it, and so is my body."

That is what Diogenes did: he used to go about looking fresh, and he caught the attention of the crowd by his very appearance. But if a Cynic is an object of pity he looks like a beggar; everybody turns away from him, everybody is offended by him. He ought not to look dirty either, or he will drive men off that way too; his very roughness ought to be clean and attractive.

A Cynic ought also to have a great deal of natural grace and quickness — and if he does not, he is a stupid fellow and nothing more. He needs these qualities so as to be able to meet any circumstance that arises readily and aptly.

So Diogenes replied to a man who said, "Are you the Diogenes who does not believe there are gods?" And how can that be, said Diogenes, when I think that you are hateful to them?

And on another occasion, when Alexander stood over him while he was asleep and quoted Homer's line,

	A man who is a councillor should not sleep all night,

he answered, still half asleep,

	the guardian of the people, weighed down with cares.

But before everything, the Cynic's governing part must be purer than the sun; and if it is not, he must be a cunning rogue and a man of no principle, since he will be reproving others while he is tangled up in some vice himself.

See how the matter stands. Kings and tyrants have their guards and their weapons, and these give them the power of reproving certain men and even of punishing wrongdoers, bad as they are themselves. But for a Cynic, what gives that power, instead of weapons and guards, is his conscience.

When he knows that he has kept watch and laboured for mankind; that he has slept pure and that sleep has left him purer still; that whatever he thought, he thought as a friend of the gods, as their servant, as a sharer in the power of Zeus; and that on every occasion he is ready to say

	Lead me, Zeus, and you too, Destiny,

and also, if it so pleases the gods, so let it be — then why should he not have the confidence to speak freely to his own brothers, to his children, in a word to his own family?

That is why a man in this state of mind is neither over-inquisitive nor a busybody. He is not meddling with other men's affairs when he is watching over human affairs; he is looking after his own. Otherwise you might as well say that a general is a busybody when he inspects his soldiers and examines them and watches them and punishes the disorderly.

But if you rebuke other people while you have a cake under your arm, I shall say to you: will you not rather go off into a corner and eat what you have stolen? What have you to do with other men's affairs? Who are you? Are you the bull of the herd, or the queen of the bees? Show me the marks of your supremacy, such as they have from nature. And if you are a drone claiming sovereignty over the bees, do you not suppose your fellow citizens will put you down as the bees put down the drones?

The Cynic must also have such power of endurance that ordinary people take him for a stone with no feeling in it. Nobody insults him, nobody strikes him, nobody offends him: he gives his body over for any man who likes to do what he likes with it. For he keeps in mind that the inferior must be overpowered by the superior in whatever it is inferior in, and the body is inferior to the many, the weaker to the stronger. So he never goes down into a contest where he can be overpowered. He gives up at once what belongs to others; he lays no claim to what is servile.

But where will and the use of impressions are concerned, there you will see how many eyes he has — so many that you would call Argus blind beside him. Is his assent ever hasty? Is his impulse ever rash? Does his desire ever fail of its object? Does what he would avoid ever come upon him? Is his purpose ever unfulfilled? Does he ever find fault, is he ever humiliated, is he ever envious? To these he gives all his attention and energy; and as to everything else, he lies on his back and snores. All is peace. There is no robber who can take away his will, and no tyrant.

But what about his body? There is. And his property? There is. And offices and honours? What does he care about them? So when anybody tries to frighten him with these, he says: be off, look for children — masks are frightening to them; I know they are made of shell and there is nothing inside.

This is the sort of matter you are deliberating about. So, if you please, I urge you in God's name: put the thing off, and first consider whether you are prepared for it. Look at what Hector says to Andromache: go back into the house, he says, and weave —

	war is the work of men,
	of all men, but of me above all.
	— Iliad vi 490.

So he was conscious of what he was fit for, and knew what she was not.

Chapter 23: To Those Who Read and Lecture to Show Off

First tell yourself who you want to be, and then do what you are doing accordingly. We see this in nearly everything else. Men who take up athletics first settle what they want to be, and then do what follows. If a man is a long-distance runner there is a certain diet, a certain kind of walking, rubbing and exercise; if he is a sprinter, all of it is different; if he is a pentathlete, more different still. You will find the same in the crafts. If you are a carpenter you will have such and such things; if a metalworker, such and such.

Whatever we do, if we refer it to no end we shall be doing it for nothing; and if we refer it to the wrong end we shall miss the mark.

There is a general end and a particular one. First of all we must act as a man. What does that include? Not being like a sheep, gentle though it is; and not being like a wild beast, out to do harm. The particular end has to do with each person's way of life and his choice: the lute player acts as a lute player, the carpenter as a carpenter, the philosopher as a philosopher, the public speaker as a public speaker.

So when you say, "Come and hear me read," take care first that you are not doing it without a purpose; and then, if you find you are doing it for a purpose, consider whether it is the right one. Do you want to do good, or to be praised?

At once you hear him saying, "What is the praise of the crowd worth to me?" And he says it well — it is worth nothing to a musician as a musician, nor to a geometer.

So do you want to be useful? Useful in what? Tell us, so that we can come running to your lecture room. And can a man do anything useful for others who has not received something useful himself? No — any more than a man can do anything useful in carpentry unless he is a carpenter, or in shoemaking unless he is a shoemaker.

Do you want to know whether you have got any benefit, then? Produce your judgments, philosopher. What does desire promise? Not to fail of its object. What does avoidance promise? Not to fall into what it flees. Well — do we keep their promise? Tell me the truth; and if you lie, I shall tell you.

The other day, when your audience came in rather cold and did not applaud, you went away humiliated. And the other day, when you had been praised, you went round asking everybody, "What did you think of me?" — Marvellous, master, I swear by everything I hold dear. — "But how did I handle that particular passage?" — Which? — "The place where I described Pan and the nymphs." — Superb.

And you tell me that in desire and avoidance you are acting according to nature? Be off; try that on somebody else.

Did you not praise a certain man against your own opinion? Did you not flatter a certain senator's son? Would you want your own children to be like them? I hope not. Then why did you praise and flatter him? "He is a well-born young man and listens well to discourses." How so? "He admires me." There is your proof.

And what do you think? Do these very people not secretly despise you? When a man who knows perfectly well that he has never done any good and never thinks about it finds a philosopher saying, "You have great natural talent, and a frank and good disposition" — what else do you suppose he says to himself but "This man wants something from me"?

Or tell me: what act has he shown that indicates a great mind? Look — he has been in your company a long time, he has listened to your talks, he has heard you read. Has he become more modest? Has he been turned to reflect on himself? Has he seen what a bad state he is in? Has he thrown off his conceit? Is he looking for somebody to teach him?

He is. A man to teach him how to live? No, fool — how to talk. That is what he admires you for as well. Listen to what he says: "This man writes with perfect art, far better than Dion." That is a different thing altogether. Does he say, "This man is modest, trustworthy, undisturbed"? And even if he did say it, I would ask him: since this man is trustworthy, tell me what a trustworthy man is. And if he could not tell me, I would add: first understand what you are saying, and then say it.

So you, in your wretched state, gaping after applause and counting your audience — do you mean to be useful to other people? "Many more attended my lecture today." Yes, many; let us say five hundred. That is nothing; suppose there were a thousand. "Dion never had so many hearers." How could he? "And they understand what is said beautifully." What is fine, master, can move even a stone.

There — those are the words of a philosopher. That is the disposition of a man who is going to do others good. Here is a man who has listened to discourses, who has read what was written about Socrates as Socratic writing and not as compositions by Lysias and Isocrates. "'I have often wondered by what arguments...' No: by what argument. That is more exact than the other."

Have you read the words in any way different from the way you read little songs? If you read them as you ought, you would not have been attending to such matters. You would have been looking at these words instead: "Anytus and Meletus can kill me, but they cannot harm me." And: "I have always been the sort of man to pay attention to nothing of my own except the reasoning that on examination seems to me best."

So who ever heard Socrates say, "I know something and I teach it"? He used to send different men to different teachers. And so they used to come to him and ask him to introduce them to philosophers, and he would take them and recommend them.

No — instead, as he walked along with them, he would say: "Hear me lecture today at the house of Quadratus."

Why should I hear you? Do you want to show me that you put words together cleverly? You do put them together, man — and what good will it do you?

"Only praise me."

What do you mean by praising? "Say to me: admirable, wonderful." Very well, I say it. But if that is praise, then whatever it is philosophers mean by the name of good — what have I to praise in you? If speaking well is a good, teach me and I will praise you.

"What then — ought a man to listen to such things without pleasure?" I hope not. For my part I do not listen even to a lute player without pleasure. Must I stand and play the lute for that reason?

Hear what Socrates says: "Nor would it be seemly for a man of my age to come before you like a young man putting together speeches." Like a young man, he says. And in truth this little art is an elegant thing: to pick out words and put them together and come forward and read them or speak them gracefully, and while reading to say, "There are not many who can do this" — I swear by everything you hold dear.

Does a philosopher invite people to hear him? As the sun draws men to it, or as food does, does the philosopher not draw those who will get some benefit? What doctor invites a man to be treated by him? Though I do hear that these days even the doctors in Rome invite patients; when I lived there, doctors were invited.

"I invite you to come and hear that things are in a bad way with you; that you take care of everything except what you ought to take care of; that you do not know good from bad and are unlucky and unhappy." A fine sort of invitation. And yet if the philosopher's words do not have that effect on you, he is dead, and so is the speaker.

Rufus used to say: if you have leisure to praise me, I am talking to no purpose. And in fact he used to talk in such a way that every one of us sitting there supposed somebody had denounced him to Rufus — so exactly did he touch on what we were doing, so completely did he put every man's faults before his eyes.

Gentlemen, a philosopher's school is a surgery. You ought not to leave it feeling pleasure, but feeling pain. You are not in good health when you come in: one man has a dislocated shoulder, another an abscess, a third a fistula, a fourth a headache.

And am I to sit and utter neat little thoughts and exclamations so that you may praise me and go away — one with his shoulder in the same state it came in, another with his head still aching, a third with his fistula or his abscess exactly as they were? Is this what young men are to leave home for, and leave their parents and friends and family and property, so that they can say "Wonderful!" while you are making your exclamations? Was this what Socrates did, or Zeno, or Cleanthes?

"What then — is there no such thing as the style of exhortation?" Who denies it? As there is the style of refutation and the style of instruction. But who ever counted a fourth style beside them, the style of display?

What is the style of exhortation? Being able to show a man, or many men, the struggle they are actually in, and that they are thinking about anything rather than what they really want. They want the things that lead to happiness and they look for them in the wrong place.

And in order to do that, a thousand seats have to be set out and men invited to listen, and you have to go up into the pulpit in a fine robe and describe the death of Achilles. Stop, I beg you by the gods, spoiling good words and good acts as far as you possibly can.

Nothing has more power in exhortation than a speaker showing his hearers that he needs them. But tell me: who, hearing you read or lecture, is anxious about himself, or turns to reflect on himself? Who, going out, says, "The philosopher hit me there; I must stop doing these things"? Does he not rather say to somebody — even if you have a great reputation — "He spoke finely about Xerxes"? And another says, "No, about the battle of Thermopylae." Is this what listening to a philosopher means?

Chapter 24: Why We Should Not Be Moved by Longing for What Is Not Up to Us

Do not let what is against nature in another man be an evil to you. You were not made by nature to be depressed along with others, or unhappy along with them, but to be happy along with them.

If a man is unhappy, remember that his unhappiness is his own fault. God made all men to be happy and undisturbed, and gave them the means: certain things belonging to each as his own, and other things not as his own. The things subject to blocking and compulsion and loss are not a man's own; the things not subject to blocking are. And the nature of good and evil — as was fitting for one who takes care of us and protects us like a father — he made our own.

"But I have parted from somebody, and he is grieving."

Why did he count as his own what belongs to another? Why, when he looked at you and was glad, did he not also reckon that you are mortal, and that it is natural for you to leave him for another country? So he is suffering the consequences of his own folly.

And why do you bewail yourself, and what for? Because you also gave no thought to these things? Like worthless women, you enjoyed everything you took pleasure in as if you would enjoy it forever — places, and men, and conversation — and now you sit weeping because you do not see the same people and do not live in the same places.

Indeed you deserve it: to be more wretched than crows and ravens, which can fly where they please and change their nests and cross the seas without lamenting or pining for their old condition.

"Yes, but that is because they are creatures without reason."

Was reason given us by the gods, then, for the purpose of unhappiness and misery, so that we might spend our lives in wretchedness and lamentation? Must everybody be immortal, and nobody go abroad, and must we ourselves never travel but stay rooted like plants? And if one of our friends goes abroad, must we sit and weep, and when he comes back must we dance and clap our hands like children?

Shall we not wean ourselves at last and remember what we heard from the philosophers — if we did not listen to them as though they were street performers? They tell us that this world is one city, and that the substance it was formed out of is one; that there must be a certain cycle; that some things must give way to others, some be dissolved and others come in their place, some stay where they are and others be moved; and that everything is full of friendship — first of the gods, and then of men, who are made by nature to be one family. Some must be together and others must be separated, rejoicing in those who are with them and not grieving for those who have been taken away.

And a man, besides being naturally of a noble temper and having contempt for everything outside the power of his will, also has this property: not to be rooted, not to be naturally fixed to the earth, but to go at different times to different places — sometimes because some occasion presses, and sometimes simply for the sake of seeing.

That was how it was with Odysseus, who saw

	the cities of many men, and learned their ways.

And earlier still it fell to Hercules to visit the whole inhabited world,

	seeing men's lawless deeds and their good government,

throwing out the lawlessness and clearing it away and putting good government in its place. And how many friends do you suppose he had in Thebes, how many in Argos, how many in Athens? How many do you think he made as he went about? And he married too, when it seemed to him the right occasion, and had children, and left them without lamenting or pining or feeling he was leaving them orphans — because he knew that no man is an orphan; it is the father who takes care of all men, always and without a break.

It was not as mere hearsay that he had heard Zeus is the father of men: he thought Zeus was his own father, and called him so, and looked to him in everything he did. And so he was able to live happily wherever he was.

It is never possible for happiness and a longing for what is absent to come together. What is happy must have everything it desires; it must be like a man who has eaten his fill, with neither thirst nor hunger.

"But Odysseus felt a longing for his wife and wept as he sat on a rock."

Do you take Homer and his stories as authority in everything? And if Odysseus really wept, what was he but an unhappy man? And what good man is unhappy? In truth the whole is badly administered if Zeus does not take care of his own citizens so that they may be happy as he is. But it is neither lawful nor right to think such things. And if Odysseus did weep and lament, he was not a good man.

For who is good if he does not know who he is? And who knows what he is, if he forgets that the things that have been made are perishable, and that it is not possible for one human being to be with another always? To desire the impossible, then, is the mark of a slavish character, and it is foolish: it is the mark of a stranger, a man fighting against God in the only way he can, by his opinions.

"But my mother laments when she does not see me."

Why has she not learned these principles? And I do not say we should not take care that she does not lament; I say we ought not to want what is not our own, by every means.

Another man's grief is his own; my grief is my own. So I will stop my own grief by every means, because it is in my power; and another man's grief I will try to stop as far as I can, but I will not attempt it by every means. If I do, I shall be fighting against God, opposing Zeus, setting myself against him in the administration of the universe. And the penalty for this fighting against God and this disobedience will be paid not only by my children's children but by me — startled by dreams by night and by day, thrown about, trembling at every piece of news, with my peace of mind depending on other men's letters.

"Somebody has arrived from Rome. I only hope nothing has gone wrong." But what harm can come to you where you are not? "Somebody has come from Greece; I hope nothing has gone wrong." In this way every place on earth can be a source of misfortune to you. Is it not enough to be unfortunate where you are, without being unfortunate across the sea as well, and by letter? Is this what having your affairs in a state of security looks like?

"Well then, suppose my friends have died in places far from me."

What have they suffered but the condition of mortals? Or how can you want at the same time to live to old age and never see the death of anybody you love? Do you not know that over a long stretch of time many different things must happen — that a fever will carry off one man, a robber another, a tyrant a third? Such is the condition of things around us, and such are the people who live in the world with us. Cold and heat, unsuitable diets, journeys by land and voyages by sea, winds, all the various circumstances round us — these destroy one man, exile another, throw one into an embassy and another into an army.

So sit there in a flutter over all of it, lamenting, unhappy, unlucky, dependent on somebody else — and not on one or two, but on tens of thousands.

Is this what you heard when you were with the philosophers? Is this what you learned? Do you not know that human life is a campaign; that one man must stand watch, another go out as a scout, and a third fight? It is not possible for everybody to be in one place, and it would not be better if they were.

But you neglect to carry out the general's orders and complain when something harder than usual is put on you — and you do not notice what you are turning the army into, as far as it is in your power: if everybody copied you, nobody would dig a trench, nobody would throw up a rampart, nobody would keep watch or expose himself to danger, and everyone would turn out useless for the purposes of an army.

Again: if you go to sea as a sailor, keep to one spot and stick to it. If you are told to climb the mast, refuse; if to run to the bow, refuse. What ship's master will put up with you? Will he not throw you overboard as a useless thing, an obstruction and a bad example to the other sailors?

So it is here too. Every man's life is a kind of campaign, and it is a long one and full of variety. You must do a soldier's duty and do everything at the general's nod — guessing his wishes if possible, since there is no resemblance between that general and this one, either in strength or in quality of character.

You are placed in a great command, not in a mean position; you are always a senator. Do you not know that such a man must give little time to his household affairs and be often away from home — as a governor or as one governed, or discharging some office, or serving in war, or acting as a judge? And then do you tell me you want to be fixed to the same places and rooted, like a plant?

"Yes, because it is pleasant." Who says it is not? But soup is pleasant, and a handsome woman is pleasant. What else do the men who make pleasure their end say? Do you not see whose language you have been using — that it is the language of Epicureans and of men who have lost all shame about sex? And then, while you are doing what they do and holding their opinions, do you speak the words of Zeno and Socrates to us? Will you not throw away, as far as you can, the things belonging to other men that you decorate yourself with, when they do not fit you at all?

What else do such men want than to sleep without being blocked or compelled; and, having got up, to yawn at their leisure, and wash their faces, and then write and read what they like, and then talk about some trifle and be praised by their friends whatever they say; then to go out for a walk, and having walked a little to take a bath, and then eat, and then sleep — the kind of sleep such men have? Why need I say how? It is easy enough to guess.

Come — tell me your own way of passing the time, the one you want, you who are an admirer of truth and of Socrates and Diogenes. What do you want to do in Athens? The same as everybody else, or something different? Then why do you call yourself a Stoic?

Well: men who falsely claim to be Roman citizens are severely punished, and are men who falsely claim so great and venerable a thing and name to get off unpunished? Or is that not possible, and is this the divine and strong and unavoidable law — the one that exacts the heaviest punishments from those who commit the greatest crimes?

And what does this law say? Let the man who lays claim to things that do not belong to him be a boaster and a vain man. Let the man who disobeys the divine administration be base and a slave. Let him grieve, let him be envious, let him pity; and in a word, let him be unhappy and let him lament.

"Well then — do you want me to pay court to a certain man? To go to his door?"

If reason requires it for the sake of your country, for your family, for mankind, why should you not go? You are not ashamed to go to a shoemaker's door when you need shoes, or a gardener's when you want lettuces; and are you ashamed to go to a rich man's door when you want something?

"Yes, because I stand in no awe of a shoemaker." Then stand in no awe of the rich. "Nor will I flatter the gardener." Then do not flatter the rich either.

"How am I to get what I want, then?"

Do I tell you to go as though you were certain to get it? Do I not tell you only that you may do what becomes you?

"Then why should I go at all?"

So that you shall have gone; so that you shall have discharged the duty of a citizen, of a brother, of a friend. And remember besides that you have gone to a shoemaker, to a seller of vegetables, men who have no power over anything great or noble, however dear they sell.

You go to buy lettuces: they cost a penny, not a fortune. So it is here as well. The matter is worth going to the rich man's door for — very well, I will go. It is worth talking about — very well, I will talk with him. But you must kiss his hand as well and flatter him with praise. Away with that: that costs a fortune. It is no profit to me, nor to the state, nor to my friends, to have done the thing that ruins a good citizen and a good friend.

"But you will look as though you were not keen about the business, if you do not succeed."

Have you forgotten again why you went? Do you not know that a good man does nothing for the sake of appearances, but for the sake of doing right?

"And what advantage is it to him to have done right?"

And what advantage is it to a man writing the name Dion to write it as he ought? The advantage is to have written it.

"Is there no reward, then?"

Do you look for a greater reward for a good man than doing what is good and just? At Olympia you want nothing more; it seems enough to you to be crowned at the games. Does being good and happy seem to you so small and worthless a thing?

The gods have brought you into this city for these purposes, and it is now your duty to take up a man's work — and do you still want nurses and a mother? Do foolish women move you with their weeping and make you soft? Will you never stop being a foolish child? Do you not know that a man who behaves like a child is all the more ridiculous the older he gets?

In Athens did you see nobody by going to his house? "I visited any man I pleased." Here too be ready to see people, and you will see whom you please — only let it be without meanness, and without desire and without avoidance, and your affairs will go well. But that result does not depend on going, or on standing at doors: it depends on what is inside, on your judgments.

When you have learned not to value things that are external and not dependent on the will, and to consider that not one of them is your own — that these things only are your own, to judge well, to form opinions, to move toward an object, to desire, to turn away — where is there room left for flattery, and where for meanness?

Why do you still long for the quiet there, and for the places you are used to? Wait a little and you will find these places familiar too. And then, if you have so ignoble a nature, when you leave these as well, weep and lament again.

"How am I to become an affectionate man, then?"

By being of a noble disposition, and happy. It is not reasonable to be mean-spirited, or to bewail yourself, or to depend on somebody else, or ever to blame God or man. Become an affectionate man that way, I beg you, by keeping these rules. But if through this affection, as you call it, you are going to be a slave and wretched, then there is no profit in being affectionate.

And what stops you loving another person as one who is subject to death, as one who may go away from you? Did Socrates not love his own children? He did — but as a free man, as one who remembered that he must first be a friend to the gods. That is why he violated nothing that becomes a good man, neither in making his defence nor in proposing his own penalty, nor earlier in his life when he was a councillor or a soldier.

But we are fully supplied with excuses for a mean temper — one man for a child's sake, another for a mother's, another for his brothers'. It is not fitting for us to be unhappy on anybody's account; we should be happy on everybody's account, and chiefly on God's, who made us for this end.

Well then — did Diogenes love nobody, who was so kind and so much a lover of mankind that he willingly took on so much labour and bodily suffering for mankind in general? He did love mankind — but how? As a servant of God should: caring for men and at the same time being subject to God. That is why the whole earth was his country and no particular place; and when he was taken prisoner he did not pine for Athens or his friends and companions there, but even became familiar with the pirates and tried to improve them. And when he was sold afterwards he lived in Corinth as he had lived in Athens; and he would have behaved just the same if he had gone to the country of the Perrhaebi.

This is how freedom is got. That is why he used to say: ever since Antisthenes set me free, I have not been a slave.

How did Antisthenes set him free? Hear what he says. Antisthenes taught me what is my own and what is not. Possessions are not my own, nor family, nor servants, nor friends, nor reputation, nor familiar places, nor my way of life; all of these belong to others.

What is your own, then? The use of impressions. He showed me that I have this free of blocking and free of compulsion; nobody can put an obstacle in my way, nobody can force me to use impressions otherwise than I wish. Who has any power over me, then? Philip, or Alexander, or Perdiccas, or the great king? How should they have it?

For if a man is going to be overpowered by a man, he must long before have been overpowered by things. So if pleasure cannot subdue a man, nor pain, nor fame, nor wealth, and if he is able whenever he chooses to spit out his whole poor body in a man's face and leave life — whose slave can he still be?

But if he had lived in Athens with pleasure and been overpowered by that way of living, his affairs would have been at everybody's command; the stronger man would have had the power to grieve him. How do you suppose Diogenes would have flattered the pirates to make them sell him to some Athenian, so that he might one day see that beautiful Piraeus, and the Long Walls, and the Acropolis?

In what condition would you see them? As a captive, a slave, a mean creature — and what use would that be to you?

"Not so: I should see them as a free man."

Show me how you would be free. Look: somebody has caught you and is leading you away from the place you are used to living in, and says, "You are my slave, because it is in my power to stop you living as you please. It is in my power to treat you gently or to humble you, whenever I choose." And on the other side you are cheerful and go off to Athens elated.

What do you say to the man who treats you as a slave? What means have you of finding somebody to rescue you from slavery? Or can you not even look him in the face, but without another word beg to be let go?

Man, you ought to go to prison gladly, hurrying, going ahead of the men who are taking you there. And then, I ask you, are you unwilling to live in Rome, and do you long to live in Greece? And when you have to die, will you fill our ears with your lamentations then too, because you will not see Athens and will not walk about in the Lyceum?

Is this what you went abroad for? Was this why you looked for somebody you might get some benefit from? What benefit? So that you could resolve syllogisms more readily, or handle hypothetical arguments? And was it for this that you left brother, country, friends, family — to come back having learned these things?

So you did not go abroad to get steadiness of mind, or freedom from being thrown about; not so that, being safe from harm, you might never complain of anybody, or accuse anybody, and nobody could wrong you, and you might keep up your relations to other men unblocked?

This is a fine line of trade you have gone abroad for — syllogisms and trick arguments and hypotheticals. If you like, take a place in the marketplace and cry them for sale like a drug seller. Will you not deny even everything you have learned, so as not to give your theorems a bad name as useless?

What harm has philosophy done you? How has Chrysippus injured you, that you should prove by your acts that his labours were useless? Were the evils you had at home not enough — the ones that were the cause of your pain and lamentation even if you had never gone abroad? Have you added more to the list? And if you make other acquaintances and friends, you will have more causes for lamentation; and the same if you grow fond of another country.

Why do you live to heap sorrow upon sorrow round yourself, and be unhappy through them? And then, I ask you, do you call this affection? What affection, man! If it is a good thing, it is the cause of no evil; and if it is a bad thing, I want nothing to do with it. I am made by nature for my own good; I am not made for my own harm.

What is the training for this, then? The first and chief one, standing as it were in the doorway, is this: when you are delighted by anything, be delighted with it as with a thing that can be taken away — as with an earthenware pot, or a glass cup, so that when it is broken you may remember what it was and not be upset.

So here too. When you kiss your own child, or your brother, or your friend, never give the impression free rein and never let your pleasure go as far as it likes. Check it and curb it, as the men who stand behind generals in their triumphs do, reminding them that they are mortal. Remind yourself in the same way that the one you love is mortal, and that what you love is nothing of your own. It has been given to you for the present, not so that it cannot be taken away, and not for all time, but as a fig is given, or a bunch of grapes, at its appointed season of the year. If you want them in winter you are a fool. So if you want your son or your friend when it is not allowed to you, you must know you are wanting a fig in winter.

For what winter is to a fig, every event that comes from the universe is to the things it takes away in the course of nature.

And further: at the times when you are delighted with a thing, put the contrary impressions in front of yourself. What harm is there, while you are kissing your child, in saying under your breath, "Tomorrow you will die"? And to a friend, "Tomorrow you will go away, or I shall, and we shall never see each other again"?

"But these are words of ill omen."

Some charms are of ill omen too; and because they are useful, I do not care about that — only let them be useful. Do you call anything a bad omen except what signifies some evil? Cowardice is a word of ill omen, and meanness of spirit, and sorrow, and grief, and shamelessness. Those are words of ill omen; and even so we ought not to hesitate to say them, in order to protect ourselves against the things.

Do you tell me that a word signifying something natural is of evil omen? Then say that the reaping of the ears of corn is of evil omen, since it signifies the destruction of the ears — though not of the world. Say that the falling of the leaves is of ill omen, and the dried fig taking the place of the green one, and grapes being turned into raisins. All these are changes from a former state into other states: not destruction, but a certain fixed ordering and administration.

So is going abroad: a small change. So is death: a greater change, not from what is now to what is not, but to what is not now.

"Shall I no longer exist, then?"

You will not exist, but you will be something else, of which the world now has need. For you came into existence not when you chose, but when the world had need of you.

And so the wise and good man, remembering who he is and where he came from and by whom he was made, attends only to this: how he may fill his place in good order and obediently to God.

Do you still want me to exist? Then I will go on existing as a free man, as a man noble by nature, as you wanted me to exist — for you made me unblocked in what is my own. But have you no further need of me? I thank you. I have stayed until now for your sake and for nobody else's, and now in obedience to you I depart.

How do you depart? Again, as you have pleased: as a free man, as your servant, as one who has known your commands and your prohibitions.

And as long as I stay in your service, what do you want me to be — a prince or a private man, a senator or an ordinary person, a soldier or a general, a teacher or the head of a household? Whatever place and position you assign me, I will die ten thousand times, as Socrates says, rather than desert it.

And where do you want me to be? In Rome, or Athens, or Thebes, or Gyara? Only remember me there, where I am. If you send me to a place where there are no means for a man to live according to nature, I shall not leave life in disobedience to you, but as though you had given me the signal to retreat. I do not leave you — far be that from my intention — but I see that you have no further need of me. And if the means of living according to nature are allowed me, I will look for no other place than the one I am in, and no other men than the ones I am among.

Let these thoughts be ready to hand night and day. These you should write; these you should read; about these you should talk to yourself and to others. Ask a man, "Can you help me at all toward this?" And then go to another, and another.

Then if something happens against your wish, this reflection will relieve you at once: that it was not unexpected. It is a great thing in every case to be able to say, "I knew that the son I fathered was mortal." So you will also say: I knew that I am mortal; I knew that I may leave my home; I knew that I may be thrown out of it; I knew that I may be taken off to prison.

And then, if you turn round and look at yourself and look for the quarter the thing came from, you will remember at once that it comes from the region of things outside the power of the will, things that are not my own. So what is it to me?

Then you will ask — and this is the chief thing — and who was it that sent it? The commander, the general, the state, the law of the state. Give it to me, then; I must always obey the law in everything.

And when the impression gives you pain — for it is not in your power to prevent that — fight against it with the help of reason. Beat it. Do not let it gain strength, and do not let it lead you on to the consequences by raising whatever images it pleases and however it pleases.

If you are on Gyara, do not picture the way of living at Rome, and how many pleasures there were for a man living there and how many there would be if he went back. Fix your mind on this instead: how a man who lives on Gyara ought to live on Gyara, like a man of courage. And if you are in Rome, do not picture life in Athens; think only about life in Rome.

Then, in place of every other delight, put this one: being conscious that you are obeying God; that not in word but in fact you are performing the acts of a wise and good man.

What a thing it is for a man to be able to say to himself: whatever the rest of them may be saying solemnly in the schools, and may be thought to be saying in a strange way, this I am doing. They are sitting and discoursing about my virtues, and enquiring about me, and praising me — and Zeus has willed that I should get the proof of it from myself, and should know for myself whether he has the soldier he ought to have, the citizen he ought to have; and whether he has chosen to produce me before the rest of mankind as a witness of the things that lie outside the will.

See that you fear without reason. See that you foolishly desire what you desire. Do not look for the good in externals; look for it in yourselves — and if you do not, you will not find it.

For this purpose he leads me here at one time and sends me there at another; shows me to men as poor, without authority, and sick; sends me to Gyara, takes me into prison — not because he hates me, far be such a thought from him, for who hates the best of his servants? nor because he does not care about me, for he neglects not even the smallest things — but in order to train me and to use me as a witness to others.

Appointed to such a service, do I still care what place I am in, or whom I am with, or what men say about me? Do I not turn my thoughts entirely to God and to his instructions and commands?

Keep these things always to hand, and exercise them on your own, and hold them in readiness, and you will never be in want of somebody to comfort and strengthen you. For it is not shameful to have nothing to eat; it is shameful not to have reason enough to keep off fear and sorrow.

And if you once win exemption from sorrow and fear, will there be any tyrant for you any longer, or any tyrant's guard, or any of Caesar's attendants? Will any appointment to office at court cause you pain? Will the men who sacrifice on the Capitol on being named to certain functions cause any pain to you, who have received so great an authority from Zeus?

Only do not make a proud display of it, and do not boast of it; show it by your acts. And if nobody notices, be satisfied that you yourself are in a healthy state, and happy.

Chapter 25: To Those Who Give Up

Look at the things you set out to do at the start: which you have secured and which you have not; and how pleased you are when you remember the one and how pained about the other. And if it is possible, recover the things you failed at.

For we must not flinch when we are engaged in the greatest of contests; we must take the blows. The contest before us is not in wrestling or in the all-in fight, where both the winner and the loser may have great merit or very little, and may in truth be very lucky or very unlucky. Our contest is for good fortune and happiness themselves.

Well then — even if we have given up the contest for those, nothing stops us starting the fight again. We are not forced to wait another four years for the games at Olympia to come round. As soon as you have recovered and pulled yourself together and put the same keenness into it, you may take up the fight again; and if you give up again, you may take it up again. And if you once win, you are like a man who never gave up at all.

Only do not, out of the habit of giving up, begin to give up with pleasure — and then, like a bad athlete, go the whole circuit of the games after being beaten, like quails running away.

"The sight of a beautiful young girl overpowers me. Well — have I not been overpowered before? An inclination comes on me to find fault with somebody; and have I not found fault before?"

You talk to us as though you had come out of these things unharmed — just as a man might say to the doctor who forbids him to bathe, "Have I not bathed before?" And the doctor can say to him: well, and what happened to you after the bath? Did you not have a fever, did you not have a headache?

And when you found fault with somebody the other day, were you not doing the act of a spiteful man, of a trifling chatterer? Did you not feed the habit in yourself by adding the corresponding acts to it? And when you were overpowered by the girl, did you come off unharmed?

So why do you talk about what you did before? You ought, I think, to remember what you did as slaves remember the blows they have taken, and to keep off the same faults.

But the two cases are not alike. With slaves it is the pain that causes the memory; and with your faults, what is the pain, what is the punishment? When have you ever been in the habit of running from bad acts? Then sufferings of the testing kind are useful to us, whether we choose them or not.

Chapter 26: To Those Who Are Afraid of Being Poor

Are you not ashamed to be more cowardly and meaner than runaway slaves? How do they leave their masters when they run? What estates do they count on, what servants do they rely on? Do they not steal a little, enough for the first few days, and after that make their way over land or sea, contriving one method after another of keeping themselves alive? And what runaway slave ever died of hunger?

But you are afraid that necessities may fail you, and you lie awake at night over it. Wretch, are you so blind that you cannot see the road that the want of necessities leads to?

"Well, where does it lead?"

To the same place a fever leads, or a stone falling on you: to death. Have you not said this yourself to your companions, often? Have you not read a great deal of this kind, and written a great deal? And how often have you boasted that you were easy about death?

"Yes — but my wife and children will go hungry too."

Well then, does their hunger lead anywhere else? Is there not the same descent to the same place for them? Is there not the same condition down there for them? Will you not choose to look toward that place, full of boldness against every want and shortage — the place the richest men and the men who have held the highest offices, and kings themselves, and tyrants, all have to go down to? You will go down hungry, if it happens that way; they will go down burst with indigestion and drink.

How rarely have you seen a beggar who was not an old man, and of extreme old age at that? Chilled with cold night and day, lying on the ground, eating only what is absolutely necessary — and they come close to being unable to die at all.

Can you not write? Can you not look after children? Can you not be a doorkeeper at somebody else's house?

"But it is shameful to come to such a necessity."

Then learn first what things are shameful, and after that tell us you are a philosopher. For the present, do not allow it even if somebody else calls you one.

Is a thing shameful to you when it is not your own act, when you are not the cause of it, when it has come to you by accident, like a headache or a fever? If your parents were poor and left their property to others, and if while they live they do not help you at all, is that shameful to you? Is this what you learned from the philosophers?

Did you never hear that what is shameful is what deserves blame, and that what deserves blame is worth blaming? Whom do you blame for an act that is not his, that he did not do himself? Did you make your father what he is? Is it in your power to improve him? Is that power given to you? Well then — ought you to want things that are not given to you, or to be ashamed if you do not get them?

And in studying philosophy, have you got into the habit of looking to others and hoping for nothing from yourself? Then lament and groan, and eat in fear that you may have no food tomorrow. Tremble about your wretched slaves, in case they steal, in case they run away, in case they die.

Live that way and go on living that way, you who have come near philosophy in name only, and have disgraced its theorems as far as you can by showing them to be useless and unprofitable to the men who take them up. You have never looked for steadiness, or freedom from being thrown about, or freedom from the passions. You have never sought out anybody for that purpose, though you have sought out plenty for syllogisms. You have never thoroughly examined any of these impressions on your own: am I able to bear this, or am I not? What is left for me to do?

Instead, as though all your affairs were secure and in order, you have settled on the third topic, the one about things staying unchanged — so that you may possess unchanged what? Cowardice, meanness of spirit, admiration of the rich, desire that reaches nothing, and avoidance that fails in the attempt. These are the things you have been anxious to make secure.

Ought you not first to have gained something from reason, and then made that secure? Whom did you ever see building a parapet all round and not putting a wall under it? What doorkeeper is stationed to guard where there is no door?

But you practise so as to be able to prove — what? You practise so that you may not be tossed about as at sea by sophisms. Tossed about away from what? Show me first what you are holding, what you are measuring, what you are weighing; and show me the scales, or the measure. Or how long will you go on measuring dust?

Ought you not to be demonstrating the things that make men happy, that make things go for them as they wish, and that show us why we should blame nobody, accuse nobody, and accept the administration of the universe? Show me those.

"See, I show them: I will resolve syllogisms for you."

That is the measure, slave — it is not the thing measured.

So now you are paying the penalty for what you neglected, philosophy. You tremble, you lie awake, you take advice from everybody; and if your deliberations are not likely to please everybody, you think you have deliberated badly.

Then you fear hunger, as you suppose. But it is not hunger you fear. You are afraid that you will not have a cook, that you will not have a second man to buy provisions for the table, a third to take off your shoes, a fourth to dress you, others to rub you down and follow you about — so that in the bath, when you have taken your clothes off and stretched yourself out like a man being crucified, you may be rubbed on this side and on that, and the attendant may say to the slave, "Move him; give me his side; take his head; show me the shoulder." And then, when you have left the bath and gone home, you may call out, "Is nobody bringing anything to eat?" and then, "Take the tables away; wipe them down."

This is what you are afraid of: that you may not be able to lead the life of a sick man.

Learn instead the life of men who are in health: how slaves live, how labourers live, how genuine philosophers live; how Socrates lived, who had a wife and children; how Diogenes lived; how Cleanthes lived, who ran the school and drew water. If you choose to have these things, you will have them everywhere, and you will live in complete confidence.

Confidence in what? In the only thing a man can be confident in: what is secure, what cannot be blocked, what cannot be taken away — that is, your own will.

And why have you made yourself so useless and good for nothing that nobody will choose to take you into his house or take care of you? If a whole and useful pot were thrown out, any man who found it would pick it up and count it a gain. Nobody will pick you up; every man will count you a loss. So can you not do the work even of a dog, or of a cock? Then why do you choose to go on living, being what you are?

Does any good man fear he will run out of food? It does not run out for the blind, or for the lame: will it run out for a good man? A good soldier does not lack somebody to pay him, nor does a labourer, nor a shoemaker: and will the good man lack such a person?

Does God so neglect the things he established — his servants, his witnesses, the only men he uses as examples to the untaught, to show that he exists and administers the whole well and does not neglect human affairs, and that for a good man there is no evil, living or dead?

And what about when he does not supply him with food? What else is he doing but giving me, like a good general, the signal to retreat? I obey. I follow, agreeing with the commander's words and praising his acts. I came when it pleased him and I shall go when it pleases him; and while I lived, my duty was to praise God, both by myself and to each man and to many.

He does not supply me with much, or with plenty; he does not want me to live in luxury. He did not supply Hercules either, who was his own son: somebody else was king of Argos and Mycenae, and Hercules obeyed orders and laboured and was trained. And Eurystheus was what he was, king neither of Argos nor of Mycenae, since he was not even king of himself; while Hercules was ruler and leader of the whole earth and sea, who cleared away lawlessness and brought in justice and holiness — and he did it naked and alone.

And when Odysseus was cast ashore from the shipwreck, did want humiliate him? Did it break his spirit? How did he go off to the girls to ask for necessities — begging, which is thought the most shameful thing of all?

	Like a lion bred in the mountains, trusting in his strength.
	— Odyssey vi 130.

Trusting in what? Not in reputation, not in wealth, not in the power of an office, but in his own strength — that is, in his judgments about what is in our power and what is not. These are the only things that make men free, that let them escape being blocked, that lift the heads of men who are cast down, that let them look at rich men and tyrants with a steady eye.

And this was the gift given to the philosopher. But you will not come out bold; you will come out trembling for your paltry clothes and your silver plate. Unhappy man — have you wasted your time until now like this?

"And what if I fall ill?"

You will be ill in the way you ought to be.

"Who will look after me?"

God. Your friends.

"I shall lie on a hard bed."

But you will lie on it like a man.

"I shall not have a comfortable room."

Then you will be ill in an uncomfortable one.

"Who will provide the food I need?"

The people who provide it for others. You will be ill as Manes is ill.

And what will the end of the illness be? Anything but death?

Then will you not consider that this — not death, but the fear of death — is the chief of all evils for a man, and the chief mark of a mean spirit and of cowardice? Train yourself against that fear, then; that is my advice. Let all your reasoning tend that way, and your exercises, and your reading. And you will know that this, and only this, is how men are made free.

Book Four

Chapter 1: On Freedom

He is free who lives as he wishes to live; who is subject to no compulsion, no blocking, no force; whose impulses to action are unobstructed, whose desires reach their object, and who does not fall into what he would avoid.

Who chooses to live in error? Nobody. Who chooses to live deceived, liable to mistake, unjust, uncontrolled, discontented, mean? Nobody. So not one bad man lives as he wishes, and therefore not one of them is free.

And who chooses to live in grief, in fear, in envy, in pity, desiring and failing of his desires, trying to avoid something and falling into it? Not one. Then do we find any bad man free of grief and free of fear, who does not fall into what he would avoid and does get what he wants? Not one. So we find no bad man free.

Now if a man who has twice been consul hears this, and you add, "But you are a wise man; this has nothing to do with you," he will forgive you. But if you tell him the truth and say, "As far as not being a slave goes, you differ in nothing from the men who have been sold three times over," what else should you expect but blows?

"What," he says, "I a slave? My father was free, my mother was free. Nobody can buy me. I am of senatorial rank besides, and a friend of Caesar, and I have been consul, and I own a great many slaves."

In the first place, most excellent senator, perhaps your father was a slave in the same kind of servitude, and your mother, and your grandfather, and all your ancestors in a rising line. But even if they were as free as it is possible to be, what is that to you? What if they were of a noble nature and you of a mean one; if they were fearless and you a coward; if they had the power of self-control and you cannot exercise it?

"And what," you may say, "has that to do with being a slave?"

Does it seem to you nothing to do a thing unwillingly, under compulsion, with groans — has that nothing to do with being a slave?

"It is something," you say. "But who is able to compel me, except the master of us all, Caesar?"

Then you have admitted yourself that you have one master. And do not let it console you in the least that he is, as you say, the common master of all; understand instead that you are a slave in a great household. This is how the people of Nicopolis are in the habit of shouting, "By the fortune of Caesar, we are free."

However, let us not speak about Caesar for the present, if you please. Tell me this instead: did you never love anybody, a young girl, or a slave, or a free woman?

"And what has this to do with being a slave or free?"

Were you never ordered by the person you loved to do something you did not want to do? Have you never flattered your little slave girl? Have you never kissed her feet? And yet if a man forced you to kiss Caesar's feet you would think it an insult and outrageous tyranny. So what else is slavery?

Did you never go out at night to a place you did not want to go to? Did you never spend what you did not want to spend? Did you never say things with sighs and groans, and put up with abuse, and with being shut out?

And if you are ashamed to admit your own acts, look at what Thrasonides says and does — a man who has seen more military service than perhaps even you have. First of all he goes out at night, when Geta the slave does not dare go out, and would have made a great outcry if his master had forced him, and gone out lamenting his bitter slavery. And then, what does Thrasonides say? "A worthless girl has enslaved me, whom no enemy ever enslaved."

Unhappy man, to be the slave even of a girl, and a worthless one. Why do you still call yourself free, then? Why do you talk about your service in the army? Then he calls for a sword and is angry with the man who kindly refuses to give it to him; and he sends presents to the woman who hates him, and begs and weeps; and then, having had a little success, he is elated. But even then — was he free enough neither to desire nor to fear?

Now consider how we use the notion of freedom with animals. Men keep tame lions shut up and feed them, and some take them about; and who will say that such a lion is free? Is it not the case that the more comfortably he lives, the more slavish his condition? And who, if he had perception and reason, would want to be one of those lions?

And these birds, when they are caught and shut up — how much do they suffer trying to escape? Some of them starve to death rather than put up with such a life; and those that live, barely live and waste away in suffering; and if they ever find an opening, out they go. So strongly do they want their natural liberty, to be independent and unblocked.

And what harm is there to you in it? What do you say? I am made by nature to fly where I choose, to live in the open air, to sing when I choose; you take all this from me, and then ask what harm it is to me.

For this reason we shall say that only those animals are free which cannot bear captivity, but escape it by dying as soon as they are caught. So Diogenes says somewhere that there is only one road to freedom, and that is to die content. And he writes to the king of Persia: "You cannot enslave the Athenian state any more than you can enslave fish."

How is that? Can I not catch them?

"If you catch them," says Diogenes, "they will leave you at once, as fish do; catch a fish and it dies. And if these men, once caught, are going to die, what use to you is all your preparation for war?"

These are the words of a free man who had examined the matter carefully and, naturally enough, had found the answer. But if you look for it somewhere other than where it is, what wonder if you never find it?

The slave wants to be set free at once. Why? Do you suppose he wants to pay the manumission tax to the collectors? No — but because he imagines that up to now, not having got this, he is blocked and unlucky. "Once I am set free, everything is happiness at once. I take no notice of anybody. I speak to everybody as an equal and a like. I go where I choose, I come from where I choose, I go where I choose."

Then he is set free — and at once, having nowhere to eat, he looks for somebody to flatter, somebody to have dinner with. Then he either works with his body and endures the most dreadful things, and if he can get a manger to feed at he falls into a slavery much worse than his old one; or, even if he becomes rich, being a man with no knowledge of what is good, he falls in love with some little girl and laments in his unhappiness and longs to be a slave again.

"What evil did I suffer as a slave? Somebody else clothed me, somebody else gave me shoes, somebody else fed me, somebody else looked after me when I was ill; and I only did a few services in return. But now, wretched man, what things I suffer, being a slave to many instead of to one. All the same," he says, "if I get the gold rings, then I shall live in prosperity and happiness."

First, to get the rings, he submits to what he deserves; then, having got them, it is all the same again. Then he says, "If I take a commission in the army, I am free of all my troubles." He gets his commission, and suffers as much as a flogged slave — and nevertheless asks for a second commission and a third.

And after that, when he has put the final touch to his career and become a senator, then he becomes a slave by entering the senate house; then he serves the finest and most splendid slavery of all.

Let him stop being a fool and learn what Socrates taught: what the nature of each thing that exists is, and that a man should not fit his preconceptions rashly to particular things. This is the cause of all men's troubles: not being able to fit the general preconceptions to particular cases.

But we hold different opinions about the cause of our troubles. One man thinks he is ill — not so; the fact is that he is fitting his preconceptions wrongly. Another thinks he is poor; another that he has a harsh father or mother; another that Caesar is not favourable to him. All of it is one thing and one only: not knowing how to fit the preconceptions.

Who has not a preconception of what is bad — that it is harmful, that it should be avoided, that it should be guarded against by every means? One preconception is not in conflict with another; the conflict only comes at the point of fitting them.

So what is this evil, this harmful thing to be avoided? He answers: not being Caesar's friend. He has gone wide of the mark; he has missed the fitting; he is in difficulties; he is looking for things that have nothing to do with the matter. For when he has succeeded in becoming Caesar's friend, he has still failed to find what he was looking for.

For what is it that every man is looking for? To live securely, to be happy, to do everything as he wishes, not to be blocked, not to be compelled. When he becomes Caesar's friend, then, is he unblocked? Uncompelled? Is he at peace? Is he happy?

Whom shall we ask? What more trustworthy witness have we than the very man who has become Caesar's friend? Come forward and tell us: when did you sleep more quietly, now or before?

At once you hear the answer: "Stop, I beg you, and do not mock me. You do not know what miseries I suffer. Sleep does not come to me. Somebody comes and says, Caesar is awake already, he is on his way out — and then come the troubles and the cares."

Well — when did you dine with more pleasure, now or before? Hear what he says about that too. If he is not invited he is hurt; and if he is invited he dines like a slave with his master, anxious the whole time not to say or do anything foolish. And what do you suppose he is afraid of? Being flogged like a slave? How could he hope for anything so good. No: as befits so great a man, Caesar's friend, he is afraid of losing his head.

And when did you bathe with less worry, and take your exercise more quietly? In short, which life did you prefer, the one you have now or the one before? I can swear that no man is so stupid or so ignorant of the truth that he does not lament his own misfortunes the closer he stands in friendship to Caesar.

So since neither the men called kings live as they choose, nor the friends of kings, who in the end are the men who are free? Look, and you will find; you have aids from nature for discovering the truth.

But if you are not able by yourself, going only along these roads, to discover what follows, then listen to the men who have made the enquiry. What do they say? Does freedom seem to you a good thing? "The greatest good." Is it possible, then, for a man who has got the greatest good to be unhappy or to fare badly? "No." Then whenever you see men unhappy, unlucky, lamenting, declare confidently that they are not free. "I do declare it."

So now we have got away from buying and selling and all such arrangements about property. If you have rightly agreed to these things, then if the great king is unhappy he cannot be free; nor can a lesser king, nor a man of consular rank, nor a man who has been consul twice. Very well.

Then answer me this question as well: does freedom seem to you something great and noble and valuable? "How could it not?" Then is it possible, when a man has got something so great and valuable and noble, for him to be mean? "It is not."

So whenever you see a man subject to another, or flattering him against his own opinion, declare confidently that this man is not free either — and not only if he does it for a dinner, but if he does it for a province or a consulship. Call the men who do it for small things little slaves, and the men who do it for great things great slaves, as they deserve. "That is admitted too."

Do you think freedom is something independent and self-governing? "Certainly." Then whenever it is in another man's power to block a man and compel him, declare that he is not free. And do not, I beg you, look into his grandfathers and great-grandfathers, or enquire whether he was bought and sold. If you hear him say "Master" from the heart and with feeling, call him a slave, even if twelve lictors walk in front of him. And if you hear him say, "Wretch that I am, how much I suffer," call him a slave. If in the end you see him lamenting, complaining, unhappy, call him a slave, though he wears the purple-bordered toga.

And if he is doing none of these things, do not yet call him free: learn his judgments, and see whether they are subject to compulsion, or can produce blocking, or bad fortune. If you find him so, call him a slave on holiday at the Saturnalia. Say his master is away from home; he will be back soon, and then you will find out what he suffers.

"Who will be back?" Whoever has in himself the power over anything the man wants — to give it to him or to take it away.

"So we have many masters?" We have. Before our present masters we have circumstances as our masters, and the circumstances are many. So the men who have power over any of these circumstances must necessarily be our masters.

No man fears Caesar himself; he fears death, exile, loss of property, prison, disgrace. And no man loves Caesar unless Caesar is a man of great merit; he loves wealth, or the office of tribune, or praetor, or consul. And when we love and hate and fear these things, then the men who have power over them must be our masters.

So we worship them as gods; we think that whatever has the power of conferring the greatest advantage on us is divine. And then we wrongly assume that a particular man has the power of conferring the greatest advantages, and so that he is something divine. For if we wrongly assume that a particular man has that power, the conclusion drawn from those premises must necessarily be false.

What is it, then, that makes a man unblocked and his own master? Wealth does not do it, nor a consulship, nor a provincial governorship, nor royal power. Something else has to be found.

What is it that makes us unblocked and unobstructed when we write? Knowing the art of writing. And in playing the lute? Knowing the science of the lute. So in life too, it is the science of living.

You have heard it in general terms; now examine the thing in its parts as well. Is it possible for a man who desires any of the things that depend on others to be unblocked? No. Is it possible for him to be unobstructed? No. Then he cannot be free.

Consider, then: have we nothing in our own power only? Or have we everything? Or are some things in our own power and others in the power of others?

"What do you mean?"

When you want the body to be sound, is it in your power or not? "It is not." When you want it to be healthy? "That is not in my power either." When you want it to be handsome? "Nor that." Life or death? "That is not in my power either."

So your body belongs to somebody else, subject to every man stronger than you. "It does."

And your estate — is it in your power to have it when you please, and as long as you please, and in the state you please? "No." And your slaves? "No." And your clothes? "No." And your house? "No." And your horses? "Not one of these things." And if you want your children to live by all means, or your wife, or your brother, or your friends — is that in your power? "That is not in my power either."

Have you nothing at all, then, that is in your own power, that depends on you alone and cannot be taken from you? Or have you something of the kind?

"I do not know."

Then look at the thing this way and examine it. Can any man make you assent to what is false? "No man." Then in the matter of assent you are unblocked and unobstructed. "Granted."

Well — and can a man force you to have an impulse toward something you do not choose? "He can: when he threatens me with death or with chains, he forces me to have the impulse."

If you despise death and chains, then, do you pay him any more attention? "No." Then is despising death an act of your own or not? "It is my own act." Then having an impulse toward a thing is also your own act, or is it not? "It is my own act." And having an impulse away from a thing — whose act is that? That too is yours.

"And what if I try to walk and somebody stops me?"

What part of you does he stop? Does he stop your assent? "No — my poor body." Yes, as he would stop a stone. "Granted; but I no longer walk."

And who told you that walking is your own act and unblocked? I said that this alone is unblocked: having the impulse. Where the body and its cooperation are needed, you heard long ago that nothing is your own. "Granted that as well."

And who can force you to desire what you do not want? "No man." And to intend or plan, or in short to use the impressions that come to you — can any man force you? "He cannot do that; but he will block me from getting what I desire, when I desire."

If you desire something of your own, one of the things that cannot be blocked, how will he block you? "He cannot in any way." Then who tells you that a man who desires what belongs to others is unblocked?

"Must I not desire health, then?"

By no means, nor anything else belonging to others. What it is not in your power to get or to keep when you please belongs to another. Keep away from it, then — not only your hands, but more than that, your desires as well. If you do not, you have handed yourself over as a slave; you have put your neck under the yoke, if you admire anything not your own, of everything that depends on other men's power and is perishable, everything you have taken a liking to.

"Is my hand not my own?"

It is a part of your own body — but by nature it is earth, subject to blocking, to compulsion, and slave to everything stronger than itself. And why do I say your hand? You ought to possess your whole body as you possess a loaded donkey, as long as it is possible, as long as you are allowed to. But if there is a requisition and a soldier takes hold of it, let it go, do not resist and do not complain; if you do, you will get a beating and lose the donkey anyway.

And when this is how you should feel about the body, consider what is left to do about everything else that is provided for the body's sake. When the body is a donkey, all the other things are the donkey's tackle: the pack-saddles, the shoes, the barley, the fodder. Let those go too; get rid of them faster and more readily than of the donkey.

When you have made this preparation, and have trained at this discipline — telling what belongs to another from what is your own, and the things subject to blocking from the things that are not; counting the unblocked things as your concern and the others as no concern of yours; keeping your desire steadily fixed on what does concern you and turned away from what does not — do you still fear any man? No one.

What will you be afraid about? About the things that are your own, in which the nature of good and evil consists? And who has power over these? Who can take them away? Who can obstruct them? Nobody can, any more than they can obstruct God.

But will you be afraid about your body and your possessions — about things that are not yours, that in no way concern you? And what else have you been studying from the beginning but how to tell your own from not your own, what is in your power from what is not, what is subject to blocking from what is not? And why did you come to the philosophers? So that you might be unlucky and unhappy just the same?

So in this way you will be without fear and without disturbance. And what is grief to you? Fear comes from what you expect, grief from what is present. And what more will you desire? Of the things within the power of the will, being good and present, you have a proper and regulated desire; and of the things not within the power of the will you desire none, and so you give no room at all to what is irrational and impatient and hasty beyond measure.

When you are in this state toward things, what man can still be formidable to you? What has one man that is formidable to another, whether you see him or speak to him or live with him? No more than one horse has to another, or one dog to another, or one bee to another bee.

Things are formidable to every man; and when a man is able to give those things to another or take them away, then he too becomes formidable.

So how is a citadel demolished? Not by the sword and not by fire, but by opinion. If we abolish the citadel that stands in the city, have we abolished the citadel of fever as well, and the citadel of beautiful women? Can we, in a word, abolish the citadel inside us and throw out the tyrants inside us — the ones we have standing over us daily, sometimes the same tyrants and sometimes different ones?

This is where we have to begin; this is how we demolish the citadel and throw out the tyrants: by giving up the body, its parts, its capacities, its possessions, reputation, offices, honours, children, brothers, friends, and counting all of them as belonging to others.

And if the tyrants have been thrown out of me, why should I still blockade the citadel with a wall — on my own account, at any rate? If it is still standing, what does it do to me? Why should I still throw out the tyrant's guards? Where do I feel them? They have their rods and their spears and their swords for other men. But I have never been blocked in my will, and never compelled when I did not will it.

And how is that possible? I have put my impulse to act under God's orders. Is it his will that I have a fever? It is my will too. Is it his will that I move toward something? It is my will too. Is it his will that I get something? It is my wish too. Does he not will it? Then I do not wish it. Is it his will that I die, is it his will that I be racked? Then it is my will to die, my will to be racked.

Who can still block me against my own judgment, or compel me? No more than they can block or compel Zeus.

This is how the more cautious travellers behave. A traveller has heard the road is full of robbers; he does not risk it alone, but waits for the company of an ambassador, or a quaestor, or a proconsul, and having attached himself to such men he goes along the road safely.

So the wise man behaves in the world. There are many bands of robbers, tyrants, storms, difficulties, losses of what is dearest to him. Where is there a refuge?
