How is he to pass along without being attacked by robbers? What company shall he wait for so as to pass safely? Whom shall he attach himself to? To whom in general — to the rich man, to the man of consular rank? And what use is that to me? Such a man is stripped himself; he groans and laments. And what if my fellow traveller turns on me and becomes my robber, what shall I do then?

I will be a friend of Caesar; when I am Caesar's companion nobody will wrong me. In the first place, what must I endure and suffer in order to become distinguished? How often, and by how many men, must I be robbed? And then, if I do become Caesar's friend, he is mortal too. And if Caesar becomes my enemy for some reason, where is it best for me to retire to? A desert? Well — does fever not come there?

What is to be done, then? Is it not possible to find a safe fellow traveller, a faithful one, strong, proof against every surprise? So he considers, and sees that if he attaches himself to God he will make his journey safely.

How do you understand attaching yourself to God? In this sense: that whatever God wills, a man shall will too; and what God does not will, a man shall not will either.

And how is that to be done? In what other way than by examining God's movements and his administration? What has he given me as my own and in my own power? What has he kept for himself? He has given me the things within the power of the will; he has put them in my power, free of obstruction and blocking. How could he make the earthy body unblocked? He could not, and so he made possessions, household goods, house, children and wife subject to the revolution of the whole.

Why do I fight against God, then? Why do I will what does not depend on the will? Why do I will to have absolutely what is not granted to me?

But how ought I to will to have things? In the way they are given, and for as long as they are given. And the one who gave them takes them away. Why do I resist, then? I do not say that I shall be a fool if I use force against a stronger — I shall first of all be unjust.

For where did I get anything when I came into the world? My father gave it to me. And who gave it to him? And who made the sun? And who made the fruits of the earth? Who made the seasons? Who made the connection of men with one another, and their fellowship?

And then, having received everything from another, and yourself as well, are you angry and do you blame the giver if he takes something from you?

Who are you, and what did you come into the world for? Did he not bring you here? Did he not show you the light? Did he not give you fellow workers, and senses, and reason? And as what did he bring you here? Did he not bring you as a mortal, to live on the earth with a little flesh, and to watch his administration, and to join him in the spectacle and the festival for a short time?

Then will you not, as long as you are allowed, having seen the spectacle and the solemnity, go out when he leads you out, worshipping him and giving thanks for what you have heard and seen?

"No — I would like to enjoy the feast longer."

So would the initiated like to be longer at their initiation; so, perhaps, would the crowds at Olympia like to see more athletes. But the solemnity is over. Go away like a grateful and modest man; make room for others. Others must be born, as you were; and being born, they must have a place, and houses, and necessities. And if the first men do not withdraw, what is left?

Why are you insatiable? Why are you not content? Why do you crowd the world?

"Yes, but I want my little children with me, and my wife."

What — are they yours? Do they not belong to the giver, to the one who made you? Then will you not give up what belongs to others? Will you not give way to your superior?

"Why did he bring me into the world on these conditions, then?"

And if the conditions do not suit you, leave. He has no need of a spectator who is not satisfied. He wants men who join in the festival, who take part in the chorus, so that they may applaud and admire and sing the solemnity's praises. Those who cannot bear any trouble, and the cowards, he will not be sorry to see absent from the great assembly; because when they were there they did not behave as men should at a festival, and did not fill their place properly, but lamented and found fault with the deity, with fortune, with their companions — not seeing what they had, and not seeing their own powers, which they were given for the opposite purposes: the powers of greatness of spirit, of a generous mind, of courage, and the one we are enquiring about now, freedom.

"What did I receive these things for, then?"

To use them.

"For how long?"

For as long as the one who lent them chooses.

"And what if they are necessary to me?"

Do not attach yourself to them and they will not be necessary. Do not tell yourself they are necessary, and then they are not.

This is the study you should practise from morning till night, beginning with the smallest things and the ones most easily damaged — with an earthenware pot, with a cup. Then go on in the same way to a tunic, to a little dog, to a horse, to a small piece of land; then to yourself, to your body, to the parts of your body, to your children, to your wife, to your brothers.

Look all round and throw these things away from you. Clean out your judgments, so that nothing that is not your own clings to you or grows onto you, and nothing gives you pain when it is torn away. And say, while you do your daily exercises there in the school, not that you are doing philosophy — that is an arrogant word — but that you are presenting a man who claims his freedom. Because this is really freedom.

It was to this freedom that Antisthenes called Diogenes, and Diogenes said that from then on nobody could enslave him. That is why, when he was taken prisoner, he behaved to the pirates as he did. Did he call any of them master? I do not mean the word — I am not afraid of the word — but the state of mind the word comes out of. How he reproached them for feeding their captives badly! And how was he sold? Did he look for a master? No: for a slave.

And once he was sold, how did he behave to his master? He argued with him at once, and told him he ought not to be dressed as he was, or shaved that way; and about the children, he told them how he ought to bring them up.

And what was strange in that? If his master had bought a trainer, would he have used him in the wrestling school as a servant or as a master? And the same if he had bought a doctor or an architect. And so in every matter: the man with the skill must necessarily be the superior of the man without it.

So whoever has the science of life in general — what else must he be but master? Who is master on a ship? The man at the helm. Why? Because whoever will not obey him suffers for it.

"But a master can have me flogged."

Can he do it without suffering for it, though? So I used to think too. But because he cannot do it without suffering for it, it is not in his power; and no man can do an injustice without suffering for it.

And what is the penalty for the man who chains his own slave? What do you suppose it is? The fact of having chained him. And you will admit this too, if you will hold to the truth that man is not a wild beast but a tame creature.

When is a vine doing badly? When it is in a condition contrary to its nature. When is a cock? Just the same. So a man too. And what is a man's nature? To bite, to kick, to throw men into prison and cut off their heads? No: to do good, to work together with others, to wish them well. So at that moment he is in a bad condition, whether you choose to admit it or not — when he is behaving like a fool.

"So Socrates did not come off badly?"

No — but his judges and his accusers did.

"And Helvidius at Rome did not come off badly?"

No — but his murderer did.

"How do you mean?"

The same as you mean when you say a cock has not come off badly when he has won and been badly wounded, and that a cock has come off badly when he has been beaten and is unhurt. And you do not call a dog lucky when he neither hunts nor works, but when you see him sweating, when you see him in pain and panting hard after a run.

What paradox are we uttering, if we say that the evil in everything is what is contrary to that thing's nature? Is that a paradox? Do you not say it about everything else? Then why do you think differently about man alone?

But because we say that man's nature is gentle and social and trustworthy, will you not say that this is a paradox? It is not. And is it a paradox to say that a man is not harmed when he is whipped, or chained, or beheaded? If he suffers nobly, does he not come off with an increase of advantage and profit? And is the man not harmed who suffers in the most pitiful and disgraceful way, who instead of a man becomes a wolf, or a viper, or a wasp?

Well then, let us go back over what has been agreed. The man not under restraint is free, the man for whom things are exactly as he wishes them to be; and the man who can be restrained or compelled or blocked, or thrown into some situation against his will, is a slave.

And who is free from restraint? The man who desires nothing that belongs to others. And what belongs to others? Whatever it is not in our power to have or not have, or to have of a certain kind or in a certain way. So the body belongs to another, the parts of the body belong to another, property belongs to another. And if you are attached to any of them as your own, you will pay the penalty a man ought to pay for desiring what belongs to somebody else.

This is the road to freedom; this is the only escape from slavery — to be able to say at last, with your whole soul:

	Lead me, Zeus, and you too, Destiny,
	wherever you have fixed my post.

But what do you say, philosopher? The tyrant summons you to say something that does not become you. Do you say it or not? Answer me.

"Let me consider."

Consider now? And when you were in the school, what was it you used to consider? Did you not study what things are good and what are bad and what are neither? "I did." And what was our conclusion? "That just and honourable acts are good, and unjust and disgraceful ones bad."

Is life a good thing? "No." Is death a bad thing? "No." Is prison? "No." And what did we think about mean and faithless words, and betraying a friend, and flattering a tyrant? "That they are bad."

Well then, you are not considering; you have not considered and you have not deliberated. What is there to consider? Whether it is fitting for me, when it is in my power, to secure the greatest of goods for myself, and not to secure the greatest evils? A fine enquiry indeed, and necessary, and one that calls for much deliberation. Man, why do you mock us? Such an enquiry is never made.

If you really imagined that base things were bad and honourable things good, and that everything else was neither, you would never even have approached this enquiry or come near it. You would have been able to distinguish them at once by the understanding, as you distinguish things by sight. When do you enquire whether black things are white, whether heavy things are light, and fail to grasp the plain evidence of the senses?

How then do you now say that you are considering whether things that are neither good nor bad ought to be avoided more than things that are bad? But you do not hold these judgments. These things do not seem to you neither good nor bad; you think they are the greatest evils. And those other things do not seem evils to you but matters of no concern at all.

That is how you have trained yourself from the beginning. "Where am I? In the school. And is anybody listening to me? I am talking among philosophers." But I have gone out of the school. Away with that talk of scholars and fools.

This is how a friend is crushed by a philosopher's testimony; this is how a philosopher becomes a parasite; this is how he hires himself out for money; this is how a man does not say in the senate what he thinks, while in private he proclaims his opinions.

You are a cold and miserable little opinion, hanging from idle words as from a hair. Keep yourself strong instead, and fit for the uses of life, and initiated by being exercised in action.

How do you take the news — I do not say that your child has died, for how could you bear that — but that your oil has been spilled, your wine drunk up? Do you behave in such a way that a man standing next to you while you are making a great outcry can say just this: "Philosopher, you say something different in the school. Why do you deceive us? Why, when you are only a worm, do you say you are a man?"

I should like to be there when one of the philosophers is in bed with a woman, so that I could see how he exerts himself, and what words he uses, and whether he remembers his title of philosopher and the words he hears and says and reads.

And what has this to do with liberty? Nothing but this: whether you rich men choose it or not.

And who is my evidence for that? Who but yourselves — who have a powerful master, and live at his nod and his gesture, and faint if he so much as looks at you with a scowl; who court old women and old men and say, "I cannot do this, it is not in my power."

Why is it not in your power? Did you not argue with me the other day and say you were free? "But Aprulla stopped me." Then tell the truth, slave. Do not run away from your masters, and do not deny them, and do not dare to produce somebody to assert your freedom, when you have so many proofs of your slavery.

Indeed, when a man is forced by love to do something against his own judgment, and sees the better course at the same time but has not the strength to follow it, one might think him more deserving of excuse, as being held by a certain violent and in a way divine power. But who could put up with you, who fall in love with old women and old men, and wipe the old women's noses and wash them and give them presents, and wait on them like a slave when they are ill — and at the same time wish them dead, and ask the doctors whether they are sick unto death?

And again, when in order to get these great and much-admired offices and honours you kiss the hands of other men's slaves, so that you are not even the slave of free men. And then you walk about in front of me in state, a praetor, a consul. Do I not know how you became a praetor, and by what means you got your consulship, and who gave it to you? I would not even choose to live if I had to live by the help of Felicion and put up with his arrogance and his slavish insolence — because I know what a slave is who is fortunate, as he thinks, and swollen with pride.

"And you," a man may say, "are you free?"

I wish I were, by the gods, and I pray to be; but I am not yet able to face my masters. I still value my poor body; I set great store by keeping it whole, though I do not have it whole.

But I can point out a free man to you, so that you need look no further for an example. Diogenes was free. How was he free? Not because he was born of free parents — he was not — but because he was free himself: because he had thrown off all the handles of slavery, and it was not possible for any man to get at him, and nobody had any means of taking hold of him to enslave him.

Everything about him was loosely tied; everything was merely hanging on him. If you took hold of his property, he would have let it go and be yours rather than follow you for it. If you had taken hold of his leg, he would have let the leg go; if of his whole body, the whole poor body; and the same with his friends, his companions, his country. He knew where he got them from, and from whom, and on what terms.

His true parents, the gods, and his real country he would never have deserted; he would have yielded to no man in obedience to them and their orders; and no man would have died for his country more readily. For he was not in the habit of asking when he should be counted to have done something on behalf of the whole; he remembered that everything that is done comes from there, and is done on behalf of that country, and is commanded by the one who administers it.

So see what Diogenes says and writes himself: "For this reason, Diogenes, it is in your power to speak both with the King of Persia and with Archidamus the king of the Spartans, as you please."

Was it because he was born of free parents? I suppose all the Athenians and all the Spartans, being born of slaves, could not talk with these kings as they wished, but feared them and paid court to them?

Then why does he say it is in his power? Because I do not consider the poor body my own; because I want nothing; because law is everything to me and nothing else is. These were the things that let him be free.

And so that you shall not think I am showing you the example of a solitary man, with no wife and no children, no country, no friends and no family to bend him and pull him different ways, take Socrates. Observe that he had a wife and children, but did not consider them his own; that he had a country, so long as it was fit to have one and in the way it was fit; and friends and family too, but he held all of them subject to law and to the obedience owed to it.

That is why he was the first to go out as a soldier when it was necessary, and in war he exposed himself to danger without sparing himself. And when he was sent by the tyrants to arrest Leon, he did not even deliberate about it, because he thought it a base act, and he knew he must die for refusing, if it came to that. And what difference did that make to him? He meant to preserve something else — not his poor flesh, but his good faith and his honourable character. Those are things that cannot be attacked or brought into subjection.

Then, when he had to speak in defence of his own life, did he behave like a man who had children, who had a wife? No: he behaved like a man who had neither.

And what did he do when he was ordered to drink the poison, and had the power to escape from prison, and Crito said to him, "Escape for the sake of your children"? Did he count the chance of escape as an unexpected gain? Not at all. He considered what was fitting and proper, and did not so much as look at the rest or take it into account. He did not choose, he said, to save his poor body, but to save the thing that is increased and saved by doing what is just and is damaged and destroyed by doing what is unjust.

Socrates will not save his life by a base act — the man who would not put the question to the vote when the Athenians clamoured for it, who refused to obey the tyrants, who talked as he did about virtue and right behaviour. It is not possible to save such a man's life by base acts. He is saved by dying, not by running away. The good actor too preserves his character better by stopping when he ought to stop than by going on acting past the proper time.

What are Socrates' children to do, then? "If I had gone off to Thessaly," said Socrates, "would you have taken care of them? And if I go off to the world below, will there be nobody to take care of them?" See how he gives death a gentle name and makes fun of it.

But if you or I had been in his place we should have answered at once, like philosophers, that men who act unjustly must be paid back in the same coin; and we should have added, "I shall be useful to many men if my life is saved, and if I die I shall be useful to nobody." And if it had been necessary we should have made our escape by slipping through a small hole.

And how should we have been useful to anybody then? Where would they have been staying? And if we were useful to men while we were alive, should we not have been far more useful by dying when we ought to die, and as we ought to die? Now that Socrates is dead, the memory of what he did and said while he lived is no less useful to men — it is more useful.

Think about these things, these judgments, these words. Look at these examples, if you want to be free, if you want the thing at what it is worth.

And what is the wonder if you buy so great a thing at the price of so many great things? For the sake of what is called liberty, some men hang themselves and others throw themselves off cliffs, and sometimes whole cities have perished. And will you not, for the sake of the true and unassailable and secure liberty, give back to God the things he gave you, when he asks for them?

Will you not, as Plato says, practise not only dying but enduring torture and exile and flogging — in a word, giving up everything that is not your own? If you will not, you will be a slave among slaves, though you were consul ten thousand times over; and if you make your way up to the Palace you will be no less a slave. And you will feel that philosophers perhaps say things contrary to common opinion, as Cleanthes said too, but not things contrary to reason.

For you will know by experience that the words are true; that there is no profit to men who have obtained the things that are prized and eagerly chased after; and that to men who have not yet obtained them there is an imagination that when these things come, everything good will come with them. And then, when they have come, the fever is the same, the tossing to and fro is the same, the surfeit, the longing for what is not there.

For freedom is not won by getting full possession of the things you want, but by taking away the wanting.

And so that you may know this is true: as you have laboured for those things, transfer your labour to these. Keep vigil for the sake of acquiring a judgment that will make you free. Pay court to a philosopher instead of to a rich old man. Be seen at a philosopher's door — you will not disgrace yourself by being seen there. You will not go away empty or without profit if you go to the philosopher as you should. And if you do not succeed, at least try. The attempt is nothing to be ashamed of.

Chapter 2: On Close Friendships

Before everything else you must attend to this: never to be so closely tied to any of your old companions or friends that you come down to doing the same things he does. If you do not keep this rule, you will ruin yourself.

And if the thought comes into your mind, "I shall seem disobliging to him, and he will not feel the same about me," remember that nothing is done without cost, and that it is not possible for a man who stops doing the same things to be the same man he was.

So choose which of the two you will have: to be loved just as much by the people who used to love you, by staying the same as your old self; or to be better, and not get from your friends what you used to get. If the second is better, turn to it at once and do not let other considerations pull you the other way. No man can make progress while he is wavering between opposites; and if you have preferred this one thing to everything else, if you choose to attend to this only and work at this only, then give up everything else.

If you will not do that, your wavering will produce both results: you will neither improve as you ought, nor get what you used to get. For before, by plainly wanting the things that are worth nothing, you pleased your friends.

But you cannot excel in both kinds; and to the extent that you share in the one, you must necessarily fall short in the other. You cannot be as agreeable as you used to be to the men you used to drink with, if you no longer drink with them. So choose whether you will be a hard drinker and pleasant to your old friends, or a sober man and disagreeable to them. You cannot be loved just as much by the men you used to sing with, if you no longer sing with them. So choose in this matter too which of the two you will have.

If it is better to be modest and orderly than to have a man say, "He is a jolly fellow," then give up the rest, renounce it, turn away from it, have nothing to do with such men.

But if that does not suit you, then turn to the opposite altogether: throw away all shame about sex, be an adulterer, act accordingly, and you will get what you want. And jump up in the theatre and bawl your praise of the dancer.

But characters as different as these cannot be mixed. You cannot play both Thersites and Agamemnon. If you mean to be Thersites, you must be humpbacked and bald; if Agamemnon, you must be tall and handsome and love the men placed under your command.

Chapter 3: What We Should Trade for What

Keep this thought ready when you lose something external: what you have got in its place. And if it is worth more, never say, "I have suffered a loss" — not if you have got a horse instead of a donkey, or an ox instead of a sheep; not if you have got a good action instead of a bit of money; not if instead of idle chatter you have got the peace of mind that suits a man; not if instead of dirty talk you have got self-respect.

If you remember this, you will always keep your character as it ought to be. And if you do not, consider that your opportunities are being wasted, and that whatever trouble you take over yourself you are going to throw away and overturn.

It takes only a few things to lose and overturn everything — a small deviation from reason. For a steersman to capsize a ship he does not need what he needs to save it; if he turns her a little into the wind she is lost. And if he does it not on purpose but merely by neglecting his duty for a moment, the ship is lost all the same.

Something of the kind happens here too: nod off for a moment, and everything you have gathered up to now is gone. So attend to the impressions of things, and keep watch over them; because what you have to preserve is no small matter. It is self-respect, and good faith, and steadiness, freedom from the passions, an undisturbed mind, freedom from fear, peace — in a word, liberty.

What will you sell these for? Look at what the things are worth that you will get in exchange.

"But shall I not get anything like that for it?"

Look — and if you do get something in return, look at what you are getting instead. I have decency; he has a tribuneship. He has a praetorship; I have self-respect. But I do not shout applause where it is not fitting; I will not stand up where I ought not to. Because I am free, and a friend of God, and so I obey him willingly.

But I must claim nothing else — not body, nor property, nor office, nor good report, nor in fact anything. He does not allow me to claim them; if he had chosen to, he would have made them good for me. But he has not, and so I cannot break his commands.

Preserve in everything what is your own good; and as for everything else, take it as it is allowed, and behave reasonably about it, and be content with that alone. If you do not, you will be unlucky; you will fail in everything; you will be blocked and obstructed.

These are the laws that have been sent from there; these are the orders. A man ought to be an expounder of these laws and submit to these, not to those of Masurius and Cassius.

Chapter 4: To Those Who Want a Quiet Life

Remember that it is not only the desire for power and riches that makes us mean and subject to others, but the desire for peace and leisure and travel and learning as well. To put it plainly, whatever the external thing may be, the value we set on it puts us in subjection to somebody.

So what difference is there between wanting to be a senator and not wanting to be one? What difference between wanting power and being content with private life? What difference between saying "I am wretched, I have nothing to do, I am tied to my books like a corpse" and saying "I am wretched, I have no leisure for reading"? Salutations and power are external and outside the will — and so is a book.

What do you choose to read for? Tell me. If you are aiming only at being entertained or learning something, you are a silly fellow who cannot bear hard work. But if you refer reading to its proper end, what else is that end but a life that runs smoothly and happily?

And if reading does not secure you a happy and untroubled life, what use is it? "But it does secure it," the man says, "and that is why I am annoyed at being deprived of it."

And what is this untroubled and happy life, that any man can obstruct — I do not say Caesar or Caesar's friend, but a crow, a piper, a fever, and thirty thousand other things? A happy and untroubled life contains nothing so certain as continuity and freedom from obstruction.

Now I am called to do something. I will go, then, with the purpose of observing the rules I must keep: acting with self-respect and steadiness, without desire and without avoidance where externals are concerned; and then, of attending to men, to what they say and how they are moved — and this not with any bad disposition, and not so as to have something to blame or laugh at, but turning to myself and asking whether I commit the same faults. And how am I to stop committing them? I used to do wrong once, and now I do not: thanks be to God.

Come: when you have done these things and attended to them, have you done a worse act than when you have read a thousand lines or written as many? When you eat, are you grieved because you are not reading? Are you not satisfied to eat according to what you learned by reading — and the same with bathing and with exercise?

Why do you not behave consistently in everything, then, both when you come before Caesar and when you come before anybody? If you keep yourself undisturbed, unalarmed and steady; if you look at what is being done and what happens rather than being looked at yourself; if you do not envy the men preferred before you; if the circumstances around you do not strike you with fear or admiration — what do you lack? Books? How, or what for? Is reading not a preparation for life? And is life itself not made up of certain other things than this?

This is as if an athlete wept on entering the stadium because he is not exercising outside it. This was what you used to do your exercises for; this was what the weights were for, and the dust, and the young men as opponents — and now, when it is time for action, do you go looking for those?

It is as if, in the matter of assent, when impressions present themselves, some of which can be grasped and some of which cannot, we should refuse to distinguish them and choose instead to read what has been written about grasping.

What is the reason for this? The reason is that we never read for this purpose and never wrote for this purpose — so that in our actions we might use the impressions before us in a way that is in line with nature. We stop short at learning what is said, and at being able to expound it to somebody else, and at resolving a syllogism, and handling a hypothetical one. And so it is exactly where our study lies that we find the obstruction.

Would you have, by all means, the things not in your power? Then be prevented, be blocked, fail of your purpose.

But if we read what is written about action, not to see what is said about action but so as to act well; if we read what is said about desire and avoidance so as neither to fail in our desires nor fall into what we try to avoid; if we read what is said about duty so that, remembering how things stand to one another, we may do nothing unreasonably and nothing contrary to those relations — then we should not be annoyed at being obstructed in our reading. We should be satisfied with doing the acts that fit; and we should be reckoning up, not what we have been used to reckon — "Today I read so many lines, I wrote so many" — but this: today I employed my impulse as the philosophers teach; I did not employ desire; I used avoidance only with respect to things within the power of my will; I was not afraid of such and such a man; I was not won over by another's entreaties; I exercised my patience, my abstinence, my cooperation with others. And so we should thank God for what we ought to thank him for.

But as it is, we do not know that in another way we too are like the crowd. One man is afraid that he will not get power; you are afraid that you will. Do not do it, my man. As you laugh at the man who is afraid he will not get power, laugh at yourself as well. It makes no difference whether you are thirsty like a man with a fever or dread water like a man who is mad.

Or how will you still be able to say, as Socrates did, "If it so pleases God, so let it be"? Do you suppose that if Socrates had been eager to spend his leisure in the Lyceum or the Academy and talk with the young men every day, he would have served so readily on so many campaigns? Would he not have lamented and groaned: "Wretch that I am, I have to be miserable here when I might be sunning myself in the Lyceum"?

Why — was that your business, sunning yourself? Is your business not to be happy, unblocked, unobstructed? And could he still have been Socrates if he had lamented like that? How would he still have been able to write hymns in his prison?

In short, remember this: whatever you prize that lies beyond your will, to that extent you have destroyed your will. And these things lie outside the will: not only power but a private station; not only business but leisure.

"Must I live in this tumult, then?"

Why do you call it tumult?

"I mean among a great many men."

Well, and what is the hardship? Suppose you are at Olympia; imagine it a great public assembly, where one man is shouting one thing and another doing another and a third shoving somebody. There is a crowd at the baths. And which of us is not pleased with that assembly, and leaves it unwillingly?

Do not be hard to please, and do not be fussy about what happens. "Vinegar is disagreeable, it is sharp; honey is disagreeable, it upsets me; I do not like vegetables." So too: "I do not like leisure, it is a desert; I do not like a crowd, it is confusion."

If circumstances make it necessary for you to live alone or with a few people, call it quiet and use the thing as you ought: talk with yourself, work on your impressions, work up your preconceptions. If you fall into a crowd, call it games, an assembly, a festival; try to enjoy the festival along with everybody else.

For what is a pleasanter sight to a man who loves mankind than a great many men? We watch herds of horses and oxen with pleasure; we are delighted when we see many ships. Who is pained at seeing many men?

"But they deafen me with their shouting."

Then your hearing is obstructed. What is that to you? Is the power of using impressions obstructed? And who prevents you from using inclination and disinclination, and impulse toward and away from a thing, according to nature? What tumult can do that?

Only keep the general rules in mind: what is mine? What is not mine? What is permitted me? What does God want me to do now, and what does he not want? A little while ago he wanted you to be at leisure, to talk with yourself, to write about these things, to read, to listen, to prepare yourself; and you had enough time for it. Now he says to you: come to the contest; show us what you have learned, how you have practised the athlete's art. How long will you exercise alone? Now is your chance to learn whether you are an athlete worth the victory or one of those who go round the world getting beaten.

Why are you annoyed, then? No contest is without confusion. There must be many men exercising for it, many shouting at the men exercising, many trainers, many spectators.

"But my wish is to live quietly."

Then lament and groan, as you deserve to. What greater punishment is there for the untaught man, and for the man who disobeys the divine commands, than to grieve, to lament, to envy — in a word, to be disappointed and unhappy?

Would you not release yourself from these things?

"And how am I to release myself?"

Have you not often heard that you must take desire away entirely, and apply avoidance only to what is within your power; that you must give up everything — body, property, fame, books, tumult, power, private station? Because whichever way you turn, you are a slave, you are subject, you are blocked, you are compelled, you are wholly in other men's power.

Keep the words of Cleanthes ready instead:

	Lead me, Zeus, and you too, Destiny,
	wherever you have fixed my post.

Is it your will that I go to Rome? I will go to Rome. To Gyara? I will go to Gyara. To Athens? To Athens. To prison? To prison.

If you once say, "When can a man get to Athens?" you are finished. It necessarily follows that this desire, if it is not fulfilled, must make you unhappy; and if it is fulfilled, must make you vain, since you are lifted up by things you ought not to be lifted up by; and on the other side, if you are obstructed, it must make you wretched, because you fall into what you did not want to fall into.

So give all these things up. "Athens is a good place." But happiness is a much better one — and being free of the passions, free of disturbance, having your affairs depend on nobody. "There is tumult at Rome, and calls to pay." But happiness is worth all the troublesome things put together. So if the time comes for them, why do you not take away the wish to avoid them? What need is there to carry a load like a donkey and be beaten with a stick? And if you do not do this, understand that you must always be the slave of whoever has the power to release you and the power to block you, and must serve him as an evil spirit.

There is only one road to happiness, and let this rule be ready morning and noon and night: not to look toward things outside the power of the will; to think nothing your own; to give everything up to the Divinity, to Fortune, and to make them the superintendents of these things, as Zeus has made them; to observe only what is your own, the thing that cannot be blocked; and when we read, to refer our reading to this alone, and our writing and our listening too.

This is why I cannot call a man industrious if all I hear is that he reads and writes; and even if somebody adds that he reads all night, I cannot say it, if he does not know what he should refer his reading to. You do not call a man industrious if he stays awake for a girl; nor do I. If he does it for reputation, I say he loves reputation; if he does it for money, I say he loves money, not labour; and if he does it out of love of learning, I say he loves learning. But if he refers his labour to his own governing part, so as to keep it in a condition in line with nature and to live his life in that condition, then and only then do I call him industrious.

So never praise a man on account of things that are common to everybody; praise him for his judgments, because those are what belong to each man, and they are what make his acts good or bad.

Remembering these rules, take joy in what is present and be content with the things that come in season. If you see something you have learned and enquired about turning up in the course of your life, be delighted at it. If you have laid aside or reduced a bad disposition and the habit of abusing people; if you have done the same with rash temper, foul language, hastiness, sluggishness; if you are not moved by what used to move you, or not in the same way — then you can hold a festival every day: today because you behaved well in one act, tomorrow because you behaved well in another.

How much greater a reason is this for sacrificing than a consulship or the governorship of a province? These things come to you from yourself and from the gods. Remember this: who gives these things, and to whom, and for what purpose.

If you nourish yourself on these thoughts, do you still think it makes any difference where you are happy, where you please God? Are the gods not equally distant from every place? Do they not see what is going on equally from every place?

Chapter 5: Against the Quarrelsome and the Aggressive

The wise and good man neither quarrels with anybody himself nor, so far as he can prevent it, allows anybody else to. An example of this, as of everything else, is set before us in the life of Socrates, who not only avoided quarrels himself on every occasion but would not let others quarrel either.

See in Xenophon's Symposium how many quarrels he settled; how he put up with Thrasymachus and Polus and Callicles; how he put up with his wife, and with his son when he tried to refute him and pick holes in him. For he remembered well that no man has another man's governing part in his power. So he wanted nothing except what was his own.

And what is that? Not that this man or that should act according to nature — that belongs to somebody else — but that while other men are doing their own acts as they choose, he should nevertheless be in a condition in line with nature and live in it, doing only what is his own, so that others too may come into a state in line with nature. This is the object the wise and good man always sets before himself.

Is it to be a general? No; but if it is permitted him, his object in that matter is to keep his own governing part. Is it to marry? No; but if marriage is allowed him, his object in that matter is to keep himself in a condition in line with nature. And if he wanted his son not to do wrong, or his wife, he would be wanting what belongs to another not to belong to another. And being taught is precisely this: learning what things are one's own and what belong to somebody else.

So where is any room left for quarrelling, in a man who holds the opinion he ought to hold? Is he surprised at anything that happens? Does anything look new to him? Does he not expect what comes from bad men to be worse and harder than what actually falls on him? Does he not reckon as pure gain whatever they do that falls short of the extreme of wickedness?

Somebody has abused you. Great thanks to him for not striking you. "But he struck me as well." Great thanks that he did not wound you. "But he wounded me as well." Great thanks that he did not kill you.

For when did he ever learn, and in what school, that man is a tame creature, that men love one another, that an act of injustice is a great harm to the man who does it? Since he has not learned it and is not convinced of it, why should he not follow what looks like his own interest?

Your neighbour has thrown stones. Have you done anything wrong, then? "But the things in the house are broken." Are you a piece of household furniture? No: you are a free power of will.

What are you given to do in answer, then? If you are like a wolf, you must bite back and throw more stones. But if you consider what is proper for a man, examine your storehouse; see what capacities you came into the world with. Have you the disposition of a wild beast? Have you the disposition of revenge for an injury?

When is a horse wretched? When he is deprived of his natural capacities — not when he cannot crow like a cock, but when he cannot run. When is a dog wretched? Not when he cannot fly, but when he cannot follow a scent. And is a man unhappy in the same way — not because he cannot strangle lions or throw his arms round statues, since he did not come into the world with powers from nature for that, but because he has lost his honesty and his good faith?

People ought to gather and lament over such a man for the misfortunes he has fallen into — not lamenting because a man has been born or has died, but because in his lifetime it has happened to him to lose what was his own. Not what he received from his father, not his land and his house and his inn and his slaves, for not one of those is a man's own; they all belong to others, they are servile, they are subject to account, and they are given to different men at different times by those who have them in their power. I mean the things that belong to him as a man: the marks stamped on his mind that he came into the world with — the marks we look for on coins too, and if we find them we approve the coin, and if we do not find them we reject it.

What is the stamp on this coin? Trajan's. Hand it over. It is Nero's. Throw it away; it cannot be accepted; it is counterfeit.

So here too. What is the stamp on this man's judgments? Gentleness, a sociable disposition, a tolerant temper, a readiness for mutual affection. Produce those. I accept them: I count this man a citizen, I accept him as a neighbour and a fellow passenger. Only see that he has not Nero's stamp on him. Is he quick-tempered, full of resentment, a fault-finder? Does he break the heads of the men in his way when the whim takes him? Then why did you call him a man?

Is everything judged by the bare shape? If that is so, then say that the shape of an apple in wax has the smell and the taste of an apple. The external figure is not enough; the nose and the eyes are not enough to make the man; he must have the judgments of a man too.

Here is a man who does not listen to reason, who does not know when he is refuted: he is a donkey. In another the sense of shame has gone dead: he is good for nothing, he is anything rather than a man. This one is looking for somebody to meet so that he can kick him or bite him — so he is not even a sheep or a donkey, but a kind of wild animal.

"What then? Would you have me despised?"

By whom? By men who know you? And how will men who know you despise a man who is gentle and modest? By men who do not know you, perhaps? What is that to you? No other craftsman cares about the opinion of men who know nothing about his craft.

"But they will be more hostile to me for it."

Why do you say "me"? Can any man injure your will, or stop you using the impressions before you in a natural way? In no way at all. Then why are you still disturbed, and why do you choose to show yourself afraid? Why do you not come out and announce that you are at peace with all men whatever they do, and laugh most of all at the ones who think they can harm you? These slaves, you can say, know neither who I am nor where my good and my evil lie, because they have no access to what is mine.

This is how men who hold a strong city mock the besiegers: what trouble these men are taking for nothing. Our wall is secure, we have food for a very long time, and every other resource. These are the things that make a city strong and impossible to take. And nothing but his judgments makes a man's soul impossible to take.

For what wall is so strong, what body so hard, what possession so safe, what rank so free from assault? All other things everywhere are perishable and easily stormed; and any man attached to them in any way must be disturbed, must expect what is bad, must fear, lament, find his desires disappointed and fall into what he would avoid.

Then do we not choose to make secure the one means of safety that is offered to us? Do we not choose to withdraw from what is perishable and servile and to work at what is imperishable and free by nature? Do we not remember that no man either harms another or does good to another, but that a man's opinion about each thing is what harms him and what overturns him — that this is fighting, this is civil discord, this is war?

What made Eteocles and Polynices enemies was nothing but this: their opinion about royal power and their opinion about exile, that the one is the extreme of evils and the other the greatest good.

Now it is every man's nature to seek the good and avoid the bad, and to count whoever deprives him of the one and involves him in the other an enemy and a traitor, even if he is a brother, a son, a father. Nothing is closer kin to us than the good. So if externals are good and evil, then a father is no friend to his sons, nor a brother to a brother, and the whole world everywhere is full of enemies and traitors and informers.

But if the will, being as it ought to be, is the only good, and the will, being as it ought not to be, is the only evil — then where is there any strife, where is there any abuse? About what? About things that do not concern us? And strife with whom? With the ignorant, the unhappy, men who have been deceived about the chief things?

Remembering this, Socrates ran his own household and put up with a very bad-tempered wife and a foolish son. And how did she show her bad temper? By pouring water over his head as much as she liked, and by trampling on the cake somebody had sent him. And what is that to me, if I think these things are nothing to me?

But this is my business: no tyrant shall check my will, and no master; the many shall not check me, who am only one; the stronger shall not check me, who am the weaker. God gives this power of being unchecked to every man.

These judgments make love in a household, agreement in a state, peace among nations, and gratitude to God. They make a man confident about externals in everything, as about things belonging to others, as about things of no value.

We are certainly able to write and read these things, and to praise them when they are read, but we do not come anywhere near being convinced of them. So what is said about the Spartans — lions at home, but foxes in Ephesus — will fit us too: lions in the school, but foxes outside it.

Chapter 6: Against Those Who Complain of Being Pitied

"I am hurt," a man says, "at being pitied."

Is the fact of your being pitied something that concerns you, or the men who pity you? And is it in your power to stop the pity?

"It is in my power, if I show them I do not need pity."

And are you in the condition of not needing pity, or are you not?

"I think I am not. But these people do not pity me for the things it would be proper to pity me for, if they ought to pity me at all — I mean my faults. They pity me for my poverty, for not holding honourable office, for diseases and deaths and other such things."

Then are you prepared to convince the crowd that not one of these things is an evil, and that it is possible for a man who is poor and holds no office and enjoys no honour to be happy? Or to show yourself to them as rich and powerful?

The second of these belongs to a boastful, silly, good-for-nothing man. And consider by what means the pretence has to be kept up. You will have to hire slaves, and own a few pieces of silver plate, and display them in public if you can — the same pieces over and over, and you will have to try to hide that they are the same. You will need splendid clothes and everything else for show. You will have to appear a man honoured by the great, and try to dine at their houses, or at least to be thought to dine there. And as to your person, you will have to use some mean tricks so as to look handsomer and better born than you are.

These are the things you must contrive if you choose the second road so as not to be pitied. And the first road is both impossible and long: attempting the very thing Zeus has not been able to do, to convince all men what things are good and bad. Is that power given to you? Only this is given to you: to convince yourself. And you have not convinced yourself.

And then I ask you: do you attempt to persuade other men? Who has lived so long with you as you have with yourself? Who has so much power to convince you as you have? Who is better disposed toward you and closer to you than you are to yourself? How is it, then, that you have not yet convinced yourself, so as to learn?

Are things not upside down at the moment? Is this what you have been in earnest about doing — learning to be free of grief and free of disturbance, not to be abject, and to be free? Have you not heard that there is only one road that leads to this end: giving up the things that do not depend on the will, withdrawing from them, and admitting that they belong to others?

So what sort of thing is it for another man to have an opinion about you? A thing outside the will. Then is it nothing to you? It is nothing. And when you are still annoyed and disturbed by it, do you think you are convinced about good and evil?

Will you not leave other men alone, then, and be both pupil and teacher to yourself? Let the rest of mankind see to whether it is in their interest to live in a state contrary to nature; no man is closer to me than myself.

What is the meaning of this, then — that I have listened to the words of the philosophers and I agree with them, and yet in fact I am no more at ease than before? Am I so stupid? And yet in everything else I have taken up I have not turned out very stupid: I learned my letters quickly, and wrestling, and geometry, and how to resolve syllogisms.

Has reason not convinced me, then? And in fact there is nothing I have approved and chosen from the beginning so much as this; and now I read about these things, hear about them, write about them. I have found no stronger reasoning than this so far.

Where am I falling short, then? Have the contrary opinions not been rooted out of me? Have the notions themselves not been exercised, not been put into practice — laid aside like armour and rusted, so that they no longer fit me?

And yet in the wrestling school, and in writing and reading, I am not satisfied with merely learning: I turn the syllogisms that are put to me upside down, and I make others, and trick syllogisms too. But the necessary theorems, working from which a man can become free of grief, fear, passions and blocking, and become a free man — in these I do not exercise myself, and in these I do not do the proper practice. And then I care what other people will say about me, whether I shall look worth noticing to them, whether I shall look happy.

Wretched man, will you not see what you are saying about yourself? What do you look like to yourself — in your judgments, in your desires, in your avoidances, in your impulses, in your preparation, in your plans, and in the other acts that suit a man? And do you trouble yourself about whether other men pity you?

"Yes — but I am pitied when I do not deserve it."

Then are you pained at that? And is a man who is pained an object of pity? "Yes." Then how are you being pitied when you do not deserve it? By the very fact that you suffer over being pitied, you make yourself deserving of it.

And what does Antisthenes say? Have you not heard? "It is a royal thing, Cyrus, to do well and be ill spoken of."

My head is sound and everybody thinks I have a headache. What do I care? I am free of fever, and people sympathise with me as if I had one: "Poor man, you have not been rid of that fever for so long." And I say, with a sorrowful face, "It is true — I have been ill a long time now." And what will come of it? Whatever God pleases. And meanwhile I laugh to myself at the people who are pitying me.

What stops the same thing being done here? I am poor, but I have the right judgment about poverty. Why do I care if they pity me for it? I am not in office, and others are; and I have the judgment I ought to have about having office and not having it. Let the men who pity me look to it. I am neither hungry nor thirsty nor cold; but because they are hungry and thirsty they think I must be too.

What am I to do for them, then? Go about proclaiming, "Do not be mistaken, gentlemen; I am very well. I do not trouble myself about poverty, or lack of power, or in a word about anything but right judgments. I have those unrestricted; I care about nothing else at all"?

What foolish talk is this? How do I possess right judgments, when I am not content with being what I am but am uneasy about what I am taken to be?

But you say: others will get more, and be preferred to me. And what is more reasonable than that men who have laboured at something should have more of the thing they laboured at? They laboured for power, and you laboured at your judgments; they laboured for wealth, and you for the right use of impressions.

See whether they have more than you in the thing you laboured at and they neglected: whether they assent better than you do with respect to the natural measures of things; whether they are less often disappointed in their desires; whether they fall less often than you into what they would avoid; whether in their intentions, in what they set before themselves, in their purposes, in their movements toward an object, they take better aim; whether they keep proper behaviour better, as men, as sons, as parents, and so on through the other names by which we express the relations of life.

But if they hold power and you do not, will you not choose to tell yourself the truth — that you do nothing for the sake of power, and they do everything? It would be most unreasonable for a man who looks after a thing to get less of it than the man who does not.

"Not so. Since I care about right judgments, it is more reasonable for me to have power."

Yes, in the matter you do care about, in judgments. But in the matter they have cared about more than you, give way to them. It is exactly as if you thought that because you have right judgments you ought to hit the mark better with a bow than an archer does, and do better in metalwork than a smith.

Give up your earnestness about judgments, then, and busy yourself with the things you want to get. And then lament if you do not succeed — you will deserve to lament. But as it is, you say that you are occupied with other things, that you are looking after other things; and the crowd say truly that one act has nothing in common with another.

The man who has got up in the morning is looking for whom to salute, whom to say something agreeable to, whom to send a present to, how to please the dancer, how by behaving badly to one man he may please another. When he prays, he prays about these things; when he sacrifices, he sacrifices for them. He takes the saying of Pythagoras — "Let sleep not fall upon your weary eyes" — and applies it to these things. Where have I fallen short in the matter of flattery? What have I done? Anything like a free man? Anything like a man of noble mind? And if he finds anything of the kind, he blames and accuses himself: why did you say that? Was it not in your power to lie? Even the philosophers say there is nothing to stop us telling a lie.

But you, if you really have cared about nothing but the right use of impressions, should reflect as soon as you have got up in the morning: what do I need in order to be free of the passions and free of disturbance? What am I? A poor body, a piece of property, a thing that gets talked about? I am none of these. What am I, then? A rational creature. What is required of me, then?

Go over your acts. Where did I leave out the things that lead to living well? What have I done that was unfriendly or unsociable? What have I failed to do in these matters that I ought to have done?

The difference in desires and actions and wishes being so great, then, would you still have the same share as other men in things you have not laboured at and they have? And are you surprised when they pity you, and annoyed?

But they are not annoyed if you pity them. Why? Because they are convinced that they have what is good, and you are not convinced. That is why you are not satisfied with your own but want what they have; while they are satisfied with theirs and do not want what you have. Because if you were really convinced that where the good is concerned it is you who possess it and they who have missed it, you would not even have given a thought to what they say about you.

Chapter 7: On Being Free from Fear

What makes a tyrant formidable? The guards, you say, and their swords, and the men of the bedchamber, and the men who keep out those who want to come in.

Then why is a child not afraid when you bring him to the tyrant while the guards are there? Is it because the child does not understand these things? And if a man does understand what guards are, and that they have swords, and comes to the tyrant precisely because he wants to die on account of some circumstance and is looking for an easy death at another man's hand — is he afraid of the guards? No: because he wants the very thing that makes the guards formidable.

And if a man comes to the tyrant neither wanting to die nor wanting to live by all means, but only as it may be allowed him, what stops him coming without fear? Nothing.

And if a man has the same opinion about his property as the man I have described has about his body — and about his children too, and his wife; if he is in a word so affected by some madness or despair that he does not care whether he has them or not; if, as children playing with shells care about the game but not about the shells, he too has set no value on the materials but values the pleasure he gets from them and the occupation — then what tyrant is formidable to him, or what guards, or what swords?

So it is possible for a man to be disposed toward these things through madness, and for the Galileans to be so through habit; and is it not possible for anybody to learn from reason and demonstration that God made everything in the universe, and the universe itself, completely unblocked and complete, and made the parts of it for the use of the whole?

All the other animals are incapable of taking in how it is administered; but the rational animal, man, has the capacities for considering all this, and for understanding that he is a part, and what kind of part, and that it is right for the parts to be subordinate to the whole.

And besides this, being naturally noble and great-spirited and free, man sees that of the things around him some are unblocked and in his power and others are subject to blocking and in the power of others; that the unblocked ones are within the power of the will, and the ones subject to blocking are not.

And for this reason, if he thinks his good and his interest lie only in the things that are unblocked and in his own power, he will be free, prosperous, happy, unharmed, great in spirit, reverent, thankful to God for everything, never finding fault with any of the things that were not put in his power and never blaming them.

But if he thinks his good and his interest lie in externals and in things not within the power of his will, then he must necessarily be blocked and obstructed and a slave to whoever has power over the things he admires and fears; he must necessarily be irreverent, because he thinks God harms him; and he must be unjust, because he is always claiming more than belongs to him; and he must necessarily be abject and mean.

What stops a man who has clearly separated these things from living light-hearted and holding the reins easily, quietly expecting whatever can happen and enduring what has already happened?

Would you have me bear poverty? Come and see what poverty is when it has found somebody who can play the part of a poor man well. Would you have me hold power? Let me have power — and the trouble of it too. Exile? Wherever I go, it will be well with me there; because here, where I am, it was not the place that made it well with me but my judgments, and I shall carry those away with me. No man can take them from me; my judgments alone are mine and cannot be taken away, and I am satisfied as long as I have them, wherever I am and whatever I am doing.

"But now it is time to die."

Why do you say to die? Do not make a tragedy of the thing; say it as it is. It is now time for the matter to be resolved into the things it was put together from. And what is formidable in that? What is going to perish, of the things in the universe? What new or marvellous thing is going to happen?

Is this what makes a tyrant formidable? Is this why the guards seem to have swords that are large and sharp? Tell that to other people. I have thought all of this over; no man has power over me. I have been made free; I know his commands; nobody can lead me away as a slave now. I have a proper man to assert my freedom, and proper judges.

Are you not master of my body? What is that to me? Are you not master of my property? What is that to me? Are you not master of my exile and my chains? Well — from all of these, and from the poor body itself, I depart at your bidding, whenever you please. Try your power, and you will find out how far it reaches.

Whom can I still fear, then? The men in charge of the bedchamber? In case they do — what? Shut me out? If they find that I want to go in, let them shut me out.

Why do you go to the doors, then? Because I think it becomes me to join in the game while it lasts. Then how are you not shut out? Because unless somebody lets me in, I do not choose to go in; I am always content with what happens. I think what God chooses is better than what I choose. I will attach myself to him as a servant and a follower; I have the same impulses as he has, the same desires; in a word, I want the same things. There is no shutting out for me — only for the men who try to force their way in.

Why do I not force my way in, then? Because I know that nothing good is handed out inside to the men who go in. But when I hear a man called fortunate because Caesar has honoured him, I say: what does he happen to get? A province. Does he also get the judgment he ought to have? A prefecture. Does he also get the power of using the office well? Why should I still strive to get in?

A man scatters dried figs and nuts: the children snatch at them and fight one another over them; men do not, because they think it a small matter. But if a man throws shells about, not even the children snatch at them.

Provinces are handed out: let children see to that. Money is handed out: let children see to that. Praetorships and consulships are handed out: let children scramble for them, let them be shut out and beaten, let them kiss the hands of the giver and of his slaves. To me they are dried figs and nuts.

What then? If you fail to get one while Caesar is scattering them about, do not be troubled. If a dried fig falls into your lap, take it and eat it; a fig may be worth that much. But if I have to stoop down and turn another man over, or be turned over myself, and flatter the men who have got into the chamber, then a dried fig is not worth the trouble — nor is anything else among the things that are not good, which the philosophers have convinced me not to think good.

Show me the guards' swords. See how big they are, and how sharp. And what do these big sharp swords do? They kill. And what does a fever do? Nothing else. And a falling roof tile? Nothing else.

Would you have me marvel at these things and worship them and go about a slave to all of them? I hope that will not happen. Once I have learned that everything which has come into existence must also go out of it, so that the universe may not stand still or be obstructed, I no longer consider it makes any difference whether a fever does it, or a tile, or a soldier. And if a man must make a comparison between them, I know that the soldier will do it with less trouble to me, and quicker.

So when I neither fear anything a tyrant can do to me nor want anything he can give, why do I still look on at him in wonder? Why am I still confused? Why do I fear the guards? Why am I pleased if he speaks to me in a friendly way and receives me, and why do I go and tell other people how he spoke to me? Is he a Socrates, is he a Diogenes, that his praise should be proof of what I am? Have I been eager to imitate his character?

No — I keep up the game and go to him and serve him, so long as he does not order me to do anything foolish or unreasonable. But if he says to me, "Go and bring Leon of Salamis," I say: find somebody else, I am not playing any more.

"Take him away to prison." I follow; that is part of the game. "But your head will come off." Does the tyrant's head always stay where it is? And the heads of you who obey him? "But you will be thrown out unburied." If the corpse is me, then I shall be thrown out; but if I am something other than the corpse, then speak more accurately, as the fact is, and do not think of frightening me.

These things are formidable to children and to fools. But if a man has once entered a philosopher's school and does not know what he is, then he deserves to be full of fear and to flatter the men he used to flatter afterwards — if he has not yet learned that he is not flesh, or bones, or sinews, but the thing that uses these parts of the body and governs them and follows the impressions of things.

"Yes, but this talk makes us despise the laws."

And what sort of talk makes men who use it more obedient to the laws? The things in a fool's power are not law. And yet see how this talk disposes us as we ought to be even toward such men, since it teaches us to claim against them none of the things they are able to beat us at.

This talk teaches us to give up the body; to give up property as well; to withdraw from children, parents and brothers, and give up everything. It makes an exception only of our judgments, which Zeus himself has willed to be every man's own private property. What breaking of the laws is there in that? What folly?

Where you are better and stronger, I gave way to you. And on the other hand, where I am better, you give way to me — because I have taken care about this and you have not. Your study is to live in houses with floors of many-coloured stone, and how your slaves and dependents shall wait on you, and how you shall wear fine clothes and keep a lot of huntsmen and lute players and tragic actors. Do I lay claim to any of it?

Have you made any study of judgments, or of your own rational capacity? Do you know what parts it is made of, how they are put together, how they are connected, what powers it has and of what kind? Then why are you annoyed if another man, who has made it his study, has the advantage of you in it?

"But these things are the greatest things."

And who stops you being occupied with them and looking after them? And who has a better stock of books, of leisure, of people to help him?

Only turn your mind to these things at last; attend, if only for a short time, to your own governing part. Consider what this thing is that you possess, and where it came from — the thing that uses all the others and tests them and picks and rejects.

But so long as you busy yourself with externals, you will possess those as no other man does — and you will have this thing exactly as you choose to have it: dirty and neglected.

Chapter 8: Against Those Who Rush to Put On the Philosopher's Cloak

Never praise or blame a man on account of things that are common to everybody, and do not credit him with any skill or lack of skill on that account; and so you will be free of rashness and of spite.

This man bathes very quickly. Does he do wrong, then? Certainly not. What does he do? He bathes very quickly. Is everything done well, then? By no means: acts that come from right judgments are done well, and acts that come from bad judgments are done badly. But until you know the judgment a man does each thing from, neither praise nor blame the act.

And a judgment is not easily discovered from external acts. This man is a carpenter. Why? Because he uses an axe. And what has that to do with it? This man is a musician, because he sings. And what does that prove? This man is a philosopher — because he wears a cloak and long hair. And what does a street conjurer wear?

So when a man sees a philosopher behaving indecently, he says at once, "Look what the philosopher is doing!" — when because of the indecent behaviour he ought rather to say that the man is not a philosopher.

If the preconception of a philosopher, and what he professes, were to wear a cloak and long hair, men would be right. But if what he professes is rather to keep himself free of faults, why do we not take the name of philosopher away from him, since he does not make his professions good?

That is what we do in every other art. When a man sees another handling an axe badly, he does not say, "What is the use of carpentry? See how badly carpenters do their work." He says the opposite: this man is not a carpenter, because he uses an axe badly. In the same way, if a man hears somebody singing badly, he does not say, "See how musicians sing," but rather, "This man is no musician."

It is only in the matter of philosophy that people do otherwise. When they see a man acting against the profession of a philosopher, they do not take his title away; they assume he is a philosopher, and then, from his acts and the fact that he is behaving indecently, they conclude that philosophy is of no use.

What is the reason for that? Because we set a value on the notion of a carpenter, and of a musician, and of the other craftsmen likewise, and not on the notion of a philosopher; and we judge from externals only that it is a confused and ill-defined thing.

And what other kind of art takes its name from dress and hair, and has no theorems and no material and no end? What is the material of the philosopher? Is it a cloak? No — reason. What is his end? To wear a cloak? No — to have his reason in a right state. What sort of theorems are his? Are they about how the beard is to be grown big or the hair long? No: rather what Zeno says — to know the elements of reason, what each of them is like, and how they are fitted to one another, and what follows from them.

Will you not see first whether he does what he professes, when he behaves unbecomingly, before you blame his pursuit? As it is, when you are behaving soberly yourself, you say, because of what he seems to you to be doing wrong, "Look at the philosopher" — as though it were proper to call a man who does these things a philosopher; and further, "This is how a philosopher behaves."

But you do not say "Look at the carpenter" when you know a carpenter is an adulterer, or when you see him a glutton; and you do not say "See the musician." So up to a point even you understand what a philosopher professes — and then you fall away from the notion and get confused through carelessness.

But even the men called philosophers themselves go after the thing by starting from what is common to them and everybody else. As soon as they have put on a cloak and grown a beard, they say, "I am a philosopher." No man will say "I am a musician" because he has bought a plectrum and a lute, or "I am a smith" because he has put on a cap and an apron. The dress fits the art, and men take their name from the art and not from the dress.

This is why Euphrates used to put it so well. "For a long time," he said, "I tried to be a philosopher without anybody knowing it, and it was useful to me. In the first place, I knew that when I did anything well I did it for my own sake and not for the spectators. I ate well for my own sake; I kept my expression and my walk composed — all of it for myself and for God. And then, as I struggled alone, so I was alone in the danger. If I did anything base or unbecoming, philosophy was not endangered through me; and I did not injure the many by doing anything wrong as a philosopher. So the men who did not know my purpose used to wonder how it was that, though I talked and lived entirely among philosophers, I was not a philosopher myself. And what was the harm in being known for a philosopher by my acts instead of by outward marks?"

See how I eat, how I drink, how I sleep, how I bear and forbear, how I work with others, how I employ desire and how avoidance, how I keep the relations that are natural and the ones I have taken on, how free of confusion, how free of blocking. Judge me from that, if you can. And if you are so deaf and blind that you cannot conceive even Hephaestus to be a good smith unless you see the cap on his head, what is the harm in going unrecognised by so foolish a judge?

Socrates too was not known to most people to be a philosopher; men used to come to him and ask to be introduced to philosophers. Was he annoyed, as we are, and did he say, "And do you not think I am a philosopher?" No: he would take them and introduce them, satisfied with one thing, with being a philosopher — and pleased, too, at not being thought one, and not annoyed, because he was thinking about his own occupation.

What is the work of an honourable and good man? To have many pupils? By no means; the men who are keen on that will see to it. Was it his business to examine difficult theorems carefully? Others will see to that as well. In what, then, was he, and who was he, and whom did he want to be? He was engaged in the thing where harm and advantage lie.

If any man can damage me, he says, I am doing nothing. If I am waiting for another man to do me good, I am nothing. If I want something and it does not happen, I am unlucky. To such a contest he invited every man, and I do not think he would have refused the contest with anybody.

What do you suppose — was it by proclaiming, "I am such a man"? Far from it: by being such a man. Because this is exactly the mark of a fool and a boaster, to say, "I am free of the passions and of disturbance. Do not be unaware, my friends, that while you are uneasy and disturbed about things of no value, I alone am free of all disturbance."

So is it not enough for you to feel no pain, unless you make this announcement: "Gather round, all of you with gout, headaches, fever; you who are lame and blind — and observe that I am sound and free of every ailment"? That is empty and unpleasant to hear, unless like Asclepius you are able to show at once what treatment will free them from their disease too, and unless you show your own health as an example.

For such is the Cynic whom Zeus honours with the staff and the crown, and who says: so that you may see, gentlemen, that you are looking for happiness and peace of mind not where they are but where they are not — look, I have been sent to you by God as an example. I have no property, no house, no wife, no children, not even a bed, or a coat, or a piece of household furniture; and see how healthy I am. Test me; and if you see that I am undisturbed, hear the remedies and how I was cured.

That is both humane and noble. But see whose work it is: the work of Zeus, or of the man Zeus may judge worthy of the service, so that he shall never show the many anything by which he would cancel out his own testimony — the testimony he gives for virtue and against externals:

	His handsome face does not grow pale, nor does he
	wipe a tear from his cheeks.
	— Odyssey, xi 524

And not only that: he neither desires nor looks for anything — not a man, not a place, not an amusement, as children look forward to the vintage or the holidays. He is always fortified by self-respect, as other men are fortified by walls and doors and doorkeepers.

But as it is, these men are merely stirred toward philosophy the way men with a bad stomach are stirred toward certain foods they will soon be sick of — and they rush straight at the staff and the royal power. They grow their hair, they put on the cloak, they bare one shoulder, they quarrel with the men they meet; and if they see a man in a thick winter coat, they quarrel with him.

Man, first train yourself in winter weather. Look at your own inclinations, and see that they are not those of a man with a bad stomach, or of a woman with a craving. First make sure that nobody knows what you are; be a philosopher to yourself for a while.

This is how fruit grows: the seed has to be buried for a time, hidden, growing slowly, so that it may come to perfection. If it produces the ear before the stem has jointed, it is imperfect — the produce of an Adonis garden. You are that sort of poor plant: you have flowered too soon, and the cold will scorch you.

See what farmers say about seed when the warm weather comes too early. They are afraid the seed will grow too lush, and then a single frost will catch it and show how forward it was.
