Consider it yourself, my man. You have shot up too soon; you have rushed toward a little fame before the proper season; you think you are something, a fool among fools. The frost will catch you — or rather you have already been frostbitten at the root, while your upper parts still blossom a little, and so you think you are still alive and flourishing.

Let us ripen in the natural way. Why do you strip us bare? Why do you force us? We cannot bear the open air yet. Let the root grow; then let it put out the first joint, then the second, then the third. And then the fruit will force its own way out naturally, even if I do not choose it.

For who that is pregnant and full of such great principles does not also feel his own powers and move toward the acts that answer to them? A bull is not ignorant of his own nature and his own powers when a wild beast appears, and does not wait for somebody to urge him on; nor does a dog when he sees a wild animal.

And if I have the powers of a good man, shall I wait for you to prepare me for my own proper acts? At present I have not got them — believe me. So why do you want me withered before my time, as you have been withered?

Chapter 9: To a Man Who Had Turned Shameless

When you see another man holding office, set against it the fact that you do not have the desire for office. When you see another man rich, see what you possess instead of riches; because if you possess nothing instead of them, you are miserable, but if you do not have the desire for riches, know that you possess more than he does, and something worth much more.

Another man has a handsome wife: you have the satisfaction of not desiring a handsome wife. Do these things seem small to you? And how much would these very men give — the rich, the powerful, the men who live with handsome women — to be able to despise riches and power and the very women they love and enjoy?

Do you not know what the thirst of a man in a fever is like? It is nothing at all like the thirst of a healthy man. A healthy man stops being thirsty when he has drunk; but the sick man is pleased for a moment and then feels sick, turns the drink into bile, vomits, gets cramps, and is thirstier than before.

That is what it is to desire riches and possess riches, to desire power and possess power, to desire a beautiful woman and sleep with her. Add jealousy to it, and the fear of losing the thing you love, and indecent words, indecent thoughts, unseemly acts.

"And what do I lose?" you will say.

My man, you were modest, and you are not now. Have you lost nothing? Instead of Chrysippus and Zeno you read Aristides and Evenus: have you lost nothing? Instead of Socrates and Diogenes you admire the man who is best at corrupting and seducing women.

You want to look handsome and try to make yourself so, though you are not. You like to show off splendid clothes so as to attract women; and if you find some fine hair oil, you imagine you are happy. But once you never gave a thought to any such thing; you only thought about where there might be decent talk, and a man worth knowing, and a generous idea. And so you slept like a man, walked out like a man, wore a man's dress, and talked in a way that becomes a good man.

And then you say to me, "I have lost nothing"? Do men lose nothing but coin, then? Is self-respect not lost? Is decent behaviour not lost? Has a man who has lost these suffered no loss?

Perhaps you think not one of these is a loss. But there was a time when you counted this the only loss and damage there was, and were anxious that nobody should shift you from those good words and acts.

Notice: you have been shifted from those good words and acts by nobody but yourself. Fight with yourself; restore yourself to decency, to self-respect, to liberty.

If anybody had ever told you this about me — that somebody was forcing me to commit adultery, to wear clothes like yours, to perfume myself with oils — would you not have gone and killed the man who slandered me with your own hand? And now will you not help yourself? And how much easier this help is. There is no need to kill anybody, or put him in chains, or insult him, or go to law: you need only speak to yourself, who will be the most easily persuaded of all, and over whom nobody has more power of persuasion than you have.

First of all, condemn what you are doing; and then, having condemned it, do not despair of yourself, and do not be like those mean-spirited men who, having once given in, surrender completely and are carried off as if by a flood.

See what the trainers of boys do. Has the boy fallen? Get up, they say, and wrestle again until you are made strong. Do something of the same kind yourself — for be assured that nothing is more workable than the human soul. You have only to exercise the will, and the thing is done, it is set right. And on the other hand, only nod off, and the thing is lost; because ruin comes from within, and so does help.

"And then what good do I get?"

And what greater good than this are you looking for? From a shameless man you will become a modest one; from a disorderly man, an orderly one; from a faithless man, a faithful one; from a man of unbridled habits, a sober one. If you are looking for anything more than that, go on doing what you are doing: not even a god can help you now.

Chapter 10: What We Should Despise and What We Should Value

All men's difficulties are about external things; their helplessness is about externals. What shall I do? How will it be? How will it turn out? Will this happen, or that? These are all the words of men who are turning toward things outside the power of the will.

Who says, "How am I to avoid assenting to what is false? How am I to avoid turning away from the truth?" If a man has a good enough disposition to be anxious about these things, I shall remind him: why are you anxious? The thing is in your own power. Be easy; do not be hasty in assenting before you apply the natural rule.

On the other side, if a man is uneasy about his desire, in case it fails of its purpose and misses its end, and about his avoidance, in case he falls into what he would avoid, then first I shall love him for it, because he throws away the things other men are in a flutter about, and their fears, and turns his thoughts to his own affairs and his own condition.

Then I shall say to him: if you do not choose to desire what you will fail to get, or to try to avoid what you will fall into, then desire nothing that belongs to others and try to avoid nothing that is not in your power. If you do not keep that rule, you must necessarily fail in your desires and fall into what you would avoid.

Where is the difficulty in that? Where is there room for the words "How will it be?" and "How will it turn out?" and "Will this happen or that?"

Now, is what will happen not outside the will? Yes. And is the nature of good and evil not in the things within the power of the will? Yes. Then is it in your power to treat whatever happens according to nature? Can anybody stop you? Nobody.

So do not say to me any longer, "How will it be?" However it may be, you will dispose of it well, and the result will be a fortunate one for you.

What would Hercules have been if he had said, "How am I to keep a great lion from appearing to me — or a great boar, or savage men"? And what do you care about that? If a great boar appears, you will fight a greater fight; if bad men appear, you will rid the earth of bad men.

"Suppose I lose my life that way, then."

You will die a good man, doing a noble act. Since we must certainly die, a man must necessarily be found doing something — following the farmer's work, or digging, or trading, or serving in a consulship, or suffering from indigestion or from dysentery.

What do you want to be doing when death finds you? For my part I should want to be found doing something that belongs to a man: something generous, useful to the common interest, noble. And if I cannot be found doing anything so great, then at least doing the thing I cannot be blocked from doing, the thing that is permitted me: correcting myself, cultivating the capacity that uses impressions, working at freedom from the passions, giving the relations of life their due. And if I get that far, then also touching on the third topic, security in forming judgments about things.

If death catches me busy about these things, it is enough for me that I can stretch out my hands to God and say: the means I received from you for seeing your administration and following it, I have not neglected. I have not dishonoured you by my acts. See how I have used my senses; see how I have used my preconceptions. Have I ever blamed you? Have I ever been discontented with anything that happens, or wished it otherwise? Have I ever wished to break the relations of things? That you gave me life, I thank you for what you gave. As long as I have used what is yours, I am content; take it back and put it wherever you choose, because it was all yours, and you gave it to me.

Is it not enough to depart in that state of mind? And what life is better and more becoming than that of a man in that state of mind? And what end is happier?

But for this to be possible, a man must take on no small things, and the things he must go without are not small either. You cannot both wish to be consul and to have this, and be eager for lands and have this too, and be anxious about slaves and about yourself. If you wish for anything that belongs to another, what is your own is lost.

That is the nature of the thing: nothing is given or had for nothing. And what is surprising in that? If you want to be consul, you must go without sleep, run about, kiss hands, wear yourself out at other men's doors, say and do a great many things unworthy of a free man, send gifts to many and daily presents to some.

And what is the thing you get? Twelve bundles of rods; to sit three or four times on the tribunal; to put on the games in the Circus and give dinners in little baskets. Or if you do not agree, let somebody show me what there is besides these.

And then, in order to secure freedom from the passions, and peace, and sleeping well when you sleep and being really awake when you are awake, and fearing nothing and being anxious about nothing — will you spend nothing and give no labour?

But if something of yours is lost while you are busy at this, or badly wasted, or another man gets what you should have got, will you be annoyed at once at what has happened? Will you not set on the other side of the account what you are getting, and for what — how much for how much?

Do you expect to have things so great for nothing? And how could you? One kind of work has nothing in common with another. You cannot have both external things, after taking care of them, and your own governing part. If you want those, give this up. If you do not, you will have neither the one nor the other, while you are pulled both ways.

The oil will be spilled, the household pots will be smashed — very well; but I shall be free of the passions. There will be a fire when I am not at home, and the books will be destroyed — but I shall treat impressions according to nature.

"Well, but I shall have nothing to eat." If I am so unlucky, death is a harbour. Death is the harbour for everybody; this is the refuge; and that is why nothing in life is difficult. As soon as you choose, you are out of the house, and the smoke does not trouble you any more.

Why are you anxious, then? Why do you lose your sleep? Why do you not say at once, having considered where your good and your evil lie: both of them are in my power. Nobody can deprive me of the good, and nobody can involve me in the bad against my will. Why do I not throw myself down and snore? Everything I have is safe.

As for the things that belong to others, the man who gets them will see to them, as they are handed out by the one who has the power. Who am I to want them this way or that? Was a power of choosing them given to me? Has anybody made me the distributor of them? What I have power over is enough for me: I ought to manage that as well as I can, and let the master of the rest choose about the rest.

When a man has these things before his eyes, does he lie awake and toss about? What would he have, and what does he regret — Patroclus, or Antilochus, or Menelaus? When did he ever suppose that any friend of his was immortal? When did he not have it in front of his eyes that tomorrow or the day after, he or his friend must die?

"Yes," he says, "but I thought he would outlive me and bring up my son."

You were a fool for that, and you were reckoning on what was uncertain. Then why do you not blame yourself, instead of sitting crying like a girl?

"But he used to set my food in front of me."

Because he was alive, you fool; now he cannot. Automedon will set it in front of you, and if Automedon dies too, you will find somebody else. If the pot your meat was cooked in gets broken, must you die of hunger because you have not got the pot you were used to? Do you not send out and buy a new pot?

He says:

	No greater evil than this could fall on me.
	— Iliad xix 321.

Why is this your evil? Then instead of getting rid of it, do you blame your mother for not foretelling it to you, so that you might have gone on grieving from that moment?

What do you think — do you not suppose Homer wrote this so that we might learn that men of the noblest birth, the strongest, the richest, the handsomest, are not prevented from being utterly wretched and unlucky, when they do not hold the judgments they ought to hold?

Chapter 11: On Cleanliness

Some people raise the question whether the social feeling is contained in man's nature. And yet I think these same people would have no doubt that a love of cleanliness certainly is; and that if man is distinguished from the other animals by anything, he is distinguished by this.

When we see some other animal cleaning itself, we usually remark on it with surprise and add that the animal is behaving like a man. And on the other hand, if a man blames an animal for being dirty, we say at once, as though making an excuse for it, that of course the animal is not a human creature. So we suppose there is something superior in man, and that we first receive it from the gods.

Since the gods are by nature pure and free of corruption, then so far as men come near them by reason, so far do they hold to cleanliness and to the habit of it. But since it is impossible for a man's nature to be altogether pure, being mixed out of such materials, reason is applied as far as it can be, and reason tries to make human nature love cleanliness.

The first and highest cleanliness, then, is the cleanliness in the soul; and we say the same about dirt. Now you could not detect dirt in the soul as you could on the body; but in the soul, what else could you find but what makes her filthy with regard to the acts that are her own? And the acts of the soul are movement toward an object and away from it, desire, avoidance, preparation, purpose, assent.

So what is it in these acts that makes the soul filthy and unclean? Nothing but her own bad judgments. Therefore the dirt of the soul is her bad opinions, and the cleaning of the soul is the planting of proper opinions in it; and the clean soul is the one that has proper opinions, since the soul alone in her own acts is free of disturbance and pollution.

Now we ought to work at something like this in the body too, so far as we can. It was impossible for the nose not to run, when man has such a mixture in his body; and so nature made hands, and made the nostrils themselves as channels for carrying the fluids off. So if a man sniffs them back up, I say he is not doing a man's act.

It was impossible for a man's feet not to get muddy and dirty when he goes through dirty places; and so nature made water, and hands. It was impossible for no dirt to be left in the teeth after eating; and so, she says, clean the teeth. Why? So that you may be a man and not a wild beast or a hog.

It was impossible that from sweat and the pressure of clothes some dirt should not be left on the body needing to be cleaned off; and so water, oil, hands, towels, scrapers, soda and sometimes every other kind of means are necessary for cleaning it.

You do not behave so. But the smith will take the rust off the iron and will have tools ready for the purpose; and you wash the dish yourself when you are going to eat, unless you are altogether filthy and dirty. And will you not wash your body and make it clean?

"Why?" he answers.

I will tell you again. In the first place, so that you may do a man's acts; and then, so that you may not be disagreeable to the people you spend your time with.

You are doing something of the kind even in this, and you do not notice it: you think you deserve to stink. Let it be so — deserve to stink. But do you think that the men who sit beside you, who recline at table with you, who kiss you, deserve it too? Either go off into a desert, where you deserve to go, or live by yourself and smell yourself; because it is only just that you should have the enjoyment of your own filth to yourself. But when you are in a city, to behave so thoughtlessly and foolishly — what sort of character do you think that belongs to?

If nature had entrusted a horse to you, would you have overlooked him and neglected him? Consider now that you have been entrusted with your own body as with a horse. Wash it, wipe it, take care that nobody turns away from it and nobody gets out of its way. And who does not get out of the way of a dirty man, a stinking man, a man whose skin is foul — more than he gets out of the way of a man smeared with dung? That smell is from outside and put on him; the other comes from want of care, from inside, and from a body in a manner rotting.

"But Socrates washed himself seldom."

Yes — but his body was clean and handsome, and so agreeable and pleasant that the most beautiful and the most noble young men loved him and wanted to sit beside him rather than beside men with the handsomest bodies. He could have gone without the bath and without washing altogether if he had chosen; and even so his rare use of water had its effect. If you will not wash in warm water, wash in cold.

But Aristophanes says:

	The pale ones, the unshod — those are the men I mean.
	— The Clouds, line 102.

Aristophanes also says of Socrates that he walked on air and stole clothes from the wrestling school. But everybody who has written about Socrates gives exactly the contrary evidence in his favour: they say he was pleasant not only to hear but to look at. And they write the same about Diogenes.

For we ought not to put the crowd off philosophy even by the look of the body. As in everything else, a philosopher should show himself cheerful and untroubled; and so he should in the things that concern the body. See, gentlemen, that I have nothing and want nothing; see how I am without a house, without a city, an exile if it happens so, and without a hearth — and I live with less trouble and more happily than all the well-born and the rich. And look at my poor body too, and see that it is not damaged by my hard way of living.

But if a man says this to me who has the look and the face of a condemned criminal — what god will persuade me to come near philosophy, if it makes men like that? Far from it. I would not choose to, even if it were going to make me a wise man.

For my part I would rather a young man taking his first steps toward philosophy came to me with his hair carefully trimmed than dirty and rough; because there is a certain notion of beauty in him, and a desire for what is becoming, and where he thinks it is, there he tries to have it. It is only necessary to show him what it is, and to say: young man, you are looking for beauty, and you do well. Then you should know that it grows in that part of you where you have the rational faculty. Look for it where you have your movements toward things and away from them, where you have your desires and your avoidances; that is what you have in you of a superior kind. The poor body is by nature only earth. Why do you labour over it for nothing? If you learn nothing else, in time you will learn that the body is nothing.

But if a man comes to me smeared with filth, dirty, with a moustache down to his knees, what can I say to him? By what likeness am I to lead him on? What has he busied himself with that resembles beauty, so that I could change him and say: beauty is not in this, but in that? Would you have me tell him that beauty does not consist in being smeared with dung but lies in the rational part? Has he any desire for beauty? Has he any picture of it in his mind? Go and talk to a hog and tell him not to roll in the mud.

This is why the words of Xenocrates touched Polemo — because he was a lover of beauty. He came into the room with certain sparks in him of the love of beauty, but he was looking for it in the wrong place.

Nature has not even made the animals dirty that live with man. Does a horse ever wallow in mud, or a well-bred dog? The hog does, and the filthy geese, and worms and spiders, which are kept furthest from human company. Then will you, being a man, choose not to be like the animals that live with men, but rather like a worm or a spider?

Will you not wash yourself somewhere, sometime, in whatever way you choose? Will you not wash the dirt off your body? Will you not come clean, so that the people who keep company with you may take pleasure in being with you? And do you come with us into the temples in this state, where it is not permitted to spit or blow the nose — being a heap of spit and snot yourself?

What then? Does anybody require you to adorn yourself? Far from it — except to adorn what we really are by nature: the rational faculty, the judgments, the actions. As for the body, only so far as cleanliness goes, only so far as not to give offence.

But if you are told that you ought not to wear clothes dyed with purple, off you go and smear your cloak with dung, or tear it.

"But how am I to have a neat cloak?"

Man, you have water. Wash it.

Here is a young man worth loving; here is an old man worth loving and being loved by in return; a fit person to be entrusted with a son's education, to whom daughters and young men shall come as the occasion arises — so that the teacher may deliver his lessons to them on a dunghill.

Let it not be so. Every deviation comes from something that is in man's nature; but this deviation comes near to being something that is not in man's nature at all.

Chapter 12: On Attention

When you have let your attention go for a short time, do not imagine you will get it back whenever you choose. Have this thought ready instead: because of the fault you committed today, your affairs must be in a worse state for everything that follows.

In the first place — and this causes the most trouble — a habit of not attending is formed in you; and then a habit of putting your attention off. And by putting it off, you drive away, again and again, the happiness of life, and proper behaviour, and being and living in line with nature.

If putting attention off is profitable, then leaving it out altogether is more profitable still. And if it is not profitable, why do you not keep your attention going all the time?

"Today I want to play."

Well — ought you not to play with attention?

"I want to sing."

And what stops you singing with attention? Is there any part of life left out, that attention does not reach to? Will you do a thing worse by using attention and better by not attending at all?

And what else in life is done better by men who do not attend? Does a man who works in wood work better by not attending? Does the captain of a ship handle her better by not attending? Is any of the smaller acts done better by inattention?

Do you not see that once you have let your mind loose, it is no longer in your power to call it back — either to decency, or to self-respect, or to moderation? You do whatever comes into your head, obeying your inclinations.

What ought I to attend to, then? First to the general principles, and to having them ready — not sleeping, not getting up, not drinking, not eating, not spending time with men, without them. That no man is master of another man's will, and that in the will alone lie the good and the bad. So no man has the power either to procure any good for me or to involve me in any evil; I alone have power over myself in these things.

And when these things are secured to me, why do I need to be disturbed about externals? What tyrant is formidable? What disease? What poverty? What insult from anybody?

Well — I have not pleased a certain person. Is pleasing him my work, my judgment? No. Then why should I trouble myself about him? "But he is supposed to be somebody." He will see to that himself, and so will the men who think it. But I have one whom I ought to please, one I ought to submit to and obey: God, and those next after him.

He has placed me with myself, and put my will under my own control alone, and given me rules for the right use of it. And when I follow those rules in syllogisms, I do not care about any man who says something different; in trick arguments I care about nobody. Then why, in greater matters, am I annoyed by men who blame me?

What is the cause of this disturbance? Nothing but that I am not trained in this matter. For every science despises ignorance and the ignorant — and not only the sciences but the crafts. Produce any shoemaker you like, and he laughs at the crowd where his own trade is concerned. Produce any carpenter.

So first we ought to have these rules ready and do nothing without them, and we ought to keep the soul aimed at this mark: to chase nothing external, nothing that belongs to others, but to do as the one who has the power has appointed. We should chase the things within the power of the will altogether, and everything else as it is permitted.

Next to this, we ought to remember who we are and what our name is, and try to direct our duties toward the character of each of our relations: what is the season for singing, what the season for play, and in whose presence; what the consequence of the act will be; whether the men with us will despise us or we shall despise them; when to make a joke, and whom to laugh at; and on what occasion to go along with people and with whom — and, in going along with them, how to keep our own character.

Wherever you have wandered from any of these rules, the damage is immediate — not from anything external, but from the act itself.

What then? Is it possible to be free of faults, if you do all this? It is not possible; but this is possible: to direct your efforts unceasingly at being faultless. We must be content if, by never letting this attention go, we escape at least a few errors.

But when you say, "Tomorrow I will start paying attention," you must be told that what you are saying is: "Today I will be shameless, careless of time and place, mean. It will be in other men's power to give me pain. Today I will be quick-tempered and envious."

See how many evil things you are allowing yourself. If it is good to use attention tomorrow, how much better to use it today? If it is in your interest to attend tomorrow, much more today — so that you may be able to do it tomorrow too, and not put it off again to the day after.

Chapter 13: Against Those Who Too Readily Tell Their Own Business

When a man seems to us to have talked frankly about his own affairs, how is it that we are drawn into revealing our own secrets to him in turn, and think this is being frank ourselves?

In the first place, because it seems unfair to have listened to a neighbour's business and not tell him ours in return. And next, because we think we shall not look like frank men if we say nothing about our own affairs. Indeed men often say, "I have told you all my business, and will you tell me nothing of yours? Where is that done?"

And besides, we have the idea that we can safely trust a man who has already told us his own business, because the notion comes into our minds that this man could never give our affairs away, since he would be careful that we should not give away his.

This is also how the incautious are caught by the soldiers at Rome. A soldier sits down beside you in ordinary clothes and begins to speak ill of Caesar; and then you, as though you had been given a pledge of his good faith by his having started the abuse, say what you think as well — and then you are led away in chains.

Something of the kind generally happens to us too. Now this man has confidently entrusted his affairs to me: am I to do the same with any man I meet? No — because when I have heard, I keep quiet, if that is my disposition; but he goes out and tells everybody what he has heard.

And then if I hear what he has done, if I am a man like him, I decide to get my own back and give away what he told me. I disturb others and am disturbed myself. But if I remember that one man does not injure another, and that every man's own acts injure or profit him, then I secure this much: that I do nothing like him. And still I suffer what I suffer, through my own silly talking.

"True — but it is unfair, when you have heard your neighbour's secrets, to tell him nothing in return."

Did I ask you for your secrets, my man? Did you tell me your affairs on terms, that you should hear mine in return? If you are a babbler and think everybody you meet is a friend, do you want me to be like you too?

And why, if you did well in entrusting your affairs to me, and it is not well for me to entrust mine to you, do you want me to be so rash? It is exactly as if I had a watertight cask and you had one with a hole in it, and you came and deposited your wine with me so that I could put it in my cask, and then complained that I would not entrust my wine to you — because you have a cask with a hole in it.

Where is the equality there? You entrusted your affairs to a man who is faithful and self-respecting, a man who thinks that only his own actions harm or help him and that nothing external does. And would you have me entrust mine to you — a man who has dishonoured his own faculty of will, and who wants to gain some small piece of money, or some office, or promotion at court, even if you were going to murder your own children like Medea?

Where is the fairness in that? Show me instead that you are faithful, self-respecting and steady; show me that your judgments are friendly ones; show me that your cask has no hole in it — and you will see how I shall not wait for you to trust me with your affairs, but shall come to you myself and ask you to listen to mine.

For who does not choose to use a good vessel? Who does not value a kindly and faithful adviser? Who will not gladly welcome a man ready to take a share, as we might say, in the difficulty of his circumstances, and by that very act to lighten the load by taking part of it?

"True — but I trust you, and you do not trust me."

In the first place, you do not even trust me. You are a babbler, and for that reason you cannot hold anything in. Indeed, if it were true that you trust me, you would entrust your affairs to me alone. But as it is, whenever you see a man at leisure you sit down beside him and say: "Brother, I have no friend kinder than you or dearer; I ask you to listen to my affairs." And you do it even to men who are not known to you at all.

And if you really do trust me, plainly you trust me because I am faithful and self-respecting, not because I have told you my affairs. So allow me to hold the same opinion about you.

Show me that when one man tells another his affairs, the man who tells them is faithful and self-respecting. If that were so, I would go about telling my affairs to every man, if that would make me faithful and self-respecting. But it is not so; it takes principles that are not common.

So if you see a man busy about things that do not depend on his will, and putting his will in subjection to them, you must understand that this man has ten thousand people to compel him and block him. He needs no pitch and no wheel to make him declare what he knows: a little girl's nod, if it comes to that, will move him — the coaxing of somebody from Caesar's court, the desire for an office or an inheritance, and endless things of the same kind.

So among your general principles you must remember that talk about secret matters requires good faith, and requires judgments to match it. And where are we to find those easily now? Or if you cannot answer that, let somebody point me out a man who can say: I care only about what is my own, about what cannot be blocked, about what is free by nature. This I hold to be the nature of the good; and let everything else be as it is allowed to be. I do not concern myself.
